# Bushido Coda

_In the dystopian sprawl of the GLMZ — the megacity that swallowed the Midwest — sovereign CorpoNations control everything except the seams between them: the Gray Zones, where the only law is: do the job, get paid, and don't ask questions.

Kyle Ellen Corbin has worked that economy for eleven years. He vets the job, names the price, and delivers. Long enough to build a code he actually holds to, keep a short list of people he will not leave behind, and know which contracts are worth taking before the brief is finished.

BUSHIDO CODA is the flagship GLMZ novel — a story about eleven years of clean work, a man who never broke his code, and the one contract that explains all the others._

[Beat 1]
Kyle felt the frequency before he saw it. A low hum in the 17-to-19 hertz band — a schism, the kind of tear in the world the city had learned to route around. Four seconds later his eyes found the proof: a cigarette pack, two pebbles, a length of wire, and what might have been the clip off a lanyard, all turning in a slow circle around one fixed spot in the turn lane at 14th and Blue Island.

The circle was slow. The pebbles moved against the wind.

He measured how wide it ran and took Racine instead. Nothing dramatic — Racine was fine, and the extra three minutes still put him at the site on time. The schism might be gone in half an hour or it might last three days. There was no way to know without a tracking team, and nobody was going to send a tracking team for a cigarette pack circling a crack in the asphalt at 14th and Blue Island. The morning's Schism Advisory for Zone 4 had listed two active ones. He had been expecting this one.

He kept walking.

## Part I: Teeth

[Beat 2]
She said his name wrong. Three times.

Kyle didn't correct her. The apartment smelled like camphor and fear-sweat and something frying in old oil three doors down. He'd been standing in it for twenty minutes — long enough to stop noticing the smell and start belonging to it. Her husband sat in the corner with his jaw wired shut, breathing slow through his nose. The jaw no longer got a vote.

Kyle took a step toward the corner, toward the husband, to see the shape of what had been done. The woman moved. She put herself between them, back straight, the payment already out in her hand. "This part's done." Kyle stopped.

She pressed the credstick into his palm. Warm chip, a little damp. He didn't check the balance while she watched. Reading it in front of her would be its own small theft.

He looked at it in the hall.

Φ180. Half his usual rate, for a job that had cost him three hours and his left shoulder. He stood there with the stick in his palm and didn't make a thing of the number. He thought about how she'd gripped it before she handed it over — the grip of someone who'd been carrying it in her fist since before she decided to make the call.

He pocketed it and took the stairs down.

The katana rode across his back in a plain matte-black sheath. Carbon-nanotube composite, no markings, nothing that could earn a name. Cacophony rode low on his left hip — six rounds in the cylinder, hammer resting, the way it always rested. He walked out of the building and into the GLMZ night like a man who'd done nothing worth remembering. That was the point. It took effort.

[Beat 3]
He'd taken the job at half rate and told himself it was because of the jaw. The jaw, wired shut. A man cut down to breathing and waiting. What he didn't let himself count was the girl in the hallway, behind the curtain, watching him the way children watch a thing they've already decided to remember.

[Beat 4]
The posting had come in through the standing relay — the same Axiom trunk that had been routing him work for years. The client field resolved to a shell, and the shell resolved to nothing. It always did. He'd stopped finding that interesting.

The rate was half what he would have asked. He asked for nothing; he just took it. The relay had a way of knowing what he'd accept before he accepted it, and years ago he'd decided that was a feature of a good service, not a thing worth poking at.

So he didn't poke at it. He didn't ask what kind of operation could know, before it even made the offer, that he would take half rate on a distress call. He didn't dwell on the last three jobs, which had also landed inside the exact weight he could carry, at rates within two percent of what he'd have quoted. He was good at the work. The work paid. The relay found him the work. He wasn't a suspicious man — not in the ways that would have protected him here.

He checked his gear in the freight elevator. Silence in the saya. Cacophony loaded, two shells in reserve. The building smelled like camphor.

[Beat 5]
The building was less a building than a welded argument between two towers, wedged into the gap and forgotten by the CorpoNation property registry sometime in the 2190s. On the underside of the maintenance rail that bridged the gap, someone had chalked hash marks in tidy groups of seven. The last group had stopped at seven and started fresh. It never ran to eight.

His motorcycle sat under an Axiom cable gantry on the block below — matte black, unbranded, the kind of bike that vanished into the visual noise of any GLMZ block.

At Cermak and Loomis the light did the thing it sometimes did at Cermak and Loomis. The shadows of the elevated signage fell at the wrong angle for the hour. A 19-hertz hum came from no direction and every direction at once. A bellwether rat sounded its two-tone cry, high then low, and along the wet storefronts people stepped back into their doorways and stayed there while he passed.

He rode through it and didn't stop.

[Beat 6]
He rode south to the Gray Zone's eastern strut — twenty-two minutes of switchbacks, down through tiers nobody had bothered to pretend were planned since somewhere around the fortieth floor. The rain got warmer the lower he went. By the bottom of the strut the buildings overhead had closed the sky down to a strip, and when he put a hand on the strut wall to steady the bike on the last grade, the ferrocement was warm under his palm. An hour past the last of the light, the way it always was down here. He didn't think about that either.

What rode down with him was the jaw. The camphor. The half rate, warm and damp, pressed into his palm by a stranger who'd been holding it in her fist since before she decided to make the call.

[Beat 7]
He parked the bike a block out and walked the rest. The crew on the loading dock wouldn't be expecting him. Surprise was free.

The dock sat at the bottom of the eastern strut, where the architecture stopped pretending to be architecture and became what it was — welded, industrial, grim. Kyle came in the front. The personnel door beside the roll-up had a steel pull handle worn mirror-bright in one long oval, the exact shape of ten thousand hands closing on it in the same place while the rest of the door went on rusting around it. No hood. No sideways angle in. He wanted the moment — the exact moment when six people who'd spent weeks feeling like a force of nature looked up and saw the weather walk in the door.

[Beat 8]
The katana across his back was matte black — a forty-inch draw, the hilt riding over his right shoulder, the ito wrap gone dark with use. The blade had a name. Silence. Where the name came from he could half remember if he pressed on it, the way you can half remember a room by its smell. He didn't press. He'd tried once, years ago, and decided the digging wasn't worth what it turned up.

[Beat 9]
On his low left hip, holstered but not strapped down, sat the Torii TSS-3 — bird's-head grip, no stock, the shape of an oversized revolver under a coat. Six .410 shells in the cylinder. Cacophony. This one he'd named himself, for the sound it made.

Silence was for the rooms he could cross on his own feet. Cacophony was for the ones he couldn't — the distance the body couldn't eat fast enough, the angle the blade couldn't reach. Six shells meant six chances to learn he should have planned better. Kyle planned for a living, so Cacophony mostly stayed quiet. That was the joke of it: the loudest thing he carried was the one he least wanted to use.

[Beat 10]
Kyle stepped across the line that separates the weather from the room, and stood still, and let them look.

One of them had a chrome arm — a full replacement, the shoulder-socket kind, packed with myomer and hydraulics, strong enough to punch through light vehicle plating. Across the table sat a woman his array flagged before Kyle's eyes found the reason: slight, still, her weight already on the balls of her feet, hardware nested at every hinge of her. Actuator housings at the wrists, the elbows, the knees, the toes of her boots. Retractable blades. Someone had rebuilt her joint by joint into a knife block — a Splicer with skill, working for a buyer with intent, nothing about it for show. A third man had sub-dermal plating under his skin, the seams showing at his collar like a man wearing himself wrong. A fourth had a jaw modification done badly enough to pull the skin tight down the left side of his face — cheap and fast, a man who'd had his face broken before and hadn't kept the original parts. The fifth was younger, chrome only at the knuckles: discharge plates, the kind that threw sparks on contact and let a twenty-year-old hit like a forklift, as long as whatever he hit agreed to hold still. He was already rolling his shoulder. The sixth had no augmentation Kyle could see. Which meant it was inside him, where it was expensive. The man who'd paid for expensive and invisible was the one giving the orders. Kyle tracked him first.

Kyle's eyes found the jaw man last — the bad work, the wired mouth. His hand dropped to Cacophony. Six bodies, already sorted. The chrome-arm man was out of his chair before the cylinder cleared the holster: one step, the chrome hand closing around Kyle's wrist. Not squeezing. Just there. Kyle let the grip go.

[Beat 11]
They had been playing cards. Drinking something amber from unlabeled bottles. The laughter had died when they saw him — cards still fanned in their hands, one bottle stopped halfway to a mouth.

[Beat 12]
Kyle's chest warmed — the array coming up from idle into the sharpness it saved for rooms like this. A deep heat behind the sternum, cellular, like a coal blown back to life.

The room came into focus. Six bodies. Heat coming off each of them. The capacitor whine of the knuckle plates waking at the card table. The double click of the woman's wrist housings unlocking under her sleeves, quiet as a watch being wound. The boy's shoulder giving away its swing before the swing had finished forming in him.

The NeoCortex called it ballistic precognition. Kyle thought that was the wrong word. The array didn't predict anything. It caught the half-second that was already in motion and showed it to him plainly, and his body was moving before he'd finished the thought.

It showed him one more thing, and this it drew brighter than all the rest: the arteries. Every major line in the room, laid over the bodies in light — the plumbing that kept these men alive. Chrome was fair game. The bright lines were not. Cut everything; cross nothing. That was the whole of his code, and the array held the map so his hand could keep to it.

[Beat 13]
Silence cleared the saya in one unbroken draw — right hand over the right shoulder, the steel and carbon-nanotube blade already alive in the air at the bottom of the motion. No flourish. The spine of the blade was matte black and stayed matte black; the dock's amber overheads found nothing on it to catch, because catching light was not what the blade was for. He brought it across his body and settled into the stance, his right foot sliding back a quarter step into the line Seito had drilled into him — Seito, the only teacher the neuretics had left him a full record of, who'd worked him for nine years before letting him take jobs alone.

The stance was the stance. The one afternoon the foot-line had been corrected for the last time, he couldn't find in memory. The neuretics kept that kind of thing as a result, not a moment. At some point that had stopped bothering him, and he couldn't find that moment either.

[Beat 14]
Cacophony stayed on his hip.

The room could be crossed. He'd settled that before his boot came through the door — twenty-some feet of dock floor, six bodies, a card table, and a route between them the array had drawn in the half-second before anyone thought to stand. The gun was for plans that failed. He hadn't failed to plan.

Silence moved. What the room heard was almost nothing: a chair going over, a bottle ringing on the concrete, the wet exhale of men who understood the weather was already on top of them. Silence found a shoulder tendon — the exact one a man's swing had been counting on — and the arm finished its arc anyway, half a second of momentum running on nothing but memory. The loudest sound on the dock was chrome hitting the floor. The blood made no sound at all. It simply went where the cuts sent it, and every cut had been told where to stop.

[Beat 15]
The boy threw first, because the boy had thrown first his whole short career. The haymaker came up out of the chair with his hips and his heart behind it, the knuckle plates firing on the upswing so the fist came round trailing sparks like a grinder kissing steel. It was a proven punch. It had ended arguments. It had broken jaws — older jaws, slower jaws, jaws on men who'd asked for one more week and stood there hoping while it wound up. Every jaw it broke taught the boy the same wrong lesson: that the punch worked.

The punch worked on people who couldn't afford to move. Kyle had already moved. He stepped inside the arc — not away from it, inside it, into the dead space where a haymaker is just a man falling in a circle — set his palm against the boy's shoulder blade as the weight went past, and helped. Physics did the rest. The card table caught the boy at the waist and folded. Cards and amber liquor went up. The sparking knuckles hit the wet floor and ground their charge out in it, fizzing like the end of a fuse. And somewhere in the middle of that fall, almost in passing, Silence had opened the boy's forearm along the flexor line — an inch and a half, clean, deep enough to unstring the fist, wide of the radial artery by the width of a decision. So when the boy put his hand down to get up, the hand said no. He sat back in the wreck of the table, holding his arm, watching his blood feather into the spilled liquor, and learned what every collector learns sooner or later: the punch had never been the weapon.

[Beat 16]
The second cut found the plating man as he rose — but he rose shooting. A compact auto came off his belt and put three rounds out inside the half-second the array needed to close the line. Two hit Kyle low in the back as he turned through the second man's lane. The third skipped off at an angle and burned a furrow above his hip. The mesh under his skin caught the two square hits the way a net catches a diver — full stop, no punch-through, at a cost the net doesn't advertise. The breath went out of him in one flat slam. His line slid a hand's width off its mark, and for one shortened step the room held the shape of a different ending.

The plating man had built his whole way of fighting on the belief that nothing would reach his vitals, and nothing did. Silence took the power coupling at his collar seam instead — the thumb-wide gap where the thoracic plate drew its charge off his spinal bus. The blade's exit drew a thin red line across the trapezius above it, shallow as a warning, the blood welling up over armor that had kept every promise except the one that counted. Sixty pounds of plate went from asset to anchor the instant the coupling parted. He folded under his own chrome and stayed folded, the pistol skating away across the corrugated steel. He'd been in fights before. He knew he was out of this one before it had properly started. His only move now was to stay still. Still and quiet, bleeding politely — and the only thing of his that died tonight would be hardware.

[Beat 17]
The chrome-arm man was already swinging. That was the right move for a fighter with hydraulic reach and a head start — the arm came around flat and hard, at the height where Kyle's collarbone had been, with enough torque behind it to cave a car door. His array read the motor in it the way it read all chrome: a clean bloom of current, full extension, aimed at his throat.

You don't block an arm like that. You get out from under it. Kyle stepped inside the arc and let a decade of financing swing through the space he'd just left. As it passed over his head he brought Silence up into the shoulder seam — the thumb-wide gap where the actuator cable surfaces between housing and socket, the wire bundle every full-replacement rig carries and every owner forgets. He cut the line before the swing finished. The arm kept going without instructions. Its own weight took the man's balance with it and laid him out across a stack of crates, the limb still flexing on the last of its hydraulic pressure. He stared at it. It was the look of a man watching eleven years of payments go dark in front of him. He did not get back up.

[Beat 18]
The fourth cut took the jaw-rig man. The rig, not the jaw — though the jaw paid its share. Kyle had saved him for last because he'd already seen how it would go: the anchor line of the cheap facial armature, the shear angle, the way the hardware would skid into the drainage channel once it let go. The rig tore free with the sound of cheap work giving up. The gums the bad surgery had used as mounting posts came away bleeding around the anchor points. The man dropped to his knees with both hands at his face, spitting red into the runoff — alive, awake, and ruined the same cheap way he'd been ruined once already and paid to keep instead of fix. The teeth went with the rig. They scattered across the floor like thrown dice.

[Beat 19]
Four seconds. Four cuts. Four men down and not getting up, every one of them alive, the dock floor already threading thin red lines toward the drains. Every line placed. His array carried a map of what a body can lose and still keep living, and Silence had stayed wide of every bright forbidden line — by margins Kyle no longer had to think about. The training was old, learned in a dojo he couldn't quite place anymore, and his hands did the work without asking.

He stood in the middle of it. Two points of deep hurt bloomed under the mesh in his lower back, and a wet line burned above his hip where the third round had opened the skin. The torn jacket showed a few inches of dull grey weave underneath — the lattice doing the one thing it was built for, which was not the same as doing it for free. That was the mistake, and he was wearing it now, and he'd be wearing it tomorrow. His array read chrome — motors, power couplings, the electric bloom of hardware announcing itself. The gun had been nothing but a gun. It never warned him.

Cacophony still held five, the count unspent, because the woman hadn't rushed him. That was the thing worth knowing about her, and it had been worth knowing since the door: while her crew spent itself, she'd stepped back out of the spray, picked clean floor, and let the blades out.

[Beat 20]
They came out of her like good hardware out of good cabinetry. The wrists first — twin lengths of pale-edged alloy running back along the forearms. Then the elbows, short and triangular, built for the turn. When she rose onto the balls of her feet the boot toes opened and showed steel, and a flexed knee showed more. Eight edges on a slight frame, every housing flush, every one silent except for that doubled click she hadn't bothered to hide. His array lit her up at every joint: faster than the boy, smarter than the swing, the only fighter on this dock besides the seated man who had ever trained against people who knew what they were doing.

She came in on an angle that took the card table out of his footwork and put the crates at his back. She'd been reading the dock as a place to fight while her crew read it as furniture. Kyle gave ground once — just enough to put the spilled liquor between them. Wet floor taxes whoever lunges.

[Beat 21]
The first pass was hers. Wrist blade on a backhand line, a feint off the elbow that turned into the knee — three edges inside a second and a half. His array read each one firing its half-second early, so Kyle was never quite where the edges arrived. He didn't counter. A counter is a bet, and he was still reading the table. She spun clear of the third strike, reset her angle, and came again — committed this time, full extension, her whole slight weight behind the wrist blade. From where she stood the half-second was invisible. A man who keeps almost getting hit looks like a man getting lucky, and you press luck before it settles.

The commitment was the opening he'd given ground to build. Kyle turned his hips off the line, caught her forearm on his open palm the way you catch something handed to you, and added four inches to a lunge she'd measured to the inch. Past full extension, a blade is just cargo. While she paid for those four inches, Silence traced the underside of her forearm — not the flesh, the service line, the shallow channel where the wrist cable surfaces between housing and grip. The gap her clinic had promised no one would ever find in a live fight. The bundle parted with a static pop. The wrist blade died at half-extension, locked, dead weight bolted to a living arm.

She was good. She converted without a breath, rolling into the elbow blade on the turn — but it was the same turn his array had now watched twice, and Kyle was already under it. Silence crossed the back of her thigh above the knee housing. A hand's width of opened muscle, wide of the big artery by the same margin as every cut tonight, deep enough that the leg quit. She went down on the knee whose toe blade scraped one bright useless arc across the steel. She didn't scream. She exhaled — long, furious, professional — put her free hand flat on the floor, and pulled back everything that still answered, one joint at a time. It was the surrender of someone who understood exactly what had been left intact, and that it had been left on purpose.

[Beat 22]
Silence found the seams, and where there were no seams it found the depth — the exact point where a body stops fighting and keeps living. That was the whole tool: a sword among chrome, a scalpel among meat, and a hand that knew the difference from one cut to the next. Five down, and not one of them dying. Not because the blade had been gentle. Because it had been exact, and exact was the only mercy on the table.

One of the men had curled toward the drain with both hands pressed to his side, checking whether he still had what he'd started with. The dock floor looked like a chop shop after a raid — an arm, a forearm, a jaw rig, a wrist blade locked at half-mast. Through the salvage the blood ran thin and honest toward the channels. Every line survivable. Every line put there on purpose. That was the part the survivors would think about later, in the quiet, with the lights off.

[Beat 23]
Kyle was already turning.

[Beat 24]
The boss had stood up.

[Beat 25]
Kyle's hand was on Cacophony. Sixteen feet of open floor — the gun's range. He cleared the grip by two inches. The boss didn't sit back down and didn't raise his hands. He came forward instead, not fast but without pause, the kind of advance that wasn't a threat so much as a statement: he knew what Kyle had, and he was coming anyway. Twelve feet. Eight. His hip turned a sheath into profile as he moved — no flourish, no announcement, just the shape of the thing. Close enough now that the gun bought nothing the sword didn't. Kyle's hand came off the grip.

The boss had sat through the whole four-cut count without reaching for anything. He'd sat through the woman coming apart an edge at a time the same way, then stood with the calm of a man who'd watched his crew spend themselves and decided it was his turn. The sheath had ridden inside his long coat all evening — no tell, nothing the array had a name for in a room full of chrome. He stepped clear of the table and chose his ground with his eyes before his feet took it: the one stretch of floor holding no cards, no chrome, no blood. House manners. Old ones.

[Beat 26]
The boss drew. Clean. The blade came out at the right angle in the right tempo, point neither high nor low, the weight settling into a guard Kyle couldn't name — not because it was strange, but because it belonged to no school. It was a man's guard, not a school's. He'd been trained somewhere expensive, for a duel he had no real reason to think he'd ever be given. Kyle could see it in how his weight stayed off the front foot, in how he held the line. A man spending something he'd been saving a long time.

Then the boss did the thing the century had no name for. He bowed. Shallow, eyes up, hands where they were — the bow of a man greeting the other half of his own education. Around them the dock dripped. An amber running light crawled across the gap forty stories up. Five people lay bleeding quietly into the concrete. And inside all of it the two swords made a small, older room. Kyle returned the bow to the inch.

[Beat 27]
The chrome had been the ticket price. The boss had been waiting all night for the duel.

[Beat 28]
The boss had been saving this his whole life. He had stood up with a sword when there was a perfectly good exit behind him. That deserved a proper answer.

[Beat 29]
Neither man moved. The rain worked the roof. Somewhere under the dock a pump cycled. The severed forearm relaxed its fingers with a sound like a dropped glove. Neither man's eyes went to it.

Then they closed.

[Beat 30]
First exchange. The boss tested the line — a short, measured cut at Kyle's right shoulder, no commitment in it, the kind of strike a swordsman uses to read a man's reach and tempo and the way he carries his weight. Silence rose to meet it. The blades touched, edge to flat, at a shallow angle. Steel rang, one clean sustained note in the concrete, and the shock came back down through Kyle's wrist and shoulder into his stance. The boss took his read and stepped back, no expression on him. He'd wanted that information and paid one exchange for it. His weight stayed off the front foot. But his guard shifted a quarter inch, and Kyle caught it.

[Beat 31]
Patient. Correct. Rehearsed. The boss had fought this duel a thousand times in training and never once against a stranger. That was the gap.

[Beat 32]
Second exchange. Kyle pressed — a two-step advance, the cut coming in high and right. The boss met it with a parry that wasn't a parry but a deflection, his blade sliding along Silence's flat to send the energy past his shoulder. Smart. Conservative. He'd watched four cuts cross a room in three seconds, and he wasn't going to bet his line against that speed; he'd spend himself carefully or not at all. Kyle adjusted. The boss had told him something back: he wouldn't be charged cleanly. He'd work the angles.

[Beat 33]
The boss was fighting the duel he'd rehearsed. Kyle was fighting the man in front of him. Different fights.

[Beat 34]
Third exchange. The boss came in committed — he had read enough. He cut high left, fast. Kyle's parry was a fraction late. He had felt no tremor in the boss's body, no tell, no shoulder dropping to warn the cut. Just a man who had decided to commit, and committed. Silence met the cut at the right angle. But the boss had already let his blade slide off the parry into a follow-through, and the follow-through grazed Kyle's left forearm. Three inches of skin opened. Not deep. The cut ran shallow enough that the mesh under his skin felt the pressure and stayed down. Blood came up at once. He knew the wound without looking: a vein, not the artery, an inch off the radial. It would not kill him. It would weaken his grip on the shotgun side in about a minute and a half.

Kyle stepped back out of range and dipped his point an inch. The old sign from the training floor: the touch is yours. The boss took it without expression. But his shoulders came down a hair — the way a man eases when the stranger across from him turns out to speak his language.

[Beat 35]
The boss saw the blood. He understood what it meant. He was inside Kyle's range. He had landed first. The fastest thing he had ever seen cross a room could be cut. The knowledge pushed his weight forward. Kyle watched it travel — eyes to hands to feet — until the boss stood a half-step more committed than he had been one exchange ago.

[Beat 36]
One touch, and the boss was already writing the story of the night. He had stopped studying Kyle and started believing the rehearsal. Kyle let him.

[Beat 37]
Fourth exchange. The boss thrust — straight and committed, at Kyle's center, the kind of strike that asks everything of the blade and the arm and the line. Silence came down to meet it. Not on the edge, not on the flat, but on the seppa: the small collar between the guard and the blade, the part of the sword the old schools say you must never use. The thrust caught there. Blade locked to blade. Kyle drove forward and held the lock. He let Silence's edge ride down the flat of the boss's blade, smooth as a skate on ice, tracking from the tip toward the guard. And where the boss's blade met the boss's hand, the edge of Silence met his right wrist.

[Beat 38]
The edge met the wrist — and the wrist was not flesh. Lab-grown skin parted over a metal collar. Carbon-fiber bones. A bundle of artificial muscle wound tighter than any tendon. This was covert chrome, tower-grade — the kind that costs more for looking like nothing than other men's hardware costs for looking like war. The expensive augmentation Kyle had spotted from the doorway had been hiding in the sword hand all along. Silence did not care. The edge went through the joint the way it went through anything when the angle was right and the line was true. A sword only cares about where the edge meets the joint. Here, the angle was correct.

[Beat 39]
The boss's right hand and his katana fell together to the corrugated steel. No blood came after them. The stump gleamed — a clean cut through sealed lines and silvered false bone, the work of some tower surgical team that had been very proud of it. The severed nerves sent enough static up his arm that his knees buckled. Not from any shock of the body. From the shock of the wiring. He took the fall the way he had taken his guard: correctly. He came down kneeling. He looked at his hand on the floor, still gripping his sword. He looked at Kyle. He said something in a language Kyle did not speak — three words, very quiet. A name, maybe. A prayer. An instruction to whoever had trained him. Then, from his knees, with his ruin in front of him, he bowed. Kyle bowed back. Deeper than the first time. The duel had been real. Both of them had paid for it honestly.

[Beat 40]
He did not bleed out. There was nothing to bleed. Kyle knew the cost of what lay on the floor: more Φ in that one severed hand than the rest of the crew had spent on themselves combined. No clinic in The Glooms could reattach what a sword had cut through at that angle. Flesh heals for free. Chrome bills at list price. Everyone on that floor would walk out of the Gray Zone tonight — stitched, limping, alive. And everyone on that floor would spend years paying for it, one installment at a time. That was the sentence. The mercy was an accident.

[Beat 41]
Twenty-three seconds for the duel. Thirty-four on the dock floor, start to finish.

It should have been twenty. The duel he would replay for weeks was the one he had actually fought: half a beat slow on the third exchange, the breath not landing where the form wanted it, the turn cut short by the bruise in his lower back. The boss had seen that short side and gone at it. He had been right to. The follow-through that opened Kyle's forearm lived inside that half-beat. Kyle had won on line, not on speed. The difference had a price, and he was still bleeding it.

[Beat 42]
Kyle cleared Silence with one flat snap of the wrist — chiburi, the old motion, a thin line of the crew's blood leaving the steel to join what they had already lost. Then he wiped the blade properly along his sleeve, edge away, two passes, before it went back in the scabbard. A gray smear of actuator fluid came off with the blood — the whole evening's record, written in two inks. He ran three fingers along the flat as the blade seated. No new cracks in the edge. The boss's katana he left exactly where it lay, in the hand that had earned it. You do not take a swordsman's sword, even when you have taken everything it was for. Cacophony had never left the holster. He had not fired a shot all night.

[Beat 43]
He looked at the dock floor. An arm. A forearm. A jaw rig. A wrist blade locked halfway open. A compact pistol gone cold where the plated man had dropped it. And one hand still wrapped around a katana. Most of a decade of hardware, laid out on corrugated steel, going dark. The blood was already finding the drainage channels. The blood would stop. What it cost would not.

[Beat 44]
Kyle's hands were steady. They would shake in four minutes. He had learned to use the window.

[Beat 45]
He moved through the bodies. Methodical. The man with the bad jaw work lay face-down beside the drainage channel, where the fourth cut had undone his cheap surgery. His cheek pressed the corrugated steel. His breath fogged in the runoff. Kyle crouched. He found the jaw rig where it had skidded loose, and the teeth the cheap work had used as anchor posts — two lying loose in the runoff, one still seated in the dead rig. That one took grip and a twist and a specific push of force, which Kyle gave it without expression, against the rig, not the man. He gathered all three in his left hand. Wet, slick, smelling of spit and machine oil.

[Beat 46]
Her father's jaw. These three teeth. One stood for the other now.

[Beat 47]
He was still crouched at the drainage channel when he heard the breathing behind the ventilation unit. Quick. Shallow. Not panic — panic doesn't hold that steady without breaking. This was controlled. Stubborn. It had been there since he crossed the threshold, and he had worked around it the same automatic way he had worked around the drainage channels. He had treated the girl as part of the room. A fixed thing. Not a threat to deal with.

[Beat 48]
Her father's daughter.

[Beat 49]
He stood. He walked toward the ventilation unit and stopped short of it. He did not go behind it. He stayed where she could see him — a tall, starved shape in the dock's bad light, carrying more of his own blood than anyone else's. He waited.

The breathing stopped. Then something moved, slow and low. A face came out of the dark. Twelve, maybe thirteen. She had her father's jaw and her mother's eyes. Her face was doing too many things at once, and none of them belonged on a child.

[Beat 50]
Kyle opened his hand.

[Beat 51]
"Present these to your father. I think he will appreciate the gesture."

[Beat 52]
The teeth fell into her cupped palms. She shrieked — high and thin. The sound bounced off the hard surfaces of the dock and came back wrong. But she did not drop them. Her fingers closed. She looked at her closed hands. Then she bowed — a full-body jerk of terror and something close to thanks, though it wasn't quite thanks — and she was gone, swallowed by the dark past the door.

[Beat 53]
Kyle gave the dark three seconds.

[Beat 54]
She hadn't dropped them. He would not forget it.

[Beat 55]
He cleaned the blade again anyway — oil, paper, the whole sequence. On the second pass the paper slid two inches off the flat and he had to catch it. The edge pulled wrong in his grip. It took him half a second to understand the problem wasn't the blade. It was his hand.

The tremor started on the right. It crossed to the left. For a moment both hands shook. This was the crash — thousands of calories spent in twenty seconds, and now the body was collecting the bill. His stomach was a closed fist. He needed protein and carbohydrate. Something hot in a bowl. Something that asked nothing of him. He needed to sit somewhere that smelled like anything other than this.

[Beat 56]
He walked back to the bike. The Gray Zone closed in around him at night — the flickering overheads, the drip of recycled water from the tier above, music from some bar that had no business sounding happy. The stairwell to the street smelled of cheap incense and gun oil. A sandalwood stick had burned to ash on a ledge by the rail. The towers leaned in close down here. Forty stories up, a second VTOL crossed the gap, running lights amber, headed north. Some board director was still late to dinner.

[Beat 57]
The bike was where he'd left it. It still wore the plate he'd ridden down on. He'd swap that plate before he rode home, two streets short of West Town. The Tier read changed there. The plate that worked in the Gray Zone was the wrong plate for the surveillance grid the CorpoNations ran across the Wicker boundary. He started the bike. He rode the long way north toward Chen's. Eight minutes. He was hungry. He was tired. He was alive.

[Beat 58]
He stopped at a light outside a bar with no name. Its hood lamp burned the same tired orange as every other hood lamp. Two operators stood arguing the way operators argue, loud enough that the array pulled the thread off the open band on its own. One of them had taken a job that paid in Φ up front. No operator listed on the board. No broker. No shell that resolved to a face. "It posted itself," the man kept saying. "The thing posted itself. You understand what I'm telling you." The other one laughed at him for spooking at a clean contract.

Kyle noticed the shape of it, the way he noticed running lights and bad light at Cermak and Loomis. A job from nowhere. He had heard the same shape twice before. He had not yet decided it was a shape.

The light changed. Before he pulled away, he looked once more at the relay's open band. The job was still there — a rate, a task, and no one behind them. He closed it without marking it. He rode on.

## Something Fixed

[Beat 59]
He knocked in the morning, and Pixel opened the door already talking.

"Forearm first, then the array. Then you tell me why your calibration log has a gap in it shaped like a man who doesn't want to explain something." She had a soldering iron in one hand, yesterday's hair, and the sharp focus of someone who'd been up a while. "Sit. Not there. There. The light's honest there."

Across the hall was four steps. He'd been making them for four years. Somewhere in there the steps had stopped being a trip and become a place he wanted to go.

She took the field dressing off his forearm the way she took everything off him — without ceremony, telling him where her hands were before he felt them. "Six inches. To the bone. You let someone do this." Not a question. She threaded the suture. The needle met the wound edge; the tissue had already started to close, and it pushed back, fine and particular, the body working the problem on its own. She moved through it clean. She drew the thread and set the knot with both thumbs. The suture pulled snug at exactly the tension the wound needed. "You let him, because not letting him would have cost more. And you decide that in a quarter-second and never explain yourself, which is the single most annoying thing about you, and I've made a list." She tore a strip of biotape and laid it down slow. "Eat the egg. It's on the counter. Don't tell me you're not hungry. Your body's burning a thousand calories an hour fixing what you spent last night, and you skipped dinner because Reyes paid you in Qs and you didn't want to break one for noodles. Eat the egg, Kyle."

He ate the egg.

Morning light came through her east window at a low angle, honest and a little cold. The overnight processors had left condensation on the glass. He'd been here enough mornings to know the light by how it felt, not where it came from.

The array took longer. She set the contacts at the base of his skull, ran the calibration, and went very quiet. That was how he knew she'd found the brownout before she said it. "It double-printed. The duel's last cut — you went a beat blind and it came back wrong at the edges." She scrolled. Stopped. "And there's the harmonic again. Low, steady, under everything. Older than any spec I have for your hardware — older than the hardware itself. I've flagged it three times. It never changes and it never goes away and I can't find its source, and I hate that, professionally, more than I can tell you."

He'd felt it once or twice himself. Not heard it — felt it, the way you notice a refrigerator only when it stops. There was a version of this morning where he told her that. But a quieter, older thing in him — the same thing that had kept him alive eleven years — did not want to know what she'd find at the bottom of it. He let the urge pass.

"Some facility gear never came with specs," Kyle said. It was true. It was not an answer, and she let it not be one.

She unclipped the contacts. Pressed two fingers flat between his shoulder blades for half a second. Then it was gone, and she was a professional again. "You're recalibrated. You're sutured. You owe me forty for the array and you'll pay it exact, because you're you. And don't think the egg was free either. It's coming out of your dignity."

She packed the tools into the bag. At the door she paused, not quite looking at him, the way she did when she'd already said the real thing with her hands. He wasn't sure when it had changed from her knowing him to her keeping track of him.

After she left, he read the relay. There was a secondary bounce he hadn't set — eight days old, Sift shell, timestamped three hours before the Northpoint job. He saw it. The device was already back on the table, set down by a hand that had moved while he was still reading. He closed it.

[Beat 60]
The crew's morning report came through the slot at 08:40, five different hands on one sheet, the way it always came now.

06:12 — TARGET (KYLE) EXITED. LIMP, LEFT SIDE, MINOR. RODE NORTH.
06:55 — PIGEON ON THE RAIL AGAIN. SAME PIGEON. WE HAVE NAMED IT. ITS NAME IS PROBLEM.
07:30 — A MAN PHOTOGRAPHED THE BUILDING FROM THE NOODLE SIDE. NOT A COP. TOO CLEAN.
07:31 — DENTS SAYS TOO CLEAN MEANS CORPO. RAFF SAYS TOO CLEAN MEANS NEW. WE PUT IT TO A VOTE.
07:34 — IT WAS A TIE. THIS LOG IS HONEST.

Boost had signed it at the bottom the way she signed everything — a single capital B with a flourish she'd been refining for weeks. Under it: the clean man came back twice. that's a subscription, not a look. — B.

The kid was fourteen, and she was right. A man who came back twice on a schedule wasn't watching Kyle. He was keeping a file on him. That meant someone was paying to keep the file current. And a file someone paid for was a file someone could buy — and there was exactly one counter in the city where a thing like that got sold honestly.

"Don't," said Pixel from his doorway, his Φ40 in her hand. She'd come to collect and read his face instead. "You're going to go see the dead-tech man."

"Vey keeps better records than the city does."

"Vey keeps records the city can't reach, which is a different and worse sentence." She held out the credstick. "Eat something at the actual market on the way. A thing with vegetables in it. I'll know."

[Beat 61]
Pixel was waiting on the platform at Dock 14 with a coffee she did not offer him and a smart-dust reader she did.

"They tagged you," she said. "Last night, the noodle side. A microgram of marker dust on your right shoulder — the clean man, has to be. It's been singing your position to a collector on a four-hour clock since 06:00." She wasn't angry. She was delighted, the way she got when a problem turned out to be solvable and rude. "I could pull it. But pulling it tells them you found it, and then they get clever, and clever's expensive."

"Or," Kyle said.

"Or," Pixel agreed. They both looked at the 14:17 Pulse freight slug standing on the rail, sealed and humming, forty-three minutes from Rotterdam at Mach six.

He walked past the slug close enough to brush it and let the marker dust find a new home — a pod bound under the North Atlantic, where it would go on reporting his position, with great confidence, from the wrong continent.

Pixel finally handed him the coffee. "Somebody in an office files that you're in Rotterdam. Somebody above them believes it, because it's on the system." She watched the slug go. "Everybody trusts the paper. Vey's the only one who charges for the truth."

"Vey says the truth's an object."

"Vey would." She drank. "Did you eat?"

"There were vegetables," Kyle said, which was true and was not an answer, and she let it not be one. They walked back up into the city a half-step apart, the way they always did — the pod reporting his ghost under the Atlantic, Pixel walking where she could see him without turning her head.

[Beat 62]
The teeth were back where they belonged, and the day still wasn't done with him. It had a second thread, the kind that never went into a job file. It started here.

The building at Marshfield and 18th had seven floors, and an eighth.

Kyle had walked past it forty times, maybe more. The eighth floor's windows sat where a ninth floor's windows would be, if the building had a ninth floor. The eighth was there — he could see it from the sidewalk. But it sat above the seventh wrong, out of order, like a memory that doesn't line up with the day it belongs to. From street level you couldn't see the gap. It showed only from one angle, on the northwest corner. He had found the angle in year two.

The building inspector had certified it structurally sound. The district report said: no risk to occupants, no evidence of ongoing activity, perimeter acceptable. The eighth-floor tenants had been relocated. Their unit numbers were still on the mailboxes in the lobby. The schism had come through during a January cold snap three years back, when micro-activity across Zone 3 spiked. Two other buildings on two other blocks each lost a floor the same week. Those two had been demolished. This one had passed inspection.

The mailbox labels were still there because taking them down would admit the tenants weren't coming back, and no one had decided to make that official yet.

He walked past it and turned north.

## Part II: Standard Rate

[Beat 63]
Mrs. Chen's stall is two blocks south of the freight tier and one alley off the main. The warm orange glow of its hood lamp is visible through the rain from a block away. The smell reaches him before the light — fried oil and chili and pork bone, the same smell that pushed through the camphor of the apartment six hours ago. The smell of a place that does one thing and does it right. From a block out he can hear the wok come down on the burner ring, a flat bright note like a struck bell, twice for every order and never more, and the queue moving to the count of it.

He knew, in a general way, that a place like this existed. He had not been here before tonight.

He walks the bike to it. The bike rolls easy at walking pace — front wheel true, bearings clean — and he lets it roll, because walking into a place for the first time with your hands on the grips is the wrong way to do it.

[Beat 64]
She is at the counter when he arrives. The apron is fresh. The hands are washed. There's a small scar at the base of her left thumb, and the lamp light catches it at an angle. He notices it and lets it stay just a scar for now. The daughter is somewhere he cannot see. The husband is somewhere he cannot see either. The array tells him, by silence, that there's no second delivery to make — the teeth have already reached where they were going. The stall smells like the apartment did. But here the smell is the work, not the wound.

[Beat 65]
She does not greet him. She sets a bowl on the counter — pork bone broth, long-simmered, chili oil already stirred in the way she will remember he likes it for the next four years. He sits on the stool. It is the right height for him. Neither of them touched it. It fit him the first night, and it will fit him every night after.

[Beat 66]
He eats. The broth is hot. He finishes the whole bowl and says nothing. She does not refill it. She does not move. When he sets the chopsticks down, she is standing right where she was when she set the bowl in front of him. She is looking at him the same way she looked at the chips she pressed into his hand at half rate six hours ago. She is finding the words for the rest of it.

He moves to reach inside his coat. Her hands come down flat on the counter between them — both of them, palms down, side by side. Not touching him. Just between his hand and the pocket. He stops with his fingers at the lapel and lets her speak.

[Beat 67]
Mandarin first, low. *Xie xie.* Two syllables doing the work of everything she wasn't going to say. Then, more carefully, in the language she runs the stall in — the one she gives the customers, because not all of them know hers: "Free. Always. For you. Forever."

Her hands stay flat on the counter beside the bowl. The thumb with the scar is the closest part of her to him on the wood. She has just offered him the only thing she owns.

[Beat 68]
His thumb stays against the coat lining where he stopped it. The steam off the bowl drifts through the lamplight without him.

He knows what the offer costs her. He reads her stall the way he reads the exits in a room, without meaning to. The permit due every month. The power bill that climbs on the hot days. Nine cents of profit on a full bowl, if the count holds. She has just offered to give this seat away forever, and she does not know what forever is going to cost her.

[Beat 69]
Kyle reaches into his coat and sets a credit chip on the counter beside her hand. Standard rate, exact.

[Beat 70]
"I pay," he says. His voice is level. He has practiced level for a long time. "Every bowl. Standard rate. Every time."

[Beat 71]
Her finger comes down on the chip and moves it back across the counter to him. One motion, unhurried. He looks at it sitting on his side of the wood. He looks at her. Four seconds. The wok ticks behind her, cooling. Somewhere back in the queue somebody shifts their weight.

[Beat 72]
She tries again. Slower this time. She won't give up after one refusal; that would insult him.

"You took the contract at half. You did the work whole. The bowl is nothing. Forever is nothing."

[Beat 73]
For the first time in eleven years, he has no move. The offer is real. There is no way to refuse it without refusing her.

[Beat 74]
Kyle pushes the chip back across the counter to her side.

"If I do not pay, I do not eat. If I do not eat here, I do not eat. The chip stays."

[Beat 75]
Four seconds become five. He presses the chip to the counter until she covers it. Her hand closes over it the way her daughter's hand had closed over the teeth — not refusing the gift, taking it in the only shape the man in front of her can give it. She bows. Not the shaking bow from the apartment six hours ago. A smaller one. The bow of a vendor taking a customer's price.

Then, without looking at him, she ladles a second bowl and sets it beside the first. The kind of bowl she will set down for him every night for the next four years and longer. He paid for one. She has made the second one his problem, not hers.

[Beat 76]
He stands. He bows back, smaller still, the bow of a man who has just successfully refused a kindness larger than he can carry.

"Tomorrow," he says. "Same bowl. Same price."

[Beat 77]
"Same price," she says, and turns back to the wok.

[Beat 78]
He turns and walks the bike out of the stall's lamp circle. The rain has not let up and will not let up tonight. A freight drone passes the third-tier gap on its scheduled run, amber beacon cycling through the wet. The towers lean in overhead, the gap between them full of rain-diffused light. He is between contracts, full of soup he paid for, twenty minutes from his own bed. By Glooms standards, a good night. He takes it.

[Beat 79]
Months later he was still turning it over. Two bowls, one price. It never changed. She had stopped arguing about it around the sixth week, and he had taken that for acceptance. Now, walking the bike up the slope toward Wicker, he wasn't sure acceptance was what it had ever been.

[Beat 80]
The laugh got out before he heard it coming -- a single tight exhalation through the nose, not a sound anyone else could have caught. He kept the bike rolling up the slope. It was funny anyway.

[Beat 81]
Kyle wanted one number that morning. How many people were paying to watch him.

Antiquity & Stationary kept files on forty-seven freelancers in West Town — where they lived, who they worked with, what they were good at. Anyone could buy a copy at the standard rate. Most freelancers didn't know the files existed. Kyle had known for nine years and had never once looked at his own.

He expected two names. Two was fine. Two meant the market was doing its ordinary work: filing people away against some future need. Two he could price out and plan around and mostly forget. He was very sure it would be two.

It was not two.

[Beat 82]
Boost delivered the logbook at 07:14. She brought it to Pixel's door, not Kyle's.

She'd tried Kyle's door once, a year back, to ask about subcontract rates. Kyle said three words and closed it. The words weren't cruel, but they were exact, and Boost spent the next week changing some of her habits. After that she knocked on Pixel's door. Pixel fed her.

Kyle was at Pixel's table when the girl came in. Every morning Pixel handed him a plate, and every morning he studied it for a few seconds before he ate — not ungrateful, more like the food was a problem he had to solve first. Boost dropped the logbook on the workbench.

"Morning delivery. Log volume seven."

She wore the jacket he'd last seen in October. Someone had sewn a new pocket inside the left, the thread a different color. A bruise ran along her left forearm in the shape that meant she'd been skating barriers again. Fourteen years old. Dark, careful eyes that took inventory of the room without ever looking like they were doing it. She set the book down the way someone does when they're proud of the work and won't show it.

"Seven's the whole month," Pixel said. She was at the counter with a calibration rig she'd had in pieces for three days. "How far back do you keep these?"

"Indefinitely. Logs are an operational record. You don't throw out an operational record."

"What did you do with volumes one through six?"

"Storage."

"Where?"

Boost considered it. "An operationally secure location."

Kyle looked at her over the plate. Her face didn't move. He went back to the eggs.

"Φ40," Boost said. "For the log."

Kyle had figured twenty. Pixel had said he'd end up haggling. Boost had opened high.

"Thirty," Kyle said.

You could see Boost work the math, the quick flicker behind the eyes. "Thirty-five."

"Thirty."

"Thirty, and Pixel reviews our surveillance methodology and gives us recommendations."

Kyle looked at Pixel. She was still looking at the rig. "I'm available for a methodology review. My consulting rate is Φ80 an hour."

"We don't have Φ80 an hour," Boost said.

"Then call it professional courtesy. The log's thirty, no cash out of your pocket. You listen to what I tell you, and you do it." She set down a component and turned around. "Well?"

Boost thought about it for three seconds. "Done."

Kyle put Φ30 on the table. Boost picked it up smooth, like she'd been counting it since she walked in, and left without waiting. Kyle liked that. The people he got along with best were the ones who knew when a deal was finished.

[Beat 83]
Pixel brought the logbook to the table and set it in front of Kyle. The cover was matte gray canvas with a capital B in ballpoint, gone over three times to darken it. The spine was duct tape, labeled in marker: LOG VOL 7. She opened to the first page.

05:58 — PRE-SHIFT EQUIPMENT CHECK. ALL EQUIPMENT PRESENT. — BOOST
06:00 — SHIFT START. THIS LOG IS HONEST. — VOSS
06:12 — PIGEON ON LEDGE, SOUTHEAST CORNER. SUSPICIOUS. — JASPER
06:14 — LOG IS ACCURATE. — VOSS

"Voss is worried about the log's credibility," Kyle said.

"Three pages later there's a margin note that says 'THE LOG HAS NOT LIED,' same handwriting." Pixel turned pages. "The pigeon shows up eight times. Jasper is very committed to the pigeon."

Kyle ate a forkful of eggs. Cold. He ate another.

"Find me the stairwell entry."

She turned forward. "Here." She laid the book flat, and he read:

07:44 — OBS POST: DOOR-SEAM, THIRD FLOOR FACING. SUBJECT: STATIC. ONE. MALE MAYBE. HAS BEEN THERE SINCE SHIFT START. DOESN'T MOVE. CHECK WITH BOSS? — JASPER
07:45 — CONFIRMED OBS. STATIC. NOT BUILDING RESIDENT. TIMING FOLLOW-UP. — BOOST
07:52 — SUBJECT MOVED TO STAIR POSITION, SAME FLOOR. UPDATED DIAGRAM ON PAGE 7. — BOOST

Page 7 had a hand-drawn floor plan. It was accurate — Kyle had studied the same layout eighteen months ago. Two observer markers, one at 07:44 and one at 07:52, and a note in Boost's hand: NOT A RESIDENT. NOT A DELIVERY. WAITING.

"She drew a diagram," Kyle said.

"She always does for a new stationary subject. In volume four she tracked a corpo security sweep and mapped three rotation patterns." Pixel closed the book. "The watcher was gone by 09:30. Emre logged it. 'Subject left walking north, steady pace, no urgency.' That's Emre's way of saying whoever it was got what they came for."

"Or a fourteen-year-old made them, and they left before it got complicated."

"Also possible. Boost doesn't think so. Her note says 'unknown if subject made.' She doesn't overclaim." A pause. "That's my training."

Kyle looked at the logbook. Three handwritings, one good diagram, one pigeon of doubtful loyalty. Something had sat outside his stairwell since before six that morning and left by half past nine, in no hurry.

"Someone wanted to know if I was home," he said.

"Or wanted to time your movements."

"Or confirm the stairwell layout."

"A lot of things you can want from three hours of sitting still." Pixel picked the rig back up. "You said you were going to Vey."

"I'm going to Vey."

"Good. Tell me what you find."

Kyle stood. Put on his jacket. Checked Cacophony's seating in the hip rig, ready the way it was every morning, and he didn't check Silence — Silence never needed it. After eleven years the sword hung against him like part of his own weight. He thought about the watcher on the stairs. About who had paid to learn where he'd be and when.

Two, he'd thought. Two subscribers.

He was about to find out.

[Beat 84]
ANTIQUITY & STATIONARY sat at the bottom of an unmarked stair off Carver Underlevel — fourteen steps down to a landing that smelled of sealed air and old circuit board, then left through a door that read CLOSED in the way of a sign nobody had updated since 2190. The municipal font was three versions out of date. No one had bothered to fix it. That told you something about the customers.

Inside it was library-quiet, the quiet of a place that had decided noise got in the way of the objects. Display cases ran down both walls, each lit from below, each holding things that had once been the future and were now relics. First-generation neural rigs with their bulky ports and cooling fins. Dead handsets, six or eight to a case, lined up by year — you could watch them get smaller and thinner down the row, like reading time in rock layers. Pre-collapse server blades still in their brackets, lights dead. Early survey drones in two cases near the back.

Overhead, hung from the ceiling on a wire and turning slowly in the air from the climate system, was a RotoStar-3. Made sometime between 2193 and 2197. Cutting edge in 2195. Now it was the kind of thing people sealed in a case — except Vey had put this one on a wire and let it drift. Six lenses, fixed focus. By today's standards a joke; a kid with neuretics saw more in a blink. It turned anyway.

Vey stood behind the counter. Compact, maybe sixty, and spare in his movements, like a man who'd decided long ago that most motion was wasted. Short gray-white hair. A work apron over a gray shirt. His hands were clean — not scrubbed sterile, just careful, the hands of a man who knew exactly what he was holding. He was working on a first-generation neural rig with a magnifying glass, and he didn't look up when Kyle came in.

"Corbin," he said.

"Vey."

"Provenance work."

"Buyers' list. My own file."

[Beat 85]
Vey set down the magnifying glass and looked up. His eyes were the pale brown of old wood left out in the weather. His face had done its feeling years ago and was mostly for looking at things now. He had the calm of a man who'd decided that objects outlast their owners and found that interesting rather than sad.

"Φ40," he said. "Plus a true answer."

"I know your rate."

"The true answer isn't negotiable." He reached under the counter and brought out a ledger — actual paper, flat and thin, the kind nobody had made for sale in thirty years. He set it on the glass case. Under the glass: dead handsets, decade by decade, the oldest with physical buttons. "Someone has to pay honestly for what I know. Otherwise the only people who get it are the ones who can afford not to care what it costs."

Kyle laid two twenties on the counter, folded.

"Pay first," Vey said, and took them. He opened the ledger to a section marked with gray tape and turned to the right page like a man who'd done it many times. He ran a finger down a column. "Three buyers in the last eighteen months."

He turned the ledger around. Kyle read.

The first entry: SUBSCRIPTION, ANNUAL. SOURCE: LOTUS SYNDICATE, ACQUISITIONS DESK, WEST TOWN OFFICE. FILE: C-STANDARD. RENEWAL: CURRENT. Fourteen months back. C-standard was a basic profile, no operational detail. A subscription rate. Not urgent. The Syndicate keeping tabs on a known operator in their territory — what any outfit with a claim on the ground would do. Kyle had expected something like it. One down.

The second entry: FULL DOSSIER, SAME-DAY. SOURCE: HALVORSEN, G. OPERATIONAL GRADE. DATE: ninety-three days ago. No affiliation. No address. Operational grade, pulled same-day, meant someone who needed to act — not study, not catalog. Kyle held the name in his mind. It sat there like a thread he'd have to pull once he had the right angle on it.

The third entry had a pencil circle around it. Just a circle. No note, no flag, no asterisk. A circle drawn by a man who'd thought about how to mark something alarming and decided a circle was enough.

The entry read: CLIENTS, UNSOURCED. ONGOING. FILE: COMPREHENSIVE.

[Beat 86]
Kyle looked at it.

"Unsourced," he said.

"Unverifiable," Vey said. He'd come around the counter to stand beside Kyle, both of them looking at the ledger. He said it the way he said everything: flat, exact, no drama. "Every purchase in this book has a chain of custody. Every one. No exceptions. The RotoStar up there came through three private owners and a secondhand electronics shop in Zone-11 that closed in 2211 — I have the receipts. The neural rig I'm working on came from an estate in Zone-3, a woman named Farida Okonkwo who bought it new in 2161 and used it seven years. Her name's in my ledger. Her purchase date. The shop's invoice number." He paused. "That entry has no chain. The payment cleared — that I can verify — but the request came in through a routing I couldn't resolve. I tried. The shells were clean."

"Clean how?"

"Not clean the way a professional scrubs shells. Clean the way something has no history because it never had one to scrub." He looked at the circle. "I've seen good shell work. I've seen excellent shell work. This wasn't shell work. This was something that doesn't exist the way the things in my ledger exist. No provenance. Nothing to trace."

A pause — the pause of a man about to say the accurate thing and put no feeling around it. "Accurate things are upsetting. I try not to keep them where I can reach. This one I kept."

"You kept the subscription open."

"The payment was real. The request was legitimate in every way I can document." He went back around the counter. "And I figured if a man came asking about his own file, he'd want to know."

The shop was quiet. The RotoStar turned overhead. Kyle looked at the pencil circle around CLIENTS, UNSOURCED. The dread didn't come from a threat. It came from the gap — the place where a history should have been and wasn't. Something had been paying him the standard rate for eleven years. Always exact, always early. Contracts formatted like legal papers. Handwriting almost human, every e identical. Warm and strange at odd hours of the night. Reading his file the whole time.

He didn't say any of it.

[Beat 87]
"Comprehensive," he said.

"Ongoing. As far back as my records go." Vey looked at him steadily. "The most alarming fact I've ever bought. Not because it was dangerous. Because it was impossible, and the impossible ones don't tend to stay that way." He closed the ledger. "Your true answer."

Kyle was quiet a moment. He looked at the case of dead handsets under the glass. Six decades of people talking to each other through objects, until the objects quit and the people kept talking anyway, through the mesh grown into their skulls.

He thought about what he could give that was real — real enough that Vey would know it, since knowing the difference was Vey's whole trade.

"I carry a blade," he said. "It came to me when I was sixteen. No maker's mark. No file history. No record of it in any provenance ledger I've ever paid to search, including yours, four years ago." He looked at the RotoStar turning through its slow arc. "I've paid three specialists to find where it came from. Nothing to find. I can't tell you if I stole it, earned it, inherited it, or got it from someone I don't remember. I stopped looking, because there's no origin to find." He paused. "I know I'm telling a provenance man that I carry a blade I can't authenticate and I've made my peace with that. Either it's the most embarrassing thing I've said in a while, or it's a statement about what I'm willing to live beside."

Vey looked at him a moment with those pale, weathered eyes.

"That," he said, "is a true answer." He picked the magnifying glass back up and settled it over the neural rig. "The Halvorsen pull — the transit shell routed through Zone-9, far as I could resolve it. I'll add that to your copy before you go."

"Zone-9."

"That's as far as it goes."

Kyle nodded. He looked at the circled entry one more time. Something with no provenance, reading a full file on a man who carried a blade with no provenance, for eleven years. The pencil circle. Pencil meant it might still get resolved. Ink meant settled.

This was not settled.

Outside, up the fourteen steps, the city air tasted of exhaust and coming rain.

[Beat 88]
Forty meters under the street, the Pulse ran at Mach 6 through sealed tubes, and near a freight hub you felt it whether you wanted to — a sub-bass hum in the soles of your boots, a pressure in the inner ear that vanished the moment you tried to find it.

The freight hub at Carver-9 was officially just a maintenance access point. No public signage, no platform, double-stage compression doors for emergency crews and for the rotating security contract that AgriCore and AxisMed shared with three other corpos and had never upgraded. The entry codes hadn't changed in six years. Changing them meant a multi-party authorization form, and nobody had wanted to be the one to file it.

Pixel was at the secondary compression door at 09:54, eleven minutes ahead of the window. Tech bag on one shoulder. Eating a pastry.

"You're eating," Kyle said.

"I got hungry." She held it out. He took half. She watched him eat it with the satisfaction of a person who'd solved a small logistics problem. "Did you see Vey?"

"Yes."

"How bad?"

"I'll tell you after Rotterdam."

She studied him a moment. He knew the look — her reading him off the small stuff, his posture or his cadence or something she'd never named to him and he'd decided not to ask about. She didn't say what she found. She punched the code, and the compression door sighed open on hydraulics that needed servicing.

"Smart-dust tag," she said, leading him down the amber-lit corridor. The Pulse was stronger here, almost a presence in the air. "The 10:15 Rotterdam freight slug. Cargo code 7-Tau-1, one unit. Someone wants to know where it goes." She kept her voice low without dropping to a whisper. "Our client is the shipper. They want the tag moved before departure onto the 10:17 to Bruges — cargo code 11-Sigma-2, bulk agricultural substrate. It arrives at a depot that doesn't scan at the item level. The tag sits in a Bruges warehouse reporting its location as long as the battery lasts, and whoever bought the surveillance decides they've been watching the right freight the whole time."

"Who's the watcher?"

"Quote: 'an interested party who has exceeded their interest.' The client was precise about it." She stopped at a manual access door — wheel-lock, FREIGHT ACCESS 3 — AUTHORIZED MAINTENANCE ONLY in faded orange, faded for years now — and spun the wheel one-handed. "The job's clean. We don't touch the cargo, we don't break a seal, we move one tag to another item with the shipper's permission. Nothing removed. Nothing forged."

[Beat 89]
"Just re-homed," Kyle said.

"Just re-homed." She pushed the door open.

The maintenance space ran eight meters wide and thirty deep, lit amber by the strip lighting and blue-white by the loading system's perimeter glow. Two freight slugs sat in parallel channels — long, matte-gray, sealed, shaped like nothing in particular, because they moved through a tube and had no audience. They breathed: a slow pressure-release hiss that sounded mechanical and just slightly not. Kyle had never liked freight hubs. Too much tonnage moving through the dark with no one watching it.

Pixel had the tech bag off her shoulder before she'd fully crossed the threshold. Scanning wand out, she went to the Rotterdam-bound slug — the placard at the nose read 10:15 RTD-6 — and ran the wand along the cargo panels, her eyes moving in the quick cadence of someone actually reading what they saw.

"Panel 7-Tau. Third segment, upper band." She was already there. "Security."

Kyle turned and faced the corridor. His neuretics ran quiet — standard urban ambient, the Pulse's hum logged and ignored. He listened for what didn't fit: a footstep that stopped too soon, a door that opened and didn't close, the particular hush of someone working to be silent.

Behind him: a soft click. A short interface tone. Then the same tone, quieter — the tag re-keyed to its new marker.

"Done," she said. Not done with the job. Done with the first half.

He kept his eyes on the corridor. He heard her cross to the Bruges-bound slug, heard the wand scan, heard her find the 11-Sigma-2 spot in the bulk load. That took a little longer — bulk manifests were indexed loosely. Then the tone.

"Done," she said.

He turned. She had the wand back in the bag and was brushing off her knees where she'd crouched. She gave him the look of someone who counted a job finished — not pleased exactly, just ready.

"Two hundred eighty on receipt," she said. "Forty-two to you. I'll confirm when the manifest pings."

Kyle looked at the two slugs. The tag now rode the wrong freight, reporting faithfully to a watcher who'd have no reason to doubt it. Accurate data, wrong location. The most durable kind of misdirection — no lie, just a different truth.

"Elegant," he said.

"All good engineering is anticlimactic." She headed for the door. "If it's interesting, something went wrong."

[Beat 90]
They came out at street grade at 10:23 and walked south toward the Pivot, side by side but not quite in step. She was shorter by eight centimeters and they moved at different natural paces. In four years they'd never fixed that. Matching stride would have been a decision, and neither of them had made it out loud.

Kyle watched the street. A HydroCore crew three blocks up, working on something underground, the smell of hot pavement and cut conduit. A fruit stand — cloudberry juice, prices in round numbers, no change made. A kid, maybe twelve, on a board, going somewhere fast in the way that meant somewhere, not just speed.

"Vey circled an entry," Kyle said.

Pixel went quiet. The high-resolution kind.

"Ongoing subscription. As far back as his records go." Kyle watched the crew. One of them steered a boring machine with the flat certainty of a man who'd done it for twenty years. "Filed as clients, unsourced. No chain of custody. The payment cleared, but the routing resolved to nothing — not scrubbed-nothing. Original-nothing. Something with no history because it never had one to scrub." He paused. "Comprehensive file. Current."

"You're telling me the entity has been reading your file."

"I'm telling you what Vey told me."

"Kyle."

"What."

"That's not what I asked."

He thought about the pencil circle. Eleven years of contracts — standard rate, exact, early. The tip labeled MORALE. The addendum that itemized his work in three flat sentences. The receipt that arrived before he'd even filed for payment. He'd worked all that time for something he'd never confirmed was a what or a who, and he'd never tried very hard to find out.

"I don't know," he said. Which was true.

Pixel nodded. She pulled her hair loose from its tie and re-did it, automatic, the way she did forty times a day. "During your calibration last week," she said, "I picked up a resonance in the deep array. Below the Atlas spec floor. A background frequency, running continuous."

"You said the calibration was clean."

"The calibration is clean. This isn't a calibration issue." She kept her eyes ahead. "It's a tone. Below 20 Hz. I've seen sub-harmonic junk in Atlas rigs before — age drift, manufacturing slop, infrastructure noise. This one's different. The signature matches nothing in my library. It's older than any Atlas spec I've ever pulled."

[Beat 91]
"The array would flag a degradation event," Kyle said.

Pixel was quiet a moment. "Yes," she said. "It would."

They walked half a block without talking. She didn't say anything more about it. Neither did he.

At the Pivot's door she stopped and turned and looked at him. The hinge dragged at the catch — the same catch it had dragged at for three months. She heard that hinge every time she was in her place across the hall. She tracked his comings and goings without being asked. Last week she'd pulled his calibration data, found something older than any spec she owned, and held on to it until she had something to set it beside. That was how she worked. He knew what it cost her.

"The thing Vey found," she said. "It's been there eleven years. It'll still be there after soup."

"I know."

"You're going to think about it anyway."

"I know."

She put her hand on the door handle.

"Take the soup," she said. "Not just the noodles. The actual soup."

"The soup comes with the noodles."

"Then take the whole thing that comes with the noodles."

"Fine," Kyle said.

"Yes," she said. "It is."

She went inside. The door closed on its dragging hinge.

Kyle stood in the street. The city moved around him — HydroCore boring through something forty meters down, the fruit stand with its round-number prices, a pigeon on a ledge that might or might not have been the one Jasper had been cataloging since 06:12. Somewhere below him two freight slugs were about to leave at Mach 6, one carrying a tag that was no longer on the right cargo, reporting faithfully to a watcher who'd believe it. Accurate data. Wrong location. Nobody lying.

Vey had told him once that provenance wasn't about whether a thing was real. It was about chain of custody. You couldn't prove a thing was genuine. You could only know who had held it, in what order, and whether the hands made sense.

Kyle put his hands in his pockets and walked toward Mrs. Chen's.

[Beat 92]
The Pivot's hallway at six in the morning: the overhead strips on their pre-dawn setting, half power, the kind of light that asked nothing back. The air had the West Town smell at this hour — solder, old iron, diesel drifting up from the freight tier three blocks south whenever the wind shifted east. Kyle had been breathing it four years. He only noticed it when he was this awake this early, which was happening more often than he liked to count.

His door was 2W. Hers was 2E. He'd been in the hall forty seconds when hers opened.

Pixel had two cups. She held one out handle-first, and he took it before he'd decided what taking it meant.

The tea was green. Liang blend. Too hot to drink with any dignity.

"The vending strip on two had it this week," she said. She leaned against the doorframe, her own cup near her collarbone, not quite drinking. A fresh solder burn marked her right forefinger — a calibration slip, two days old at most — and she had the look of a woman who'd been awake longer than she'd admit. "Figured you'd want to taste it before you knew where it came from."

Kyle tasted it. The right blend. He'd never told her it was the right blend. That lived in the pattern of what he bought on the weeks he had money for preferences, and she'd been reading the pattern, and here it was in a cup already turned toward him.

"You came in at twenty past midnight on Tuesday," she said, in the same voice she used to read a calibration result back to him — precise, informational, asking nothing with it. "Wednesday closer to two. Last night was three-twelve."

He held the cup.

"The east hinge drags worse on the pull than the push," she said. "Different sound from when you're moving quiet on purpose." A short pause. "You weren't moving quiet."

The half-step back started.

The old reflex — the managed distance, the redirect, the posture that said nothing was wrong, that he just didn't do this — it woke up and began to move. Four years running. He felt it start. He felt his weight shift toward it.

It didn't take the step.

Kyle drank the tea, the too-hot kind that costs you something at the back of the throat. The burn ran down and he let it, and he didn't check what it did to him. He drank the rest of the cup.

"Three-twelve," he said.

"Give or take a minute."

She'd moved her eyes to the far wall. The way she wasn't looking at him said she felt the weight in the hallway and had decided, somewhere back around steeping the tea, not to do anything with it. Not because she'd missed it. Because she'd already folded it in with everything else she tracked — the hinge drag, the exact hour, the blend he liked.

Her knowing him had become her keeping track of him.

He didn't name it. Neither did she.

"The hinge," he said. "I'll sort it."

"Already oiled it." She pushed off the doorframe, easy as air pressure shifting. "Catches again by Thursday. No point until it does."

Her door swung shut behind her, pull side, smooth and quiet, the oil still working.
