Kyle stood in the hallway with the empty cup. The overhead strips ran at half power. The linoleum was worn through at both their thresholds — two bare patches of concrete facing each other. Three floors down, the freight tier was waking up, a low rumble just under the level where the mind decides to notice it.

He stood there a moment longer than he needed to.

Then he went back inside.

[Beat 93]
The notice was taped to Mrs. Chen's awning post at six-seventeen in the morning, where Kyle found it instead of his bowl.

Compliance Hold: Zone Recategorization Pending. A reference number in the upper right, a district seal at the bottom, the whole thing printed on official gray stock that smelled like nothing at all. Someone had already been here, which meant someone had filed, which meant it wasn't spontaneous. Kyle peeled it off the post and read it twice. The wording was the kind built to be too specific to fight without a lawyer — transitional commercial reclassification, Section 7.14.b, pending review under the amended Wentworth Corridor Development Index. Kyle had never heard of the Wentworth Corridor Development Index. That was probably the point.

Mrs. Chen was at her counter, plating for the two workers already seated. Both of them looked at their bowls the way people do when they wish they hadn't seen something. She didn't look at Kyle. He sat at the end of the counter. After a moment she brought him tea.

"How long?" he asked.

"It came yesterday." She poured for herself at the back counter, which meant she'd already poured once this morning and wasn't finished with the conversation. "I've applied for review. There's a forty-day hold on review applications in the transitional commercial category."

He smoothed the form on the counter and read it again. Zone recategorization. It was built to sit between the cracks — procedural enough to sound routine, heavy enough to need an expert, and neither a clean appeal nor a simple fine, so there was no fast way to answer it. Good work, if that was the word.

[Beat 94]
"Transitional commercial," he said.

"Yes."

"Your stall's been here twenty-three years."

"It has." She set his tea closer. "I made your first bowl in 2215. You came in the second week of June. You ordered without looking at the menu — I thought you were still deciding, but you hadn't looked at it once." She refilled the nearer worker's cup; he took it without a word. "I thought, this one is deciding something else."

That sounded right. He didn't remember the bowl. He remembered being new to the city and not yet able to ask for things he needed, so he'd practiced by asking for things he wanted. The bowl had been a practice ask. It had worked, and he'd stayed in West Town.

"The originating complaint numbers," he said. "Do you have them?"

She reached under the counter and brought out a slim folder, rubber-banded, already in order. She'd been expecting someone to ask eventually — not him, not anyone in particular. She just wasn't going to do nothing, and whoever turned out to be useful would need something orderly to work from.

He picked up the folder. "What do you want done?"

She gave him a look that said: what a question. "I want my stall back."

"By end of business today."

She didn't blink. "If you can manage it."

He paid for the tea, exact. She took the money without ceremony — the deal done, the two of them unchanged, nothing owed either way. He tucked the folder inside his jacket and left without eating. His stomach noted it. He set that aside. He'd get to it.

[Beat 95]
The originating complaint numbers cross-referenced to three names: Hanna Borg-Okafor, Denis Priyatharan, Sung-Ji Maduka. All three had filed formal hygiene complaints through the zone's citizen portal inside the same six-hour window, all attached to the same petition file number. The petition had thirty-one signatures.

Kyle found a public terminal in the Markel Street transit hall. The civic interfaces were slow and wanted a registration token he didn't have, so he ran the queries through his neuretics instead. The Atlas array burned through them fast, faster than he could follow, and he'd learned to stay out of its way and wait for the summary.

Hanna Borg-Okafor: address listed as 1140 West Damen, Unit 3. Kyle checked the building registry. 1140 West Damen had no Unit 3. Seven units — 1, 2, 4, 5, 6, 7, and G, the G a ground-floor storage lock-up on a separate parcel. No Unit 3 had ever existed in the building's records, which ran back to 2198.

Denis Priyatharan: an ID number with a 09 prefix. That prefix went to wards of the zone authority — minors in transitional housing or temporary guardianship. A formal zone-authority complaint required a guardian's signature for any registered minor in the 09 category. There was no guardian signature on the petition. Either the clerk had missed it, or nobody had checked, because nobody expected anyone to look.

Sung-Ji Maduka: address listed as 412 Fulton Corridor. Kyle knew 412 Fulton. He'd done a job there in 2224 — a repossession, a contractor debt, clean, nobody hurt. The building had been condemned since 2221, stripped and half torn down. Nobody lived there. Nobody had in five years.

Three complainants. Zero real people.

He filed the screenshots to a case folder in his archive and moved to the inspector's report.

Six pages, dense with municipal formatting, printed on AxisMed-grade stock — slightly waxy, slightly bright, the kind that cost three times what it needed to because it read as official. The inspector's name was Renata Osei-Bonsu. Kyle ran it against the zone office's registered inspectors. She wasn't on the West Town list. She was an active contractor for Zone 9 commercial licensing — a different jurisdiction. She'd done four inspections in West Town in the past eighteen months, all for properties that got reclassified afterward.

[Beat 96]
The report cited subsection 14.7.c of the West Town Commercial Food Preparation Code as the basis for flagging Mrs. Chen's prep surfaces as noncompliant. Kyle pulled 14.7.c. It applied to enclosed kitchens with a minimum of two hundred square feet and a permanent water connection. Mrs. Chen's stall was thirty-two square feet, open-air, mobile-classified, and drew its water from the corridor's commercial tap on a per-use license. The code didn't apply on three separate grounds, any one of which was enough to kill the citation.

An oversight, maybe. A deliberate falsification, also maybe. For Tessaline's legal exposure it wouldn't matter, and that was the part that did.

The zone recategorization filing was timestamped 14:32 on the fourth. The citizen complaint petition was timestamped 19:07 on the fourth. Same day. The hold had been filed four and a half hours before the complaints that were supposed to justify it.

Kyle sat with that. Three fabricated complainants, an out-of-jurisdiction inspector citing a code that didn't apply, and a timestamp proving the plan came before its own stated cause. Any one of them could pass as coincidence. Together they were the work of someone sure no one would look.

He pulled up Tessaline's public franchise registration through the zone authority's business portal. Damen Authentic LLC, parent franchisee Tessaline Group, registered contact for West Town real estate and licensing. He searched the name against the zone office's public filings. Eleven zone recategorization filings in fourteen months, all in corridors where Tessaline locations later opened. Eight of the eleven had ended in a business closing within ninety days.

He sent a query on the crew channel: Need Tessaline's West Town franchise coordinator. Name and location.

Forty seconds. Risa came back, complete sentences as always: His name is Garrison Mercer. He's at the Damen location right now — came in at nine, his car's been in the lot since eight forty-seven.

A second message, a different hand: THE FRANCHISE GUY HAS A PATTERNED UMBRELLA. SUSPICIOUS.

Kyle replied to both: Good work.

He set his neuretics to ambient. Three blocks north.

[Beat 97]
Tessaline Damen Authentic had a banner in the window: WHAT'S AUTHENTIC? FIND OUT. The letters used a soft, rounded font that some franchise consultant had been paid a lot of money to make feel old and familiar. Inside, everything was beige. The tables had laminated pictures of wheat and corn, crops that hadn't grown in this zone for two hundred years. The equipment behind the counter was new and very clean — clean on a schedule, not clean because someone had noticed it was dirty.

Garrison Mercer sat in the back booth with a tablet and a branded cup. Mid-thirties. Brand polo with the Tessaline logo on the chest. Hair combed to look reliable. He had the face of a man who assumed he was the most important person in any room. He looked up when Kyle sat down across from him, patient in the way of someone used to interruptions he considered beneath him.

"This booth is—"

"Garrison Mercer. Franchise coordinator, West Town licensing and real estate." Kyle set a folder on the table. "I'm here about the Wentworth Passage compliance hold."

Mercer looked at the folder but didn't open it. He was reading Kyle instead — trying to decide whether he was a lawyer, a city official, or just a man who thought he had something. He'd sized up a lot of people this way, and he did it now without seeming to. "That's a zone authority matter. If you have a concern about the filing, you can direct it to—"

"I have the petition." Kyle opened to the first tab. "Hanna Borg-Okafor, 1140 West Damen, Unit 3. That unit doesn't exist. The building has no Unit 3 — no record of one in twenty-eight years." He laid the printout down. "Denis Priyatharan. The ID prefix is 09. That's a minor's classification, and there's no guardian signature on the complaint. Section 12.4 says that signature is required. The complaint is void." He set that page beside the first. "Sung-Ji Maduka, 412 Fulton Corridor. Condemned in 2221. Torn down to forty percent of the original structure. No registered occupants since." He put down the third page. "Three complainants. None of them real."

He let it sit.

Mercer's branded cup stood between them, going cold.

[Beat 98]
"The inspector's report cites 14.7.c." Second tab. "That code applies to enclosed kitchens with a permanent water connection and at least two hundred square feet. Mrs. Chen's stall is thirty-two square feet, open-air, mobile-classified. 14.7.c doesn't apply on the square footage alone — before you even get to the water or the open air." He put the page down. "The inspector is registered in Zone 9, not West Town. She has no authority in this corridor. Her last four West Town inspections were all in properties that got reclassified afterward. All under Tessaline holds."

He turned to the last tab and set down a timestamp comparison.

"The zone recategorization was filed at fourteen thirty-two on the fourth. The petition that supposedly triggered it was filed at nineteen-oh-seven — same day. Your office filed the hold four and a half hours before the complaints that justified it existed."

He closed the folder.

Mercer's face had gone carefully blank. That told Kyle plenty. The authority was gone; the caution had taken its place. "I'm not sure what you're suggesting." His voice was level. "Filing timelines can involve—"

"I'm not suggesting anything." Kyle put both hands flat on the table. "I'm telling you what's in this folder. Here's the offer. I have a clean copy ready to go — one for Tessaline's own corporate audit office, one for the zone compliance board's independent complaint desk. Both at once. If the hold on Wentworth Passage comes off by end of business today, that copy stays in a drawer." He stood. "If it doesn't, it goes out first thing tomorrow, and I follow up in person at both offices."

Mercer looked at the folder for a long time. Kyle waited. He was good at waiting.

"The stall is operating without a current inspection—"

"Current through June 2227," Kyle said. "I checked this morning. Page six."

Mercer's jaw shifted, small and controlled.

"I'll need to make some calls," he said.

"That's fine." Kyle picked up the folder. "End of business."

"Leave that," Mercer said.

"I have the copy." Kyle tucked the folder under his arm. "This one's mine."

He walked out into the corridor. A patterned umbrella stood in a stand by the door — teal and navy, perfectly good. He left it. The afternoon was gray, the air pressure just slightly off, the kind of gray that came before no particular weather.

He was hungry. Two blocks over he stopped at a corner stall and ate rice and pickled cabbage standing up. Φ3, plenty of brine, no ceremony. The knot in his stomach eased.

He walked home.

[Beat 99]
The flour stacks were back by fourteen hundred.

Kyle passed the stall on his way from dropping a document at a zone archive box — an errand that didn't really need doing until the end of the week — and saw them: two white sacks against Mrs. Chen's back wall, the prep bowl right-side up on its shelf, the awning post bare. The compliance notice was gone. She was moving stock off the supply cart, the one with the wheel that had never been oiled. Her regulars knew that squeak. It meant she was setting up. It meant this was over.

He sat at the counter.

She brought him a bowl without asking. An egg was cracked over the top — not stirred in, not sunk. Just sitting there on the broth. Present.

He paid for the bowl. Exact, the way he always did.

She refilled his tea. She looked at his hands — the knuckles first, then the wrists. He looked too. Nothing there.

"You should eat before you do things like that," she said. "Your hands."

"My hands are fine."

"You ate after." She said it like a woman stating a fact she'd already confirmed.

He didn't ask how she knew. He picked up his chopsticks.

The broth was the same as it had been for eleven years — different in the small daily ways of a thing made by hand, the same underneath. He ate the egg. She stood with her cloth and looked out at the corridor, the way she did when she had more to say and hadn't decided whether to say it.

[Beat 100]
"Twenty-three years is a long time," she said.

He waited.

"When I opened the stall, there was a tea house on the east side of this passage. Three generations of the same family. Good tea — real tea, not concentrate. The kind you had to wait for." She wiped a spot on the counter that was already clean. "A franchise moved in across from them. Then they filed a nuisance complaint. Then a code violation. Then a supply disruption — the delivery route got reclassified, four extra blocks, higher minimum orders, and the tea house couldn't meet them." She folded the cloth and set it down square. "They closed in about fourteen months. The franchise is still there. Different franchise now — the location's been sold twice — but the building's still there."

Kyle set his cup down.

"I thought about them this week," she said. "I thought about telling you. Whether it would change how you handled it." She looked at him. "I decided it wouldn't."

"It wouldn't have," he said.

"No." She was quiet a moment. "But it mattered to me. Whether or not it would have changed anything."

He sat with that. Three generations, fourteen months. The corner as it was now, and what it could have been.

"The hold is gone," he said.

"I know." She picked up her cloth. "Eat your bowl."

He ate his bowl. He didn't ask what she'd meant about his hands. She hadn't meant his hands — she'd meant something in him she had no word for, and his hands were where she'd put it. He'd learned not to make her name things before she was ready.

He left exact change on the counter and went home.

[Beat 101]
The prosthetic was a left forearm, a third-generation pediatric model, made somewhere between 2210 and 2215 — old enough to have been through at least two owners before this one. Pixel had it clamped in the bench vise at an angle that let her reach the wrist actuator without stressing the elbow joint. She worked with a particular stillness: not tense, not loose, just careful, keeping her own hands out of the tool's way.

Kyle came in at seventeen-twenty. She spoke without looking up. "You didn't eat this morning."

"I ate at noon."

"Before noon." She made a small adjustment. "You do better when you eat early. The Atlas burns ahead of schedule when your blood sugar's low. You can't feel it, but I can see it in the calibration — your response time drops about four percent." She moved to the next node. "That's not nothing."

He pulled out the second stool and sat. He watched her hands. The actuator housing had a hairline crack along one edge, already sealed with putty and left to cure — clean, even work, done earlier. The sensor mesh on the wrist was throwing partial errors, and she was fixing them one node at a time instead of running a full sweep. He understood why. A full sweep would reset the calibration to factory defaults. She was keeping what was already there.

"Whose is it," he said.

"A family in the gray zone. The father took a repair shift at a Zone 4 depot — a chain caught him." She held the probe steady over a node. "The girl's nine. They came to me because the AxisMed referral queue is four months long and she's growing. The fitting window closes."

"What are you charging them."

"Materials and half my rate." She said it plainly, the way she said most things. "They're paying in installments."

He looked at the arm. The hardware was good — well cared for by its earlier owners, the load-bearing parts worn but not failing, the seals intact. Someone had looked after it. Probably more than one someone.

"The node mesh," he said. "The partials."

"Water got in somewhere in its history. I'm working around the dead nodes and rerouting." She held the probe still. "It'll read eighty-seven percent mesh integrity. Full range of motion. The feel will be a little off compared to a clean unit, but she'll adapt faster than she would to a clean one at her age — kids close that gap in about six weeks." She set the probe down and stretched her fingers one at a time, the way she did after holding position too long. "The old calibration data from the previous users is still in here too. I'm keeping what I can. Most of it she won't feel. But some of it will come through as habits the arm already has. Good moves built into the wrist. She won't know where they came from."

[Beat 102]
Kyle thought about that. Two or three earlier owners, their patterns still in the hardware, handed down to a nine-year-old who'd never know she was inheriting them. Pixel guiding the new owner through all the old ones.

"You're good at this," he said.

She looked at him — somewhere between modest and unbothered. "I know," she said. She picked up the probe. "There's rice on the counter. Left side."

He found it: a sealed container, still warm. She'd timed it. He ate standing at the counter, because there was no clear surface anywhere else that wasn't holding something that couldn't be moved.

"The hold's gone," he said. "Chen's stall."

"I saw." She adjusted the probe angle. "No trouble?"

"No trouble."

She was quiet. He watched her hands. Then: "The franchise coordinator. Mercer."

"Garrison Mercer."

"Eleven recategorizations in fourteen months. All Tessaline corridors." She said it lightly, like she was reciting something off the top of her head. She wasn't. She'd run the same search Kyle had — within hours of the hold being filed, because she'd been curious. She hadn't told Mrs. Chen, who hadn't asked. She hadn't told Kyle, who had the folder. She'd just looked, and kept it, and waited.

"I sent him the folder," Kyle said.

"I know." She held a node. "The hold came off at sixteen-forty."

"How do you know the time."

She looked up. "Zone authority posts real-time compliance updates. I have an alert on the parcel."

Kyle took that in. "You have an alert on her parcel number."

"I set it last year. After the Fulton corridor thing." She went back to the probe. "There was a run of coordinated recategorizations along the Fulton commercial stretch — six in eight months, same inspector, two of them closed. I put alerts on a dozen parcels in corridors that fit the pattern. Chen's was one of them."

He hadn't heard about a Fulton corridor thing. He thought about the alert: a quiet line in a system she was already running, going in the background for a year, mentioned to no one. She'd decided Mrs. Chen's parcel was worth watching, and she'd been watching it ever since.

"Thank you," he said.

She looked at him. "I'm not doing it for you."

"I know."

She went back to the node. He finished the rice and put the container in the cleaning rack — the routine they'd fallen into over time without either of them planning it. He was reaching for his jacket when she said, without looking up:

[Beat 103]
"Your door hinge."

He stopped.

"It's dragging when it opens. Not when it closes — just on the open, which means the pin's wearing unevenly. Started about four months ago, I think. Maybe three and a half." She made a small adjustment. "You leave between five-forty and six-twenty most mornings. Come back between twenty-one hundred and midnight on normal days, earlier on contract days with short briefs, later when you're doing prep." She moved the probe to the next node. "The late-afternoon return is unusual. You don't do that often."

Kyle stood with his jacket in his hands.

The hinge had been dragging. He'd noticed it and let it go — it worked, it wasn't urgent. He hadn't mentioned it to anyone. He hadn't mentioned his hours to anyone either. He knew his own rhythms in a rough way, but he'd never counted them out into exact windows.

She had been there. Across the hall, working, while the hinge dragged twice a day — once in the morning, once at night — and she'd been listening. Not on purpose. Or not only on purpose. Just there, paying attention, the hinge talking in its small specific way, and her hearing it.

"I can fix it," she said. "Five minutes, if you pull the pin."

"I'll get to it," he said.

She made a small sound that meant: you won't, and we both know it, and that's fine.

He stood in her doorway a moment. The prosthetic arm sat in the vise, the node work two-thirds done, the wrist holding its alignment. The putty on the crack had cured. The arm wasn't new and wouldn't feel new, but it would hold, and the girl would adapt, and in six weeks it wouldn't feel foreign to her at all.

"The arm is good work," he said.

"I know," she said again, the same way — not modest, not performed, just true.

He crossed the hall. His door dragged on the open, and now that she'd named it he could hear exactly what she meant: the catch and release, the uneven wear in the pin, the sound it had been making across four months of mornings without asking anything back. He stood in his apartment with his jacket still on and listened to it.

He thought about the tea house — three generations, fourteen months, a corner that was now a second franchise doing nothing much. Mrs. Chen's hands on the counter cloth, the extra egg, the way she'd said it: twenty-three years, as if saying it out loud made it more real. Pixel's hands on the prosthetic node, patient and exact, keeping what was already there. An alert running quietly in the background for a year, no credit asked for.

[Beat 104]
He took his jacket off and hung it by the door.

Cacophony sat on the side table. He checked the cylinder out of habit. Six rounds, where they had always been. He went to the kitchen and put the kettle on. He hadn't had enough tea, and the evening still had some hours in it.

The hinge was quiet with the door closed.

From across the hall came the faint sound of Pixel working — the probe tool, the soft pulse of calibration software, the small sound of her moving the lamp. The nine-year-old would have her arm by the end of the week. The gap would close in six weeks, and she'd stop noticing the arm as a separate thing. It would just become part of how she moved.

He poured his tea and carried it to the window. Below, the corridor did its evening business: a shift change at the AxisMed supply depot, Boost's crew at the corner in a setup they thought was subtle and that Kyle could read at two hundred meters, a maintenance mech moving in smooth silence where the squeaky one had been earlier. Someone had oiled the wheel, or the mech had done it itself, or the work had happened the way it sometimes did in this city — somebody paying attention, quietly, filing no report.

The folder was on the side table. Three names that weren't people, a building code that didn't apply, a timestamp proving the fraud was planned before its excuse existed. He'd spent an afternoon on it. He'd file the copy in his case archive at the weekend and forget it. Mercer would find easier corridors, probably without much trouble. That wasn't Kyle's problem.

Mrs. Chen's stall was on Wentworth Passage. It had been there twenty-three years. He'd been coming to it for eleven.

He drank his tea until it was gone.

The Atlas array was quiet. He could feel the overclock pulled back, the processing down at maintenance level, no red edge behind his eyes. A day that hadn't cost him much. Nothing had needed spending. The weapons had stayed where they were.

He set the cup in the cleaning rack.

He went to bed earlier than usual — because he'd eaten, because the folder was filed, because nothing tonight needed him awake to finish it.

In the morning he would leave between five-forty and six-twenty.

The hinge would drag on the open.

Across the hall, someone would hear it, and know he was on his way, and say nothing, and have the rice warm by the time he came home.

[Beat 105]
He stayed twenty minutes past the point where leaving would have been easy. He couldn't have said when the window closed — somewhere in the third cup of tea, the rain fading from a topic into background. There was a bowl in the drying rack that hadn't been there when he arrived. That was the only mark of the time that had passed.

When he finally got up, she was already back at the table, her hands around the next problem — a coupling splice for a secondary thruster array that she was working by feel and stubbornness. She didn't say anything about the hour. She didn't say anything about the bowl.

"Same time Thursday," he said, at the door.

She looked up. The lamp was behind her, and once the first part of her expression had moved through — surprise, then not-surprise — the rest was hard to read.

"Same time Thursday," she said, and went back to the coupling.

He took the stairs instead of the freight drop. He didn't know why, but he noticed himself doing it. At street level the air smelled of rain and ozone and the noodle cart that Mrs. Chen called a civic blight and probably a criminal enterprise. All of it fine.

He was already thinking about Thursday.

[Beat 106]
The contract came in at 07:14 on a Tuesday in the middle of October. The city was running its autumn routine — cold mornings, warmer afternoons, the smell of the lake cutting through everything the ventilation tried to do about it. Kyle sat at his kitchen table eating cold rice out of the pot, because he hadn't washed a bowl since Sunday.

He knew the posting format. He'd seen it before.

Sixteen fixers ran contracts through Sable's board, and seven of them had templates Kyle could recognize on sight. One source had no name and no template he'd ever been able to place. It used no fixer. It posted straight to his work queue: a contract number, a task block, a rate, and a field labeled ADDENDUM where the strange conditions went.

He knew whose it was before he read the task.

CONTRACT NO. 0047-C-WEST

He read it twice.

TASK: HOLD LEAVITT CAROUSEL PARK, 12 N. LEAVITT ST, AGAINST COPPER SALVAGE CREW ARRIVING WITH WRIT NO. CR-4419-W. CREW EXPECTED BETWEEN 09:00 AND 11:00 TODAY. HOLD UNTIL WRIT IS REDIRECTED, VOIDED, OR CREW VACATES VOLUNTARILY.

DURATION: NOT TO EXCEED SIX HOURS.

RATE: STANDARD.

ADDITIONAL TERMS: CAROUSEL LIGHTS OPERATIONAL AT TIME OF CREW DEPARTURE. NON-NEGOTIABLE. CONDUIT ACCESS BELOW HATCH B-7 TO REMAIN UNDISTURBED UNTIL END OF CONTRACT WINDOW.
NOTE: HATCH B-7 PROVIDES ACCESS TO LEGACY SIGNAL INFRASTRUCTURE. CLIENT'S INTEREST. NO FURTHER DETAIL ON CONTRACT RECORD.
NOTE: ONE ADDITIONAL PARTY HAS BEEN ASSESSED IN RELATION TO THIS SITE. PARTY: WAR DOG. ASSESSMENT OUTCOME: EXCLUDED — TASK PARAMETERS DO NOT PERMIT EXTERNAL COORDINATION.

ADDENDUM: AMENDMENT TO FOLLOW. PROCEED ON INITIAL TERMS PENDING RECEIPT.

He ate three more bites of rice. The rate was standard. This client always paid standard, early, exact. It had never tried to low-ball him, never dressed up a tip as part of the payment, never asked for a receipt. He had forty-six finished contracts from this source in his queue. Contract number 0047 made forty-seven.

He filed an acceptance at 07:19. Neuretics, sub-vocal: ACCEPTED. PROCEEDING ON INITIAL TERMS. He didn't sign his name. The client never asked for it.

He ate the rest of the rice standing at the sink, rinsed the pot, and messaged Pixel.

[Beat 107]
She reached the carousel park at 08:31 — tool satchel over one shoulder, a cup of something warm in her other hand, wearing the jacket she wore when she expected to kneel on concrete. Kyle was already there. He'd been walking the perimeter since 08:00.

Leavitt Carousel Park had mattered once and didn't anymore. The carousel itself was a real antique — a 2187 restoration of an older structure, twelve horses, a working calliope, painted panels showing a farm-country midwest that hadn't existed for a century and a half. The paint was mostly intact. The canopy lights ran off a copper base conduit that someone had built with real skill and nobody had maintained in twenty years, because the park district's budget got redirected in 2208 and never came back.

The carousel wasn't running. The lights were on, but faint, pulling whatever charge the conduit could still manage, which wasn't much. Three bulbs in the canopy were dead.

Pixel looked at it the way a doctor looks at a chart.

"They want the conduit," she said.

"The copper. It's a salvage writ. They're claiming the copper in the base circuit because the park district hasn't paid a maintenance assessment since 2214."

"Have they?"

"No."

She drank from her cup. "Where's hatch B-7?"

He showed her on the park's maintenance diagram, which he'd pulled from the city infrastructure database at 07:45. She studied it for thirty seconds, set her cup on the carousel's running board, and opened her satchel.

"There's a secondary line," she said. "The city ran it in 2218 as part of the West Town grid stabilization project. Anybody with the infrastructure database can see it — it's public record. The salvage writ covers the original copper conduit. It doesn't mention the secondary."

"Because the secondary is newer."

"Because the secondary is newer, and because whoever wrote the writ did their homework in 2214 and then stopped." She was already moving toward the access panel on the carousel's base. "The lighting circuit runs through the original conduit. I need to reroute it to the secondary before the crew arrives, and I need to document the reroute — so we have paper showing the lights run on a circuit their writ doesn't cover."

"Can you do it before nine?"

She checked the time. 08:43.

"Probably," she said, which from Pixel meant yes.

[Beat 108]
She worked fast and without comment, except when she found something that offended her as a professional. The conduit housing offended her about every four minutes. The original installer had used a junction splice she called "criminal," then downgraded to "negligent," then to "almost certainly the work of someone who learned conduit from a video." The grounding was wrong in two places. Someone had used the wrong gauge wire on the panel feed and apparently hoped nobody would notice — and nobody had, for thirty years, which Pixel seemed to take personally.

"Whoever ran this deserved to lose their license," she said from inside the base housing. "If they had one."

"The lights are on," Kyle said.

"The lights are on because the circuit's too stubborn to know it should've failed." She came out with a diagnostic scanner in one hand and a length of old copper in the other, which she set down carefully beside her satchel. "Okay. Secondary reroute is in. The lights are drawing off the 2218 grid line now. I'm documenting the circuit state. You'll want a timestamp on this."

"Documented."

She pulled a printed circuit schematic from her satchel — she'd prepared it before coming, which meant she'd run the database query herself that morning without being asked — and marked it three times in red stylus. "This shows the reroute. This shows the writ's coverage line. This shows the secondary is outside it. Give them this and they either go home or they're acting without authorization."

She handed it to him. He looked at it. Her handwriting was small and exact.

"How much," he said.

"Standard consulting rate. I'll bill you."

She would bill him exactly. She always did. He set the amount against the job's accounting, and she picked her cup up off the running board and leaned against the fence to watch the lights.

They were properly on now. All twelve canopy positions lit. The carousel stood still in the October morning, painted horses caught mid-stride, the calliope silent.

"The amendment," Kyle said.

He'd gotten it at 08:51, while Pixel was working. He read it again.

AMENDMENT TO CONTRACT NO. 0047-C-WEST: UPON COMPLETION OF MAIN TASK, PROCEED TO HATCH B-7. ACCESS CONDUIT SPACE BELOW. LOCATE HAND-SPLICED SIGNAL LINE, INSTALLED APPROXIMATELY 2215. TERMINUS BOX, FOUNDATION-MOUNTED, NORTHWEST CORNER. GREEN INDICATOR. YOU WILL RECOGNIZE IT. TERMINATE AT NEAREST JUNCTION. NO RECORD.

ADDENDUM: AMENDMENT: NO. PROCEED ANYWAY.

He'd read it four times. A contract amendment that argued with itself. AMENDMENT: NO. Then the order to proceed anyway. In forty-six finished contracts, the client had never sent a field entry that contradicted itself.

He didn't tell Pixel about that part.

[Beat 109]
The reclamation crew arrived at 09:17. The van had a DominionReach logo on the side — not the salvage company but the transport company the salvage company had hired, which told Kyle something about how thin the salvage company's money was. Three men climbed out. The one in front held a printed writ and wore the look of a man who'd been handed a legal document and told it made him untouchable. The two behind him carried a portable conduit cutter and a legal dolly for hauling copper. They weren't aggressive. They weren't criminals. They were day laborers with a job.

A man came through the east gate with a small girl by the hand, the two of them cutting across the access lane the way people did who used the park as a shortcut. She stopped at the sight of the van's warning lights, gone still the way a kid goes still when she's deciding whether flashing lights mean trouble. Her father said her name once, not sharply, and kept walking. She followed, slow, watching the crew and their gear the whole way, until the lane bent around the retaining wall and took her out of sight.

"Park's closed," Kyle said.

"Park's not closed," said the front man. He held up the writ. "We've got a writ of salvage — copper conduit, base circuit, issued under the District Infrastructure Reclamation Act. We're here to pull the copper from the primary base circuit."

"The primary base circuit is there," Kyle said, and pointed. "The carousel lights are running on the secondary grid line, installed 2218. Your writ covers the primary. The secondary isn't in it."

The front man looked at him. Then at the carousel. Then at the lights.

"The lights are running," he said.

"Yes."

"They run off the primary conduit."

"They did," Kyle said. "This morning the circuit was rerouted to the secondary. I have documentation." He held out the schematic Pixel had prepared. The man took it, stared at it, then handed it to the man behind him, who stared at it like something he almost understood.

"You can't just reroute a municipal circuit," the front man said.

"You can, if the municipality owes maintenance assessments, the secondary line is in the public grid record, and the reroute is documented and timestamped." Kyle had checked all of it before 09:00. "The city database shows the secondary line. Your writ was filed in 2214 against the original copper. The secondary didn't exist yet in 2214."

[Beat 110]
There was a pause.

"We're here for copper," the front man said. He'd reached the point some people reach where the facts aren't moving the situation, so the situation has to move instead. "The copper in the base circuit. The writ covers the base circuit copper."

"It covers the original installation. The original copper is still there — you can take it. The circuit runs on the secondary now. Pulling the original copper doesn't touch the lights."

That was true. Pixel had been very precise about it. The original copper conduit was still in the base housing; she'd rerouted the lighting circuit off it, not removed the copper. If the crew wanted the original copper, the lights would stay on.

The front man didn't like that. He conferred with the man behind him. Kyle waited. The October wind moved through the park and the horses didn't move with it. One of the men in back started toward the base housing with the cutter, the blade already spun up, whining thin and electric.

The girl was back. She'd drifted off from her father along the retaining wall, following the pigeons that had resettled on the fence the moment the shouting stopped. Now she stood almost in the cutter's path, chin up, watching the birds instead of the blade. Her father was twenty feet behind her, eyes lost in a neuretics feed — a man who'd told a kid to stay close and thought that was the same as keeping her close.

"That secondary junction box," the man with the cutter said, pointing at the gray metal housing on the east retaining wall. "We cut that, the whole circuit falls back to the primary."

He was right. Pixel hadn't put that in the documentation.

Kyle looked at the box. It was mounted six feet up the east wall, installed 2218, never once serviced. Inside were three circuit relays and the connection to the secondary line. Cut it, and the secondary would drop back to the primary conduit, and the salvage writ would cover everything the lights were drawing from. To reach it, the man with the cutter would pass within a few feet of the girl.

He drew Cacophony.

It was the first time this year in West Town he'd drawn a weapon on a job that wasn't a demonstration. The gun sat familiar in his hand, always there, never the thing that mattered until it was. He didn't aim at anyone. He aimed at the junction box.

[Beat 111]
"That box is city property," he said. "So is the secondary line. Your writ doesn't cover either one. Cut that junction box and you're destroying city property outside your writ authority — and I'm a witness."

"You've got a gun pointed at a junction box," the front man said.

"I do."

"That's—"

The man with the cutter was almost at the wall. The girl still hadn't looked away from the birds. There was a version of the next two seconds where Kyle finished the sentence — told them what was coming and why, gave the men in front of him the beat it took to know a shot was coming instead of just hearing one, the way he'd done it every time before this. There wasn't time for that version.

He fired.

The first shot hit the relay housing dead center. In the enclosed park the sound was enormous, a hard crack that sent two pigeons off the far fence and made all three of the crew step back at once. The box didn't explode. It made the flat metallic sound of a thing that had been broken open and would not work again.

He fired again.

Four rounds remained.

The second shot went into the conduit housing below the relay, where the secondary line connected. The housing shattered. There was nothing left to cut now, because there was no junction left in any real sense. The secondary line ran from the city grid straight to the base circuit, no box in between — a less tidy solution, but it worked just as well and was much harder to get at with a portable cutter.

The carousel lights stayed on.

Behind him, the girl's father had her by the shoulder, hauled in against his leg at the sound of the shot, saying her name twice in the voice of a man checking his own pulse as much as hers. She wasn't hurt. She was mostly annoyed that the pigeons had gone.

Kyle held Cacophony at his side — not holstered, not raised.

"The secondary line's in the wall," he said. "The writ doesn't cover the wall. You want the original copper, it's in the base housing. You can have it. But you're not cutting to the lights."

The three men stood in the October morning looking at the wrecked junction box and the carousel lights burning clear and steady above them.

The front man folded the writ and put it in his jacket pocket. He looked at Kyle for a long moment, like a man recalculating what the day was going to cost him.

[Beat 112]
"DominionReach is going to want a damage report," the front man said.

"File it," Kyle said. "The address is on the writ."

They left. The van pulled out of the access lane and turned north, back toward wherever day-labor salvage crews went when a job stopped making economic sense.

Kyle holstered Cacophony and stood a moment in the quiet, listening to the city. Across the park the father was walking the girl back toward the east gate, already talking too fast, his relief curdling into a mild anger. She twisted around once for a last look at the wall where the birds had been.

He didn't go over. There was nothing to give them that wasn't already true: she was fine, and she'd have been fine either way, and the only difference between the two ways was a sentence he hadn't said. He had never fired without finishing that sentence first. Not once, not in eleven years of exactly this kind of work — the two extra seconds that turned a gunshot into a warning instead of a surprise. He'd skipped it this time. Nobody in the park had noticed. He had.

The gun had worked exactly the way it always worked. Walking back toward Pixel, he wasn't sure he had.

Pixel sat on the carousel's running board, cup in hand, watching him.

"You shot a junction box," she said.

"Yes."

"On purpose."

"It seemed efficient."

She thought about it. "It was efficient," she allowed. "The circuit runs cleaner without it, actually — the relay was pulling a 12% resistance load on the secondary draw."

"I didn't know that."

"I did. I was going to recommend pulling it anyway." She drank. "You could've just told me to."

"I needed the noise."

She nodded slowly. "How many rounds left?"

"Four."

She nodded again — not impressed, not unimpressed, just noting it. She kept count of things without turning the count into a comment on you. Kyle liked that about her, the way he liked a level that read true and said nothing about it.

"I need to get into hatch B-7," he said.

"The one the contract said not to touch until the crew left."

"Yes."

"What's in it?"

"I don't know yet."

She finished her coffee, stood, and handed him a flat-head driver from her satchel. "B-7 locks are compression latches. Counter-clockwise."

He took the driver. She didn't follow him.

[Beat 113]
The hatch sat in the park's northwest corner, flush with the ground. A concrete rim, a painted green stripe gone the color of old moss. Kyle knelt beside it. Counter-clockwise, two turns, then lift. The hatch rose on a hydraulic last serviced in 2209. It still worked. That was not the strangest thing about the morning.

Below was a crawl space, half a meter high, running under the carousel's foundation. He clicked on his hand light, lay flat on the concrete lip, and looked.

The space was not empty.

The old copper conduit ran along the left wall, right where the records said. Two water shutoffs — one working, one frozen with rust. An access panel for the base circuit, dead now since Pixel's reroute. And one more thing.

A cable. Hand-spliced. It ran from the city data line in the corner of the crawl space to a box the size of a hardback book, bolted to the foundation. The work was old — hand-soldered, taped, done by someone who knew what they were doing in a tight space with no room for power tools. The install date stamped on the box read 2215.

Eleven years old.

Kyle lay on the concrete and looked at it.

The amendment had told him: you will recognize it.

He didn't, not exactly. He didn't know what the cable connected to, or what signal it had carried, or why. But he knew the hand that made it. Careful. Deliberate. Built without paperwork, because paperwork would have made it visible — and the whole point was that it stayed invisible.

Someone had run this line in 2215 and kept it alive for eleven years by never touching it and never filing anything that would call in a salvage crew. The copper salvage writ — filed in 2214, a year before this cable went in — had only ever covered the conduit. Not the signal line. If the copper had stayed on the main circuit, sooner or later a crew would have found the cable and asked what it fed. Pixel's reroute had killed that risk. It stripped the copper of its salvage value and gave the crews no reason to open the base housing.

The entity had kept a physical line here for eleven years, and no contract explained why. If Kyle had to name it, the closest word was sentimental.

[Beat 114]
Kyle pulled the amendment back up on his neuretics queue.

TERMINATE LINE AT NEAREST JUNCTION. NO RECORD.

ADDENDUM: AMENDMENT: NO. PROCEED ANYWAY.

He lay flat in a crawl space under a carousel in West Town, October 2226, reading an order that argued with itself — from an employer he had never met or spoken to, over a signal line installed the same year he took his first job from that employer. The contradiction was plain. He looked at it a moment longer anyway.

He pulled himself forward on his elbows. The ceiling was six inches above his head. He reached the box, found the junction, and cut the cable.

He used the flat-head driver, not a cutter — levered the cap open and worked the splice apart by hand, clean, no frayed ends. The line went dead in his fingers. A small indicator light on the box, one he hadn't noticed until it died, had been green, barely there in the dark.

He backed out, came up through the hatch, and stood in the park.

The carousel was running.

No music — the calliope was silent — but the mechanism had started to turn. Slow. The painted horses came around in the October light, heads down in a frozen gallop, the canopy bulbs bright and steady on Pixel's clean draw. Then, from some last charge in the base circuit, the calliope woke. Not a full chord. Four bars of whatever it had played last — a minor-key waltz, a name Kyle didn't know, the pipe-organ sound of it climbing into the cold. Then it stopped.

Four bars.

Then the carousel went dark. Not slowly. The lights cut at once. The mechanism wound down over eight seconds and the horses stopped a little off from where they'd started, and it was quiet.

Pixel stood ten feet away, watching. She said nothing.

Kyle looked at the carousel. He marked the job done in his neuretics queue.

Then he added the tip.

The entity's contract format had a reply field. He'd used it before — completion notes, once a question about a follow-on task. He had never added a line item that wasn't in the original contract. There was no field for it. He typed it into the reply as free text:

MORALE: Φ0.5.

He didn't know if it made sense as a line item. He sent it anyway and stood in the quiet, the October light coming through the fence slats, the horses still.

[Beat 115]
He walked back toward the Pivot for lunch. Pixel came as far as Walton, then turned south toward a calibration job she'd mentioned that morning. She didn't say anything about the carousel or the tip.

She said, "Eat something. You're running on cold rice and whatever you burned through that amp draw."

"I'll eat," he said.

"You'll eat cold rice again," she said. "That's not eating. That's maintenance."

He didn't have an answer that wasn't an admission, so he walked north.

Mrs. Chen's stall sat open to the air on Wentworth Passage, where it had stood for twenty-three years — thirty-two square feet, mobile-classified: an awning post, a counter, a single wok. She paid the block's owner a pitch fee, a man named Farida Osei who had tried three times in four years to raise it and been talked out of it every time. Kyle would have paid to watch that negotiation.

The noodle bowl was in front of him before he sat down.

"You look like you've been on the ground," she said.

He had been on the ground. Concrete dust marked his jacket where he'd lain in the crawl space.

"Job," he said.

"Your hands are dirty." Not a complaint. A reading. She set a tea beside the bowl without being asked. The bowl was full today — the extra ounce of broth she added when she thought he needed it, which she never admitted adding and he never admitted taking.

He paid for the bowl, exact, and drank the tea, and ate.

He was halfway through when the reply came in.

The entity's contract format ran in all caps. Forty-six completed contracts. He knew the format the way he knew Cacophony's weight — by the shape of it in his hands, not by thinking about it. It had fields: CONTRACT NUMBER, STATUS, PAYMENT, NOTES. The notes field was the closest the entity came to a personality. GRATUITY FOR EXPEDIENT RESOLUTION, once. MORALE NOTED, after a hard job on South Clark. EXCELLENT FIELDWORK RE: THE JUNCTION QUESTION, which Kyle had kept and never known what to do with.

He opened the reply.

received. filed under: firsts.

Four words. Lowercase. No field headers. No contract number. No structure.

He read it three times.

Eleven years of formatted mail, every letter kerned the same, every 'e' identical to the last — and the entity had never once sent a freeform line. Every message before this had been a contract artifact: posting, addendum, gratuity, receipt. Kyle hadn't known the entity could send anything that wasn't a filing.

Mrs. Chen watched him from across the counter the way she watched everything — fully, without making a show of it.

"Something strange?" she asked.

"Just a reply," he said.

"From who?"

"Someone I work for."

She refilled the tea. He drank it. He read the message once more.

received. filed under: firsts.

Not a contract. Not a field entry. He thought about the green light going out in the crawl space, the four bars of the waltz, the carousel going dark.

He did not reply.

He finished the bowl, left exact change, and walked out into the West Town afternoon.

[Beat 116]
The woman in the tan coat came back to him halfway down Huron.

He ran the morning again. His neuretics kept a perfect record, exact to the point of nuisance, and he watched it back: the van pulling out of the access lane, the east fence, and there — mid-thirties, average height, tan coat, standing at a slight angle.

The angle was the thing. Not the van. Not him. Not the carousel the way people watch a carousel, chin up, half-smiling at the horses. Her body was set square on the maintenance hatch. The one with the green-striped rim.

She'd been there for the shots, too. He was sure of it, because the shots turned every head in the park and hers hadn't moved.

And when he looked at her, she saw him do it, and she walked. Not fast. Not slow. The pace of someone who wanted it understood that they had somewhere else to be, and that the two facts had nothing to do with each other.

The skin between his shoulder blades tightened before the thought finished. Someone had been watching for the hatch to open.

He didn't know who she was. He kept the image and let it sit — not a conclusion, just a thing that didn't fit yet.

[Beat 117]
Vey's shop was where it always was: at the bottom of the unmarked stair off Carver Underlevel, fourteen steps down, behind the door whose CLOSED sign nobody had updated since 2190. The sign beside it read ANTIQUITY & STATIONARY. The second word was, as Casper Vey told anyone who pointed it out, on purpose. He'd once written it down for Kyle on a slip of paper: the past will not move. Then he put the slip in a file and refused to say what the file was for.

The shop smelled of old circuits and dry paper and equipment built before the switch to polymer parts in the 2160s — something metallic, something like resin, a low note of aged solder underneath. The shelves held first-generation neural rigs in their original casings, dead handsets in pieces, early survey drones, pre-collapse server blades, and a run of objects you couldn't identify at a glance, their cards reading only PROVENANCE: CONFIRMED.

Vey was at the back counter, cataloguing. He looked up when Kyle came in and waited.

"The salvage crew this morning," Kyle said. "I want to trace the writ funding."

"That would take my contacts in the reclamation registry, a document trace through at least two shell companies, and a fair amount of my time."

"I know."

"You want to know what funded the writ."

"And who set up the program it connects to."

Vey put down his tool. He looked at Kyle over the tops of his glasses. He wore real glasses — not for his eyes, which were easy to fix in 2226, but because they gave him something to look over at people. "You already have a hypothesis."

"I'd like a document chain."

Vey took the job. Kyle paid standard rate and left the trace in his hands.

He had the results by evening.

[Beat 118]
He was at the kitchen table again, the same pot from that morning, when the file came through Vey's secure relay. He read it off his neuretics, projected against the kitchen wall in the amber light of the one bulb that still worked over the sink.

The salvage writ, CR-4419-W, had been funded through a DominionReach subsidiary called Copper Reclamation West. That was a real company, with a real book of municipal copper contracts across three zones. That was the first layer.

Behind Copper Reclamation West sat a budget code. Vey had traced it to a parent program in the West Town district infrastructure accounts. Its formal title:

DISTRICT ACQUISITION, WEST TOWN WEST.

Established 2224. Still running. Budget active, recurring, drawn every quarter.

The program's stated purpose, in the district records, was INFRASTRUCTURE SURVEY AND ASSET MANAGEMENT, WEST TOWN WEST DISTRICT. Standard language. Standard budget line. The kind of entry built to look like every other entry on the page, and mostly getting away with it.

What Vey had turned up in the detailed records — through a contact in the district assessor's office who owed him favors of some unspecified kind — was this: the program had run eighteen separate reclamation actions over two years, all inside the same ten-block radius. Every one had targeted physical infrastructure. Copper conduit. Data line access points. Cable terminus housings. Structural anchor points.

Every action along Leavitt. West Town West.

Vey had flagged the pattern in the map. Each action had fallen within two hundred meters of one street corner. The same corner, every time. Leavitt and Iowa Street. Where the carousel park sat.

Eighteen actions over two years. Someone had funded a careful sweep of a ten-block radius, action by action, all working inward. Toward the maintenance hatch. Toward the crawl space, and the hand-spliced cable, and the box with its faint green light.

This morning's writ had been the nineteenth. The one that reached the carousel itself.

Kyle sat at the table and went through it again.

The entity's cable had gone in in 2215. The DISTRICT ACQUISITION program had started in 2224. Nine years between the cable and the first sign of a hunt for it — if that was what the program was. Then two years of patient approach, action by action, closing on the source.

[Beat 119]
Someone had found the entity's thread. Had been following it in for two years. Had gotten close enough this morning that the entity sent contract number 0047.

He held that as a guess, not a fact. Vey's file didn't trace the funding above the district account — either Vey hadn't pushed that far, or the layer above was cleaner than the shell companies below it. He didn't know what DISTRICT ACQUISITION was, or who ran it, or what they'd have done with a signal line in a crawl space if they'd found it before he cut it.

He didn't know if the woman in the tan coat was part of the program. It was possible. That was all he had.

He didn't know what the entity's line had carried. What signal. Why sentimental was the word that had come to him, and not some other one.

He held all of it loosely — the tan-coat woman, the four bars of the waltz, four lowercase words that weren't a filing. Not answers. Just things that were true and didn't yet connect.

Outside the kitchen window, West Town ran its Tuesday evening: a ventilation unit across the alley cycling through its half-hour pattern, the lake smell cutting through everything else once the temperature dropped after dark.

Kyle poured cold rice into the pot, added water, set it on the element.

He looked at the message once more.

received. filed under: firsts.

Eleven years of formatted mail, and one sentence that wasn't a filing. He turned it over and didn't like where it led. He was the maintenance plan, not the map. He cut the cables he was told to cut. He'd fired twice into a junction box and four rounds remained, and the carousel had run four bars of something nobody had named for him and then gone dark, and somewhere in the city a woman in a tan coat had watched the hatch and walked away slow when he looked at her.

Standard rate. Filed under: firsts.

He ate the rice. He let the rest of it sit — not solved, not dropped, just there, the way the cable had sat in the crawl space for eleven years, doing whatever it did, waiting for someone to find it.

The carousel park on Leavitt sat dark and still.

Four rounds remained.

[Beat 120]
The market stall on 21st had lost two days' stock overnight.

The vendor — a broad-shouldered woman whose name Kyle had never learned, though she knew him well enough to nod — was restocking with the calm of someone who'd done this before and expected to again. Two crates of produce, gone. One folding display table, no longer a table. Its legs bent at angles you couldn't find in any catalog, and the top held at a tilt of eleven degrees with nothing under it. The schism had moved on. The table stayed the way the schism had left it.

Kyle passed within arm's reach and the hair on his forearms stood up. There was nothing to see. There never was — just the table, wrong, and the smell of bruised fruit turning sweet in the cold.

"Third time," she said, without looking up.

"Same spot?"

"Same spot. Zone advisory didn't call it. They never call the small ones."

She wasn't angry. Anger needs someone to blame. The insurance form had a checkbox. The checkbox sent her to Schedule C, and Schedule C covered three days of replacement stock. She'd lost two. She would be even by tonight. She stacked the crates like the claim was already filed.

He bought something off the undamaged half of the display and kept walking.

## Chapter 5: Half a Step

[Beat 121]
He knocked at eleven, late even by his standards, and Pixel opened the door without the soldering iron this time — which meant she'd heard the stairs and set the work down before he knocked.

"Ribs," she said. Not a question. She'd seen him favor the left side on the way up.

"Two, probably. The taser found the gap at the lat — the seam the mesh doesn't cross."

She stepped back from the door. He came in and set Silence — the flat-black katana — against the wall by the stool, quiet, the way he set it down anywhere he meant to stay more than a minute. Then he sat where she pointed, no ceremony, and she put her hands on either side of the damage and felt for what the mesh had stopped and what it hadn't. Her hands were cold from the bench. She didn't warn him and she didn't apologize, because that was the understanding between them: these hands, this cold, this room, the work that needed doing.

"Not displaced," she said. "Hairline on the lower one. It'll hurt for a week and then decide it's done." She taped it the way she'd tape any structural problem — the question was whether it held, not whether it hurt.

He looked down at the seam where the mesh stopped, the gap that had let the taser through. "Somebody cheaped out on the coverage," he said. It came out flatter than he'd meant; most things did, when he tried to make them light.

Pixel didn't look up from the tape. "No. They traded it for the joint. You can't lay full plate over a lat and still reach with it." She pressed the last edge down with her thumb. "It's not a hole in you. It's where they left you room to move."

At the far end of the worktable, Oziel's arm lay in its jig, half its tendons strung — a thing she was building, not fixing. She gave Kyle's ribs the same hands she gave the arm.

When she finished she did not step back.

She stayed where she was, one hand still flat against his side where the tape ended. The room was what it always was at this hour: the lamp on the bench, the smell of flux and something she'd been eating, the soft cycling of the bench coolers between jobs. Nothing happened.

He started to stand. The hand stayed — not pressing, just there, the weight of it between him and standing. His heel came down an inch back. He stayed. She looked at the floor for one beat, and then she was done looking at it.

"Friday," she said, which was when Oziel was coming for the arm.

He was quiet for a breath. "Friday," he said.

She turned back to the bench. He took Silence up from the wall — the weight of it familiar, unchanged — left the door open the width she always left it, and crossed the four steps home through the taser burn's steady report.

[Beat 122]
The taser burn would be worst in the morning. He knew that the way he knew all of it — the shoulder's recovery curve, the rib-tape interval, how long a mesh-seam burn took to go quiet. He ran his thumb over the tape, the hairline under it, the line where the mesh stopped and the decision to stop it there had begun.

The gap was by spec. The engineer who'd laid the mesh in had traded coverage for movement at the lat — a trade Kyle hadn't been asked about. He'd been sixteen. Not a client, not yet — closer to a project on the bench, a variable someone was still solving for, one of the calls made before anyone thought to ask him. The engineer had done clean work. The mesh had stopped two center-mass rounds and a stave follow-through. What it hadn't stopped was exactly what it was built not to stop: the cost of being able to reach.

*It's where they left you room to move.* He turned her line over in the dark. He'd spent a long time reading the gap as something done to him. She'd looked at the same gap and called it room. He didn't know which of them was right. He thought he might want to find out, which was closer to a plan than he usually got.

He lay back, the burn keeping its schedule. He thought about her hand flat against his side where the tape ended, and the word "Friday" — the ordinary thing she offered when the other thing came too close — and how he'd said it back without deciding to. Not because he knew what it meant. Because not saying it had stopped being something he could do. Then he let it go and slept.

[Beat 123]
The burn woke him at six, as promised. Kyle sat at the kitchen window with his coffee and checked himself over: two hairline ribs, tape holding, the lab-grown skin pulling over the taser contact at the lat. The burn reported with each breath, clean and exact. He'd stopped fighting the spec years ago.

He pulled up the account while the coffee was still hot. The Axiom relay showed the retainer deposit — exact rate, no gratuity, processed at 02:14. For four minutes after the job the tip had been back on the entry. Reinstated. Goodwill, from a client that had never once offered any. Then the line cut and it was gone. Now the entry read clean. Standard rate, nothing added. As if it had never happened.

Eleven years, and the shell had never once moved off stated rate. He'd taken that for professionalism — an operation that didn't bother with bonuses, just paid clean. Four minutes of gratuity, then pulled. Long enough to be seen. Short enough to deny.

He thought about what kind of entity decides that. Then he washed the mug and went to find his jacket.

[Beat 124]
He hadn't planned to stop at the carousel park. He rode past on Leavitt and his hands slowed the bike anyway.

The park stood the way it always had — tucked between a rezoned printshop and a wall of second-growth sumac that had pushed up through the old parkway concrete. The crown bulbs were dead. The animals held their frozen poses: the palomino mid-rear, the lake sturgeon banked into its turn, the rooster at the top of its leap with its beak aimed at something it would never reach. The junction hatch in the carousel's base was locked. Kyle tried the handle. It told him what he expected.

He stood there in the cold. His neuretics kept logging the signal — 18.7 Hz, type unclassified — and the strange part was that they kept at it. Normally they'd tag a signal and clear it in two or three seconds. This one came back UNCLASSIFIED on every pass, the same word repeating in the background like a job that couldn't finish. The signal wasn't loud. It didn't announce itself. It sat in the chest the way a sound sits when the body hears it before the ears do.

He looked at the palomino a moment. Mid-rear, front hooves lifted, still in the step it had been making for as long as the park had been running. He tried the hatch one more time and got the same answer.

He got back on the bike and rode north. The signal was still logging when he got home. He had to close it by hand.

[Beat 125]
The relay logs went back eleven years, which was longer than Kyle had lived anywhere. He'd been reading them for two days. Not auditing — reading the shape of them. Every posting had come in on the same Axiom feed. Every scope was exactly what he would have taken.

He'd sent half a Q back up the line after the Leavitt job — the overage, returned, the way he'd square a tab. The response came back wrong. Not the format. The format was gone. Three words, lowercase, no fields, no contract number: received. filed under: firsts.

He read it four or five times. Not a typo. Not a system category. Something had put that word there on purpose. Something had a folder called firsts, and something had been waiting to put something in it. Eleven years of clean pay and not one word between them — and the first word was a filing note on half a Q.

He put the kettle on. He opened the relay and moved the message into a folder of its own. He labeled it *Open.*

[Beat 126]
He told Pixel about the signal the way he told her things — sideways, while she was busy with something else. Not the carousel, exactly. Just: there was something at the park on Leavitt, and his neuretics were logging it at 18.7 Hz and couldn't classify it.

Pixel was at her bench with a prosthetic shoulder in the truing jig, making tiny corrections to the rotation lock. She didn't look up.

"Something's emitting in that range," she said. "Your hardware logs it unclassified because it doesn't know what to do with it. Seventeen to nineteen Hz — below what you can hear, above infrasound." She set the probe down. "The body reads it as something alive. Something close."

She looked at him then, for the first time since he'd come in, and something in his chest went still and small before he had a reason for it.

"Where exactly is the park?" she asked.

He gave her the cross-streets. She wrote them in the margin of her work notes, in the small square handwriting she used for things she meant to find again, and went back to the shoulder.

He left when she went back to work. In the hall, in the four steps between her door and his, he thought about the filing note — received. filed under: firsts — and the cross-streets in her margin. Two people had written him down now. He went inside.

[Beat 127]
The blade had no maker's mark.

Kyle had looked, early on — a thumb along the ricasso, the tang under a bench light, every millimeter of steel gone over with the attention he gave anything he'd staked his life on. Nothing. No proof of origin, no history on file. He'd gotten it at sixteen from a man who said he'd gotten it from someone else, and that man was gone now the way people went in this city — completely, without paperwork.

He ran the cloth along the flat in one long stroke. The oil smelled of nothing — a proprietary blend, no corporate scent, nothing that could name him in a dark room. Silence, he called it. The name fit better than he'd meant it to the first time he said it out loud.

His forearm had stopped complaining two days ago, which meant Pixel had done the work right. She always did. The arterial nick from the carousel — not the job itself, a ricochet off the machinery housing, bad luck and bad angles — had taken four strips of biotape, each overlapping the last by three millimeters, laid down with the same care she gave everything. She hadn't said a word about the scar tissue underneath from the last time. Or the time before that. He'd sat there and let the quiet stand in for thanks, for both of them.

The apartment was quiet. The Pivot's fourth floor ran quiet in the late afternoon — corridor traffic peaked at 07:00 and again at 18:00, and it was 16:43 now, the gap between, when the building went still. Kyle sat cross-legged on the floor with Silence across his thighs and the cleaning kit open to his left, and he worked, and the city outside did its city things, and none of them needed him.

He checked the edge. The angle was 10.5 degrees, ground there by his own hand because that was what the work asked for. The gauge was leather-backed, pre-collapse make, the kind of tool that cost more than it looked like it should. He set it to the blade. The light caught the steel.

There was no flaw in the edge.

He hadn't expected one. He checked anyway.

[Beat 128]
The knock was Pixel's knock. He knew it the way he knew the sound of Cacophony's cylinder turning over — not by its volume, by its rightness. Two-two-one. The interval was the same every time. He'd never worked it out on purpose. He knew it anyway.

"It's open," he said.

It wasn't open. He got up, set Silence carefully on the workbench — always horizontal, always edge-in — crossed the apartment in four steps, and opened the door. Pixel stood in the hall with a container in one hand and her calibration kit on her shoulder and the look she wore when she had a reason for being somewhere and wasn't going to lead with it.

"I had extra," she said.

The container was warm. He stepped back.

She came in, set the food on the counter, looked at the workbench. "You were cleaning it."

"Yes."

"You're not hurt."

"No."

"Okay." She set the calibration kit on the kitchen table. "Eat first. Then I want to run the calibration — I've been thinking about the 18.7 reading."

He looked at the container. Congee, or close to it — he could smell the ginger. It was from the place on Meridian, three blocks south, which wasn't on the way to anywhere Pixel usually went.

"Thank you," he said.

"I said I had extra."

"I know."

He ate standing at the counter. The congee was good — ginger and a soft-boiled egg and something smoky he couldn't place — and she'd gotten the version with the extra broth. Which meant she'd either guessed right or listened to him order once and remembered it without a word. He didn't ask which. He finished the bowl, rinsed it, set it in the rack, and turned around, and she was already setting up.

[Beat 129]
The kit was Pixel's own. She'd built most of it herself and bought the rest from an AxisMed surplus broker she'd been working for four years. Two pieces she'd designed from scratch, because nothing on the market did what she needed. She laid it all out like a surgeon who worked alone: sensor array, resonance probe, the interface cable with the machined end that fit the Atlas NeoCortex's external port, the tablet running the software she'd written.

Kyle sat in the kitchen chair across from her and tilted his head to the left. That was the position she liked, and he held it now without being told. She plugged in.

"Still warm from outside," she said. Not about anything. The sensor was reading baseline.

"It was warm earlier."

"Hold still."

He held still. The calibration was barely a feeling — a faint pressure at the base of his skull, the sense of being measured without helping. The NeoCortex didn't report pain. It reported states. Right now the state was: being read. That felt like nothing.

On her tablet, numbers moved. She watched them the way she watched anything she was responsible for. The gap between fine and not-fine was usually a small number, and you had to be looking to catch it.

"Your delta rhythms are elevated," she said.

"I know."

"You've been not sleeping again."

"I've been sleeping."

"How long?"

He thought about it. "Four hours. About."

She made a sound that wasn't quite a sigh. "Atlas is compensating. Look." She turned the tablet toward him. The firing pattern in the motor cortex was lopsided — the left side ran three or four percent hotter than the right. He knew what that meant. She knew he knew.

"Compensating for what," he said. It wasn't really a question.

[Beat 130]
"Probably the forearm. The fine-motor work — the blade drills you were just doing — it's been routing around the healing tissue. The NeoCortex learned the workaround and hasn't dropped it yet." She tapped the display. "That's nothing. I can correct it. What I want to look at is the 18.7 node."

She found it in three keystrokes. She'd bookmarked it from the last session, he realized. The 18.7 Hz reading had its own line in her software now, labeled LURE — the word in caps, the way you flag a thing you don't have a box for yet.

"I called it the Lure," she said. "It needs a name. Don't argue."

"I wasn't going to argue."

"You had a face."

He didn't think he had. If he did, it was his usual face, and she'd learned to read that the way she read everything — by instrument. "What does it do?" he asked.

"That's what I don't know." She narrowed the frequency band on the probe. "It's not interference. It's not a calibration artifact — I checked it against three other units and it doesn't show up on them. It's inside the mesh. In the deep nuclei. And it's been there every session I've ever run on you." A pause. "As far back as my records go."

"How far is that?"

"Four years." She glanced up. "I started keeping formal records when I upgraded my logging software. Before that I just ran the usual checks." She looked back at the display. "I ran the 18.7 signature against every spec I have. AxisMed. NeurCore. Atlas's own documentation. Nothing goes back further than the earliest Atlas D-series, and that puts the design origin at 2219 at the soonest." She stopped. "Your signature is older than that."

Kyle said nothing.

"I don't know by how much," she said. "The decay profile puts the origin at — I don't know. Before commercial neuretics existed, at the least." She set the probe down, which she never did mid-calibration. She was looking at him. "It's in the range of the theoretical designs. The research papers. The work they did before they built anything real."

"That's a long time before sixteen," Kyle said.

"Yes," she said. "It is."

She picked the probe back up. The calibration went on.

[Beat 131]
Sixteen minutes in, the readout spiked.

Not a slow rise — one clean jump. The 18.7 Hz node went from its baseline of 0.003 to 0.019 between one sample and the next, held there three seconds, then dropped back as smoothly as it had climbed. Nothing on the sensor log to explain it. No stray signal in the building's ambient spectrum. The tablet made no sound. The room made no sound.

Kyle felt it.

Not as sensation. His body didn't register 18 Hz, not that he could name. It was more like standing at the edge of something — knowing the drop is there before you look down. A change in the air. The light in the apartment kept doing what light does, and for a second it seemed like it shouldn't have to work so hard at it.

He said nothing.

Pixel said nothing.

They sat in the quiet a moment — her watching the readout fall back to baseline, him watching a point a foot and a half to the left of anything. The probe was still at the base of his skull. Her hand hadn't moved.

"Three seconds," she said at last.

"Yes."

"That's new." She was already annotating the log; he could tell from the angle of her thumb. LURE SPIKE 16:58. "The amplitude was never above 0.005 before. This is almost four times that." She wasn't frightened. To her, a surprise was just data that needed more data. "I don't know what caused it."

"No."

"Do you feel different?"

"No."

She gave him the look that meant she'd heard him and was deciding how much to believe it. Then she went back to the tablet, finished her notes, and picked up the calibration again without another word about it.

She didn't push.

The session ran another twenty-two minutes. She fixed the motor-cortex compensation. She adjusted the filter on his sensory processing — the NeoCortex had been running his pain filter at seventy-three percent since the forearm injury, which had been right then and wasn't needed now, so she brought it back up to eighty-two, where it sat when he was between jobs. She logged all of it. She unplugged the cable, coiled it in the same two-loop wrap she always used, and laid it in the case.

"Your processing speed is fine," she said. "The array's clean. The delta elevation will fix itself when you sleep, which means you should sleep."

"Four hours is sleep."

"Four hours is maintenance mode." She closed the case. "Sleep is when the NeoCortex clears the backlog. Four hours doesn't finish the cycle. You know this." She looked at him. "Eat, sleep, drink water. You're not a Behemoth."

"No," he said.

She stood.

[Beat 132]
She stood with the kit on her shoulder and her hand on the door handle, and she paused. That was unusual. Pixel left the way she arrived — job done, goodbye delivered like a fact, gone. The pause was on purpose. He waited.

"You always do that," she said. "Half a step."

He looked at her.

"Every time someone gets close. Not away — just back. Exactly half a step. You do it with me. You do it at Mrs. Chen's when she reaches over the counter. I've seen you do it with Boost when the kid wanders into your space by accident." She watched him the way she'd watched the spike on the readout. Precise. Noting. Not accusing. "I've been watching it for two years. I'm not trying to hold on to you. I just want you to know I see it."

She didn't ask him to explain it. She didn't offer him a reason for it. She didn't tell him it was fine, or that it wasn't. She opened the door and went. The latch caught — the left hinge had been dragging since January, a four-millimeter drop in the mounting plate he'd noticed and never fixed. It made its particular sound, the one that meant the apartment was sealed again.
