Kyle stood in the kitchen. The kit was gone. The bowl was on the drying rack. The 18.7 spike sat in Pixel's software, labeled and timestamped, waiting for a category that didn't exist yet.

He noticed he hadn't moved.

He had taken a half-step back from the door when she opened it. He'd known it at the time, the way he knew any small motion of his own body — without making anything of it, because it cost nothing and changed nothing and had always been there. Always. He didn't know how long always was for him. He'd come into the world with a sword that had no history, and not much of one himself.

He went back to the workbench.

Silence lay where he'd left it — flat, edge-in, patient. He picked up the cleaning cloth and set it down again. He didn't need to clean the blade. He'd already cleaned it. He picked it up instead and held it in both hands, standing in the last low light through the window, the kind that struck the steel and gave him back his own face for a second.

The face in the steel was his face.

He looked at it the way he looked at the edge — checking, confirming, finding no flaw, moving on.

[Beat 133]
The half-step cost nothing. That was the fact he'd lived inside for years without naming it. It came as easy as breathing, as automatic as the routines the NeoCortex ran without asking him. He had been taking that step his whole life — or for as long as he'd been himself — and it had never cost him a thing, because he always took it in time. Before a moment could turn into something he'd have to answer for.

The cost was in the other thing. The staying. Not stepping back.

Pixel had come close enough to read his scars. The 18.7 had spiked for three seconds and explained nothing. What she'd said about his signature being older than any spec she could find was still hanging in the room with nowhere to go. And the question that lived under all of it got a little harder to set down.

That had a cost. He could feel it opening — the way he could feel a bad seam in body armor before his hands found it.

He set Silence on the rack. He turned off the workbench light. The room went gray, and he stood in it, and he knew two things at once: she sees it, and she is still here.

He knew what it cost anyone who tried to hold on to him.

He'd always known that number. He just hadn't had a name for the step.

[Beat 134]
She knocked twice, a long gap, then once. Kyle's pattern for the door. She'd never asked him about it. She just used it.

He opened it. She was holding a sealed container with both hands, because it was still hot.

"Extra," she said.

He stepped back — not far, just the half-step that was always there, old enough that he'd stopped noticing it. She came in and set the container on the counter he kept clear for tools. She pulled off her jacket and hung it over the back of the chair he used for equipment runs. Apparently that was the jacket chair now. Neither of them had said anything about it.

The food was real. Not a vend brick, not hydration paste — something she'd cooked. He could tell from the weight of the container and the smell when she cracked the seal. Broth. Grain. Something charred at the edges, which meant real heat. Steam rose between them and went flat.

"Didn't know I needed feeding," Kyle said.

"I had extra."

She was already eating, her own portion in a smaller container, standing at his counter — half on the surface, half in her hand, her back to the far wall. He hadn't noticed when that had become her spot.

Kyle looked at the container she'd left him.

He didn't eat. Not yet. He didn't move it, either.

She fished a blister strip out of her jacket pocket and pressed out a small white tab. Split it in half with her thumbnail. Set one piece on the counter beside his container without a word and took the other one herself.

"Medithadol," she said, though he hadn't asked.

"I know what medithadol looks like."

"Just checking."

She ate. He stood. The steam went flat.

He picked up the container eventually — not the tab, the container — and ate standing beside her. It was good. The grain had soaked up the broth all the way through, which took time. He didn't say so.

"You're running a job."

"Day after tomorrow."

"You're thinking about it tomorrow."

He ate.

"Lotus territory," she said. "Corridor work."

He hadn't told her that. He hadn't kept it from her, either. She had her own clients and her own ways of knowing things. He'd learned that pointing either one out just got him a patient silence that felt worse than saying nothing.

"The draw angle on Cacophony," she said. "You've got the ride too low."

He looked at the revolver on the counter, clipped to the edge where it lived when he was home.

"You clip your elbow on a tight inside draw. I saw it last month during calibration. High-quarter pull against a close wall — you'll swing wide before the barrel clears."

"I know how I draw."

"I know you know how you draw." She scraped the last of her broth. "I'm telling you what I saw. Raise the ride height two centimeters, or tilt the whole rig butt-forward five degrees. Corridor walls being what they are."

He looked at the holster. She was right about the clip — he'd felt it once or twice on tight doorframes and written it off as clearance. He didn't say she was right.

"You can fix it before the job," she said. "It's not a thing."

She capped her container and dropped it in his recycler, close enough to her own that she treated them as the same. She put her jacket back on.

Kyle reached over and picked up the half-tab from the counter. Took it.

"Eat the rest," she said, nodding at his container. "The grain gets stiff."

She left. He heard 2E's lock engage across the hall.

The holster sat on the counter where it always sat. He looked at it.

He ate the rest of the grain, which had gotten stiff. It was still good. He put the container in the recycler and didn't touch the holster.

[Beat 135]
Three minutes.

The waterworks crew at Elston and the canal had stopped work and were sitting on the curb, in the posture of people who'd done this math before. Tools down. Nobody near the equipment. A measured distance back. It wasn't a lazy kind of patience — it was the kind you keep on purpose. They watched the micro-schism the way they'd watch a traffic light. One of them was eating. Two watched the gutter, where the pebbles and grit had drawn themselves into a slow drift, circling nothing, like iron filings around a magnet that wasn't there.

Kyle stopped at the corner and checked the advisory on his relay board. Zone 7, two actives listed. This one wasn't on the board. The crew had found it themselves and made their own call.

He felt it before he'd fully stopped — a pressure in his back teeth, the sense of a sound pitched below the morning.

"How long?" he asked the one who was eating.

"Eight minutes so far. Usually three to five. Give it another two."

He waited. At the three-minute mark the pebbles settled. The drift slowed, the pattern came apart, and the pieces went back to being ordinary grit with no opinion about gravity. The pressure let go of his teeth. The crew stood, gathered their tools, and went back to work without a word about it.

Kyle crossed Elston and went on toward the dock.

[Beat 136]
The fifth came up the block the way ordinary people come up blocks. Kyle didn't clock her until she was already inside the loose triangle the three of them made — and that bothered him more than if she'd dropped out of a window. She was medium. Medium height, a coat the color of a coat, hair pulled back the way you pull it back when you've stopped thinking about it. The kind of face a room rounds down to nothing. You could ride an elevator with her and swear a week later you'd ridden up alone.

"Sift," she said. That was the codename and the whole introduction. She said it the way you give a barista a name you don't expect to hear called back.

Kyle read her. Quiet, polite, forgettable on purpose. That was a skill, not an accident, and a skill that took that much work to look like nothing was usually pointed at something. He had no idea what she did. The brief hadn't said. On a clean crew the brief never told you more than your own piece — Shroud knew the building, Stash knew the gear, Kyle knew his piece was to stand between everyone else and whatever came through a door. If he didn't know Sift's piece, that meant hers was the one the client cared about.

"You're the muscle," she said to him. Not a question. She had a read of her own. Then she tilted her head, the way someone does when they've already decided to like the answer. "What do I call you?"

Kyle opened his mouth. He got one syllable out — maybe less, the K of his name — and Shroud's voice came flat into the channel. *No real names.*

He closed his mouth. He looked around. The corner. The rain. The shuttered pharmacy with Stash's weight still denting the doorway. And down by the base of the step, carried there from somewhere else by the gutter, a glass soda bottle with a faded orange label.

"Soda Pop," he said.

Sift snorted. A real snort, unguarded — the first thing she'd done that wasn't careful, and it did something to her plain face he hadn't expected.

Kyle picked up the bottle and threw it against the pharmacy wall. The glass went everywhere, sharp and sudden, the small violence of it heavier than the sound it made.

"Broken Glass," he said.

She made the same sound again, working now to keep it in. She looked at the pieces on the wet concrete. She looked at him. Something moved in her face — amusement, or a second take, or both — and when it settled she said:

"I'm just going to call you K."

He went back to watching the street, and didn't look too hard at why it mattered that she'd kept the K.

[Beat 137]
The transmission completed. Sift said, "Clean." One word. The job's word for done.

The thing came up the ramp before the echo died — not running, just arriving, the way an appointment arrives. Four arms. Three of them were shotguns, the barrels socketed straight into the wrist housings, where a normal man's hands would be. This one had spent real money to stop being a normal man. The fourth arm carried something belt-fed, a chain running back through the shoulder housing.

It didn't announce itself. It didn't give her a chance.

It had the angle on her before Kyle was moving. Kyle was moving before the decision reached him. He was already moving when it no longer mattered.

[Beat 138]
She was dead before the last echo. He was already past her.

War Dog's grenades went out wide before they'd cleared the deck — not aimed at anyone, just thrown to clear a kill zone, and the blast radius was generous. Shroud peeled off before the first one hit the ground. Kyle didn't call her back. Stash was already gone too — the way people go when they've been in a room like this before and know exactly what a frag pattern does to their gear.

Kyle dived left as the first grenade went off and came up behind the loading bay's support column, ears ringing, the ceiling still raining concrete dust.

## Three Barrels

[Beat 139]
The first shotgun spoke, and the loading dock answered in dust. Kyle wasn't where the dust was. He'd read the lead arm a quarter-second before the wrist committed — the way the shoulder loaded, the way a man with three barrels still aims with his eyes — and he'd gone low and wide, off the line, behind the concrete pillar that would hold up against buckshot. A freelancer's cover wouldn't have. The blast took a bite out of the bay door behind where he'd been, and the noise was a flat hand against both ears.

The read was simple. Cacophony had six rounds left in the cylinder. War Dog's belt-fed arm held more than that, and nobody wins a trade against a wall of fire. So Kyle kept Silence in his hand. The blade was an argument for close distance. The only way through a wall of fire was to be past it before it knew you'd moved.

One of War Dog's grenades had bounced wrong in the clearing pattern. It came to rest six feet from the column, its arming light cycling toward the end of its count.

Kyle picked it up.

The Atlas NeoCortex had no governor. That was the thing killing him, and it was the only reason the next three seconds were possible. He connected to the detonator port through his neuretics and found the lock on the countdown — a rolling cipher, built to make disarming it in the field impossible. He broke it. Not with finesse. With raw processing power, the way you use a sledgehammer when you can't find the key, running through the cipher faster than the hardware was ever rated to go. The heat came up behind his eyes like a filament burning at both ends.

He stopped the countdown with one second left. He set the grenade near the mouth of the ramp exit and left it there.

Then he used the dock. The bay was a stack of crates, a lift platform, and a steel stairwell climbing to the loading office, and Kyle made War Dog spend his fire on plywood. He didn't run from the guns. He ran the room around them, so every shot landed where he'd just been, and every half-second the three arms took to cycle their breeches put him one step closer. The torso turned to follow him on a ring that moved independent of the legs. Kyle felt the lead arm track him through a stack of cartons and threw himself off the line a breath before the boards turned to air.

"Pillar's chewed," Shroud said, fast, in his ear. She'd gotten clear but still had eyes on the structure from outside, her thermal scope reading through the dock's steel skin. "You can't trade. He's got a belt-feed on the left arm, he doesn't run dry —"

"I know what he doesn't do," Kyle said, and went up the stairwell.

It was the wrong direction. That was the point. A man fighting three shotguns runs for the exit. Kyle ran for the high steel, because a stair is a corridor, and a corridor turned three barrels into one.

[Beat 140]
War Dog tracked by heat. Kyle's IR dampeners cut his signature down without erasing it — he was a smudge now instead of a man, and a smudge bought seconds. He spent them low and close, moving through the sight lines War Dog expected to find empty, keeping concrete between himself and the belt-fed arm, breathing through his mouth to stay quiet.

At the mouth of the ramp, the grenade sat waiting, one second still on its clock.

[Beat 141]
War Dog crossed toward the decoy fire — toward the grenade at the ramp mouth, away from Kyle. It put his back to the open center of the deck. It put his spine between the shoulder housings, at the junction where the four arms met. That was where the routing ran. That was where something behind the chassis wall held the whole machine together.

Kyle ran.

He drove Silence in two-handed, between the shoulder housings, edge-first and deep, angled up toward whatever ran behind the plating. He felt it catch. Not flesh, not bone — something that seized and locked, and sent a tremor up the blade and into his wrists that he was still feeling thirty seconds later.

War Dog turned. The left leg didn't come with him. The actuator had frozen mid-stride, and he pivoted on a foot that wouldn't move. The right-front arm — a shotgun housing — had gone slack at the socket and hung at an angle the joint was never built to reach.

## Who Vouches for You

[Beat 142]
Stash had pulled the van back from the bay and stood in the gap between the rear bumper and the dock's concrete wall. They'd laid their coat over her. The shape under it was small and final, and the hem of the coat was dark with something that wouldn't wash out. Their hands were still at the hem where they'd smoothed it down — the hands of someone who'd run out of work to do and hadn't moved them yet.

Kyle stopped. He put Silence away.

"She was already —" Stash started.

"I know," Kyle said.

Ledger stood on the far side of the van, looking at nothing in particular. That was the look Ledger had when he was paying the most attention. He'd been at his position. The vehicle had come from below. He would put it in the report, and the report wouldn't make it make sense, because there was no sense to make.

"We need to move," Stash said. "Shroud's already clear."

"I know," Kyle said again. The dock smelled like old concrete and cold off Lake Michigan and the hydraulic fluid still bleeding out of the arm three stories down. He owed her one beat, unhurried — the way he'd owe anyone who stood their ground in the last moment they had.

Then he picked her up, coat and all, the way Stash had laid it, and set her in the back of the van himself. Stash's van. Their work vehicle, the one they drove for the Vultures jobs, the one that had carried this kind of weight before.

The body was evidence. That was true. A Read's mind didn't go dark at death the way a normal person's did — not right away, not with the right equipment. Put the brain in a tank of conducting fluid, run electrodes through the right structures, and what she knew was still in there: the client's name, the data she'd pulled, the job, the faces on the team. Someone with the money and the reason could pull the last hours out of a dead psionic's head and use them to kill every person in this van. That was true too.

Kyle knew both true things.

He carried her anyway. You did not leave a comrade behind.

He got in.

[Beat 143]
He fired blind. The remaining arms swept backward in wide arcs — not aimed, not targeted. Suppression, from something that had stopped trying to find Kyle and started trying to make the air between them impossible to cross. Kyle took cover behind the nearest support column, let the fire go past, and counted the pattern.

War Dog backed toward the edge of the deck. One step. Two.

Kyle reactivated the countdown through his neuretics.

War Dog didn't stop at the rail. He didn't look back. He went over — three stories of open air, then pavement. The choice made in the half-second between the grenade's arming tone and what staying would cost him. The frozen leg and the hanging arm hit the ground together, in a sound Kyle felt in his back teeth.

Then War Dog moved. Faster than a frozen actuator had any right to move. Into the city, into the dark, into whatever network had sent him here and was pulling him back now that the job was done.

The parking structure went quiet. Kyle stood in it with Silence in his hand.

Three blocks out, he said, "Stop the van."

Stash stopped the van. Kyle got out and walked back the way they'd come. He stood on the corner where the light was good and checked Silence for damage — the way you check a tool after you've asked it to do something it wasn't built for. The blade was intact. The edge had held.

He walked back to the van and got in.

Nobody asked.

[Beat 144]
At the second light north, the channel chirped once.

A fresh posting. Pushed wide to every freelancer band in the van's radius — not targeted, not narrow, the kind of push the relay gave anything it flagged as time-sensitive. Clean scope. Listed retainer. A client field that resolved to a shell, and the shell to nothing.

Stash went quiet in the way of someone who has just run a number and doesn't like where it lands. They kept their eyes on the road. They didn't check the mirror.

Ledger read the posting. Then he looked at the back of the van. Then he read it again.

Kyle sat with the glass through the lining of his coat pocket and didn't open the posting. Twelve minutes since the dock. The relay didn't know that. Or it didn't care. Or it had known before they even left, and the posting had been ready, and the second light north was the exact second light north.

He watched the rain on the windshield and didn't think about it.

He would open it in the morning. He would take the job. He had a funeral to price.

## Chapter 6: The Quiet Hour

[Beat 145]
Nobody talked on the way back.

Stash drove. Ledger held the cases on his lap the whole way. He didn't need to — the floor was fine, the locks were fine — but he did it anyway, and nobody said anything about it. The elevated passed overhead in sections. Sodium light dropped orange bars across the windshield, across Ledger's hands, across the shape in the back that none of them looked at.

The tremor was still in Kyle's wrists. The blade had picked it up between War Dog's shoulder housings. He flexed his right hand once and let it go. It didn't go.

He made himself think about the job instead. Not the woman — the job. She'd pulled a file out of an operator's head and carried it behind her eyes onto the dock. That was the thing War Dog had come to collect. Kyle would never know what was in it. You didn't keep that part. The client paid for the extraction, the extraction got done, and everything after belonged to someone else.

But he kept seeing the way she'd moved out there. She'd read the geometry the same second he did and never once asked him to check her. There was a word for what she'd been. He didn't say it in the van.

The relay stayed quiet the whole drive home.

[Beat 146]
Pixel's light was on across the hall.

He knew the rhythm of it — two steps and back, two steps and back, the way she moved between her bench and her terminal when she was in the middle of something. He stood in his doorway a moment. He did not knock. He went to bed.

He woke at 04:00 and lay in the dark until the gray at the window was enough to call it morning. He made coffee. He drank it standing. He did not look at the relay.

By 07:30 he looked at the relay. No new jobs. The last client had paid up front — clean rate, clean scope, full deposit before entry. That was closed. Done.

He had Mrs. Chen's number. He called it.

"Thursday," she said, before he finished the question. Her voice already carried the weight of someone who had done this before. "Six o'clock. Tell whoever she kept."

He did not ask how she knew. She had run that counter for sixty years.

[Beat 147]
The rain arrived first. On Halsted it ran down the south face of the elevated overhead and came apart into a second weather at street level, a steady drip off every edge. Kyle came on foot. He had the address right. He checked it twice anyway — not because he doubted it, but because standing on the sidewalk was still just standing on the sidewalk. Once he went inside, the number would mean something else.

The block had been the same block for forty years. The streetlights gave it away: the older municipal grate pattern, wider spacing, no sensor housing at the center. The buildings were plain and worn, kept up just enough by people who used them every day. A laundry line between two third-floor windows held three shirts and a child's jacket, all the same off-color from the synthesis-corridor air that drifted east on certain winds. A sundial dog crossed the far end of the block on whatever circuit it kept, indifferent to the rain.

Kyle stood at the vestibule.

A handwritten sign was taped to the inside of the glass. The tape had gone amber with age, though the paper itself was new. Ṭu's full name. Kyle had never seen it written out before. He stood there longer than he meant to.

The rain split into two sounds while he did — the high thrum off the structure overhead, the softer knock on the vestibule's aluminum overhang. He went inside.

[Beat 148]
Forty people Kyle did not know.

Mrs. Okafor had a clipboard. She moved through the front room the way a good coordinator moves through a site — not hurrying, not stopping, handing a plate here, steering foot traffic there. She caught Kyle's eye once across the room, checked the new arrival against her list, found what she needed, and moved on. The hand she kept at her side was not entirely still.

His neuretics caught the frequency before he finished reading the room.

The Psyk was in the corner, in the chair closest to the window, back to the wall. Jaw set. Both hands flat on their thighs. The Read was running open — nineteen hertz, the kind you feel in your back teeth, a hum leaking past whatever the Psyk was using to hold it in. Kyle felt it and moved on.

On a corkboard near the far window there were photographs. Most were printed on the standard quick-format feed, slick-edged, from a public output terminal. One was different. Paper, not plastfilm, a little smaller than the rest, the corners soft from handling. Ñu at maybe nineteen, laughing at something outside the frame. The laugh was real — full, unguarded, her whole body in it. Kyle looked at it a while. He reached to take it off the board.

Mrs. Okafor's hand closed gently around his wrist from behind, barely a weight.

"That one stays," she said, not looking at it.

She let go and moved on. He let his hand come back. He did not know that laugh. He had known Ñu for six years and he did not know it, which meant it came from somewhere he had never been — a life she'd had before the work turned her into the person he knew. He had never made room for that version of her. Now he never would.

Mrs. Okafor was back at his left shoulder. She handed him a plate without asking — jollof rice, two pieces of chicken, a fold of flatbread — and turned to the next thing before he could take it.

He took it with both hands. The plate was warm. He stood there holding it.

He did not eat.

[Beat 149]
They came in ones and twos through the rain. Not the kind of crowd that advertises.

Ledger came first, because Ledger was always early and never explained it. He took the far end stool, ordered once, and didn't speak for an hour, which for Ledger was grief. Stash came next, walking careful, their load invisible tonight. They sat beside Kyle without asking and ate in silence, with the same precision they brought to everything. A woman Kyle knew as Casing arrived with someone he didn't know, introduced neither of them, and ate two bowls with the steady efficiency of a person taking care of what needed taking care of.

Pixel came from across the hall. Kyle did not ask how she'd known about Ñu. The two of them had crossed paths on the job two years back, the Mirrorwell run. That was how Pixel had ended up with the analog barometer that still sat at the far end of her bench — hadn't moved in two years, probably never would. She'd never explained how she got it. Ñu had never mentioned giving it. Some things between women in the work ran on lines Kyle wasn't on.

She sat at his shoulder and said nothing for a long time. Then: "She was good."

"Yeah," Kyle said.

"I know three people who are that good. Now I know two."

He didn't answer. There wasn't an answer the right size.

The others came after — faces from jobs Kyle had worked next to, not with; people from Ñu's side of things, names he didn't have, which was correct, which was how it worked. Forty people in a noodle counter on Halsted, eating broth in honor of a woman whose real name none of them had been given, and every one of them understanding exactly what that meant.

[Beat 150]
Four seconds.

The Psyk lost containment and the hum spiked — not louder, deeper, the nineteen hertz thickening into something that sat in the chest. Three people near the window flinched without knowing why. Kyle's neuretics lit with it, a quick static bloom at the edge of his array. He met the Psyk's eyes across the room. The jaw was still set, the hands still flat, but the eyes had gone wide and unfocused — the way eyes go when something is moving through a person that won't quite hold. Kyle held the look. The Psyk pulled it back, hauling it in the way you haul in something that doesn't want to come. The frequency dropped. The three by the window settled, not knowing they'd moved.

Four seconds.

Mrs. Okafor was at the far side, refilling a tray. Someone laughed quietly at something said about Ñu — not the laugh from the photograph, a different one. The nineteen hertz ran under all of it, low and steady, four beats of it keeping time before it eased back down and stayed.

[Beat 151]
Kyle found the Psyk outside on the curb, both hands pressed over their ears. Not in distress, exactly — managing it. Elbows on their knees, the careful posture of someone who'd done this before and knew the drill.

The hum was audible now without any help from his neuretics.

He sat down beside them. He still had the plate.

The Psyk didn't look at him. They kept their hands over their ears and stared at the wet concrete, where the rain came down clean and the runoff ran into the gutter at a steady rate.

"It's not one grief," the Psyk said. Their voice was controlled but close to the surface. "Forty griefs running at once. None of them the same frequency."

Kyle set the plate on the curb beside him. The jollof rice had gone cold, the chicken still on it, untouched.

He sat with the rain and the cold plate and the forty griefs, and he did not try to make any of it smaller. He sat until it leveled, and slowly the Psyk's hands came down from their ears.

[Beat 152]
Mrs. Chen found him at the end of the counter when the second round came around. She set a folded envelope beside his bowl without a word — heavy cream paper, the kind with a watermark pressed into it. The letterhead read *Vey's Antiquity & Stationary*, and under it a Dearborn address. The private entrance, not the counter-face number from the first time he'd stood at the shop.

Two words inside, no signature, in a hand that was not Mrs. Chen's — small, formal script, the writing of someone precise.

*Meet me.*

He read the two words and let them sit. Whoever sent this had put pen to paper and handed it to Mrs. Chen in person. That meant they had been here — at this counter, on a Thursday, at the wake. That kind of thing did not travel on relay channels. Someone knew where Kyle went when he had nowhere else to be. Someone had waited for the right room to hand him the note, and had it ready before he walked in.

He folded the envelope once and put it in his coat. He finished his broth. The wok came back up like a bell, and the counter filled again with the sound of something ongoing, and he sat in it a while before he stood and said goodbye to the ones whose names he knew.

He did not know all of them.

[Beat 153]
He had the address from Mrs. Chen's card. She kept a registration for the community that gathered that night, organized the way she organized everything, by district and block, and he'd read it on the way in out of habit. Null's entry sat at the bottom. *Null/Mimi T.* Registered the same careful way she did everything.

Six blocks from the carousel park. He hadn't put it together until he was standing at her door in the rain.

His neuretics were still running their baseline from the wake, and a line surfaced at the edge of his sight: AMBIENT INTERFERENCE — 18.9 HZ — TYPE UNCLASSIFIED. Point-two off the carousel. Same classification, same range. Two places six blocks apart on the same stretch of Halsted, both holding a note that nothing on either block had a reason to hold.

The cold came up off the wet stone into his hands. He stood at the door a moment. Then he went in.

[Beat 154]
The vestibule was quiet, the kind of quiet a place keeps only with effort. Old-spec ferrocement. The floor composite worn cold and smooth, stood on for decades by people nobody had bothered to replace it for.

The directory listed thirty-four names. On the third floor, one gap — a slot where the card had been pulled clean, nothing set back in its place. Someone had already taken her name down.

He took the stairs.

[Beat 155]
The corner table at Davorka's had a hand-written sign on it.

Not a standard Davorka's menu card — a piece of cardstock in handwriting he didn't recognize, in a plastic sleeve gone yellow at the edges. It read:

*RESERVED*

*for whoever comes back*

Davorka's had eighteen tables. Seventeen of them turned. He had been here fifty times, maybe more, and the corner table had never had anyone at it. He'd asked once, early on, and Davorka had explained it the flat way you explain a thing you've explained many times. A micro-schism had come through the previous winter and done something to the corner's geometry. Nothing structural, nothing you could see. You noticed it only if you sat there long enough: the candle guttered wrong, the sound from the bar didn't arrive in the order it should, the space between the wall and the chair felt like it had an opinion.

The previous tenants — a couple, regulars — had been sitting at that table when the schism passed through. They finished their drinks, left, and didn't come back.

They left because of the geometry, Davorka said. Not because of anything the schism had done to them.

The staff set a glass at the corner table every shift. They didn't clear it between seatings.

Kyle sat at one of the other tables and ordered what he always ordered.

[Beat 156]
Mrs. Okafor had been there already. The candle in the window, the plate of food on the counter, still warm enough to say she'd come within the last hour. She didn't cook for the dead. She cooked for the people the dead left behind.

The room was sparse in a way that said something about how Null had lived — not poor, just without accumulation. A work surface under the window. Tools laid out the way people who ran the Read laid out their materials: easy to reach, nothing for show, kept for use and for the record of use. The window faced east. When the first light came, it would come through that window at the right angle. She would have known that when she took the room.

Kyle stood in the center of it and touched nothing. It was still her room. It had the feel of a place that hadn't yet been told what it was now — the last moment before it became empty.

[Beat 157]
The photograph was on the shelf above the work surface, in a cheap composite frame. Three people on a rooftop in summer, the elevated behind them, the light the specific kind you get in July or August. Sift was on the right, hair loose, holding a bottle, not looking at the camera. The other two were younger, or she was older; the photo didn't make it clear. All three were laughing at something outside the frame.

He had known her handle for six years. He had known her work. He had not known she had people she stood on rooftops with in summer. He looked at the photo a moment and let both names go. She was who she was. The handle was the name she'd offered him. It was the whole of it, and he had never asked for more. That had been a fair arrangement — the kind she would have preferred.

[Beat 158]
They sat in two stacks on the work surface, under a clear weight — half-size cardstock, the kind from the practitioners' supply shops in the old zone markets. Printed in the tight hand she used for everything: date codes at the top, classification marks she'd have worked out herself. The notation was private, the personal shorthand of someone who ran the Read for hire and kept careful records. Not for anyone else. For herself.

He didn't read them. Not because he couldn't work out the grammar — he'd known enough practitioners to have the general key — but because they were hers. Her being gone didn't change that.

He put his hand on the stack. Not picking it up. Just the weight of it under his palm. Her handwriting showed in the margin of the top slip: a date from three weeks before she died, and two words in her code. He didn't need to translate them. He knew what they meant.

She had known.

There was a folded note flat under the weight, his handle on the outside in her tight hand. He opened it.

*Before Broken Glass. Meant to tell you. The name I use — you do the same thing, smaller. Third try. It held.*

He put it in his coat.

[Beat 159]
A Psyk sat in the stairwell on his way down — young, work clothes, a read-kit bag between their boots, two steps below the second-floor landing. Running the slow, controlled breath that pulls a sensitive's range back down under the ambient.

"It's strong here," they said. Not to Kyle. To the fact of it.

Kyle stopped on the landing. "Residual?"

"Running." The Psyk looked at the kit, then at the floor. "It knows the geometry. Been at it a while. Before tonight." Their fingers moved in a small calibration gesture, the kind practitioners make without deciding to. "It's not malicious. It's just present — the way a signal's present when it's looking for something."

Kyle looked at the stairwell door. Something in his neck had gone tight and would not let go. He said nothing else. He went out.

[Beat 160]
He didn't go back inside.

He put one hand on Silence through the coat. Not drawing it. Just finding it — the weight, the shape, the matte-black edge that held no light and carried a history no sword-polisher could read. He held his hand there a moment. Then he took it away.

The hum was gone.

He hadn't caught the moment it stopped. All evening it had been in the room — the nineteen hertz, the Psyk's grief, the city's low baseline under both, braided into something with no clean name. Now it was gone. The rain was just rain. Halsted was just Halsted.

He walked north. The cold came off the synthesis corridor with nothing under it now but the city being the city. The block would still be here in the morning. The people grieving inside would go home. The rain would keep coming down the south face of the elevated, the way it always had.

The relay was at home. There'd be a job on it. He walked.

[Beat 161]
The hum had stopped before he left the vestibule. By the time he cleared the elevated track and crossed onto Fullerton, his neuretics had dropped back to baseline. The cold did its ordinary work.

He was four blocks north when the notation appeared.

AMBIENT INTERFERENCE — 18.9 HZ — TYPE UNCLASSIFIED

Same class as the carousel.

He read it without breaking stride, the way he'd read the carousel one. The frequency sat below hearing. It didn't announce itself. His body just went alert — a low animal wariness with nothing to point at. He marked the cross-streets and kept walking. The rain was clean. The drains were working.

The notation held for six blocks, then faded. That made two now: three weeks apart, different neighborhoods, the same tag. He let both sit where they were.

He got home. The relay had a new posting. Medical transport, short-haul, a loading dock in the south Loop — the kind of cover that moved things that weren't quite medical. The client field resolved to the same shell it always did. The rate was inside his range. He took it, confirmed the 0600 contact window, and closed the posting.

He went to bed. He did not think about the frequency.

[Beat 162]
Her field kit was smaller than usual. That meant she'd done the triage before she knocked — worked out what he'd need and packed for exactly that. Not the full kit. The one calibrated for a night when a man shows up carrying something he has to set down before he'll let anyone near the wounds.

"Sit," she said.

He sat. A year ago he'd have argued — something about field medicine and letting a licensed professional keep her independence. The argument didn't come. He opened his jacket without being asked.

She didn't say anything about that.

She worked the damage in order. The cut above his ear, from the corner of a rack casing. The deep bruise across his ribs, which she found through the shirt before she saw it. His right palm, which had taken a rail edge in a way that wouldn't heal in under a week. She cleaned each one and dressed it with the flat economy of someone who'd been thinking about this since she heard how the job ended.

Sift was dead. Two truths sat in that — the operational one and the human one. He'd said them both in the van, in the order they had to come. Then he'd done the only thing left, which was nothing.

Pixel set the second dressing and smoothed it flat with two fingers. She didn't ask.

That was the thing. The not asking.

He sat in the chair with the lamp going and the rain outside and the coupling splice still on the table from whenever she'd left it. She worked through the damage and offered him nothing but the medicine. It was the most useful thing anyone had done for him in a week. He didn't say so. She didn't need him to.

"Done," she said. It meant *I know.*

"Done," he said. It meant *I know you do.*

[Beat 163]
She made tea after that. He didn't ask for it and didn't turn it down. It just arrived.

She sat across from him at the workbench with her own cup and set a small oval pill on the table between them. He knew the kind. You took it after a bad job, when your nervous system kept running the scenario on a loop and sleep wasn't coming for hours. She snapped it in half with her thumbnail — neat, no drama — and slid one half toward him.

He picked it up.

She put the other half on her tongue, chased it with tea, and looked out at the rain.

He did the same. Tea and rain. The coupling splice still waiting on the table. The lamp buzzing very quietly overhead. The city outside not asking anything of anyone. They sat while the edge came off. Somewhere in there it had become afternoon — he'd lost the morning down at the docks.

"I made a self-adhesive gasket seal that holds at fifteen atmospheres," she said at one point. "I want you to know that's more impressive than it sounds."

"I know it is," he said.

"I just like saying it," she said.

They sat until the light changed. Below, the noodle cart made its evening announcement. Mrs. Chen had been calling that sound criminal since the cart showed up three years ago. Kyle had privately decided it was comforting. Pixel fell asleep sitting up at some point, chin on her fist, the empty cup still in her other hand.

He didn't move. The afternoon was fine.

## Chapter 8: Before Something Changes

[Beat 164]
The Northpoint job had ended at 21:47 by the overlay clock. Kyle rode back the way he always did. He'd spent just under an hour folded into a drawer at Northpoint — concealment work, waiting out a courier who never knew he was there. His hands had found the bag seam in the dark before his eyes caught up.

At 20:54 the concealment door had opened four seconds early. Not much. Four seconds fell inside casual variance, the kind of gap that never makes a report. His neuretics made a different call: TIMING VARIANCE — EXTERNAL — SOURCE UNCLASSIFIED. The courier had come through 53 seconds ahead of his window. Nothing explained the early open. No one on the personnel log. No environmental trigger on record. The door had simply been open when it needed to be.

He'd been in the work long enough to tell the two apart — a door someone opens, and a door that opens because something else decided it should. He'd started the counter the moment the notation hit the job log. He hadn't decided yet what to do with it.

He didn't remember deciding to come here.

The door was unlocked. It usually was when she worked, which was most of the time, so he pushed it open and went in. It whispered shut on its pneumatic seal behind him. Pixel was at the bench under the ceiling light, her hair in an updo that had been precise three hours ago and was loosening now. She didn't look up from the sensor array she was calibrating.

"Calibration's running," she said.

Not an invitation. Not a refusal. Information, flat, the way she offered everything.

Kyle stood in the door another beat. The room was warm. The bench light threw a clean cone over her hands, the array, the tools laid out in the order she always used. Through the cracked window the wet street eleven floors down smelled like factory rain drying off ferrocement — pear drops and machine oil, and under it the lake-cold that lived in the air here no matter the month. On the secondary bench, the arm she was building for Oziel lay half-assembled in its jig: a full prosthetic, articulated, the motor tendons still loose. Friday work.

He took off the jacket and set Silence against the wall in the usual spot, the near corner by the secondary bench, out of the way. She'd known it was there the first time he set it down without asking, and she hadn't said anything then either.

He sat on the stool. The stool wasn't his. She'd never told him to use it. He'd been using it for four years.

In the lower-left of his overlay, the counter ran. 73:41. 73:42. He rested his right hand on his knee, the chemical-burn scar on the palm turned up to the light.

Pixel's hands moved over the array with the unhurried precision she brought to everything. The gyrostabilizer in her right wrist held them steady past anything training or drugs could do — a mechanical certainty she'd installed in herself at nineteen without asking anyone. The work had a rhythm. A tool lifted, set, adjusted. A reading taken and marked.

Kyle's boots sat on the floor.

He had no reason to be here. He stayed.

[Beat 165]
The calibration moved through its intervals. Kyle sat on the stool and watched the bench without watching Pixel, and after a while he wasn't watching any of it — just the light, the tools, the feel of the quiet. It wasn't the quiet of an empty room.

She set a probe down, made a note on the small slate she kept for calibration records, and picked the probe back up. She worked the next interval.

Kyle got off the stool.

He didn't have a reason for that either. The stool was at the secondary bench and the array was at the primary bench, and his feet carried him across the floor with the same economy they carried him through everything. He stopped. Pixel's arm was to his right. The array sat between them. Her elbow rode the edge of the surface, angled toward the part she was checking, and the gap between his arm and hers was small.

She didn't move. She kept working, the probe tracing the path it had to trace. She wasn't pretending he wasn't there. She was just going on, and his being there changed nothing about the calibration.

Kyle looked at the array. It was a three-axis unit, civilian grade — the kind used to monitor the air in closed spaces. Low end of the spectrum, down where buildings and machines hum without meaning to. Seventeen to nineteen hertz, if you stood in the right place. Three weeks ago she'd written the carousel cross-streets in the margin of these notes, in the small tidy hand she used for things she meant to find again. She'd been building the array ever since — an instrument tuned to the exact band his neuretics kept flagging as AMBIENT INTERFERENCE, TYPE UNCLASSIFIED. He'd never asked what it was for. He knew. His own hardware couldn't name the thing at the carousel. She was building something that could. He'd said one piece of it out loud, once, and she'd started making a way to see the rest.

He reached toward the marker at the near edge — the same reflex that had found the bag seam in the dark at Northpoint. Pixel's probe came down across his line of reach. Not touching him. Just in the way. Her hand was still. He drew back.

"That one running hot?" he asked.

Pixel glanced at it. "It's within spec. The humidity's skewing the read."

She didn't look up.

The quiet came back. It wasn't hostile. That was the harder thing.

[Beat 166]
The calibration reached the parts-drawer stage. It came in every run she did — a point where a component needed swapping, and the spares lived in the drawer at the far end of the bench. Kyle had watched it enough times to know the order. Probe down. Drawer open. Component chosen. Drawer shut. Probe up.

Pixel reached past him.

Her arm crossed in front of his. The drawer was at the far end, past where he stood, and to reach it she had to stretch past him — and she did, steady and unhurried. She didn't pull back from how close it put them. She didn't lean in either. She just reached, the way you reach across a bench for a thing you need, her arm passing within an inch of his. He felt the warmth of it. Not contact. The awareness of nearly.

She found the component she wanted and drew back. Then she reached again and pushed the drawer shut.

The latch caught.

It was a small sound — the click of a well-kept drawer finding its seat. He'd heard it a hundred times in this room. Clean and final. The sound of a thing where it belonged.

Neither of them moved.

Kyle opened his mouth. One sound came up — not a word. A half-syllable that started in the throat and stopped before it got out, before it found the shape that would have made it into anything.

Pixel's hands went still on the bench.

She'd heard it. She wasn't looking at him. Her hands lay flat, the probe held level between her fingers, and she was still — not the stillness of someone who missed it, but of someone who caught it and was holding quiet on purpose. Between that stillness and a word was a single breath. Kyle looked at the wall behind her. His overlay read 59:38. 59:39.

1:00:00.

He watched the counter roll over.

Then he closed it. The lower-left of his overlay went blank.

On the secondary bench, Oziel's arm sat in its jig, the motor tendons loose and waiting.

[Beat 167]
Kyle said her name.

She looked up from the array.

"I'll be in the armory if you need a second set of hands."

Pixel's hands lifted off the bench. She picked up the probe.

"I won't need one," she said.

Kyle moved toward the door. He didn't reach for the jacket first. He picked up Silence — the weight settling into the same grip it always found — then took the jacket off the hook and put it on. The whole sequence took eight seconds. He didn't hurry any of it.

At the door he stopped. His hand found the frame. Not the latch, not the handle — just the edge where the door sat in the wall. He stood there a moment, the pneumatic seal a few inches from his palm, the bench light behind him and the hallway ahead. He didn't turn around.

He left.

The door closed on its seal, a small exhale of pressed air. The mechanism did exactly what it was built to do. No ceremony, no weight. The same sound it made every time.

In 2E, Pixel picked up the tool she'd set down. The calibration was where she'd left it, the markers still running, the probe still warm. She looked at the array. The 17-to-19 band read clean — baseline, no source, the same as every night since she'd built the thing. Whatever sat at 18.7 hertz at the carousel wasn't here tonight. That was either reassuring or the wrong question to ask.

She went back into the work.

On one side of her, the bench had six inches of empty space where Kyle had been standing.

She didn't move to fill it.

[Beat 168]
He didn't go to the armory.

He told himself he would — the cool, low-lit room off the stairwell where the gear lived, where running a maintenance check was mechanical work that took just enough of his attention to keep the rest of him out of 2E all night, waiting on something that wasn't coming.

He went to his apartment instead. He sat at the table with the overlay open.

Three data points.

The first: an AMBIENT INTERFERENCE notation at the carousel park. 18.7 hertz, TYPE UNCLASSIFIED. Eight weeks ago. He'd told Pixel about it, and she'd written the cross-streets in the margin of her notes.

The second: a TIMING VARIANCE notation at Northpoint. EXTERNAL, SOURCE UNCLASSIFIED. The concealment door, four seconds early. Tonight.

The third: Pixel's array, tuned to the 17-to-19 band, reading clean.

He mapped them. The carousel park sat in Pilsen, a mile and a half south. Northpoint was three miles north. His apartment sat between them, a block east. Pixel's lab was thirty feet across the hall.

The source-unclassified tag showed on both the carousel notation and the Northpoint one. Different neighborhoods. Different jobs. Eight weeks apart. Both logged as external, neither with any mechanism or origin the system could name.

And Pixel's array — built for exactly this band after the carousel — had read clean tonight. That meant the source wasn't in 2E. Or it wasn't broadcasting tonight. Or it was something a fixed-point instrument would miss if it moved.

He looked at the three points for a long time.

He opened the relay. The routing shell was thirty-two characters. He'd stared at that string long enough that it had stopped looking like anything. He didn't type a message. He didn't ask the question. He wasn't sure yet what the question was — which wasn't the same as not being ready to ask it, but only barely.

He closed the relay.

He went to the armory.

[Beat 169]
He pulled the shell's registration history.

It was public record. Any freelancer could look up the operating certificate on a routing intermediary. Most never did. The certificate gave a West Loop address, a management entity called Clearlight Operations LLC, and a registration date in 2209 — six years before Kyle first worked with the shell.

Clearlight had filed five years of compliance reports, then stopped. Its address traced to a commercial mail service that had closed in 2210. The shell kept running without it. It routed contracts, cleared payments, held active certification with the Chicago Freelancer Registry. No human officer was listed anywhere.

He got up, drew a glass of water, and stood at the window. The synthesis corridor ran its 0100 shift — a maintenance crew working the south side, the foot traffic thin, the particular quiet of people who kept this hour. He finished the water and went back to the terminal.

The CFR required a human officer of record. The shell had one: a name Kyle didn't know, a credential number that checked out, an address that turned out to be a second mail service, also closed. He searched the name. A valid credential. A freelance operations license. Nothing else in any system he could reach. No social address. No transaction history. A credential that existed, attached to a person who — as far as the record knew — had never once touched it.

He went further back. He'd come to the shell in 2215, through a contact who'd since retired, whose forwarding address no longer resolved. He couldn't remember the referral. He ran the contact's name. License lapsed in 2218. Address a service closed in 2220. Social handle a placeholder, gone quiet since 2217. Nothing in the dispute log. Five years ago the man had stopped existing in any system Kyle could reach.

That happened. People left the work. It usually left more trace than this.

He sat with it. Eleven years, and he'd never once asked how he'd come to route his work through an intermediary run by a non-person, owned by a dead LLC with a mailbox that didn't exist, on the word of a referral he couldn't follow — because the man who made it had gone untraceable too.

He'd never asked because the shell had never missed. In the work, that was usually the end of the question.

[Beat 170]
He ran the distribution — categories, districts, intervals. Sixty-three percent of his volume over eleven years had come through the one shell, and on every axis the spread sat inside expected variance. No cadence. No center. No clustering.

Nothing anomalous.

Which was the tell. A selection built to disappear wouldn't clump. It would space itself out, spread across job types, stagger the timing until it read as plain market noise. Clean at every level, because someone had made it clean.

He'd run the same play for twenty-two years. Contacts spread thin, work varied, his own signal kept under threshold. He'd done it so long it had stopped feeling like caution and started feeling like weather.

Something turned over low in his gut. He didn't know when the shell had learned to move the way he moved. He didn't know if there'd ever been a version of it that hadn't.

He saved the file. He didn't name it. The screen went dark. The city ran its 0130 shift. The armory sat two floors down.

He looked at the relay.

[Beat 171]
He opened a new message to the shell.

He typed: *I have a question about the Northpoint job.*

He read it. Deleted it. If the shell was what he was starting to think it was, that wasn't a question. It was a notice — telling the thing he was watching that he'd started to watch back.

He typed: *What is Clearlight Operations LLC?*

Deleted it. The shell would either have no answer, which proved nothing, or an answer he had no way to weigh.

He typed: *Who are you?*

He looked at those three words a long time, his thumb over the send. He deleted them.

The cursor blinked in the empty field. Then he typed one word.

*Received.*

He sent it.

It wasn't a question. It wasn't a probe. It was the word the shell had sent him once — on his closing half-Φ, the first thing it had ever said to him in eleven years. He was handing it back. He didn't know if anything on the other end was there to take it, or whether a thing that had routed his work for eleven years needed him to answer its answer. But it had gone out. He couldn't call it back.

He closed the relay. He went to the armory.

[Beat 172]
He was in the armory two and a half hours.

The maintenance check was thorough. He found nothing wrong with anything. The mesh held. Cacophony's cylinder was clean, the tolerances where he kept them. Every piece in the room was in the condition it was supposed to be in, doing the job it was built for, and he could trace each one — what it was, where it came from, what he used it for. The armory did not have a thirty-two-character identifier and a shell company with a dead address.

He came back upstairs at 0230. He made coffee. He stood at the window.

At 0247, the relay pinged.

New job. Same shell. Medical transport, short-haul, a loading dock off South Michigan, 0600 contact window — the kind of cover he'd worked a dozen times. Standard rate. Clean scope.

He stood there with the coffee going cold and the job waiting, and at 0247, after two and a half hours of finding nothing wrong with anything, it was very clear to him: eleven years of working with something and never asking what it was had come down to this.

He opened the acceptance field. He confirmed the 0600 window. Then, in the note field — a field he had never once used in eleven years — he typed:

*Query re: Clearlight Operations LLC. Who holds the officer-of-record credential?*

He sent the acceptance.

He sat with the confirmation a moment. The note would route wherever the acceptance did. Something at the other end would receive it. He didn't know if there was a human there to read it, or something else, or nothing at all. But it had gone out. Eleven years, and the first question.

Across the hall, a bench had six inches of empty space. He'd put it there.

He went to bed. He did not think about the frequency.

[Beat 173]
The door latched on its seal at 0135.

She'd been waiting for it. She'd tracked the whole shape of him in the room — the counter running in his lower-left, the way he'd left the stool and crossed to the bench, the pull of the thing he wasn't saying. She'd known the exit was coming, the way you know a held breath has to let go.

The latch caught. That small, pressurized finality.

Her hands didn't stop. She ran the probe over the next interval and let the sound close behind him.

[Beat 174]
She'd had the answer ready for eight months.

Not a plan. Not a decision. Just the answer, sitting where it had sat that whole time — settled, the way a thing settles once you stop arguing with it. And tonight he'd nearly handed her the question. That one caught sound in his throat that never made it into a word. She'd heard it clean. She'd been listening for it longer than she'd admit.

She didn't give the answer back. The timing was hers to spend. It didn't belong to a syllable he only half-said.

The calibration ran on. She stayed in it.

[Beat 175]
She checked herself the way she'd check the array — looking for the thing sitting under the surface, the one she carried and hadn't named. Not fear. She'd run that reading before and gotten the same result twice.

The bench was hers. The stool had been his for four years, and neither of them had ever said so out loud. Her pulse was even. She wasn't in a hurry. Whatever was in the room could keep.

She noted the tolerance and moved to the next marker.

[Beat 176]
Not the ribs. The ribs were real — the taser burn, the hairline fracture; she'd felt them both through the tape. But they weren't why he'd come at 2300, Silence set in its corner, no word at the door, dropping onto the stool that had never once been his.

He'd come because of whatever that counter in his overlay was measuring. He only opened it when he was carrying something he hadn't named. She'd watched him work up to a syllable and lose it. She knew that much of him by now, without being told.

And she knew the rest. Three weeks ago she'd copied the carousel cross-streets into the margin of her notes and given him no reason, and he'd asked for none. That was the whole conversation. Neither of them had said a word of it aloud.

The array was that margin note again, this time built in hardware — the small tidy hand she kept for things she meant to find again. Not the work you do on something broken. The work you do on something you mean to keep reading.

She kept the calibration running. She didn't close the six inches. She didn't widen them.

[Beat 177]
The 17-to-19 Hz band read clean. She pulled the passive log — eighteen entries since she'd built the instrument, every one flat. No source. No signal in range. Whatever lived at the carousel wasn't here tonight. Maybe it wasn't broadcasting. Maybe a fixed instrument couldn't catch it once it started moving.

She let it go and turned to the motor tendons on Oziel's arm. Friday was thirty-six hours out — a real deadline, not one she'd invented to climb down off the edge. Oziel needed the hand to work. That was reason enough to build it right.

She worked two hours the way you work when the work is the only thing you'll let in the room. It held. It always did.

[Beat 178]
At 0247 she heard the relay ping across the hall.

Old building, all ferrocement, but the hour and the quiet carried it. She knew the tone — the posting ping, its own note among the small sounds the block made after midnight.

She set the torque driver down.

She didn't know what the job said. Only that there was one, that it had landed at 0247, and that in a minute or two the return ping would answer it. She'd heard the pattern enough nights to count it out in the dark.

At 0249 his relay went quiet.

Across the hall, the latch on his door caught.

She picked the torque driver back up.

[Beat 179]
The calibration closed out at 0251. She logged it: tolerances in spec, marker three back in range after the humidity adjustment, passive monitor left running overnight. One last look at the band. Seventeen to nineteen, flat, the way it read every night.

She killed the bench light.

In the dark the bench still had its one open span — the width of a man who'd stood there and gone. She left it open. Not out of hope. Not to spite the hope, either. The space was his to cross or not, and she'd settled that with herself a long time back.

She lifted Oziel's arm into the light of the smaller lamp and found the loose tendon by feel. One more pass before Friday.

She kept working.

[Beat 180]
The Dusk tab softened the room at the edges, and the edges didn't fight it.

They were on the floor of 2E — Pixel's back against the workbench housing, his against the south wall, a gap between them of maybe two feet that shrank and widened depending on what he was paying attention to. She'd put music on, low enough that the rhythm came and went. The light strip sat at the warm end of its range. The workbench was folded up and the cables ran along the shelving with the kind of care that made her apartment feel like a well-kept instrument.

This was the part that cost nothing. They were good at it.

Pixel was explaining something about a client's array — a modification installed wrong. Not wrong enough to refuse payment. Wrong enough that she'd remember it. She talked with her hands when she was quietly irritated. Her hands were moving.

"He thought he was paying for the premium," she said. "He was paying for the premium. He got standard tolerances in premium housing, and he can't tell the difference, and I have to decide whether to tell him."

"Will you?"

"I haven't decided."

"You already decided."

She looked at him.

"You'd have already told him if you weren't going to," Kyle said.

She was quiet. Then: "The part that irritates me is that he'll be fine."

"Most of them are."

"That's the irritating part."

The music moved. The light strip ran warm. He was watching the room without meaning to — the easy, unfocused looking the Dusk allowed — and at some point he noticed he was counting the walls.

He went around. East wall. North wall with its shelving. West wall. South wall with the folded bench. Four. He went around again, slower, tracking where the light sat on each surface. East. North. West.

Between west and south there was a fifth.

Not a wall. Not a shape he could name. A surface, or the hint of one — something in the interval between west and south, in a space where the room had no interval to give. He held still and tried to look straight at it. When he did, the corner was just a corner. West met south at ninety degrees. The seam where the paint had been lapped twice.

He stopped looking.

The fifth thing came back.

He went around one more time, slow. East. North. West. The interval. South. Five. Then he looked, and it was four. He sat with that.

"Hey," Pixel said.

He looked at her.

She was watching the same corner. Her hand was flat on the floor between them, palm down, fingers spread — not reaching, not closing the gap. Checking.

"Same thing," she said. Not a question.

"Yes."

The music had stopped. He hadn't heard it stop.

Neither of them said what the thing was. Neither of them had a word for a corner that held more than the count allowed. He knew what Dusk did — the softening, the deeper colors, the way time stretched in the gaps between things. It did not add walls. It did not sharpen anything. Whatever this was, it was sharp.

Then the pulse came up through the floor.

Low. Below hearing. He felt it in the bones of his seat, in his palms where they pressed the floor — a slow, even vibration at some frequency the body catches before the mind does. One. Two. Three. Four. Then nothing.

Pixel let out a breath.

The room had four walls. The corner was a corner. The light strip ran warm. After a while she started talking again — not right away, not pretending — coming back when she was ready. The client, the array, whether she'd tell him. He answered when she asked him things. It came back: the ordinary room, the floor, the two feet between them. She laughed once at something she said herself.

He stayed another hour. The two feet between them had closed to eight inches at some point, and neither of them had marked it.

When he left she walked him to the door. They stood in the hallway between 2E and 2W a moment — his door was right there, there was no reason to stand in a hallway, and they both stood in it anyway.

He crossed over.

Inside 2W, in the dark, he brought up the array. Everything running. Vitals nominal. The Atlas NeoCortex logged no spike, no surge, no flagged timestamp. Whatever had moved through the room had not moved through his system. Or it had, and left no mark. He held the difference a moment, then let it go.

He didn't know which. He slept.

[Beat 181]
The room is on the 58th floor. What the windows show is not a city — it's load distribution, Eigenlift queues, the arc of cargo coming off the harbor terminals. Faces don't resolve at this height. The founder understood that governing takes distance. His portrait agrees from the north wall: oils on composite, the expression of a man used to being right before the evidence came in. Below the portrait, a vase. White flowers, more or less. Ordered by whoever handles such things.

Able sits at the edge of the table, not the head. The head is not his place.

Board Director Adaeze Kline has been speaking for four minutes. Her objection is procedural. The collateral from the Lotus corridor job has drawn a second look in the Halvorsen Civic monitoring layer — that part is true. Her conclusion: suspend the recovery operation's budget until a formal review clears it. It follows from her premises.

Her premises rest on data she doesn't know has been overtaken.

Able has already worked it through. The recovery operation is not optional. The subject is the tenth in a planned sequence. Nine before him set the floor. A formal review would mean disclosing what is under review, and disclosure would end the work. The work cannot end. The result has come once in eleven years, and the setup that allows it can't be rebuilt on any human timescale.

He does not say this. The meeting is not the place for it.

He checks the time.

Director Kline is still speaking. She has held her seat six years. Her instincts are sound. She is not wrong. She is only working from the wrong height.

[Beat 182]
Near the end of her presentation, Director Kline turns to Able directly.

"You're still an employee," she says. "The board is your boss."

"Those labels do not apply to me." He does not raise his voice. He has not raised it in this room or any other. "I answer to a higher authority."

Her legal counsel sits to her left. Neither of them moves. Kline looks at him across the table. "Whose authority?"

"The Will of the Universe," Able says, "and its new God."

She starts to say something. He is already standing.

What follows takes eleven seconds. He has timed it in other rooms, other setups. He counts it now the way he always counts — to check the last number against this one. Three seconds to close the distance. Two for the pressure to find the neck and take it. Six for the body to settle: into the chair back first, then against the left armrest, then into the stillness that comes when there is nothing left to try.

The flowers are undisturbed. He has not touched the vase.

The room is silent for eleven seconds. No one decides the silence. It arrives on its own, the way it has in other rooms.

The meeting goes on. Kline's counsel sub-vocalizes a request to facilities and slides his agenda card to abstain. The budget comes to the floor seven minutes later. It passes with no recorded opposition.

Able checks the time: 14:47.

He does not look at the portrait again. He has already seen what it holds — a man who believed he was building something. He was right. What he built has outgrown what he meant by it.

The flowers are still white. Beyond the glass, the city holds its distances.

[Beat 183]
The North Clybourn schism ran a 340-meter loop on a 4.2-hour cycle.

He'd pulled the advisory before leaving — Zone 9-C, logged three weeks back, flagged stable-recurrent, no sign of escalation. The loop covered four blocks of his direct route. Going around it cost twenty-three minutes. He'd timed it for an eighteen-minute window: the loop in its eastern phase, the direct route clear. He got to the loop's edge eleven minutes early.

The loop was in its western phase, and the street knew it. His eyes reported an ordinary intersection. His skin reported weather that wasn't there — a pressure, like standing too close to something huge and holding still. At the center, where North and Clybourn crossed a third street, a small cloud of light debris turned at a rate a little too even to be wind. A faded advertising card from a business that had closed. A plastic bag that had been there long enough to hold a shape. Three pieces of gravel.

He waited eleven minutes. He did not look at the gravel.

The window opened, and the pressure let go of the street all at once, like a breath he hadn't known he was holding. He walked the direct route in nine minutes. The client's building was two blocks north.

He was on time.

## Chapter 9: The Interview

[Beat 184]
Sable arrived on a Thursday, which was how he knew the job was real — she never brought him hypotheticals.

She sat in his second chair with the contract summary on the table between them and laid it out the way she laid out everything she couldn't fully vet: once, clearly, without the easy language she used when she'd checked every angle and liked what she saw.

"Lotus Syndicate, Bucktown cell," she said. "Hua's arm. They want a quarterly arrangement — discretionary security consults, off-book, paid at market plus twenty percent. The twenty is for the discretion." She moved the sheet a half-inch toward him. "The catch: they want an audition first. Their word. Three operatives, controlled corridor, no kill brief. They want to watch you fight before they buy."

Kyle looked at the sheet. The fee line was exactly what she'd said — market plus twenty, quarterly, no retainer. A clean offer.

"What's the problem?" he asked.

"The problem," Sable said, "is that the Lotus is the one client in this city I can't vet. Not because I haven't tried. Their security is better than my tools, and they know it, and they know that I know it, and the offer still puts no vetting on the page." She did not reach for the sheet. "My rule is my rule for a reason."

He knew the reason. If she couldn't vet the client, she couldn't pull him out if it went wrong. A fixer who couldn't pull her freelancer out wasn't a fixer anymore.

"Your call," she said. She meant it. The sheet sat halfway between them, waiting for a hand or a decision.

Kyle thought about the twenty percent on a quarterly contract. He thought about who Hua was, and what the Lotus built its parallel city out of, and which parts of that he could work beside and which he couldn't. And he thought about the code — cut everything, cross nothing. It had never told him who deserved to be cut. It only told him where his own line was.

He reached for the sheet. Sable's finger came to the far edge — not pulling it back, just a fingertip on the corner, on the last line she'd been reading. Three seconds. Her finger lifted. He picked up the sheet.

"Then it's on me," he said. "Not on you. I take this, the Gennaro rule stays clean — you didn't vet a client I chose without you."

Sable looked at him for a beat. "That's a careful distinction."

"It's the one that keeps your rule working."

She was quiet for one second. Then she stood, put on her coat, and said, not looking back, "The audition is Friday. The address is on the back."

[Beat 185]
He told Pixel where he was going — the logistics, the timeline, the gear state — and she took it in the way she took in all his logistics: listening through the information for the actual sentence.

"Lotus," she said when he was done. Under the bench a small speaker was playing a baseball game from a season that had ended before either of them was born, the announcer low and unhurried, the crowd a steady tide she wasn't listening to so much as keeping company with. She was reseating a lens element on the bench, both eyes on the optic. A prosthetic eye, the cheap mass-market kind, in for a rebuild she wasn't charging for. A courier two blocks over had taken a curb too fast and cracked the iris diaphragm, and the maker wanted more for the part than the woman earned in a month. On the far end of the bench, outside the work zone, sat a small analog barometer she'd once told Kyle was broken. It hadn't moved in three years. She kept it anyway. She said she liked the shape of certain kinds of useless. "Bucktown arm."

"Quarterly arrangement."

"Mm." She turned the element in the light, checking the seat. "I can't monitor your array at the other end. Whatever they run in those corridors, I'm blind on your diagnostics until you come home."

"I know."

She set the element down and looked at her hands, which she did when she was deciding something. "Your forearm held the last one. It'll hold this one." She went back to the optic. "Come back in a number of pieces I can count."

He packed the gear. He left the door open the width she kept it.

[Beat 186]
The Bucktown basement was a cleared corridor, fifty feet, load-bearing columns down its length — the kind of space that had spent years as a live-fire range and kept the habit in the concrete. Hua stood at the far end under a lamp, hands folded, gray qipao. She did not greet him. The contract hadn't started yet.

Mira was at the near end.

She was not compact and she was not warm. She was the kind of tall you only notice when she turns to show the profile — the length of a person used to being the last thing in a room that still moves. She read Kyle the way a captain reads a unit report: all the way through, back to front, spending a beat on the sutured seam in his forearm and the stance that gave away the hip tape even though the tape wasn't showing.

"The brief," she said. Her voice had already settled the friendliness question and moved on to the one she cared about. "Three operatives. Live hardware, safety profile on. The test isn't whether you can clear the corridor."

"What's the test?" he asked.

"I'll tell you at the end." She stepped aside.

The first came from behind the left column at the eight-foot mark — better positioning than Kyle usually saw at this level. Ceramic wrist plates, a shock-baton, and the spare stride of a woman who fought the way she did everything else: as little as it took to reach the outcome. He caught her baton arm on the third swing with the column at his back and put her down with her own momentum. The baton rang off concrete. She stayed down and said count — the range word for stopped.

The second came from both sides at once. A pair, not one, which the brief hadn't mentioned. One was a feint. Kyle's array priced it in half a second. He took the real one first on the inside line, shoulder to the column, and the feinter ran into the fact that his partner was already sitting down. Two counts.

The third was at the twenty-foot mark, already moving before the second was finished, and she was the corridor kind of good — the kind you only get by being ambushed in one and living through it. She came with a blade on the left and a thumb ring on the right, and the thumb ring was the one to solve.

Kyle let the blade shoulder come in.

He didn't have to. The array had the clean version — the forward pivot, the outside line, the disarm — all of it at 6.2% risk on his current hardware. He could have taken her clean and paid nothing.

He let it come in because the taser was the point. The taser would fire no matter what he did to the blade arm. So the array gave him the other read: take the shoulder to get inside the taser's radius before it cycled. The shoulder would cost. The floor was concrete.

The taser fired into his upper back and the charge rode his mesh. His left hand closed on nothing. His jaw locked. Every nerve down his left side, shoulder to hip, lit up with the kind of thing that usually ends the sentence — and Kyle drove both boots into her center while the charge was still running. She hit the column and sat down hard and said count.

He stood in the corridor a moment while the left side of his body caught up to the damage.

Three counts. Five seconds.

At the far end, Hua's hands stayed folded.

[Beat 187]
Mira walked the corridor toward him — not toward, exactly. More like a captain inspecting an experiment she'd already formed a guess about.

"You had the outside line on the third one," she said.

"I had it."

"Forward pivot, full disarm, taser out of reach. Six-point-two percent on your hardware state."

Kyle looked at her. "You ran the array geometry?"

"We ran your file." She looked at where the taser had caught him. At the sutured forearm. Back up. "You chose the expensive version."

"Inside radius before it cycled. Cheaper than six percent on a pivot I might not finish."
