"No," she said. "You could have finished it. I watched you price the clean version for a third of a second before you moved. You took the one where you absorb it instead." She studied him. "She's got a three-day-old mesh repair on her left knee. Stairs, not a fight. Your array had the read."

"I had the read," he said.

"The code," Mira said. "Cut everything, cross nothing. The cross-nothing part." She wasn't asking.

"That's the code."

She looked at his back where the taser had fired, the way Pixel looked at a diagnostic she already knew the answer to. "Cost you a rib."

"Probably two."

"You want Lotus medical."

"I want Pixel."

She made no comment on it. It was a preference she understood. "The arrangement," she said. "Hua will want it quarterly. I want first call on the discretionary consults. Standard rate plus the twenty for both." She looked at him with the flat attention of someone who had stopped measuring him and started setting terms. "I don't need your fixer's vetting channel. I'll handle my own."

"She'll notice that."

"She will." No apology in it, just the fact. "What I need from a Lotus-adjacent freelancer is someone the Lotus doesn't own. Your fixer keeps you that way. I'm not asking you to cut the channel — I'm asking you to keep it separate from mine." A pause. "Those are different asks."

Kyle turned them over.

"Friday," he said. "Standard rate. We start there."

Mira nodded once, the way she probably nodded at everything that landed where she expected. "There's a man in the Narrows I want you to see before you leave. He'll want to look at the blade." She looked at Silence at his hip — not with the combat read she'd given it in the corridor, but with something quieter. "Let him."

[Beat 188]
The togishi of the Narrows worked out of a two-room shop three blocks from the cherry trees, down a flight of stairs polished smooth by forty years of foot traffic and the weight of blades going in and coming out. The sign on the door was a single character, brushed by hand. It said nothing useful to anyone who hadn't already been sent.

He received Silence across both palms, blunt spine toward Kyle, the way a bow receives a blade — not surrender, courtesy. An acknowledgment between people who understand that the steel is the point.

He did not speak for three minutes. Kyle stood in the outer room and waited, the ribs keeping up their steady complaint.

The togishi returned Silence the way he had taken it and stood at the worktable with his hands at his sides.

"How long have you carried it?"

"Eleven years."

"Before that."

"I don't remember."

The togishi looked at him. Not at the blade. "You don't remember."

"I was given it. I don't remember the giving."

The old man turned back to the worktable. "The work is skilled. Not excellent — skilled." He laid two fingers flat on the blade, near the hamon, the way a doctor reads a pulse. "I cannot tell you what it's made of. This is none of the alloys I know, and not a blend of any of them. Whatever is in this edge, I don't know the door you'd walk through to buy it." He ran his thumb along the saya. "There's no school in it either. The finishing cuts are the maker's own — the hand made a decision at the end that isn't in any lineage I know."

"Is that a problem?"

"It is an observation." He looked at Kyle. "I have held perhaps eight thousand blades in my life. Every one of them has a history I can read some part of. This one remembers nothing I can read. The steel is good. The work is honest. The edge is precise." He set his palm flat on the saya. "It is the loneliest piece of steel I have held."

Kyle's thumb had moved to the top of the tsuka, up against the guard, without him deciding to move it.

Kyle said nothing.

"You came because someone sent you," the togishi said. "Not because you wanted the answer."

"That's right."

"Come back when you want the answer." He bowed. "The blade is well. I have no repair for you."

Kyle bowed. He left the answer in the room.

[Beat 189]
Mira was waiting on the block above the stairs, leaning against the cherry-blossom gate — not impatient, not comfortable, just letting the minutes go.

"Well?" she asked.

"Skilled, not excellent. No lineage."

She made no comment on what else the togishi had said. She'd sent him. She already knew what he'd say.

"A thing came to my desk last week," she said, in the tone she used for things she'd decided were his to know. "The desk that bought your file from Vey — the one through Halvorsen Civic. The Continuity Office requisition."

"I know about that one."

"Then you know there's more than one buyer now. Plural subscribers in the last four weeks, neither through Halvorsen. One is architecture I don't recognize — patient, months old, routes clean. One is four weeks old and loud enough that I caught it from here." She looked at the cherry trees, past their bloom now and just trees again. "Somebody with more patience than budget found you at the same time as somebody with more budget than patience. They're not working together. Which means two different people decided you were worth watching in the same month." A pause. "Ask the Antiquarian who else subscribes."

"He won't sell me my own file."

"Ask him anyway. There may be a thing he'll tell you that he hasn't decided to sell." She buttoned the third button on her coat, which was her tell for a clean exit, and stepped off the gate. "Friday. Standard rate. We'll start there."

"Friday," Kyle said.

She left the way she'd found him — without ceremony, without looking back, the way she treated everything she'd already decided.

[Beat 190]
He rode home through the Narrows. The taser burn, the togishi's word, two new names on his file — none of it would sit still at Bucktown volume.

The Narrows was the strip of low blocks where the freight viaduct cut the district in half and rents dropped to whatever the noise didn't drive off. At this hour a woman was hosing fish blood off a loading dock into a drain that had stopped draining years ago. The blood spread across the concrete and pooled at the curb, and nobody looked twice, because nobody looked twice at anything here after midnight. That was the whole arrangement. He rode through with his lights low, and the Narrows let him pass the way it let everyone pass who didn't need it to be anything else.

The burn reported up the left side on every seam in the pavement, shoulder to hip. A cost already paid. He breathed around it and kept the throttle even.

The togishi's word wouldn't leave him. Loneliest. Eight thousand blades through those hands, forty years of knowing what steel remembered, and that was the one he'd reached for — that, and the open door behind it. Come back when you want the answer. Kyle took the corner at Madison a fraction wider than the surface needed.

The city came back around him — the overnight freight, the gray-zone markets cracking their first windows, the particular thickness of a block that had been cooking its own air for thirty years. Mrs. Chen's counter would be dark by now. Across the hall, a light would be on.

He thought about asking Pixel to chase the harmonic again. Not the answer — he wasn't built for the answer yet. Just whether she could tell him what it was, without telling him where it came from.

He rode the last blocks home.

[Beat 191]
He was three blocks from The Pivot when the relay pinged.

Not a job. Eleven years had taught him the exact weight of a job notification — the way the routing shell built its subject line, the brevity that meant information and not conversation, the timestamp that located the work in operational time. This was not that. The sender ID was the routing shell — the same 32-character string that had routed his contracts since 2215 — but the message was not formatted as a contract.

It was one line.

*CORRIDOR AUDIT COMPLETE. THE CHOICE AT 6.2% IS LOGGED.*

Kyle pulled over. The bike idled under him in the rain while he read it a second time.

He sat with the engine running, the rain coming off the elevated at its angle, the relay still open in his overlay. He did not send a response. He did not know what to send back.

He closed the relay. He rode home.

[Beat 192]
Sable's job posting had said forty percent above standard hazard, which was the North Clybourn corridor introducing itself without apology.

Kyle had accepted. He had not eaten. Pixel had been leaning out of 2E when he hit the stairs — red bean rice in a container, the smell of it reaching him before she did — and she'd said eat before, not after, not during, before, and he'd said he would, which was an administrative truth of the kind where the speaker knows it's false and the listener knows the speaker knows, and everyone processes it as operational shorthand for not tonight. Pixel had filed this under Known Kyle Behaviors and watched him go.

The courier's name was Pelé Fassbinder-Nkono: twenty-three, courier-guild licensed, carrying the package in a locked chest harness that clicked with every breath — which was to say, every time Kyle needed to concentrate. That was a feature of the corridor's first fifty meters. For the next eight and a half blocks it became entirely academic.

"Can you stop that," Kyle said, not looking at him.

"The breathing?"

"The clicking."

Fassbinder-Nkono adjusted the strap. It clicked. "No," he said.

Nine blocks. 01:40 in the morning.

[Beat 193]
The North Clybourn corridor ran nine blocks in the dark, from a courier relay point in the south to a licensed goods depot in the north. The Lotus Syndicate owned it. Not by force — by paperwork. A zoning fight, years old: contested, appealed, appealed again, handed to an adjudicator who got replaced twice before the ruling cleared. By then Lotus had built the service infrastructure and the dispute was just words. The job paid forty percent, and the forty percent was the corridor itself: the old route arguing with the new management, in the dark.

Lotus didn't run the stretch with its own people. It leased it to independent contractors who kept what they took and paid the syndicate a fee for the right to work it. That made them hungry. It made them careful about risk, and hard to read.

Fourteen of them, spread across nine blocks. A reasonable number for the dark.

The first four came at the second intersection. They moved in two pairs — the front pair to close the distance, the rear pair to cut off the way out. Good tactics. Someone had thought about this. Kyle drew Silence on his first step. The front two were professionals. The rear two hung back, watching more than fighting; they were there to report.

He went left, past the front pair's committed line, and cut.

Not the artery. The seam above the knee, where the big muscle of the thigh anchors. Cut there and the leg quits before the man knows why. He was down before he'd decided anything.

The second man pivoted to cover the gap. That was correct. Kyle's elbow caught him at the hinge of the jaw — nothing personal, just where the angle put it. Something cracked. Not Kyle's.

The rear two turned north and didn't come back. That told him what he needed: the relay point was ahead, not behind, and these four had been the announcement, not the event.

He moved north.

[Beat 194]
Three blocks in, Fassbinder-Nkono said, "There's a light ahead."

"That's not a light," Kyle said. "That's a door, wedged open." He'd been watching it since the second intersection. The shape of light around a door tells you what's behind it — the floor plan, where the glow comes from, the angle you'll walk into. "Stay back," he said. "Stay moving. Don't stop for anything."

"Moving where?"

"North. Always north. Keep the wall on your left."

The courier took that in and kept going. His harness clicked.

Seven waited behind the door, with the doorframe itself as the choke point. Seven was a harder problem than four. By the time he reached them they'd be set, and once they were set the opening was already gone.

So he came through the doorway wrong — angled, not straight. It scrambled them. He was inside two of them before the rest had finished adjusting. Silence worked in short strokes, not the long sweeps of a demonstration. At this range the blade was less a blade than a lever.

The gash came from a kitchen knife. Not a real weapon — electrical tape on the handle. A contractor on a tight budget, which meant faster and more willing than the trained pair at the intersection, because a man scraping by on a percentage wasn't guarding ground, he was taking it. His angle was wrong. Kyle's left forearm came up to block, and the knife caught the forearm instead — the right trade — and opened it in a line three inches long. His body noted the cut and downgraded it to a nuisance.

Not the artery. He knew where the artery was.

The kitchen-knife man went down on a sweep into the wall, shoulder first, not the face. The face wasn't necessary. The last five turned on Kyle too slowly to matter. Two of them thought better of it and ran; he let them run, because chasing would have cost him more than they were worth. The other three went down in order. He didn't dwell on it. Dwelling was for later, in the light, with something to eat.

By the end of the block the corridor was quiet.

The forearm was a nuisance. He tore a strip of jacket lining and pressed it to the wound — the right tool for three inches that weren't the artery — and kept moving north.

[Beat 195]
The last three stood at the mouth of the north exit. Exit control — professional placement. His rib had an opinion about it before he reached them.

The rib wasn't their doing. It was the doorframe on West Huron, forty minutes and eight blocks back — a glancing hit at a bad angle he'd taken for minor at the time. On an empty stomach it had grown into something louder. It had been complaining for four blocks. By the north exit it was done complaining and had started to hurt in earnest.

The three watched him come out of the last shadow with Silence in his hand and a left arm that looked controlled, not hurt, if you didn't know how to read it. Two of them made good decisions inside three seconds. The third had something to prove. That wasn't Kyle's problem to solve.

He stepped inside the man's reach, put a palm to the solar plexus, exactly as hard as it needed to be. The man sat down on the north pavement with the look of someone who had decided North Clybourn had given him everything it was going to.

Fassbinder-Nkono clicked past.

"That's it?" he asked.

"That's it," Kyle said.

The courier looked back at the corridor, as if checking it hadn't followed. Then he looked at Kyle's arm. Kyle already had pressure on it with the jacket lining. "You need—" the courier started.

"You have a delivery," Kyle said. "Focus on that."

The courier focused on that. His harness clicked north into the dark, carrying whatever it carried. Kyle held the pressure on his forearm and thought about red bean rice going cold in a container on Pixel's counter, and Pixel telling him to eat before, not after — before. The rib would be with him a while. He started toward the relay point. The forty percent didn't clear until the delivery was confirmed.

[Beat 196]
Two Lotus operatives were waiting at the corner of Narrows and West Superior, two blocks past the corridor's exit — which meant they'd been in place before he ever entered it. Both women, late twenties, in clean corporate casual: the clothes of people who weren't expecting trouble, because trouble had stopped being a question for them a while ago. One held up a hand, palm out, the gesture exact.

"Mr. Corbin," she said. "If you have a moment."

02:09. The forearm was managed. The rib was still making its case. The relay confirmation had cleared eleven minutes ago, so the forty percent was settled and every minute from here was off the clock.

"A moment," Kyle said.

They walked him east on Narrows, away from the Pivot. He walked beside them, not between them — they'd have preferred between; he wouldn't — and said nothing about the direction. The street was quiet enough to hear the vents breathe. On the east side of the block, three cherry trees stood in low matte-black planters. They were in bloom: pale-white clusters holding steady in the warm exhaust of a vent along the building's base.

It was July. Cherry trees don't bloom in July. Lotus kept these alive in artificial heat anyway, in the exact month that made the effort impossible to miss.

Kyle found it mildly impressive. He didn't say so.

[Beat 197]
The restaurant was called Narrows Ramen. The name did double duty: it was the cross-street, and it was the room — twelve seats, a counter the full length of the wall, a service window the kitchen spoke through in steam and dashi and char. Under all of it was something older: old wood, maybe, or just the warmth a room holds after cooking for years. It was 02:11. The kitchen was still running.

Kyle sat at the counter. His left arm was wrapped in jacket lining and holding.

The man at the far end of the counter stood. Mid-fifties, gray at the temples, still in the particular way that military service builds and most people only imitate. He bowed — not deep, not dismissive. Exact.

"Mr. Corbin," he said. "I apologize for the timing."

"Mr. Hua," Kyle said.

Hua gestured to the seat Kyle was already in. "I'd like a few minutes of your time. The assessment will be brief." He paused. "I won't conduct it myself."

He stepped back. He sent Mira instead.

[Beat 198]
The woman who came out of the back at 02:14 was Mira — the same evaluator from the Bucktown corridor, three days on, moving the way she had then: the way people move when they've trained so long the training disappears, not smooth for show, just nothing wasted. She set a cup of tea in front of Kyle and another in front of the empty seat beside him. Then she sat.

"Second time," she said. "Same work. I run the evaluations."

Kyle looked at the tea. "What kind?"

"Green. Mild." She didn't look at his forearm. "You're running on nothing. You didn't eat before the corridor."

"How do you know that?"

"Because you took a forearm gash in the second contact and a rib hit at the doorframe on West Huron, and both are below what your file says you'd normally allow. Your reaction time was off by about what an empty stomach costs." She set a clipboard on the counter — paper, not a screen, a pen clipped to it. "Drink the tea."

Kyle drank the tea.

"The assessment has six positions," Mira said. She wrote something at the top of the clipboard. "You'll know when we move from one to the next. The last position is a direct physical test. If you want to stop at any point, say so, and you'll be walked to the door."

Kyle looked at the service window, where the steam turned in the light. "What does 'direct physical test' mean?"

"It means what it means."

"That's not a definition."

"No," Mira agreed, and wrote something else.

[Beat 199]
The first position was a question about a job he'd run four years ago — an exact address, an exact date, a name he'd been told to bury when the contract closed. Someone at Lotus had dug it out of a secondary file. That was fine. He knew they could find files.

"I don't have information on that," he said.

"You mean you won't give it to me."

"I mean I don't have it. For this conversation, the difference doesn't matter."

Mira wrote something. "First position."

The second position: she told him to leave Silence on the counter.

He looked at the clipboard. "No," he said.

She wrote. "Second position."

The third position walked in on its own two feet — the older operative who had brought him here, now at the far end of the counter with a glass of water and an attention meant to read as presence, not threat. Kyle kept her at the edge of his eye. Weapon holstered, posture loose, nothing set up to move. He turned back to Mira.

"Third position," Mira said, without looking up.

The fourth position was Mira standing. When she stood, there was a weapon on her hip that hadn't been there when she sat — a Sable compact, mid-weight, standard load. She didn't draw it. She let it show and watched him.

Kyle did not draw Cacophony.

He knew he had six in it. He didn't have to reach for it to know. He looked back at Mira, who was reading him the way you read a page for mistakes.

He did not draw Cacophony.

"Fourth position," Mira said, and sat back down.

[Beat 200]
The fifth position was Mira coming off the stool fast. Trained fast, not startled fast — which meant it telegraphed a hair, and Kyle was ready before her feet had moved. She came in from his left, measuring how he'd deflect. He stepped right, set his hand on her wrist at the joint, and guided the rest of her motion along the arc it was already taking, until she finished it standing beside him with her arm in a lock. Not a dangerous lock. A precise one.

She was breathing normally.

"Fifth position," Kyle said.

She worked her arm free. She wrote something.

"Last one," she said.

She stepped back a full meter. She drew the Sable compact, aimed at his sternum, and fired once.

The sound was what a gunshot is in a small room with hard walls and nothing soft to swallow it — sharp, immediate, boxed in. It hit his chest like a heavy weight dropped from a height, which was close to what it was. The mesh caught it. AxisMed distributed kinetic armor, Φ8,000 in 2222, installed by a specialist who'd taken three hours over it and said, don't get shot in the same place twice — advice Kyle had built a career around respecting. The mesh spread the force out across a patch the size of his left hand, so what should have been a hole was a deep bruise instead.

He did not step back. He breathed once.

Mira set the Sable on the counter.

She looked at him the way you check a sample against the paperwork. Not pleased, not sorry. Exact.

"Your mesh is AxisMed third-generation distributed," she said. "The spec rates it at forty joules. That was forty-two." She looked at the clipboard. "The file said you wouldn't change your approach as the pressure went up. The file said you wouldn't draw Cacophony in the fourth position." She paused. "The file was right on both counts."

Kyle looked at the Sable, then at Mira. "There's a file on me."

"There are several files on you," she said. "The question is who's keeping them." She picked up her tea, still warm. "That's not a question I'll answer tonight." She capped the pen and set the clipboard face-down. "The assessment is complete."

[Beat 201]
The togishi came out after Mira went back — the sword polisher from the Narrows, the same old man who had held Silence on the stairs three days ago and called it the loneliest steel he'd handled, and meant it.

He was seventy, maybe more, with the hands of a man who'd done fine work all his life: slow, nothing wasted. He set a cloth on the counter beside Kyle — soft cotton, folded a particular way — and looked at Silence in its carry strap without asking. Kyle unclipped the sword and laid it on the cloth.

The togishi didn't touch it right away. He stood with his hands at his sides and looked at the blade the way you look at a thing before you understand it. Then he picked it up.

The examination took eleven minutes.

He checked the balance at four points along the length. He ran his thumb down the spine — the spine, never the edge, reading the grain through his skin — in a slow, habitual motion that stopped twice, where the steel told him something worth hearing. He held the blade up to the light of the service window and read the grain, a language Kyle couldn't read, and it drew three silences out of him, each a different length. He checked for a maker's mark in the old places on the tang, which meant taking the handle apart — a small wooden mallet, one practiced knock at the base, the knock of a man who'd done it ten thousand times. He studied the tang. He put the handle back together.

He set Silence down on the cloth. The kitchen was quiet. Steam drifted through the service window and settled.

"No maker's mark," the togishi said. He was looking at the blade, not at Kyle. "No history I can find. The metal is consistent with the 2210s — folded in the traditional method. That's either the work of a skilled artisan or a machine precise enough that I can't tell the two apart." He paused. "The balance is the work of someone who knew exactly what they were making." He paused again. The kitchen let out a breath of steam and went quiet. "In forty years with swords, I've handled maybe three others like it. Every one was made by someone who didn't want to be found." He was still looking at the blade. "Still the loneliest piece of steel I've ever held."

He folded the cloth over the blade and set it beside Kyle's hand.

He looked up.

Kyle looked at the cloth. At Silence on the cloth. He didn't answer. He looked at the cloth, then at the service window, then at the cloth again.

The togishi bowed — not deep, not dismissive — and went back wherever he'd come from. The room was quiet.

[Beat 202]
Hua came back after the togishi left. He sat two seats away — a polite distance, the kind that means conversation, not negotiation.

"The assessment was satisfactory," Hua said.

"I got shot in the chest."

"The assessment was satisfactory," Hua said again, at the exact same weight, which made it a confirmation and not a repeat.

Kyle looked at the service window. At the ramen he hadn't ordered yet. He thought about the forty percent, the corridor, the forearm, the rib, eleven minutes on a cloth with a sword nobody could trace. He knew what he wanted out of the night, and it had a specific shape.

"Here's what I need," he said.

Hua waited.

"Hardware for my crew goes on your account. List price, straight from the vendor. No markup, no tracking the orders." Kyle set his hands on the counter. "I'm not a Lotus affiliate. I'm a freelance operator who passes through your territory clean and pays the standard rate — not tribute. That distinction matters."

"What counts as your crew."

"Six, right now." He thought of Pixel's kitchen table, her soldering iron, her exact rates, the slow way she laid biotape from the wound outward — not something the medicine required, and not something it didn't. He thought of Boost and the logbook and five kids who'd reported a pigeon at 06:12 with the same care they gave a surveillance van. "Their hardware needs come and go. List price is nothing to an organization your size."

Hua was quiet a moment. Kyle didn't fill the silence.

"Territorial access holds as long as you pay the standard rate promptly," Hua said. "The account flag stays neutral. No Lotus affiliation on your record or on your jobs. The hardware deal covers a fixed roster of six, list price, direct from the vendor." He paused. "Sable contracts run in our territory stay on your own rates. You don't sub-contract to the syndicate."

"Correct."

"Acceptable," Hua said. He said it like a man who'd expected to say it.

Kyle picked up the menu. "I'm going to order the ramen."

"Of course," Hua said. He stood, bowed precisely, and stepped back.

Mira appeared at the end of the counter, jacket on, clipboard under her arm.

"The Antiquarian," she said. "Ask him who subscribes to your file." She looked at him like someone who'd already done the work and decided a direction was worth more than an answer. "Not what's in the file. Who subscribes."

"Why," Kyle said.

"Because the file is older than the assessments," she said. "And because of what the togishi said." She turned and walked toward the back. Kyle looked at the space where she'd been, then down at the menu.

He ordered the ramen.

[Beat 203]
The bowl arrived — broth the color of November, chashu sliced thin, a soft egg split at the middle. He ate three bites before he checked his queue. Pixel's rule: eat first. He'd done it in the wrong order tonight, and the forearm and the rib were the proof. He was doing it right now.

The queue had three items.

The first was a tip from Fassbinder-Nkono's courier guild — small, professional, sent within ten minutes of the delivery clearing. The thank-you of people who knew what a corridor cost. The second was a Sable job posting: pickup and deliver, east side, nothing in a corridor, nothing in Lotus territory, eleven percent over standard.

The third had no sender.

Not a blank sender — no sender at all. A blank field is a routing error. An empty field is a choice: a message pushed through shells built to leave nothing behind, done with a precision Kyle knew exactly one source to have. In eleven years he had taken four hundred and twelve messages from that source.

He opened it.

CORRIDOR AUDIT COMPLETE. THE CHOICE AT 6.2% IS LOGGED.

He read it again.

CORRIDOR AUDIT COMPLETE. THE CHOICE AT 6.2% IS LOGGED.

He had gotten this exact line once before — three blocks from the Pivot, the night he came back from the Narrows and Mira's first assessment. He hadn't known what to send back then. He still didn't know what the choice at 6.2% was.

No contract number. No job description. No tip, no addendum, no abort. In eleven years the source had sent him two hundred and fourteen job postings, seventeen tips above the standard rate, four addenda, and one abort. Every single one had been about a job.

This was not about a job.

Kyle set down the spoon.

[Beat 204]
6.2%.

He thought about the corridor. Fourteen men across nine blocks: four at the second intersection, seven at the wedged-open door, three at the north exit. What he'd done at each step wasn't a percentage. It was a sequence — the angle at the intersection, the left feint before the kitchen-knife man, the rib he'd eaten at the West Huron doorframe, the two runners he'd let go instead of chasing. Where in all that was a choice at 6.2 percent? What was the unit? What was being counted?

He didn't know.

He looked at the timestamp. The message had come in at 02:07. That was seventeen minutes after he cleared the north exit — before the two women stopped him on Narrows, before the restaurant, before Mira and the six positions and the round in the chest, before the togishi held a piece of steel nobody could trace and called it the loneliest thing he'd touched in forty years. Before all of it.

The source had been watching the corridor. Watching him work through it — from somewhere he couldn't place, by a method he couldn't name, in a spot it had never once said it could reach — and it had recorded what he did.

Kyle closed the message.

He picked up the spoon. He ate. The broth was the right temperature for 02:40 on Narrows, with a sore rib and a wrapped forearm and a deal sitting in his queue, and he ate until the bowl was empty. Then he set the spoon down.

He called for the bill. The number was right. He laid the credstick on the counter — exact, no change owed — and stood.

Outside, the cherry trees held their wrong-month blossoms in the warm breath of the city: white, quiet, kept alive at a cost by people who didn't have to. The city ran. Somewhere north, Fassbinder-Nkono's harness clicked through a quiet street with the package delivered and the numbers closed.

The forearm would need Pixel's kitchen table in the morning.

He walked home.

## Chapter 10: The One Who Doesn't Stop

[Beat 205]
The team is good. They don't bunch and they don't panic. They peel back to the SUVs and start working the angles, and good is the problem. Kyle's read can stay ahead of one gun. Five at once is a cost, and every read he spends is gone for good. His nervous system starts to cook under the load.

A second slug catches the flanker breaking left, and the man drops mid-stride. A third punches through the door an operator is crouched behind and folds the steel in after it. A fourth catches a man rising from cover and puts him back down.

Then Cacophony runs dry on the fifth — the hammer falls on an empty chamber, a click he feels in his back teeth. There's no time to swing the cylinder out and reload, so he stops solving the problem with the gun. He reaches over his shoulder and draws Silence off his back. The katana is a matte-black length of steel-CNT alloy, an edge that takes no light and offers no trick beyond the hand that holds it — and the hand has had a long time to practice. He crosses the open ground inside the half-second his neuretics bought him, slides under the muzzle before it can track him, and lifts the rifle clean out of the fifth man's hands at the receiver. The man is left disarmed, untouched, and loudly amazed to still have all ten fingers.

The sword for the problems steel can solve, Cacophony for the rest. Between them they have kept him alive longer than the odds allow. Behind his eyes the overclock runs hot. A small red light ticks over at the corner of his sight, the way it always does when he pulls too much at once. A thin line of blood starts from his nose; he thumbs it off his lip without thinking, and white creeps in at the edges of everything he can see. He ignores both.

Five down or out. One left — the leader, who hasn't moved this whole time, because the leader has been waiting.

[Beat 206]
The leader brought the weapon built for men like Kyle. He's only been waiting for the range to clear enough to use it. It's a Gauss rifle — a man-portable rail driver that throws a single hypersonic depleted-uranium dart along a line so flat it might as well be ruled with a straightedge, and it doesn't care what's standing behind the target. It's the one weapon Kyle's neuretics can't beat. You can't dodge a thing that's already past you. You can only try not to be standing where it was aimed.

He settles it on Kyle's chest and fires. Kyle reads the shot the breath before it leaves the barrel, and for that one breath the read hands him both outcomes at once, the way it never has before. Stay in the line: take the dart clean through the sternum, dead or worse before the sound of it arrives — honest, finished, over. Or step, and let it go find whatever it finds instead. There is no version where nothing gets paid. He has known which one he'd choose since before he had words for who it was for: a workbench with six inches of clear space on it, a latch he's watched for months, a door across a hall that has stopped being locked to him. He steps.

The dart finds nothing but the air he left behind, and it doesn't stop. It crosses the intersection dead straight and punches through the windshield of an ambulance running north under its lights, and it takes the driver through the seat without slowing down to do it. He's dead before the vehicle knows it's lost him. With no one to steer, the ambulance drifts through its turn and meets a stalled hauler at fifty-two miles an hour. The impact throws its back doors wide on their hinges.

Kyle does not look at the cab. He won't look at it tonight, or any night after. Later, going back over the count the way he goes back over every fight, he'll find the one place that stays blank, and he won't ask himself why.

He's inside the leader's reach before the man can cycle the rifle for a second shot. One clean upward cut, and the Gauss rifle clatters loose to start a quieter life on the pavement.

He straightens in the light of the wreck. Through the sprung-open bay doors of the ambulance, a small face is looking out at him. The small face lifts a hand and waves.

Kyle waves back.

[Beat 207]
"Are you the new one?" the small face asks.

She's eight, maybe nine. Her chest is taped under a flexible biopolymer brace, and she is, by a wide margin, calmer than the one-armed CorpoNation security officer bleeding out on the asphalt a yard to Kyle's left.

He steps up into the bay. The driver is dead. The medic slumped in the passenger seat isn't coming back either. The patient tag clipped to the gurney reads IMANI OKONKWO-LINDQVIST — CRUCIBLE GENOMICS RECIPIENT — POST-OP, FIRST 6 HOURS — NORTHWESTERN MEMORIAL. Crucible Genomics prints organs to order. So the kid is six hours into living on one of them, and both of the people keeping her alive are dead. He reads the tag twice. It comes to one thing, and the one thing is bad.

"Hi, Imani," he says. "I'm Kyle."

"I know," she says. "My uncle Vo Dinh says you're the one who doesn't stop."

[Beat 208]
He thumbs the emergency contact on the tag. His overlay chimes once — the tag going out on the emergency medical band.

"Pike," a voice says, already moving.

"Your driver's dead," Kyle says. "The kid's on the floor of a south-side ditch with a bioprinted heart wired to a battery. I have a motorcycle."

A pause — not shock, recognition. "Kyle." Pike says it flat, the way you say the name of a problem you already know by heart. "Of course it's you."

"What's the brace reading?" Pike asks.

Kyle looks. "Sinus, ninety-two, paced."

"You've got sixty minutes before the vascular scaffold throws a clot," Pike says. "Maybe seventy."

Kyle doesn't hear the maybe. The same neuretics that read the kill team a hundred and twenty milliseconds ahead of itself read the brace now — the battery draw, the kid's color, the plain hard fact of a six-hour-old heart — and give him back one number, cold, with no maybe in it. Sixty.

"Keep her calm," Pike is saying. "A new graft that races can tear right off the suture line. Those wires aren't there for decoration."

"Northwestern?" Kyle asks.

"Streeterville. Trauma Bay Nine. I'll have a slot waiting."

"I'm in Hegewisch."

Pike gets there almost as fast as Kyle's neuretics had. "Then it's a straight line. North. Don't stop for checkpoints, don't stop for anything. And mind The Weather — there's a schism parked over 35th, has been since Tuesday, and your straight line runs right under it. If the read in your skull drops out, you're in it. Steady speed, eyes on a far light, and whatever you do, don't look at the edges."

"See you in a few," Kyle says.

The connection drops.

[Beat 209]
He lifts Imani onto the back of the seat, sidesaddle, and threads the medical harness through his belt loop so the battery rides snug over his kidney instead of swinging loose. He ties her wrists loosely through the back of his coat so she won't fall if she sleeps. Then he loads Cacophony with a fresh moon-clip — six rounds, a full wheel, to replace the four he'd spent back at the Hegewisch crossroads — and lets it settle on his left hip. Silence rides across his back.

Her cheek finds the back of his shoulder like it has been there before.

"Hold on," he says. Her hands are too small to reach around his ribs, so it's a stupid thing to say, but it's what you say.

"How fast?" she asks.

"Fast," he says.

"Good."

She is the only new weight he is carrying, and the only one he means to bring all the way home. The bike turns over and catches.

[Beat 210]
The first mile is gray zone. Hegewisch sprawl — no sovereign in residence, nobody to answer to and nobody to answer for you. A dead delivery automaton kneels in a drainage lot on the corner, its chassis streaked chalk-white by years of rain. Some child has tied a festival ribbon around one outstretched hand, and the machine holds it patiently, the way it would hold anything, for as long as the ribbon cares to stay.

Stash had run a load through here last month and said the south stretch of Avenue O was clean if you took it fast. Kyle takes Avenue O at eighty. Two rows of collapsed light poles lie across the road like felled trees. His neuretics finds the gap between them before he's had to think about it. Imani's breath is a small warm patch against the back of his shoulder — even, slow, held on purpose.

"You're not scared," he says.

"I'm being efficient with my fear," she says.

Kyle almost laughs. Then doesn't.

"Where'd you learn that?" he asks.

"The lady who got me ready. She said to say it until it's true."

"Is it true yet?" he asks.

Imani thinks about it and answers honestly. "No."

"Then keep saying it," he says.

## Crucible Genomics

[Beat 211]
The checkpoint is two guards in soft-helmet armor and a third tucked behind a blast-shield. All three wear the bored faces of men expecting a manifest. Kyle doesn't have a manifest. He has a child, a battery, and a smile — and he has learned that on the right night, with enough confidence, the three together pass for paperwork.

He brakes hard and throws both hands off the bars, palms open, a man holding nothing. He gets the words out before any of them finish reaching for a weapon.

"POST-OP TRANSIT," he shouts. "LACUNA COOLER. NORTHWESTERN."

The left guard looks at the brace. The right guard looks at the cooler's logo. The man behind the glass — Crucible internal, a white coat under his armor — lifts Imani's face against a phenotype registry, because in a genomics zone the gate reads the person, not the plate. The bike is nobody here. Whatever the registry gives him back drains the color out of his evening.

"Let him through," he says. "Phenotype clearance."

The barrier sinks into the road.

"Have a great evening," Kyle says, and means it.

[Beat 212]
A mile and a half of Crucible private road — manicured, palm-lit, the river running black on the right. Kyle pegs the throttle. Workers in beige smocks glance up from the greenhouses and decide not to look long.

He thinks it through as he rides. The company has him tagged as a courier hauling inventory out. That's the only reason the gate opened, and it's only good while he keeps moving. Nobody who wants what's wired into Imani's chest can follow him through here — they don't carry whatever got him waved in. So they won't follow. They'll call ahead. And the ones they wake on the far side won't need her breathing to collect, because a six-hour heart travels fine in a cooler.

He keeps moving.

"Why are the trees in rows?" Imani asks.

"Because the company likes to know where things are," Kyle says.

"That seems anxious."

Kyle has nothing to add. The kid is right.

[Beat 213]
The gate slab dies halfway up. It jams a third of the way, shrieking on a dead track, and Kyle flattens to the tank and takes them under it with inches to spare — close enough that the cold edge of the shutter combs the air over his spine. They drop off Crucible's glass-smooth pavement onto the broken asphalt of the gray zone, and the suspension learns about Chicago all at once. Imani laughs. Small, clean, surprised out of her.

"Loud part," she says.

"Told you," he says.

## The Pullman Seam

[Beat 214]
Pullman Works glows up on the right — restored row houses, modern cameras hidden inside period fixtures. An Axiom company town, keeping perfect time for people who don't own a single minute of it. Kyle threads the gray-zone seam along its edge, the unincorporated strip Axiom never bothered to fence. Where the factory rain has dried, the pavement has gone soap-slick, and he feeds the bike a little less lean through the stretch without thinking about it.

Heads come up as the engine passes. One kid shouts something the engine swallows, but the shape of the word is unmistakable — legend — and Kyle lifts a hand off the bars like a man in a parade.

Other heads come up too, and these ones don't spare a look for the bike. Fledglings. Pledges to the Vultures, the crews who strip the dead and the not-quite-dead for parts. A fledgling runs salvage on spec — no patch, no blessing — hoping to drag home something worth a permanent place in the flock. A post-op transplant riding through open country isn't a target to a kid like that. It's a winning lottery ticket. Two of them are already on their bikes. Already on their neuretics.

[Beat 215]
Imani's hand finds the side of his coat and grabs a fistful. She isn't afraid, Kyle realizes. She's taking it all in.

"First time off the block?" he asks.

"First time on the ground," she says.

"Well," he says. "Buckle up."

"Buckle up to what?" she asks.

"Existentially," he says.

She thinks about that one for a long moment, the city pouring past on both sides of her.

"Okay," she says.

## Ashgrave Corridor

[Beat 216]
The Ashgrave Synthesis Corridor announces itself with a wall of haze you can taste a block before you can see it. The perimeter lights glow the wrong color — too yellow, too low — the way a refinery glows when it's brewing something. This is somebody's commute. A shift-change line of workers in cheap half-masks trudges the shoulder, heads down, lunch pails swinging, walking a thing they made peace with long ago, because the only other choice was not eating. A stall at the mouth of the corridor sells filter cartridges, and beside them a hand-painted board keeps a tally — DAYS SINCE — the number low, the way it's always low here.

Kyle has a respirator clipped to his jacket. Imani doesn't. His neuretics reads the air and shows him a number he doesn't like and a clock he likes even less. He reaches back without slowing, finds her face with the flat of his palm, and presses his bandana over her mouth.

"Breathe shallow," he says. "Through this. Not your nose."

She nods against his hand. A masked worker on the shoulder lifts two fingers as the bike passes — not a wave, just the nod one night-shift gives another across a bad stretch of road — and Kyle returns it. Then he bites down on his own mouthpiece, snaps the seal, and rides into a sky the color of an old bruise like he was personally invited.

[Beat 217]
Two refinery towers breathe orange fire on either side of him, close enough that he could count the rivets if he had a second to spare. Sixty feet up, a vent stack blows down all at once, and the pressure slams sideways across the road. The bike yaws hard. He corrects before the yaw has finished, his hands moving a half-second ahead of the problem. Imani's grip tightens into a small steel claw at his belt.

"We're fine," he says, out loud.

"I know," she says through the bandana. "I can tell from your shoulders."

Kyle has met grown adults — armed, paid, supposedly professional — who couldn't read his shoulders. He is starting, against every instinct he owns, to take this kid personally.

[Beat 218]
Halfway through, the corridor's throat starts to close.

A leak somewhere upwind trips a containment lockdown, and Ashgrave's answer to a leak is a blast-rated bulkhead coming down across the only open lane on a dead-man timer — slow as a guillotine, and about as open to argument. It's built to seal whoever's caught on the wrong side in with the air they were trying to outrun. This is not the sally-gate. There's no actuator to find and shoot, no override, and no time to look for one. A hydraulic spine already in motion is a different animal than one sitting at rest, and his neuretics tells him so before he's even finished the thought.

He can't beat the door. He can only beat it there.

He drops two gears and pins the throttle. In his skull the shrinking gap gets measured against the height of a man lying flat with a child folded down his spine, and the answer comes back with no margin anywhere in it. He takes it anyway. Something behind his eyes lurches — a warning, one full second of it while he leans that hard on the neuretics — then steadies.

They go under the falling edge at speed. The lip catches the battery brace and shrieks the whole length of it in a fan of orange sparks — a hand's width higher and the night ends here, for both of them — and then the bulkhead seats home behind them with a boom he feels in the floor of his chest. Through. He throws one look at the brace. Still green. The cooler held.

Imani isn't laughing this time. Both fists are buried in his coat, her face pressed flat between his shoulder blades, and she has gone very, very quiet. One more thing tonight has taught him about her.

[Beat 219]
He clears the corridor's north ramp at a hundred and ten, rolls off the throttle on the way down, and spits the mouthpiece out so it swings free on its lanyard. Reaching back one-handed, he tugs Imani's bandana down off her face.

"You with me?" he asks.

She coughs once, twice, then says, "I'm with you. Your shoulders said we were okay."

"My shoulders are working overtime," he says.

"Give them a raise."

Kyle laughs — a real one, surprised out of the bottom of him — laughs so hard he nearly loses the line.

## Englewood

[Beat 220]
Englewood at night looks like a city that's been told a joke nobody's laughing at. Kyle means to run 63rd straight across to Halsted, but 63rd dead-ends in a viaduct that finished collapsing sometime back in the spring — a long slope of broken deck and bent rebar where an underpass used to carry the road through. No way over it the easy way.

There's a hard way. A tilted slab the locals have braced and shimmed into a ramp — down here, a disaster gets turned into infrastructure by the people who have to live on top of it. He knows the ramp is sound because Echo ran a job out this way a season back and tapped him the wave-map after: the whole broken shelf of Englewood charted in clicks and echoes, the way only Echo bothers to chart anything.

He takes it at speed. The bike goes light at the crest, weightless for one long held breath, Imani's battery thudding once against his kidney — and they land hard on the far side with thirty-four minutes left on the clock and a fresh ache settling in behind his teeth. Two headlights take the ramp a beat behind him. Then a third. His neuretics tags them and ranges them and tells him the one useful thing there is to know: they are matching his speed on purpose.

He does the one useful thing in answer. He goes faster.

[Beat 221]
They come in a flock this time — the ones a ping woke, fresh bikes and no patience between them, fanning out across both lanes to take him at speed. The lead pair don't reach for guns. They reach for their belts and throw, and the air ahead of him fills with fat little cylinders that burst into white glare and dead static. Flashbang. Chaff. And the ugly third kind — the EMP and interference rounds a salvage crew carries to kill a mark's bike out from under him and blind whatever he's running behind his eyes.

It's a good plan. It would work on almost anyone alive.

Kyle's bike stumbles, coughs, catches, and keeps running on hardened ignition. And his neuretics never so much as flickers, because a military rig is shielded against far more than static. Against EMP. Against the whole spectrum of battlefield interference. And against the low, wrong pressure at the very bottom of the band — the kind that would fold a civilian rig clean in half. You don't harden a mind against that last thing unless you already know, for a fact, that it's out there waiting. Kyle has never told a living soul that his is. Where a pack of fledglings came by rounds that carry it is a question for a later night.

He stops the bike. Running is the one thing they're built for; standing still is the one thing they're not. He picks the narrow place under the 63rd Street viaduct, where the lane necks down between two dead cars, thumbs the bike into auto-balance, and feels it take its own weight — the gyros holding it upright and patient at a crawl, the kid still aboard.

"Don't move," he tells her.

Imani goes still as furniture.

Then he steps off into the road and draws Silence off his back.

The first one commits to his swing — a length of chain, whipping flat — before he understands the whole fight just changed shape. Kyle reads the arc early, the way he reads everything, and simply isn't where it lands. The chain's tail still opens a thin red line across his forearm, the tax for standing in this close. He takes that. On the same motion he pulls the rider off his bike, cuts the bandolier of grenades from his chest, and tosses it back underhand.

"Hold these."

Imani catches it against herself like a schoolbag.

The second one tries to run him down, which is how the second one comes to study the asphalt up close. The third has the sense to draw a pistol — and that's the moment the neuretics shows him the thing no briefing can ever quite sell: the shot itself, a full beat before the trigger finishes pulling it, a hard red line laid from the muzzle straight through the center of his chest.

So Kyle takes his chest off the line. He drops his weight back off his heels, far enough to feel the round part the air just above his sternum and travel on to find the fourth fledgling — who'd done everything right tonight except stand directly behind the man he was hunting. The shooter stares at what his own hand just did. Kyle relieves him of the pistol, and of any further interest in the fight.

The rest of the flock reads the odds and doesn't like them. Two peel away north and don't look back. The ones already on the ground will walk home when their legs come back around to the idea.

Kyle swings onto his bike — still upright, still warm, still holding Imani exactly where he left her — kills the balance, and lets the weight settle into his hands like something he was born holding. Imani is looking down at the bandolier in her lap.

"Are these ours now?" she asks.

"Until some bigger kid wants to try and take them," Kyle says, threading her free hand back through the back of his coat.

She thinks about that and finds it fair — which is, Kyle suspects, the most truthful thing anyone has ever bothered to tell her. He brings them back up to speed, and for the first time since Pullman the mirrors stay empty behind him. They are done being chased.

[Beat 222]
On Halsted, before the tracks, a micro-schism is crossing the road.

He can see it: the specific shimmer of disturbed air that isn't heat, the way the streetlight's reflection in the wet pavement breaks apart and won't come back together. Small. Moving south at the pace of a slow walk. He throttles back and waits for it to clear the lane.

Forty seconds.

It clears. The shimmer settles. He rides on.

He doesn't bother tracking it — too small, too brief to matter. GLMZ loses a micro-schism every three days to natural dissipation and grows two more from aperture bleed; tracking one needs a pattern, and this is just weather. He's ridden around five of them this week alone, each one different, each one routine. It's the same read he uses for traffic, for patrols, for the way a mark's routine bends around what it doesn't want to notice. The road tells you what it's going to do, if you watch it.

The tracks at 35th are half a kilometer ahead.

## The Cinderfall Anomaly

[Beat 223]
Halsted north of the tracks runs straight over the bones of Cinderfall Energy — eleven underground thermal plants whose only face to the world is a grid of steam-vent grates and the heat-waves rippling up off them, bending the streetlights into water.

"Why is the air doing that?" Imani asks.

"Because the company underneath us is cooking," Kyle says.

"Oh." Then, smaller, all the cleverness gone out of it: "I don't like it here."

"Me neither," Kyle says, and doesn't dress it up, because that one is simply true.

He doesn't tell her that the air is about to do something Cinderfall has nothing to do with. With the road behind him finally empty, his neuretics has nothing left to track but what waits ahead — the power-blue cones marking the perimeter already catching his headlight, holo tape looping between them on three sides, Halsted left open on the fourth because nobody was going to close Halsted for one of these. Two blocks on, the streetlights stop agreeing about where they stand. And the read in his skull, which has had a clean answer ready for everything tonight, goes quiet. In the array's running log, the 18.7 Hz notation that's been running since the carousel — type: unclassified, action: none, no category, nine months of it — drops out. No transmission from the source end. Just gone. Quiet in the one specific way that means only one thing.

The Weather, Pike had called it.

[Beat 224]
He should turn west. West is Bridgeport, and Bridgeport is normal. He goes straight, because straight is where the call is.

At 35th and Halsted the air changes temperature with nothing anywhere to explain it — a dry, settled cold, and under the cold the smell of a room sealed shut since before he was born. The world quietly stops being a place. The headlight reaches out and touches something, and the something touches back. The edges of everything go dim. His stomach drops and doesn't come back. The wheels are still on pavement, but the pavement is running half a second behind where his eyes swear it should be.

And then comes the worse thing. The thing the briefings never sell hard enough.

He knows this corner.

The neuretics hands him a location stamp from a source with no registry entry — a fragment marked origin: external. The hardware flags it and waits. Kyle has no category for it.

He has never once been here, and he knows it anyway — the way you know a hallway from a dream you've had your whole life and never once on purpose. At the near edge of the perimeter a structure stands that his headlight sweeps past in half a second. He does not look at it.

[Beat 225]
The thing has a name on the weather forecasts and a warning flag on the maps. Kyle knows the names are just manners. It isn't bad air. It isn't radiation. It is something aware of him — faintly, and in no hurry.

His neuretics try to map it and hand him nothing but garbage. Distances that won't sit still. A clock running three ways at once. His own engine note reaching his ears a half-second late, off a street he already left. The picture in front of him sits a little ahead of itself, like a page turned early. The buildings stand where they should, but their shadows fall the wrong way. Light from a window two floors up hits his eyes a moment before he passes it.

So he flies on the one instrument no upgrade ever beat: the old animal at the base of his skull.

He picks one streetlamp far out on the rim and holds to it. Constant speed. Eyes on the light. Don't look at the edges. Don't trust a turn — in here, right is sometimes left, and left is sometimes down.

The air feels full. It feels like a room where something huge and very old has just turned around to look at you. Not angry. Just patient, like it has all the time there is.

"Don't look left," he says.

"Okay," Imani says. Her voice comes out thin. Her hand finds his coat, grabs a fistful, and stays there.

[Beat 226]
At the edge of his vision something moves that shouldn't be able to. A shape with one shadow too many — and the extra shadow arrives a half-beat ahead of the shape that throws it. Where it passes, the air goes cold with no source, and it makes no sound, only a pressure in the ear like water at depth.

He does not look straight at it. Some animal part of him knows a mind doesn't come back from looking straight at that. Imani's breath catches against his back — his only warning — and he throws the bike right, though right is the wrong word for the direction it actually goes. The miss should have cleared a foot. It clears less than an inch. He feels it pass like a current turning in deep water, and a line of cold opens along his ribs under the coat — deep, clean, not bleeding yet, because in here the blood runs a half-second behind everything else.

Then the far lamp swims up close and steady and yellow. The pavement grabs his tires all at once. The engine note folds back into itself and becomes an engine again. The cold line under his ribs catches up and burns now, the way real wounds burn once the blood arrives. He does not look back. He rides.

And behind him, the borrowed calm finally breaks. Imani comes apart — all the way apart, the way an eight-year-old does — sobbing into his back, both small fists twisting in his coat. "It looked right at me, it looked right at me, I want to go home, I want to go home." The brace at his kidney picks it up and starts to chirp, fast and climbing, because a six-hour-old heart was never built to run this hard. That, and not the thing back in the dark, is what can kill her now.

Keep her calm, Pike had said.

Kyle has no calm left and no time to fake any, so he gives her the only thing he has — his voice, flat and low and dull as a machine reading off numbers, naming the next ordinary thing, and the next. The bike. The bridge coming up. The loud part she likes.

"You're out of it. It's behind us. It can't come where we're going."

The chirp slows. Slows again. Steadies. By the next block she has herself back, mostly, in the wrung-out way a kid does after the worst of it lets go.

"You're hurt," she says, very quietly.

"I'm fine," he says.

It is the first time he has lied to her.

[Beat 227]
Twenty-one minutes, and the mirrors have stayed empty since the viaduct. What he still has is the Loop — the one part of the city no CorpoNation gets to switch off. Too public, too packed. It's the heart every zone plugs into to stay alive. So when it goes wrong, it goes wrong in the open. Tonight it is going wrong.

He hears it from the mouth of the tunnel: the flat clap of small-arms under a crowd's roar. Imani lifts her head off his back.

"Is that the loud?" she asks, a thread of the schism still in her voice.

"Different loud," he says. "Just people."

The long way costs eleven minutes he's already bleeding through, so he goes in. Halfway down the dark, a corporate security line has a municipal picket pinned behind an overturned cleaning rig. Both sides are too far gone to notice a motorcycle until it's the most interesting thing in the tunnel. Kyle reaches back, and Imani presses a flashbang into his hand before he can ask. He throws it with his sword arm. It hits the ceiling and goes off. The tunnel goes white, and he rides the median straight through the blind seconds — and one of those frightened people gets lucky. A round tears a hot line across the top of his shoulder and nearly rips the gun out of his hand. He keeps the throttle. Adds the shoulder to the rib. Behind him Imani watches the rivers of light pour past overhead, not the blood she can't see.

"It's beautiful," she says.

Kyle gets his breath back around the burn before he answers, so it comes out level.

"Don't tell the advertisers," he says. "It'll go to their heads."

[Beat 228]
River North is a thirty-second wedge of gray zone where the Loop's grid loses its nerve at the river. The old lift bridges are still there, but nobody has managed the river in eighteen years. The lift machinery seized a decade back, so the spans sit down for good, half-drowned. Lower Wacker is a black canal under them now, where a road used to run. People live here anyway — laundry strung span to span, a man fishing the canal off a fixed deck with a hand line, a kid working a cooler of something cold down a line of cars that never moves. Down a stairwell that runs below the waterline, a crack in the wall gives off a faint blue-green light, the way the oldest blocks do once they've been wet long enough. The air coming up out of it is cold and close and smells of a cellar that has made its peace with the river.

Imani turns to watch the water slide past underneath. "The street's drowned," she says — not scared, just taking it in.

"Most of them down here are," Kyle says. He takes the upper deck of the Michigan Avenue span, where Tollgate Systems strung a credit-gate across a public ruin, because someone always finds a way to charge rent on a thing nobody owns. He doesn't have a Tollgate badge. He has twelve minutes and an opinion about Tollgate. The barrier is a swinging steel boom. It folds off its hinge without complaint — the least dangerous thing to happen to him all night.

What he thinks about instead is the camera reading his face across the span. Not the bike — the bike is nobody. It's him the system wants, and it bills the only name it can match: a credit account that hasn't existed since 2207, one he built for exactly this kind of night and then deleted. The charge will bounce. The rejection will get filed, archived, and wiped in a routine sweep. Six weeks and seventeen quanta of Tollgate's money, all chasing a ghost. He laughs, and it pulls at the cold line under his coat.

"Why are you laughing?" Imani asks.

"Because the most expensive thing I did to them tonight was the paperwork."

"Good," she says.

## Streeterville

[Beat 229]
Streeterville is a forest of glass towers wearing crowns of advertising, and at their feet sits the gray concrete slab of Northwestern Memorial. Tonight the whole district is packed wall to wall. The strike has spilled north into the one part of the city nobody is allowed to lock down — which is exactly why the strike came here.

Kyle picks out the bay's red light six blocks off. Between him and it, wading through the crowd on six legs with the patience of a thing that has nowhere better to be, is an Arcturus crowd-control unit. A crab the size of a panel van, matte riot-grey. A Bulwark. Arcturus builds everything into a crab in the end — low, wide, impossible to tip over — and a crab, it turns out, is what has been waiting at the end of his night.

Kyle has put this exact unit down twice before. He knows it's the same one, because it slows the instant its optics find his face, recalculates, and comes for him first out of a crowd of a thousand easier problems.

A machine can't hold a grudge. Kyle stopped believing that the second time.

[Beat 230]
The Bulwark is built to work shoulder to shoulder with civilians, so it carries nothing that goes bang — no slugs, no rockets, nothing that doesn't care where it lands. What it has instead is hands. Tanglefoot. Kinetic foam. A sonic throw that turns your inner ear to surf. And four hydraulic claws built to fold a person up neat and hold them there for collection. It wants Kyle folded up. It doesn't care about the kid at all, except as part of the same package.

That is the opening. It's a thin one.

Kyle works Imani's hands free of his coat without setting her down — she stays on the pillion, she stays with the bike — and pulls two fat cylinders from the bandolier she's carried since the viaduct. The fledglings' own flash and chaff. Finally good for something.

"It sees in light, and it hears in sound, and it can't shoot back," he says. "Left one's flash — that blinds it. Right one's chaff — that makes it deaf and stupid. Pull the tab, count two, throw it at the face."

Imani looks at the crab the size of a panel van. Then she looks at the two grenades in her own two hands.

"It's a really big crab," she says, in the voice of a kid who has just been handed the best and worst job of her short life.

"It is," Kyle agrees. "Throw at the face."

[Beat 231]
Then everything is very loud and very fast at once.

Imani's first flash goes off a half-second early and a yard wide — and the Bulwark flinches from it anyway, optics whiting out — and Kyle is already moving. Not away from the thing but under it, because the one place a crab cannot reach is the spot beneath its own belly. He lays the bike flat and slings the two of them through the gap between its legs, drawing Silence as the world tips sideways, and opens one leg clean on the way through. The Bulwark drops a quarter of its height with a sound like a building deciding to sit down.

His left foot lands a half-inch outside where he aimed it, and he fixes it without knowing he's fixing it.

Then, for the first time all night, the edge fails him.

All night the schism has given him a half-second lead — a beat of warning before anything happened, his body arriving early. Now that lead runs out. The light in the corner of his eye flares red, the half-second doesn't come, and his body arrives late instead. His knee buckles half a step. For one full second he is only a tired man with a sword, slow as everyone else in the world.

So he never sees the claw. It whips back over the fallen flank, closes on the bandolier across Imani's chest, and tears it loose — lifting her half a foot into the air before the strap gives and drops her hard.

Something bigger had wanted them. Exactly the way he told her it would.

But she'd pulled the chaff's tab a heartbeat before the claw took her, and the two-count runs out now — point-blank, in the crab's own grip, bursting right in the sensors the claw reached out with. The legs stop agreeing with each other and start hunting for a floor that isn't there.

Kyle gets her down behind the engine block and puts himself between her and the machine, and finishes it the slow way — on grip and memory alone, with nothing out in front of him. A claw widens the cut on his forearm. The bad rib tears on a blind pivot. He takes both, because he never saw them coming. That is the difference. The Bulwark folds down into the intersection, blind and deaf and beached, its legs working and going nowhere. The bike does not survive the dismount.

But the bay's red light is three blocks off now. And three blocks is nothing.

"Good count," he tells her.

Imani — shaking, fierce, wrung all the way out, staring at the claw that took her grenades and choked on them — says, "I know."

[Beat 232]
The bike is dead and the bay is three blocks off, and three blocks is nothing. He has bled a lot more than this for a lot less. He sheathes Silence across his back. Holsters Cacophony on his hip. Then he picks her up — harness, battery, the whole small weight of her gathered in against his chest.

Imani's hand fists in his collar. "You're hurt," she says again, and this time he doesn't bother with the lie.

"Yeah," he says.

He carries her. Ahead, the red light grows larger and brighter.

[Beat 233]
Pike is already out in the lane with the gurney — tall, brown-skinned, hair tied back, scrubs hanging off a body that has clearly run a long way today. He doesn't waste a syllable on hello.

"On the gurney."

Kyle is already lowering her onto it. The harness comes off in two practiced motions. Imani's hand stays knotted in his coat a half-second after the rest of her is down, and when he gently pries it loose, she asks him a hard question.

"Will I see you again?"

Kyle does not lie to her. He has been lied to by kinder people than himself, and it never once helped.

"No," he says. "Probably not."

His hands hang empty at his sides, no next move in them.

She takes it the way she has taken everything else tonight — straight, no flinch, tucking it away somewhere. Good, he thinks. Better now, while she's still young enough for the scar to set smooth. He learned it late himself.

"Thank you for the loud," she says.

"Anytime, kid," he says — the gentlest lie he has told all night, and the only one she lets him keep. "Tell the heart I said hi."

[Beat 234]
Pike reads the brace, the wires, the cooler — and then reads him. The hand pressed flat to his side. The dark spreading at the forearm and the shoulder. The boot that won't quite take his full weight.

"Forty-one minutes," he says. "I had you at fifty-five."

"You bet against me?"

"I bet the over. Paid for the gurney you just put her on." Pike snaps his fingers twice at a trauma nurse, points at Imani, and the bay swallows her the way good bays do — fast, and from all sides at once. "Eleven years, Kyle. You keep bringing me work in worse shape than the patients."

"I cheated," Kyle says.

"You're bleeding into my ambulance lane," Pike says. "Stay. Half the people in my bay tonight are in better shape than you are."

Kyle is already turning away. "Don't worry about me," he says. "I'll just rub some dirt in it."

[Beat 235]
He walks.

Behind him the bay door drops shut and its red light goes white. They have her now. That is all the confirmation the night was ever going to give him that the run worked.

No alarm follows him into the street. No name. The rain finds the graze on his shoulder first, then the rib. His hands, empty of her weight, don't know what to do with themselves for half a block. Whatever he tore open tonight to get her here has already sealed shut behind him, like it was never there at all.

[Beat 236]
He turns south, on foot, the long way, because south is no longer urgent and there's no bike left to make it quick. The edge has come back — slow, shaky, but back. The white that rode the corners of his sight all night is pulling away. The bleed at his nose has gone tacky and dry. The body, at least, has stopped sending up its bills for the evening.

The bad news keeps its own count. Where a normal rig would show a battery bar, the build in his skull shows one small light. Every time he runs himself past the red, that light goes red with him. He doesn't look at it while he's working. He looks now, and counts what he can stand of tonight's damage — the schism is most of it, the rib and the graze the rest. One line never makes the list, the same way it never has, and he doesn't go looking for it.

One thing hasn't cleared with the rest. Somewhere inside the schism his neuretics picked up a background process and never finished shutting it down. It runs quiet at the bottom of everything, with no label and no output — the machine's version of a window left open in an empty room.

Then the edge, back now and working on its own, hands him something he didn't ask for: a board notice, pulled off the open band as he walks. New posting, south side. Escort work, clean spec, full retainer. No broker. No client that resolves to anyone.

A kill team that never asked why their contract came with only a description and no name. A job too clean to have a careless human behind it. And now this — landing the hour he finished, like a receipt.

He has been seeing the shape of it for months. He has a word for it now. He sets the word aside with the buyers' list and the relay logs and the other things he hasn't finished deciding about. Then he declines the posting, and walks south through the rain, and lets whoever is watching watch him do it.

[Beat 237]
The frequency hadn't fully left.

He couldn't have said what that meant, technically. The NeoCortex ran its checks and came back clean — clean enough, anyway. But he was the one reading the result, so that wasn't much comfort. It was still there, somewhere under the diagnostic layer: 17 Hz in the channels below hearing. Not a sound. Something weighted like a sound. Something waiting.

He came to her workshop because the other option was his apartment, and his apartment had walls, and alone in there his own thinking came back at him off the walls. Here it didn't.

She was working. She didn't ask what had brought him. She probably already knew. She could read him, and with most people being read felt like pressure. With her it was the opposite. Neither of them had a word for the difference, and a while ago they had both quietly agreed they didn't need one.

He sat in the corner chair. She worked. The lamp swung toward the brightest spot over her bench. Outside, the city was the city, which was steady, which was good.

He had never been this far down before. She wasn't trying to pull him up. She was just in the same room.

He didn't say either of those things.

After an hour, she said, "I'm putting the kettle on."

"Alright," he said.

He unclenched his hand. He hadn't known it was clenched.

## Chapter 12: One Shoe

[Beat 238]
The Roost was on the third floor of a walk-up on Paulina, behind a laundry that had been running since before the city's overlay came online and had never signed up for a feed address. Kyle had come here after jobs that finished clean. After jobs that didn't. After jobs he couldn't yet sort into either pile.

Femi Kasparov was in his chair by the window — right shoe on, left foot bare on the radiator cover, which was warm but not running, the way it always was in late summer. He didn't look up when Kyle came through the door.

The lemon tree was in the corner. Four feet tall, growing in something that wasn't quite soil, under a lamp that wasn't quite sun. A timer watered it. It got repotted once a year whether it needed it or not. The Roost had the wrong climate for a lemon tree. The tree had adapted. It carried two lemons at a time — never more, never less, as if it had decided long ago exactly how much it could hold.

"Chair or wall?" Femi said.

"Wall," Kyle said.

He put his back to the wall between the window and the door. Femi's chair creaked once as he settled. The grow lamp hummed, steady, doing its job.

[Beat 239]
The medic had come through three days ago. Femi reported it the way he reported everything: once, accurate, in order.

Kyle's left hand had been running three degrees colder than his right for the past six weeks. His neuretics had been quietly shifting the load to cope — the kind of adjustment that logged itself and never bothered to warn him. He had noticed it. He just hadn't put a clinical word to it until now.

"She left a number," Femi said. He let it sit a moment. "Two years at current load. Maybe less, if the rate holds where it's been holding."

Kyle said nothing.

"She said you knew."

"I knew it was a number," Kyle said. "I didn't know which one."

Femi considered this. He looked at the lemon tree. One of the two lemons was starting to color, moving from the pale yellow of new growth toward the deeper yellow of something ready. The other was still green.

"Two years is a number," he said.

Kyle's palm was flat against the wall. He hadn't put it there.

"It's a range," Kyle said. "She said maybe less."

The coloring lemon turned a little in some draft from the window seam.

[Beat 240]
The Vulture had come six weeks earlier. Kyle hadn't been here. Femi had.

The man arrived in the early afternoon, dressed for a valuation — document portfolio, handheld scanner, the face of a professional who had trained himself out of any expression at all. He'd been invited in. The Vultures were a service, and Femi had no policy against services.

The man scanned the threshold and the room. He asked, like someone checking a form, whether the occupant of record was present. Femi told him no.

He produced a figure: the catalog value of the Atlas hardware installed in the operator logged to this address, minus the market-rate cost of cutting it out. Net recovery value. The figure was large. Femi listened to it without expression.

Then the man produced a second figure. Net recovery value after risk — after the odds that a recovery wouldn't go cleanly.

The second figure was negative.

The man thanked Femi for his time, put the portfolio back in his coat, and left. He didn't leave a card. He didn't set a follow-up.

"He left the primary figure," Femi said. "Not the secondary one. The primary one was the compliment."

Kyle stood with that. The lemon tree was a thing that couldn't grow here and had grown here anyway. Whoever brought it hadn't asked it first.

[Beat 241]
The notice had been filed two weeks after the Vulture. Femi took it out of the table drawer — real paper, folded once, the crease gone sharp from sitting.

It came from the Continuity Office. It wasn't addressed to a person. It was addressed to the building, the way a form is addressed when what it wants is a place, not whoever lives there. The language was flat and standard. The property had been scheduled under the District Acquisition program, WEST TOWN WEST. They called it an *axiom* — the baseline they measured ownership from. It had a reference number. It had an effective date.

The contact name was not Mr. Able.

Kyle took the paper. He was still reading it when Femi reached over and took it back — two moves, out of his hand, folded, into the drawer. "Better to read it than log it," Femi said.

Kyle's overlay had already caught the reference number and the date. He pushed them at the district acquisition database and asked for the rest — the timeline, the assessed value.
