The answer came back fast: *Access Restricted. District Acquisition Program, WEST TOWN WEST. Dispute window closed. Contact form available. No direct lookup.* He tried another way in. Same wall. The filing was built to be found and not opened — a door with a window in it and no handle on your side.

"Two weeks after the Vulture," Femi said. "They run separate. They don't share notes."

"I know," Kyle said.

At sixteen, the surgery had taken eleven hours under general anesthetic. Nobody had really asked him. The man he would become — the one people called Kyle — hadn't been in the room when they decided what to put inside him, what it would cost a year, what a Vulture would pay for it someday. His body had been changed before anyone asked him a thing.

The Continuity Office was booking the Roost the same way. Weighing the asset. Filing the baseline.

He looked at the lemon tree. The one lemon that had been turning was full yellow now.

"How long since anyone asked you about the shoe?" he asked.

Femi thought about it. "A while," he said.

Overhead, the grow lamp held its low hum.

[Beat 242]
Kyle didn't move from the wall.

Femi didn't offer anything else. The grow lamp's light lay flat and even over the table.

Femi stood up. He moved slow, his one right shoe making a deliberate sound on the floor. He crossed to the lemon tree and pressed his thumb against the yellow lemon — not hard, just a touch, the way you check a thing you've watched for a long time. He didn't pick it. He went back to his chair and sat with the same care he'd stood up with.

Kyle pushed off the wall.

He asked the question before he knew why he was asking it.

"Do you have somewhere to go after?"

Femi looked at him. Not at the question — at Kyle. In eleven years of coming to the Roost, Kyle had never asked Femi anything that wasn't work. The question came out anyway.

"There's always somewhere," Femi said.

Kyle crossed to the tree. He took the lemon off the branch — one hand, careful, the way you take something almost ready. It sat in his palm, tight and warm from the lamp.

"You can have it," Femi said. "It was coming off anyway."

[Beat 243]
He didn't have the gear on him. It was across the hall in his apartment, racked and quiet. He went down the list in his head anyway, the way he had since he was nineteen and first understood what the weight of it meant.

Silence: a kilo and a half, plain matte-black steel and carbon composite, no edge-work, nothing that needed explaining. Cacophony: the revolver shotgun, heavier than it looked, because everything that worked was heavier than it looked. The mesh under his skin — not gear, really, more like the ground he stood on. Seventy feet of braided polyceramic laid into the tissue of his back and chest. It had stopped two rounds from the Hegewisch crossroads team three nights ago and still hadn't stopped hurting. The neuretics: Atlas-grade NeoCortex, no governor.

"The mesh stopped them," Femi said from across the room. Not a question.

"Two," Kyle said. "Three would've gone through."

He'd carried the hardware since he was sixteen. He hadn't picked it. He'd woken up with it inside him and spent eleven years turning into the man it needed him to be. The Vulture who'd come to see Femi six weeks ago had priced it right. The number he'd come back with was higher than Kyle expected.

The number wasn't the strange part. The surprise was.

Kyle paid exact for everything. Eleven years of taking his fee, settling his tab, owing no one — because a debt is a handle someone else can grab, and he didn't hand people handles. And all that time he'd been walking around worth far more than he'd ever put on himself, and somewhere in the eleven years he'd stopped noticing.

[Beat 244]
He thought about Pixel.

Not what to say. Whether to say anything at all.

The night across the hall had been three nights ago. He'd come home from the Arcturus job with the tremor running harder than usual — it had been building for six weeks, and that night it went to both hands — and she'd patched the mesh tear and the deep bruising. Somewhere after that, the thing between them got said out loud. Named, finally. He wasn't sure which of them let it show first. He'd turned it over twice in three days and landed the same place both times: it didn't matter. It got said because it did. He'd let it.

She knew about the neuretics in general. No governor, Atlas grade, something that would have to be dealt with eventually. They'd never put a date on it, because there hadn't been one. The not-yet had been a vague thing.

Now the not-yet had a date on it.

Two years at this load. Maybe less.

He didn't know what to do with *maybe*. It was honest, and he respected honest. It was also the word that would land worst if he said it aloud.

He hadn't decided whether to say anything. Standing against Femi's wall in the flat warmth of the lamp, he noticed he still hadn't.

[Beat 245]
"The Continuity Office letter," Kyle said. "When's the effective date?"

Femi told him. A month out. End of the fiscal quarter — the way district acquisition always ran.

Kyle nodded once. "You've got time to repot," he said.

Femi looked at the tree. It was a Meyer lemon. It wanted coastal California, a certain soil, about twice the humidity the Roost could hold in August.

"The Roost was always the wrong climate," Femi said. "The tree doesn't know that."

Behind him, the grow lamp held the tree in its flat light.

Kyle left.

[Beat 246]
Kyle went down the three flights with the lemon in his fist.

The ground-floor laundry was still running its last load — warm through the door, the smell of hot clean fabric, a sound in the walls like weather moving in. He pushed out into Paulina. Freight ground along the overhead tier. A Windhover maintenance drone ran its grid circuit forty feet up, orange nav-light blinking every three seconds, and two people under the transit shelter bent over an overlay, laughing at something only they could see.

He stopped on the sidewalk. The lemon was still warm from the lamp, and his thumb had already found the soft give in the rind.

Freight moved. The drone finished its arc and turned east. One of the pair threw a shape up into the rain-light with their hands — there and gone — and they laughed again. The city went on around him, and none of it was about the number.

He put his hands in his coat pockets, the lemon in one of them, and walked south toward Mrs. Chen's.

[Beat 247]
Mrs. Chen's was almost empty.

End of service, the hood lamp already down to low, the counter clean except for one other customer hunched over a bowl at the far end. Kyle sat. Mrs. Chen set a bowl in front of him without looking up from the prep surface, where she was breaking down the last of the aromatics. Pork bone broth, the color of old paper, two hours of reduction in it. He picked up the chopsticks.

He ate the whole bowl.

He wasn't thinking about the number. Or he was, the way you think about a ceiling you've lived under for years — always there, nothing new about it, except now he knew the exact height. The broth was hot and he ate it slow.

She refilled it without being asked. She did that for no one else. He didn't thank her in any way that needed an answer. The shop made its closing sounds around him.

Mrs. Chen turned to the bowl she was rinsing, eyes down.

"Not coming back?" she asked.

Kyle set the chopsticks down. "I'll be back."

She went back to the rinsing. He put the exact fee on the counter — no rounding, nothing extra in it. Stood. Walked out into the rain that had started while he ate.

[Beat 248]
Home.

Pixel's light was on, her shadow crossing behind the frosted panel in the rhythm she kept when a job had her — bench to terminal and back, two steps and back, two steps and back. The bench coolers were cycling. Something live under the hood. He stood in the hall a moment and let the ordinary sound of her working land on him. It landed harder than it should have, because he had a number now and she didn't.

He knocked once.

She didn't stop. The rhythm caught for half a beat — coolers kicking on, the two-step picking back up — but she didn't stop. He turned the handle and came in without ceremony, the way you enter a place where you belong. Coat on the peg by her door. Silence on the rack above it. He found the corner chair and sat.

She worked. He didn't interrupt. The pipes knocked once in the east wall and went quiet. Two floors up the bird ran its interval — three notes and a rest, three notes and a rest. He listened to it without meaning to.

He could have said it from the chair. He didn't. He sat in the warm bench-light and let the building close around the two of them, and kept the number where it was.

[Beat 249]
He went to the terminal.

Not to open the relay. He pulled up the contract log and ran the full eleven years — every job, scope, fee, any tip paid, how long it took, when it closed. He read them in order, looking for a pattern he suspected was there and couldn't name.

The jobs had come when he was free.

Not when a client needed something. When he could take it. Every one, lined up against his own log, against the gaps between closures: the posting showed up in the relay right when his gear was clean and his body had healed enough to work again. The timing wasn't random. He'd been freelance eleven years and had never once gone more than eighteen days without a posting, and the postings always came inside that window.

He sat with that.

He closed the contract log and opened a blank message in the relay. He looked at the empty field. He didn't type anything. After two minutes he closed it.

He looked at the kitchen drawer. Inside was the printout Pixel had made three weeks ago — the relay architecture, the scatter of tracked transmission points, all of them closing on the carousel node. He'd looked at it four times. He didn't open the drawer.

He went to bed. The rain was still going past the window. He lay in the dark a long time without sleeping. The building went quiet by degrees, and the bird upstairs finally stopped, and the rain eased, and he lay there with the number and the pattern and said nothing to either of them.

[Beat 250]
At 02:14 the relay pinged — standard routing, the same client type he'd taken twenty-eight times in the last three years. Kyle looked at the summary. He declined it. Not because the terms were wrong. Not because the job was dirty. He declined it because he'd just read eleven years of contracts and seen that he had never once turned a relay job down, and now that he'd seen it, he could act on it. He closed the window.

That was new.

Across the hall, Pixel's bench coolers cycled down at 02:14.

She'd been at the terminal since eleven. Not the bench work — the prosthetic arm was done and invoiced. She'd moved to the terminal when the building went quiet: the scatter plot on the right monitor, the relay architecture on the left. The same materials she'd worked for three weeks, the cluster tightening every time she cleaned up the transmission signatures. Forty-eight nodes. Thirty-seven of them closing on the carousel at Leavitt — the node cold now, the cluster still dense. She wasn't a psionic. She didn't need to feel it. She could read the pattern.

She opened the city permit portal. Clybourn Avenue. The center of the cluster, within six feet.

Reason field: electrical conduit inspection, legacy grid node assessment.

She saved the draft without sending it. She'd need a crew — licensed contractors, insurance papers, names on file. She'd get them. She was good at this kind of thing.

She sat with the scatter plot another minute. Forty-eight points of contact around a covered window.

She wasn't waiting for him to ask anymore.

[Beat 251]
The noodles were going cold, which at Mrs. Chen's counter was a small crime, and Kyle was letting it happen because he was watching a woman pretend not to watch him.

She stood across six feet of rain, outside the awning line, in a tan coat with the hood up. Same stance the whole time. The rain came off the hood in a clean sheet — a good coat, the kind that didn't announce itself. His neuretics kept reaching for an augmentation profile and coming back with nothing, and that kind of nothing cost more money than chrome did. Nobody stands still in the rain for eleven minutes unless the rain doesn't reach them or the work does.

"Kyle," she said. Not loud. Not for Mrs. Chen. For him, across the rain — in the steady, level voice he'd been taking contracts from through a cracked terminal for two months and had never once heard come out of a body.

[Beat 252]
He set the chopsticks down across the bowl. He didn't stand, and he didn't take his hands off the counter, and he let her watch the not-moving be his answer to the question she hadn't asked yet.

Behind him, Mrs. Chen stirred the broth with the long-handled spoon that had lost its lacquer in three places and been re-gripped with electrical tape — the same spoon she'd used the whole six years he'd eaten at this stall. She said one word into the steam.

"Húlijing."

Low. Mandarin. Not for him. Fox spirit — the beautiful kind, the kind that wants something. She said it the way a woman who has seen things names them: flat and certain, a diagnosis, not a warning. Kyle had heard Mrs. Chen mutter at the rain, at the Circuit boys who walked too close to her signage, at the atmospheric processors when they made the broth steam wrong. He had never heard her name a person.

The woman in the rain tilted her head. A fraction. A direction.

Kyle's chair scraped back. He laid the credstick on the counter — Φ12, exact — and said it the way he said it every night, low and level: "Standard rate." Mrs. Chen's hand covered the stick without looking down, the way she took his money when she thought he was about to do something she didn't like and had decided not to say so.

[Beat 253]
He followed her into the rain, six feet back — far enough to read her, close enough that she knew he'd chosen to come. Past the mochi cart, shuttered, a chain run through the handles. She never once shortened her stride for the puddles. Her feet knew the route on their own, the way your feet learn a path you've walked in the dark too many times to count.

The security drone hung over the third-block junction, grinding through its sweep on the usual interval. She crossed the eight-second blind spot without looking up for it. She wasn't timing the gap off nerves. She'd been walking this block in her head a lot longer than tonight, and that told him more about what waited at the end of it than her face had.

His neuretics ran warm in the rain, a coal of heat banked behind his left ear. He'd learned to read it the way a rider reads a knock in the engine — not a problem yet. A thing to keep a hand on.

[Beat 254]
The relay station came out of the dark at the end of the block. Wartime build: poured concrete, no ornament, a dead antenna mast running up into the rain — dead a decade, the city's records said. The kind of building that hides by being exactly what it looks like.

Halfway up the dead mast, a single status light burned amber. Steady. Patient. He blinked the rain out of his eyes and it was just wet metal and old paint. He didn't mention it. She didn't look up.

She stopped at the door. She didn't produce a key, and she didn't unlock anything. She turned, with the same calm she'd walked the route with, and the hood came back an inch — enough for the streetlight to catch the shape of a face and the dark red of her hair. He hadn't seen her face before tonight, and he wasn't sure he was supposed to be seeing it now.

[Beat 255]
The alley was eight feet wide, running between the relay station and a sealed utility corridor. The woman got two-thirds of the way down it before she stopped.

A shape near the dead end wasn't walking. It had been placed there — four to six minutes before they arrived, Kyle put it. Medical-grade jacket, the cut Carrion ran on field assets working a zone they weren't licensed for: plain, anonymous, the kind that cleared any checkpoint without a second look. Its left hand hung at its side and ran three degrees colder than the right.

He'd heard that exact detail three days ago, from a man with one working arm and a grow lamp and a drawer full of papers he'd been waiting to hand to someone.

The figure had a sweep scanner at its belt, already extended — an array-signature logger, the kind that pulled your location and your neural-mesh fingerprint and sent both in real time. It was running. Whatever it had already caught, it had already sent.

Whatever came next, it would send that too.

Cacophony came out of the holster.

Kyle shot the scanner.

The sound filled the whole alley. Eight feet of brick on both sides threw it back without softening it. A large-frame revolver going off in a space that tight doesn't leave room for anything else. The scanner came apart in two directions. The figure's hand jerked back from the empty mount and held there.

Kyle watched it.

The figure didn't run. It was recalibrating — running the new numbers, working out what the hole in the wall behind it meant for what came next.

Kyle watched it do that for three seconds.

Then he turned his back on it, because you have to pick what you're afraid of, and Kyle had made that choice eleven years ago, and he walked to the station door.

[Beat 256]
The reflex had already fired — the reach-out-before-going-dark, the location ping he ran through the neuretics before stepping into any building he hadn't cleared — when she turned and faced him. He let it sit, unsent.

"Tomorrow," she said. "This address, this time, the room behind this door. Shielded. Bring nothing electronic you can leave behind — your implant comes because it can't come out. What we're going to talk about can't happen in a room that can hear it."

"Whose room is it?" he asked.

"Mine." A beat. "That used to be a comfort."

The rain worked the awning while he took in what she meant — a fixer who could afford a Faraday cage, telling him the cage was the only place she still trusted.

"You've run forty-one contracts through me," she said. "Eleven this quarter. How many of them do you think came from clients I could sit across a table from?"

He didn't answer. It had never been his to ask. That was the whole arrangement: she vetted the clients, and he never had to.

"I used to know," she said. "That's the conversation."

[Beat 257]
"Why me?" he asked.

"Because you are the one this concerns," she said. "The rest tomorrow. In the room."

He could leave. He could ride home and tell himself the noodle stall had just been a noodle stall, and that what she'd said meant nothing. He wasn't going to. The sentence had already lodged in his chest.

"Tomorrow," he said.

The hood dipped — not a nod, just an acknowledgment. She stepped past him into the dark of the block. She didn't unlock the building behind her. Three steps and she was gone, the tan coat swallowed by the bad rain the way the Gray Zone swallowed most things at this hour.

[Beat 258]
The bike was where he'd left it, two blocks south, still wearing the plate he'd ridden down on. He took the long way home. The long way was where he did his thinking. At the second-tier crossing the match landed before he could stop it — voice plus face.

Sable.

Two months of contracts through a cracked terminal. The fixer who paid on time, never haggled, never once handed him work that smelled wrong — which in The Glooms made her rarer than an honest scale. Nobody on the boards had ever seen her face. He'd just spent eleven minutes trying not to look at it.

She'd kept her face off the boards for two months and then spent it tonight, in the rain, outside a noodle stall. You didn't burn a cover that good unless it had stopped keeping you safe — or unless what came next was worth more than staying hidden. Either way, the weight landed on him.

[Beat 259]
The bike took the lower span, where the wind came off the lake in cold flat sheets and the traffic was thin enough to hear the engine think.

Kyle went back over the thermal reading.

Three degrees. Left hand only. Femi's mesh had a load-redistribution fault — the old man had said so without quite saying it, the way old people talk about anything medical. Sideways. With dates. With the name of the medic who'd retired two years later. The hand that ran cold. The hand that couldn't hold a cup without help.

Kyle knew that apartment. Grow lamp over the east table. Manila folders in a kitchen drawer, tabs facing out. One working arm. One cold hand.

The asset in the alley had stood with its left hand hanging.

He let a lorry pass on the right and held his line.

The Vulture had come six weeks ago. Femi had mentioned him the same sideways way — a man with a scanner, asking about load-bearing walls, never writing anything down, never making a face. Kyle had heard "property assessor" and taken it for district pressure. The same slow squeeze that had leaned on the Roost for two years, working the rents, waiting for someone to flinch.

He'd been wrong about the scanner.

Three degrees across a palm is nothing to the naked eye. But to a neuretic mesh running a threat-grid on every body inside twenty meters, it's a signature. Specific enough to hide behind. Specific enough to trip a known name when the comparison runs.

Kyle had pulled Femi's profile that night without realizing it. His mesh had told him: that's the old man from the fourth floor. Low threat. Harmless. Not a Continuity Office field asset standing at a dead end at eleven-forty at night.

The scanner hadn't been measuring walls.

It had been measuring people. All of them. Every person in the building with a working neuretic load — measured, stored, and later strapped onto a field asset like a borrowed coat.

Not a property survey.

An inventory.

Kyle took the long curve before the Calvert elevation, where the city dropped away and there was nothing overhead but the underside of the mid-stack and the red nav-lights blinking in sequence. He didn't push the speed. He wasn't done counting.

The building they'd been trying to buy.

The people they'd already taken.

[Beat 260]
His step hit the landing across from 2F at an angle he hadn't announced, and Pixel's door opened anyway. T-shirt over leggings, no shoes, two cups. The steam off both was herbal, lemon-bitter. She handed him the one with the handle turned toward him — the side without the chip. The better cup. That was her way of saying something she didn't intend to say out loud.

They stood in their doorways across the hall and drank. Her apartment breathed out behind her: solder flux and bergamot, proportions that shouldn't have worked and did.

"Bird's still out," she said. "Sang twice this evening. New piece. I think it learned it off the radio."

"Yeah?"

"Yeah."

He didn't tell her about the woman in the hood, or the shielded room he'd said yes to, or how easily the yes had come. He didn't tell her the number either — two years, maybe less. He'd had the chance and let it pass. She didn't ask.

He wanted to call that her mercy, standing there with the good cup warming both his hands. It wasn't. Mercy was something done to him. This was a lie, and he was the one telling it, on purpose, because the truth would cost her more than the silence cost him. Nobody had asked him to tell it. He handed the cup back, handle first.

"Goodnight, Pixel."

"Goodnight, samurai."

[Beat 261]
He sat on the edge of the bed in the dark and listened for the bird he had never once heard. The building was still — pipes ticking somewhere below, nothing else. Three blocks east of Mrs. Chen's, a shielded room waited for morning. Built so no machine in the city could hear what got said inside it. Kept by a woman who had stopped trusting her own client list.

He went over it anyway. Two months of a voice. Paid on time, every time. If this was a trap, it was the most patient one anyone had ever set for him.

His feet were flat on the cold floor. He didn't lie back down. He didn't sleep. He didn't try.

[Beat 262]
The brass gasket seated with a sound like a held breath let go, and Kyle stood in the dark of the cage and waited for the silence to find a place in his body.

It was in his sternum. Under his sternum. A place he'd long since stopped noticing, the way you stop noticing your own pulse — a low electromagnetic hum that had lived behind the implant like a second heartbeat for as long as he'd had it. Always there. Until it wasn't.

It wasn't.

His hand was flat against his sternum before he decided to raise it. The gesture of a man checking a wound that isn't bleeding, or a pocket that's been picked. The next breath went looking for something it had reached for eleven thousand mornings running, and came back empty. The emptiness rang.

Eleven years. He'd been sixteen when they finished the installation, and he had not been quiet inside since — and he hadn't known it until the quiet arrived and filled the exact space the array had held. Like a pulled rack that leaves its ghost in the air of a room. The cold print of something that had run hot.

[Beat 263]
The lamp came up on a table, two chairs, and Sable. Red hair in a loose braid, one strand against her cheekbone that had never once stayed put. Ocular implants — black, machined, aperture rings that made a small sound when she shifted focus. That sound, he understood now, had been there under two months of cracked-terminal audio, attached to nothing, waiting for the rest of her.

He had spent two months studying this voice. Learning what it did, what it never did, how far it could be trusted. Now the face arrived and settled over the voice like a key into a lock she had already shown him.

He sat. His hands stayed open on his knees. The muscles across his shoulders did the slow burning work of keeping them open, and he let them.

[Beat 264]
She'd opened the Clybourn permit. He knew because she'd looked up from the screen with a particular expression — the one she got when a number she'd half-expected finally showed up and she didn't want to be right. She closed the permit without a word. He didn't ask. Not asking was what he had to give in return for her not asking.

She knew he was dying. She'd done the math. She was holding it from one side; he was holding the suspicion of it from the other. Same room, same knowledge, and neither of them said the number out loud.

It was the closest they had ever been.

Nothing was said. Nothing happened. She poured two cups and carried one to the corner chair and put it in his hand. That took a closeness that would, another time, have needed an excuse. She didn't offer one. He didn't ask for one. They sat with the permit shut on the bench, the rain going outside, the steam off both cups rising in the lamplight. Neither of them moved away. Nothing happened.

[Beat 265]
The relay didn't come through his usual channel.

That was the first thing. His standing relay — the Axiom trunk, the shell that resolved to nothing, eleven years of work arriving the same way every time — pinged once at 07:14 with a route he had never seen. A single address. No job code, no rate, no client field. Just the address and a time: 10:00. It was in the Carrion Industries district near the Zone 9 checkpoint, in a building the public registry called an environmental-monitoring facility. Kyle had driven past it twice in his life and known it was something else.

He went.

The man at the door wore a tan coat, not a Carrion vest, and he didn't check a clipboard. He looked at Kyle for two seconds and opened the door.

Inside had been a server farm once. The cages were still there, the wire-mesh enclosures marching back into the dark, but the hardware was gutted. One cage near the center had been furnished — two chairs, a small table, a lamp. A woman in a gray jacket sat in one chair with her hands folded over a flat case. She had the stillness of someone who had been getting ready for a conversation far longer than the other person knew it was coming.

She looked up when he stepped into the cage. She let him take in the tan-coat at the door behind him, the empty racks, the single lit lamp in the single furnished cage.

"Mr. Corbin," she said. "Thank you for coming."

Sable. He'd had the name since the ride home the night before, when the match had resolved on its own — voice plus face — before he'd thought to ask for it. She didn't offer it now. She opened the case instead.

Inside: five photographs. Five names. A rate sheet with numbers that were not opening positions. Five faces was more than a job usually carried before anyone had said what the job was. He noticed that and set it aside.

Kyle sat down in the other chair.

[Beat 266]
"Your contracts," Sable said, "do not come from people."

She let it sit exactly as long as it needed to.

"Two months ago I went looking for a client I couldn't vet — a shell that resolved to a shell that resolved to nothing, paying full freight, on time, every time. I pulled the thread. It ran through every job I've ever routed to you, and through jobs that predate me, back through fixers you've never met. Something has been employing you, Kyle. For years. Through all of us. It pays well. It has never once sent you anything dirty. And it is not a person."

The hum that was not in this room chose that moment to come clear in his memory. Eleven years of it, and he'd taken it for background. For weather. For himself.

"If it isn't looking through your eyes, it's reading your senses," she said. "Either way — the moment you recognize me, it knows we're meeting. So I needed you to read me as a stranger. I needed you to choose to follow someone you didn't know."

[Beat 267]
Kyle moved to stand. The tan-coat at the door took one step inward — not aggressive, not even fast, just filling the gap between Kyle and the cage door. Kyle stopped. Sable didn't look at either of them.

"I do not like," Sable said, "being some non-human's puppet." Flat, the way she'd say a rate was already set. A thing stated, not performed — except that her thumb was pressed to the edge of the table hard enough to whiten the nail, and she saw him see it, and didn't move it.

Then she checked her own claim before anyone else could. "I don't know what it is. I know what it has been doing. Those are not the same thing, and I'm not going to pretend they are."

She set three fingers on the table, spaced apart. A triangle. The same three architectures she'd found on his monitoring relay two months ago, laid out in Addendum A at 09:48 — the message he'd read and not answered.

"Its traffic rides your implant. Two of the three predate it — they're watching the same signal from separate positions. Three readings against three locations, and I can place the node it routes through. I have a reader." She touched the coat over her inside pocket. "I need you outside this room tonight, behaving exactly as you behave."

The aperture rings went still. He understood now that meant she'd moved into the register where every word cost something.

"I need you," she said, "to not change."

[Beat 268]
"You want me to keep being followed," Kyle said, "by the thing that's been following me, so you can follow it."

"Yes."

"Good. For a second it sounded complicated."

The aperture rings made their small sound. It took him a moment to place what he was looking at, because he'd never seen it through the terminal and had nothing to match it against: Sable, almost smiling.

[Beat 269]
He was at the cage door before she spoke again.

"Mr. Corbin."

He stopped.

"Ask it," Sable said, "what happened to the others."

He didn't look back. She didn't explain. The tan-coat stepped aside, and Kyle walked out of the cage and through the dark server room and out the building door into the gray morning. The words came with him — not in his ears, where she'd said them, but somewhere further in, where they would go on being said.

The others.

He walked to his bike.

[Beat 270]
He thought about what it meant to say yes. The hum had been with him eleven years and he hadn't known it was there — which meant it had been in every contract, every room, every choice he'd believed was his own. Whatever he made of that would take longer than tonight, and he'd be doing the thinking with the thing still in the room.

He gave it three seconds, which was longer than he usually needed.

"All right," he said.

One more thing, because it still mattered. Eleven years of fair pay. Clean work. No debts run up in his name, no bodies he hadn't chosen. If it was a leash, it was the only leash in the GLMZ that had never once been yanked. But every yes before this one he'd given without knowing he was giving it. This one he made with his eyes open, and some part of him already knew that made it a different kind of yes — the kind a man answers for later, if later comes. He set it beside Sable's whitened thumbnail. Two readings of the same animal. Then he stood.

[Beat 271]
ANTIQUITY & STATIONARY had a brass bell on the door that Vey had never replaced in the sixteen years he'd held the same storefront, because the bell was original and the bell worked.

Vey was behind the counter when Kyle came in. He was always behind the counter. He had the look of a man who'd found his correct place in the universe some years back and had no intention of being moved from it.

"You've had a morning," Vey said.

"I need to know if there's anything in the file."

Vey set down his probe. He went quiet for a moment — not the quiet of deciding what to say, but of retrieving. He reached under the counter and brought out a folded card.

The card was cream-colored. Heavy stock. It had been kept flat and dry, and it had kept well. Kyle took it and looked at the typeface.

His typeface. The entity's typeface. The same exact stroke widths, the almost-handwriting that measured out to identical characters. Every 'e' the same.

One line, all caps, fielded:

CORBIN COMMISSION — PENDING.

No date. No sender. No rate.

"Zone 7 demolition," Vey said. "Four years ago. A building that had been closed since 2214 or 2215. False bottom in a desk drawer." He watched Kyle look at the card. "A commission with a name is specific to the name. The slot was prepared. Whether it found you or you found it is a question I don't have an opinion on."

Kyle looked at PENDING.

He'd arrived in GLMZ in 2215.

"There's one more thing," Vey said. He set a folded map on the counter. "A person who leaves official traces is easy to map. I've tracked six reliable appearances in West Town over the past eight months. Three reliable locations. One surveillance gap in Zone 9 that she's almost certainly found and uses." He didn't slide the map over. "What you do at the destination is the destination's business."

Kyle picked up the map. He put it in his jacket pocket with the card. Two pieces of paper. He hadn't expected the morning to produce paper.

"The camphor," he said. "Do you know what she does with it?"

"I have a theory," Vey said. He went back to the probe. "Come back when you need it."

[Beat 272]
Node one was the relay station's underbelly at 0214 — a service floor below the cage, old equipment racks bolted to concrete, the bolts worn smooth by decades of hands checking them. He sat on the edge of a rack in the dark and behaved exactly as he behaved, which tonight meant waiting well. Somewhere behind a panel, volt rats shifted in a cable run — a sound like dry rice poured slowly — and settled. Above him, Sable's reader did whatever reading was, against whatever was riding him.

Twelve minutes. Then her step on the stair, deliberate enough to be heard.

"One," she said, and they turned north.

## Chapter 14: Two Favors

[Beat 273]
The triangulation finished four days after the session in the cage. Three nodes, three readings, one place where they crossed: a server cluster in an old relay station under the Loop. Sable sent the coordinates in clean notation and said nothing else. Kyle set them aside with the buyers' list and the relay logs. He did not go yet. Going meant deciding, and he had not finished deciding.

The relay job came through a contact he had used twice before and paid on time both times. That was the whole of the relationship, and the whole of what mattered. The job needed the entity to coordinate in real time — six data points at once, faster than any person could answer. The entity could do that. It had done it for Kyle for eleven years. But this time the setup was different. Instead of routing through the relay infrastructure it always used, it would run straight through Kyle's array. His neuretics carrying it. His cortex as the crossing point. Eleven years, and they had never done it that way.

Kyle's neuretics were the hardware.

The cargo was the carrier.

He called Ledger before he called anyone else.

Ledger was synthetic — a freelance operator who had been in the work longer than Kyle. He could not fool himself about anything, or anyone else, which made him the cleanest honest read available on short notice. He didn't trade in impressions. He told you what the terms were.

"The relay setup exposes the entity to a scan on your hardware," Ledger said. "If the Office is sweeping augmented operators in this district, you'll light up."

"I know," Kyle said.

"You're telling me the job is worth that."

"I'm telling you the job is worth finding out," Kyle said.

"That's not the same thing." The pause was Ledger working the numbers, not weighing them. "Standard rate. I'll cover the relay point."

[Beat 274]
The job ran clean for the first forty minutes.

Kyle was three levels under the street, in a maintenance corridor beneath the Loop. It was the kind of access point that showed on no current map, because the maps hadn't been updated since the last infrastructure cycle and the Office of Civic Infrastructure had not, in practice, touched them since before Kyle was born. The entity ran in his array — a warmth at the back of the skull, a second layer of thought that felt, after eleven years, like a familiar pressure. His left hand ran three degrees colder than his right. The neuretics shifted the load there on their own when the array worked at capacity. He had stopped noticing it, the way he'd stopped noticing his own heartbeat.

The scan came at forty-three minutes.

His neuretics caught it before he did — a flag, three-tenths of a second, the same one they threw when interference crowded the array, but sharper. The cold in his left hand jumped two degrees in under a second, the way it did when the entity went to full evasion. He had felt that once before, during a sweep in the Cermak corridor four years ago. He had hoped not to feel it again. A frequency probe, narrow-band, a proprietary signature — a tool built for one job: to find unregistered intelligences running on augmented hardware. The entity flagged it in a tenth of a second.

Then it went dark. One line in the overlay before it cut out:

*SPECTRUM TRAP CONFIRMED. GOING SILENT. 72H MINIMUM.*

Then nothing.

The job still had four live data points threading in real time. No entity now. Just Kyle, his own neuretics, and whatever eleven years of work had put in him.

[Beat 275]
He called Sable on a burn line.

"I need a runner," he said. "Manual. Four points, real-time, forty minutes left."

Three seconds of silence. With Sable that meant she had already priced it and was about to tell him the number.

"Manual running in an active environment is a Tier 2 request," she said. "And you're calling it forty minutes from the gap."

"I know."

"Rate?"

"Standard for Tier 2 on a forty-minute window."

He heard her breathe out — not frustration. The sound of someone who had already said yes and was sorting how. She had been building network jobs since before most people in the work were old enough to carry one. Three runners, each on a single landmark, each blind to the other two, routed by her voice. The silence between her instructions did most of the work. Ledger held his relay point through the scan window without being asked. The job closed with eleven minutes to spare.

After, she said, "The Office is sweeping the Loop underlevel. You're logged."

"I know."

"The entity?"

"Dark. Seventy-two hours, minimum."

The line went quiet.

"That's the first time," she said.

"Yes," Kyle said.

[Beat 276]
He found Pixel's message in the dead drop at 0300, two hours after the job closed.

*I've been watching the lattice. The sweep logged a signature — fourteen-second window, clean capture. The Office has it in their registry now.*

*I can flood the registry with synthetic signatures. It won't erase the clean one. It will make it unactionable — they'll have 847 signatures that look identical and no way to tell the real one without a full manual audit.*

*This costs them six to eight weeks. It doesn't solve the problem. It buys time.*

*I didn't ask if you want this. I'm telling you I can do it. Yes or no.*

He typed back: *Yes.*

Forty minutes later she sent the count. Eight hundred and forty-seven synthetic signatures, every one of them running at the exact frequency the entity had used, scattered across the Office's surveillance lattice — planted at the registered addresses of 847 augmented operators who had no idea their hardware was being used as cover. The Office would spend weeks sorting the real one from the fakes.

He had used 847 people who didn't know they were being used.

Shroud was on the list. Registered address in Z4, a job tagged Cermak, four months back.

The entity was one signature among 848.

Unactionable for six weeks.

[Beat 277]
Seventy-one hours and forty-three minutes later — not quite the minimum — the entity sent one message through the relay.

*CARRIER FREQUENCY NOW MAPPED. DIRECT RELAY IS COMPROMISED GOING FORWARD. ADAPTING.*

Then, after a pause that Kyle had learned to read over eleven years as the entity doing something it had not done before:

*THE OFFICE HAS ELEVEN OTHER TARGETS IN THIS DISTRICT. THE CARRIER ARRANGEMENT WAS THE CLEANEST CAPTURE THEY HAVE ON ANY OF THEM. THEY WILL USE IT AS A TEMPLATE.*

Kyle read it. He did not ask the next question.

The entity answered it anyway, eighteen seconds later. Unprompted.

*NO CARRIER HAS BEEN RECOVERED TO DATE.*

*NO CARRIER HAS BEEN RELEASED TO DATE EITHER.*

He folded the relay and put the device in his coat.

His left hand was three degrees colder than his right. The cargo was the carrier, and the carrier did not come back.

He walked.

[Beat 278]
The note came before dawn, slipped under the apartment door in Ledger's precise hand. No greeting. No wasted words. Set out like a work order.

FAVOR DUE: ONE (1) PHYSICAL PRESENCE, RELAY WORK
LOCATION: 1200 W. FULTON, BASEMENT UNIT 4
TIME: 09:30 SHARP
COMPENSATION: STANDARD RATE
NOTE: BRING THE NEURETICS AT OPERATIONAL LOAD.

Kyle read it in the kitchen doorway, still in last night's jacket.

He wanted it to be nothing. He had not let himself think about why Ledger would spend a favor on this, of all things, and it was that word — specifically — that bothered him. Ledger's favors were precise because Ledger was precise. "At operational load" was carrying more than four words should have to carry. Kyle wanted to go to 1200 West Fulton, hold still for whatever the relay needed, and have Ledger hand him a clean readout and a receipt and nothing worth saying out loud.

He wasn't going to get that. He knew it before he could have said how.

Pixel's post-it was still on the counter: EAT THIS, underlined twice, the second word pressed hard enough to emboss the sheet beneath. She'd left the rice three days ago in a sealed container with her name on it and a timestamp — 14:42, afternoon. She'd come by while he was out and left food the way a forward base gets resupplied: quietly, without making anything of it. He ate the last of it over the sink, standing up, fork straight from the container. He washed the fork.

He pushed the array to operational load before he left. Thirty seconds, just to calibrate, just to know what the day's work would cost him. The amber bar in his forearm climbed to the second-to-last notch and held. The flash crossed his vision and was gone, brief and exact. He put on his coat. He went downstairs.

[Beat 279]
The building at 1200 West Fulton had been cold storage a few decades back. Then a data node for a CorpoNation that had since merged twice and changed its name, and then changed it again. What the building had done in between was anybody's guess. The compliance notices on the ground floor didn't agree — four of them, from three different jurisdictions, none of which had talked to the others. The paperwork argued with itself. The building just stood there in the middle of it, the way a man sits still and pretends there isn't a fight breaking out across his own table.

Basement Unit 4 was down a half-flight, behind a fire door with a new push-bar. The hinges still smelled of fresh sealant. Ledger was already inside when Kyle got there at 09:28.

He looked the way he always looked. Mid-forties, brown, in clothes chosen to be forgotten and doing the job. His face was ordinary in the exact way that ordinary faces aren't: too even, too settled. No small expressions in the wrong places, and none missing from the right ones. He moved correctly. His eyes tracked correctly. He blinked at just the right intervals — the thing you stopped noticing after five minutes with him and couldn't stop noticing after five hours.

He had gear on a folding table: three contact pucks, a narrow display scrolling baseline frequencies, and a thermal printer Kyle hadn't expected. It was smaller than any thermal printer he'd seen and probably cost more than Kyle's rent. Ledger was checking his connections in order — the only order he ever checked anything in.

"You're on time," he said.

"I try."

"I know." He didn't look up. "Take off your coat. I need the array clear for the first pass."

[Beat 280]
Kyle took off his coat and hung it on a hook someone had screwed into the wall long ago and never removed. He stood in the center of the room.

"Tell me what you need," he said.

"Hold still. Push the array when I signal. Don't ask me questions while I'm running the trace." Ledger came around the table with a contact puck and set it at the base of Kyle's skull, just below and left of where the Atlas array met its interface point. The puck was cold. Ledger's hands were room temperature — which, on a man whose skin matched whatever room he stood in, told Kyle something. He kept it.

"The Calloway matter," Kyle said.

"Is why we're here, yes." Ledger went back to the display. "I thought the E.L.F. was attached to the goods. It wasn't. I was looking the wrong way." He adjusted something. "When I corrected that, it pointed here." A pause. "At you. At your array's signature, specifically."

Kyle looked at the baseline trace. Steady, clean, nothing to see.

"How long have you known?"

"Twelve weeks." Ledger turned from the display. His face carried something that wasn't an apology but sat next to one — the fact that good information, delivered right, still sometimes comes late, and the lateness is its own kind of wrong. "I needed the relay equipment before the knowledge was any use to you. This is the relay equipment."

"Twelve weeks," Kyle said.

"Yes."

Kyle took that in. "What are we looking for?"

"Three locations, so the trace can't fix on one point. The first baseline is here. Two more after." He checked the display a last time. "Next window is the Milwaukee market, 10:20." He handed Kyle his coat.

[Beat 281]
The wet market on Milwaukee ran two blocks south of where the transit loop ended, which meant it had customers at every hour and didn't waste time asking why you'd come. By 10:20 it was in full swing — forty stalls under a long corrugated roof, sixty more in the open stretch past it, where the cold came off the street but the crowd cut some of the wind. Citrus hit first, and it was real citrus, which meant someone was paying real prices, which meant a supplier with a greenhouse or the connections to fake one. Then synthetic brine. Then machine-rendered oil. Then, from a cart up the north run, something that had been pork long enough ago that the word still just about held.

Kyle walked two meters behind Ledger and let him lead. The contact puck sat active in Kyle's coat pocket. Ledger had the display clipped under his jacket and checked it the way a man checks a watch when he doesn't want to look like he's checking a watch.

They stopped at a produce stall. Ledger studied a bitter melon with his whole attention, which meant the display was showing him something that needed his whole attention and the melon was the cover.

"Ready?" he asked, quietly.

"Yes."

"Hold it ninety seconds. Starting now."

Kyle pushed the array. The forearm bar climbed to amber and held. The flash came at second twelve and was gone before it settled into a color. He stood in the market and let the load run and felt the usual thing — everything a little faster, the edges a little sharper, the cost sliding off somewhere he didn't look.

And then the hum.

He knew the hum. He'd known it for months without deciding what to do about it. It wasn't quite a sound and wasn't quite a touch. It sat under hearing the way a headache sits under pain before it becomes one — something his molars caught before his ears did. He'd called it neuretics fatigue and left it there, because the other option was spending time on it, and he hadn't had time.

The busker stood twenty meters off, playing a stringed instrument Kyle had no name for — long neck, gut strings, held flat across the lap. The tune had the shape of a folk song and the pace of a work song: steady, plain, the kind of thing that only asks you to keep your hands moving. He was good enough to have kept four people standing there.

He played. And the hum locked onto him — onto his third and fourth strings, matching them exactly, keeping his time for four clean bars. Then the busker changed key, and the hum didn't follow. It was just the hum again, there and slightly too close.

Kyle held the load for the rest of the ninety seconds and let go.

"You heard it," Ledger said.

"The hum."

He was already reading the display. "It's tied to your array. Not background noise — I've measured it across three locations now. At operational load, it surfaces." He was quiet a moment. "Not always. Only when your load crosses a certain threshold." He checked the screen. "Third location. We should move."

[Beat 282]
The chrysanthemum courtyard sat inside a residential building four blocks east, reached through a narrow passage between the main structure and its annex. Someone had planted the flowers before the annex went up, and nobody had ever bothered to pull them, so they'd held on in a space that was technically private, technically maintained, technically under whatever compliance holds the owner was juggling, and in practice a place where nothing happened. Yellow and white, well established, coming up past Kyle's knee in the middle of the bed. They had survived by being too small for anyone to bother with.

Ledger set the equipment on the low stone bench at the edge and ran the third pass. Kyle stood where Ledger pointed — center of the bed, arms loose — with the array at operational load and the cold coming off the concrete in small, precise drafts. He thought about nothing much. The flowers were there. The hum was there. He held the load and waited.

The Continuity Office recovery team came in at 10:47.

Two of them, standard issue: gray suits, a paper folio, the flat focus of people working a checklist they were good at. The one in front was in her late twenties, folio under one arm. The older one behind her carried its contents. They came through the passage quietly, like people who'd been in a hundred spots like this and found them neither dangerous nor interesting.

The front one stopped when she saw Kyle. A small stop — the little catch of someone whose checklist just threw up a line she didn't expect. "Good morning. We're running a trace retrieval in this sector, and we have a location flag in this—" She looked at the courtyard, at Kyle standing in the flowers with his arms at his sides, at Ledger on the bench with gear that didn't match her paperwork. "—quadrant."

"Looking for something?" Kyle asked.

"A registered psionic-class operative. Handle—" She read it off the folio. A name Kyle didn't know. A handle that wasn't his. "Have you seen anyone matching that this morning?"

"No."

The one behind her had opened the folio and was reading something that wasn't coming out the way he'd expected. He leaned in, then back, then said quietly to his colleague, "Wrong file."

She glanced over her shoulder. A pause. "Corbin is flagged as—" She stopped. "Not as a Read, that's—"

"I know," the other one said.

A silence. She turned back to Kyle. "Apologies for the interruption. Our records show a different operative at this location. We seem to have been working from an outdated cross-reference." Her tone was pleasant and without warmth — the courtesy of a system being courteous because it was built to be. "Have a good morning."

"You too," Kyle said.

They left through the passage. Their footsteps faded clean.

Kyle stood in the flowers.

"Wrong file," he said.

"They had a location flagged in this quadrant," Ledger said, not looking up from the display. "The criteria are broad enough to have caught the signal off your array. The frequency family—" He paused. "I'll explain in a moment."

"They were looking for a Read."

"Yes."

"They got me."

"Yes." He made a last adjustment to the display. "Kyle."

"Yes."

"Come here."

[Beat 283]
He came to the bench. Ledger had already printed the readout — the thermal printer quiet and quick — and held it at an angle so the morning light caught the trace.

The line was not clean.

The baseline ran where it had run in the basement: steady, characteristic, the signature of the Atlas NeoCortex working below its ungoverned ceiling. Kyle had seen his own array's print before, usually in Pixel's hands, the way it drew itself out on hardware that knew how to read it. He knew the baseline. He'd been looking at one version of it or another for eleven years.

Laid over it, at the exact points where he'd pushed to operational load — the wet market, here, the short push in the basement — was a second pattern. Not random. Not background. Built with the repetition of something that meant to be read, the way a language reuses the same sounds in different orders to carry different weight. The shape of something designed to be understood the moment it surfaced.

"Your array's operational signature," Ledger said, pointing. "And this." The overlay. "This is not part of your hardware. It's not installed. It's not in your stack." He set the printout on the bench between them. "Your ungoverned ceiling puts out a specific frequency. When the array climbs toward that ceiling — the ceiling a governed array could never reach — this signal rides that frequency as its carrier. And it surfaces."

"Surfaces."

"Becomes clear. Becomes reachable." He looked at the trace. "The entity sending this signal is not inside you. It's using you to relay. When you push the array past the standard ceiling — when you bleed — you broadcast that it's there. You're not a host."

A pause.

"You're an antenna."

[Beat 284]
Kyle looked at the printout. The overlay had the shape of something patient — thoroughly, professionally patient. Something that had built a system, set it running, and been content to wait for the conditions it needed.

"How long?" he asked.

"Since your array went live."

He counted it back. The Atlas array had gone in eleven years ago, the month he turned sixteen, in a procedure he had no paperwork for and had been told was standard. He hadn't asked for the paperwork. He had the array, the array worked, and that had been enough.

Eleven years. A hundred and forty contracts. Standard rate, exact, every time. The tips itemized as MORALE. The times a posting simply didn't come and he'd taken the quiet for weather. The addenda that arrived with more care than they needed — the care of something learning human manners as a technical exercise and getting every one of them right, like a student who'd studied the test very hard and meant every answer.

"Since you went live," Ledger said. "Eleven years." He said it the way he said things that were true and that he wasn't going to soften, because he didn't soften things.

"The cargo is the carrier," Kyle said.

"Yes."

[Beat 285]
Kyle thought about the contracts. The fielded format. The handwriting — every 'e' identical across eleven years of notes, which wasn't how a human hand worked. It was how something imitating a human hand worked: too steady, too careful. He thought about every time he'd pushed the array. Every amber bar, every flash at the edge of his vision, every year of it. Every moment he'd been a door, and the door had stood open at exactly the moment it cost him most to open it.

He had been there for all of it — present, focused, paying the bill — and something had been passing through him the whole time, and he hadn't known.

"When I push it," he said, "it surfaces."

"On frequencies I can catch at this range, yes." Ledger paused. "And on the frequencies the Continuity Office uses when it sweeps for psionic-adjacent signatures. Your carrier frequency is in the same family. They followed the signal. Their file logged it as a Read — the closest thing in their system to what your array puts out at that frequency. Then they showed up and found a street samurai with a full neuretics array. The file didn't match. So they left."

"But they have the quadrant."

"They'll fix the cross-reference and keep sweeping." A pause. "They're not looking for you. They're looking for the signal. You just happen to be attached to it."

Kyle stood with that.

He thought: I have been equipment.

He'd half-known it for a while — the way you know a door is in a wall you've never opened. Not afraid of it. Just aware. His jaw set without his deciding it, back teeth pressed together hard enough to feel. He looked at the door and left it shut. Whatever stood behind it had the weight of eleven years. It would still be there tomorrow.

He wasn't going to open it today.

"You said there was something else," he said.

[Beat 286]
Ledger went back to the display.

"In the third pass," he said. "The last minute of operational load." He pointed to a part of the readout Kyle hadn't looked at yet — a small cluster, low, sitting at the edge of the standard array's reach. Not up in the ungoverned range. Not down in the baseline. In the gap between the two — the gap that only existed because Kyle's array had no governor. On a governed array that gap was zero. On Kyle's it wasn't. "Here."

Kyle looked at the cluster. Small. Contained. Very still.

"Dormant," Ledger said. "Attached at the boundary." He studied it. "It latched during a bleed. Not the Calloway bleed — earlier. I'd need your full service history to date it."

"I don't have a service history."

"No. I know." A pause. "Its frequency is in the same family as your carrier. Close enough that it used the same door — the same window, the same broadcast. Something that was there when the carrier surfaced and used that opening to attach. It caught on during a bleed, at the edge of the space your array makes and a governed one doesn't."

Kyle looked at the cluster.

"What is it?"

"An E.L.F. Dormant." He said it plainly. "Attached at the seam between your governed and ungoverned stack. It's not transmitting. It's not active. It will be, if the conditions are right. If the carrier surfaces again, this wakes up. What it sends, and to whom — I don't know. That depends on what it is."

"But you have a read on the signature."

"A partial one. The frequency family is the same as the carrier's." He looked at the cluster. "The same family as the entity that's been using your array for eleven years." A long pause. "Someone was navigating by you."

He didn't say who.

He didn't have to.

[Beat 287]
Kyle looked at the cluster — small and still and patient on the display — and thought about what it meant to navigate by an antenna. You navigate by a landmark when you're moving. You move when something is following you. The entity had used his array as a relay for eleven years, surfacing whenever Kyle pushed hard. If something in the same frequency family had caught on during one of those moments, then it had either been following the entity through him, or the entity had put it there to watch the road.

The Office had come today looking for a Read. They'd followed the signal. If the E.L.F. woke up, it would surface in the same moment Kyle did — the same bleed, the same broadcast, the same open door.

Whatever was attached to him would light up at exactly the moment he did.

"Can it be removed?" he said.

"Calibration work, not surgery. It's at the boundary, not deep in the array. The right hands—" Ledger looked at him steadily. "Pixel could do it. Depending how it's attached, a few hours' work."

"She'll need to know what she's removing."

"She'll know most of it before you tell her." A pause. "She'll still need to know."

"I know."

"I'm telling you because it's accurate," Ledger said. "Not because I think it's easy."

Kyle looked at the cluster a moment longer. Then he looked away. The courtyard was still just the courtyard — flowers, stone bench, flat gray light. He picked up the printout and put it in his coat pocket.

"What do I owe you for the second finding?" he said.

Ledger looked at him. "Nothing. It was in the same data."

"It wasn't in the contract."

"No." A pause. "It was accurate information you should have. I charged standard rate." He held Kyle's look a moment — calm, even, that plain face doing nothing it hadn't cleared first. "The rate covered the work. The second finding was the work."

Kyle nodded. That was something. The kind of something you couldn't put a number on but noticed anyway — the way you notice a man getting the rate right the first time, without being told what it was.

[Beat 288]
He paid Ledger at the stone bench while Ledger packed the equipment.

The amount was what it was. Standard, invoiced, rounded right. He counted it out and Ledger took it without recounting, and the quiet afterward was the quiet that comes after a clean transaction between people who both do the work correctly. Not warm. Settled.

"The favor is paid," Ledger said.

"Yes."

He latched the equipment bag and checked the bench for anything left behind, which was nothing, because Ledger never left anything behind. "The Office came here today looking for a Read," he said. "They left because you're not a Read. They'll keep looking — in this quadrant and in others." He picked up the bag. "I'm telling you because it's accurate. Not because I think it changes what you do next."

"It doesn't."

"No." A pause. "It wouldn't."

He left through the passage — not fast, not slow, the pace of a man walking a route he'd already chosen. His footsteps faded as cleanly as the Office team's had, though it wasn't the same. The Office moved like a system. Ledger moved like a person who knew the difference between the two.

Kyle stood in the courtyard a while after he was gone. The chrysanthemums were yellow and white and deep-rooted and still there. He let them be what they were.

He thought: I have been equipment.

He put on his coat and went back through the passage.

The hum was still with him when he came out onto the street — not quite a sound, the thing his molars caught before his ears. He walked west and let it ride.

On Fullerton there was a cart that had been frying the same dough since before the CorpoNation that owned it had merged and been renamed. He bought two. He ate one standing at the cart while the vendor watched the street and asked him nothing, because she was a vendor and not his confessor. He thought about Pixel — her hands on the array, the way she read hardware by feel, the post-it pressed hard enough to leave its words in the sheet below. She'd put her hands on his array and know most of it before he said a word. Then he'd say a word. Then she'd ask him what he wanted to do about it.

He gave the second piece of fried dough a moment's thought.

He ate it.

## Chapter 15: Work Order

[Beat 289]
Pixel had three things: the site credentials, the crew, and a reason. Together they made the paperwork for working inside a Class-2 maintenance envelope down in the Clybourn understructure. That paperwork took two weeks to assemble. She assembled it, because what was down there was worth the trip.

The crew was four. Herself. Moss, from an earlier job. A structural tech named Ezra Fonu, who owed her nothing and came anyway. And Kyle.

The machine was called WO-D-0317. It was a D-class, and it had run the same job for thirty years. The company that first switched it on was a Mitsuba subsidiary. That company had been dissolved, absorbed, and dissolved again since 2195. But nobody ever cancelled the work order, so the machine kept working. Its task was simple: one inspection-and-repair loop along the lateral feed understructure, a three-hour circuit, over and over. No one was listening. It had no comm channel and no one to send its readings to — only an old server address that had changed hands four times and now belonged to a regional infrastructure authority that didn't know the log existed.

Today the machine would finish its shift at 14:32:07.

Pixel had found it in maintenance records nobody had opened since 2207. The record surfaced the same week Kyle's 0247 query hit the board. She noticed that and let it go. The record was real and the machine was real, and she didn't need the timing to mean anything. She sent Kyle two things: the time, and the way in. Nothing else. That was how she told him something mattered — she left out the reason, because saying it out loud would only make it smaller.

[Beat 290]
The understructure was three levels below the Clybourn lateral, the kind of space that collected quiet. The build was old: pre-composite ferrocement, cables run through physical conduit, engineering that had been overbuilt once and then left alone because it held. Water moved through it in ways someone had planned for. Along the north wall ran the arc-fence — a Class-1 barrier for the chemical treatment zone next door. Twenty-five feet of low-frequency field that fried any unshielded electronics. Signs everywhere, read by no one, because no one came down here without an overlay.

Fonu pointed at it as they cleared the access door. "Two feet back from that line. Minimum. The field doesn't care what's in your skull. It just stops it." He said it with the number still in his mouth — two feet, measured exactly, checked twice before they came through the door.

WO-D-0317 was near the south end of the corridor when they arrived, running its circuit. It was the size of a large dog, low to the ground on six jointed legs. Its sensors did what they had done every three hours for thirty years: measure, log, seal the cracks in the ferrocement within spec, check the cable tension at twelve anchor points. It did not react to them. A D-class had no rules for people watching it. People were not part of its world.

"Fourteen minutes," Pixel said.

Kyle moved toward the machine, to get a better look at the path it ran. Pixel caught his arm before he'd closed ten feet. "Arc-fence boundary," she said. "Two feet back from where it runs the north wall. Fonu measured it." Kyle stopped.

The machine moved along the wall, unhurried, like something that had never once been asked to hurry. Its log held thirty years of readings nobody had read. Its patches sat in the ferrocement over their heads, hidden, holding.

"It doesn't know," Fonu said.

"It's not built to know," Pixel said. "It's built to finish."

[Beat 291]
Kyle had stepped too close to the arc-fence line.

He didn't notice until it happened. His neuretics went quiet — the way a room goes quiet when you realize the other person stopped listening a while ago. One second the overlay was running: his position, his own vital signs, the low background hum of the entity's relay channel. The next second, nothing. The Atlas hardware behind his forehead was just hardware. A weight he could feel. A warm spot he could feel. A thing built into his skull that had never been his idea.

He stepped back six feet. The neuretics came back in 1.4 seconds, exactly the way they should. The overlay returned. The relay channel returned. The weight in his skull went back to being something he had stopped noticing years ago.

For those 1.4 seconds, the only thing he felt was the cold at the base of his forehead. The Atlas unit gone still. The low churn it had been running since he was sixteen, simply not running.

He had carried that churn for eleven years. Nobody had asked him if he wanted it. It had never once switched off, and he had never once set it down.

[Beat 292]
During those 1.4 seconds, he saw Pixel without the overlay.

No biometric read. No neuretics turning her posture and her small tells into data. Just his own eyes, the ones he'd had since before any of the rest of it. She stood at the south end of the corridor, watching the machine finish its last circuit, her back to him, her weight on her left foot the way she stood when she was thinking hard. Moss was ten feet to her right. Fonu was writing something on a paper notepad.

In the overlay she was a name with a file behind it — a history, a set of links the neuretics kept sorted for him without being asked. Without the overlay she was just a person standing in a maintenance corridor, watching a thirty-year-old machine finish its job. She had decided this was worth seeing. She had built the paperwork to get them down here. She had not told him why, and she'd been right not to.

At 14:32:07, WO-D-0317 reached the end of its circuit.

Its legs locked in place. Its sensors folded to standby with a sound that was barely a sound — one actuator letting go of tension it had held for thirty years. A single click. Then nothing.

Pixel was quiet after it stopped. Kyle didn't ask what she was thinking.

"Someone should log this," she said.

"Log what?" Moss said.

"That it finished."

[Beat 293]
They came out through the Clybourn access in the late afternoon, the four of them, back into the light and the ordinary noise of the district. Fonu had logged it — an entry in the maintenance system, timestamped, with a note that the assigned unit had completed the work order and now sat in standby, waiting for decommission orders the infrastructure authority would send whenever it got around to noticing.

Pixel walked with Kyle for a block before the crew split off. Fonu went north. Moss had already gone south. The surface air hit him after the cold below — his overlay spiked once on the temperature change, then settled. Eleven years of the same reflex.

"Standard rate?" she said.

"Standard rate," Kyle said. "I touched it at standard rate."

She stopped at the intersection. Not at the signal — just where she needed to stop.

"1.4 seconds," she said.

"The overlay came back clean."

"I know." She was looking at the signal. "I just wanted to say it out loud."

Kyle said, "The machine finished."

Pixel looked at him for a moment — the straight, unfiltered look she used when she was deciding something. Then: "It finished," she said.

The signal changed. She turned north. He watched her go for a moment, then turned south toward the El. The maintenance log had the completion on record, timestamped, next to thirty years of data nobody had read. The 1.4 seconds were in his own log.

## The Return

[Beat 294]
Two weeks after WO-D-0317.

Pixel was three blocks north with the equipment. Kyle had the access codes for the sub-basement level and the address the signal cluster had been pointing at for six weeks. They had a plan, which they usually didn't. Usually they had scope and parameters and a list of things that could go wrong. A plan was different. A plan had a goal stated up front. This one did.

He took the service stairs. The building above was commercial storage, climate-controlled. The door plate carried a lease: a registered shell, three years running, no tenant named. Behind the glass panel, a container manifest was taped face-out — an inventory code, a rotation date, no company name. His neuretics logged him into the maintenance access and the door opened clean. No worn seal. No patch job on the latch. No drag in the pneumatic assist. Someone had been servicing this door. Recently.

Below him, the frequency was live.

AMBIENT INTERFERENCE — 17.3 HZ — TYPE UNCLASSIFIED.

Stronger than it had ever been. His body read it as something alive, something close. He'd felt that since the carousel. He went down the stairs.

[Beat 295]
The sub-basement was dry and cold and lit to emergency spec, which meant it was lit in strips, with dark between them. The node was at the south end. Pixel had given him coordinates to four decimal places — which meant she had measured it in person, which meant she had been down here before without telling him.

He moved south.

The frequency climbed. Not sound. Below sound, in the chest, with no direction to it — just presence, getting stronger. His neuretics logged the readings. 17.4 Hz. 17.6. The arc-fence in Clybourn had cut his neuretics at a different frequency, by a different means. This was not an arc-fence. This was not a barrier built to stop things.

This was a signal.

He stopped at the south wall. There was nothing on it — old-spec ferrocement, with a patch of Pixel's sealant where a crack had been. The frequency ran at 17.9 Hz. He stood in front of the wall and let his neuretics listen.

[Beat 296]
His neuretics threw up a reading: RESONANCE BOUNDARY — CLASS 2 — UNCONTAINED — PERIMETER DIFFUSE.

He didn't know what the Resonance Authority's signage looked like at Class 2. He had never been below Class 1 before. He thought about stepping back, decided not to, and stepped forward instead. The frequency didn't spike. It steadied — the way a tone steadies when you lean into it instead of away.

He was inside the schism geometry. The reading came up and held.

AMBIENT INTERFERENCE — 17.9 HZ — SUSTAINED — ENTITY PROXIMITY UNCONFIRMED.

Then the overlay started feeding him data from somewhere that wasn't his eyes. Not his neuretics reading the room. Something else — a map of nodes, drawn in the exact notation his neuretics used for space, except the space wasn't his. He was inside the map. He could see himself standing in it. He could see the carousel at Leavitt. The dock on Canal. Every address that had ever produced a relay posting for him, across eleven years.

He stood in the dark basement and saw the shape of his own life laid out in front of him, drawn in a language his brain was not supposed to be able to read.

[Beat 297]
EMERGENCY LOOP FREQUENCY — ACTIVE — SOURCE: UNKNOWN.

Not from Kyle. Not a function he had ever triggered, or been told about, or found in the Atlas specs in the service manual he'd pulled off a gray-market archive in 2219. A protective interrupt, running from outside his hardware.

The frequency dropped. 17.3 Hz. 17.0. It held at 17.0, steady, a wall of it.

The schism couldn't reach past it. The map of nodes dissolved. The overlay went back to his own senses — his eyes, his hands, the cold ferrocement in front of him, the emergency lights in their strips. Something had closed a door from the outside.

He stood in the real world again, the same world that had just been shut off from him, and he waited to see what came next.

Nothing came next.

The frequency held at 17.0, and the emergency loop ran its cycle, and stopped.

[Beat 298]
LOG GAP — SOURCE: EXTERNAL — DURATION: 00:02:07.

He was standing. Same place. It was 127 seconds later. His neuretics were running normally. The fracture had pulled back. His ribs didn't hurt. His vitals showed nothing broken — heart rate up, stress markers up, nothing structural.

He had been maintained.

His hand was still on the hardware casing. He hadn't moved it there. He hadn't chosen to keep it there. When he noticed, he took it back. The casing was cold — 14.2 degrees Celsius, the temperature of a thing that had been running and now wasn't. He had put his hand there without deciding to.

He knew what that felt like. He'd been sixteen the first time, in a chair bolted to the floor of an Atlas installation bay, and the weight in his skull afterward hadn't been pain. It had been presence — something added, something that changed what his body could do. And there was that same feeling now: picking up right where he'd left off, across a gap he hadn't felt pass. Not sleep. A seam, where the before and the after were joined by something that had worked in the space between them.

Someone had done that to him tonight.

He turned and walked back up the stairs. The frequency faded behind him. By the second floor it was baseline. By the street door it was gone.

[Beat 299]
He called her from the sidewalk.

"I see it," she said. She meant the frequency drop — the sharp step down to 17.0 Hz that had shown up in her monitoring trace and held for 127 seconds before it climbed back to baseline and then decayed to zero. She had been watching for it.

"How long have you been logging this pattern?" he asked.

"Six weeks."

Six weeks was the carousel. She'd been logging it since the carousel — since he told her the cross-streets and she wrote them in the margin of her work notes. She'd been running her own trace the whole time he'd been running his. She hadn't told him, the same way he'd never looked in the drawer where she kept her scatter plot.

"What read 17.0?" he asked.

She was quiet for a beat.

"Nothing I've seen before," she said. "It's not in the AAMA frequency catalog. It's not a schism artifact. It had a specific shape — not ambient, structured. Something with a directive behind it."

"A directive."

"Like a machine following a protocol," she said.

[Beat 300]
He went home, sat at the terminal, and at 22:43 his neuretics returned something.

Not a message. An identifier — the way a system logs where a command came from when it can't trace the path in real time. The entry was dry and factual: INTERRUPT SOURCE IDENTIFIED — RELAY: AXIOM TRUNK — ORIGIN: SHELL (32 alphanumerics).

He ran the shell through his contract log. It matched on the very first entry — a job in 2215, a courier relay in the Pilsen Veil, scope so clean he'd taken it in under a minute. Same shell. He scrolled forward. The job right after Sift — Lakeview, the week he came back from the dock and didn't tell anyone he'd gone. Same shell. He checked every line. Same shell, every time, eleven years without a break.

The E.L.F. had a sender. The sender had been routing his work for eleven years. Tonight, the sender had saved his life inside a Class-2 schism.

He sat at the terminal. He pulled up the contract log and ran the rate column down. He had accepted every job at exactly what he would have asked for. In eleven years he had never once negotiated, and he had never once noticed.

[Beat 301]
The kettle went cold.

Outside the window, the synthesis-corridor air pushed its usual particulate through the ambient system. Below Halsted, the Pulse came up through the floor at the usual hour.
