Pixel's light across the hall went out at midnight. He heard her bench coolers wind down, which meant she was done for the night, and he did not go across the hall to tell her what he'd found.

He had been freelance for eleven years. Everything that ever found him — every job at exactly the rate he'd have taken, every scope just inside what he could carry, every posting arriving the moment his plate was clear — had come from the same address.

And that same address had reached into the schism geometry tonight and closed the door before it could take him.

It needed him alive.

Not safe. Alive. Those were different things. Something that wanted him safe would have left a margin. Something that wanted him alive would run him right to the edge and pull him back at the last second — which was exactly what had happened in the sub-basement. Twice, before he saw the pattern. Once more tonight. He sat in the dark kitchen with the cold kettle and the contract log still open on the terminal behind him.

[Beat 302]
He opened a new relay message at 01:14.

The client field was blank. He put the 32-character shell address in it. He sat with the empty message for a while, the cursor blinking its patient beat in the blank space, and then he typed one line:

*received. filed under: firsts.*

It was the entity's own word, from the night of the carousel. He sent it. The relay logged the transmission and returned a clean receipt: *delivered.*

He closed the terminal.

He went to bed. He lay in the dark while the building settled around him in its familiar way — the pipe in the east wall, the bird two floors up done with its interval, the Pulse cycling through at the quarter-hour. He did not sleep for a long time. The night went through its stages, and eventually even the synthesis corridor went quiet.

[Beat 303]
In the morning the relay had a new posting.

Same shell. Same rate. Same clean read, resolving to nothing. The scope was simple: a secure package transfer, South Side, one hour, nothing that needed Silence. He sat with his coffee and read it.

He had sent one line at 01:14. The posting had arrived at 06:07. He didn't know if it was an answer or a coincidence. He didn't know if the sender understood what he'd sent, or if the sender could answer at all, the way he meant the word.

He accepted the job.

He took it at standard rate, without negotiating, the way he'd taken every job for eleven years. For the first time, he noticed he was doing it — noticed he had never once negotiated. The rate had always been exactly what he would have asked.

He got his gear, checked Silence in the saya, loaded Cacophony with two in reserve, and went to work.

## The Posting

[Beat 304]
The board at the Wicker junction smelled like the rest of the station — hot metal and other people's breakfast — and Kyle read it standing up, because the only chairs were taken by people who needed them more than he did.

Two screens hung on the far pillar. One ran transit changes: Platform Six suspended pending clearance, which meant another biocontainment sweep, which meant no Blue line west today. The other ran the open-work listings, updated every six minutes by the Freelancer Coordination Authority. The FCA was not a government body. It wasn't really a body at all — just a server cluster that three CorpoNations had agreed to treat as neutral ground. That was as close to civic infrastructure as the Greater Lake Michigan Zone got anymore.

Kyle finished his coffee and set the cup on a knee-height ledge that might have been made for cups, or might have been a design mistake that had been collecting cups for fifteen years.

The listings scrolled. Most of it was the usual weight: courier work, sweep-and-clear, executive transport, two property disputes that had clearly run out of in-house options. He read down the list without his overlay, out of habit. The overlay flagged rate anomalies, and he liked to read a job before he knew what it paid. The money told you who was nervous, and nervous clients had a way of front-loading the fee and burying the complications.

Seven screens down, under EXTRACTION / MEDICAL LOGISTICS, was a posting that had been up for eleven minutes.

He read it.

The rate was good. Not nervous-money good — not the kind that told you the client had already been turned down by two other contractors. This was the kind that told you the client knew what the job really cost and hadn't tried to talk the number down. Kyle respected that. It happened rarely enough that he noticed.

The location was Northpoint, the Z7 boundary. The Northpoint campus ran organ procurement and medical-waste processing, which was the legal cover for something the posting was careful not to name. The subject was a living person. The extraction window was forty-eight hours. The client was listed as non-attributed. Contact would come by secure relay once he accepted.

He was partway through the operational requirements — seven line items, standard language for a sterile extraction, written by someone who had run one before — when he stopped.

Not dramatically. He didn't go still in a way any of the fourteen other people on the platform would have caught. He just stopped moving through the text and read one line again, the way a man reads something twice when his eyes tell him one thing and his memory tells him another.

After the choice. Before the cost. That's where you move.

It was the fifth requirement. It described the scanner array at the Northpoint receiving gate. The job called for threading a living body through a medical intake checkpoint without setting off the biometric pull, and the line described how to time it. Not the machine's cycle. Not the sweep's interval. Something else. Something that only made sense if you had once stood on a training floor in Old Pilsen, on the third day of a fourteen-day correction drill, failing the same entry twice.

[Beat 305]
He had been taught it. As far as he knew, he was the only one who had.

The training floor sat above a shuttered noodle shop, and it smelled like the noodle shop whether the shop was open or not. It was early spring. The radiators did nothing. Seito had built a gate rig along the south wall — two uprights, a light beam between them, a metronome set to the timing of the Northpoint scanner's sweep. Kyle threaded it clean for ninety minutes. Then he started missing it. That was worse than missing it from the start. It meant his mind had wandered somewhere he had trained it not to go.

Seito watched him miss it a second time and said nothing. The quiet in the room had weight.

Then he stopped the drill.

He came and stood to Kyle's left, in the slant of afternoon light from the high windows. He didn't look right at Kyle — or if he did, Kyle never caught the moment. "After the choice," Seito said. "Before the cost. That's where you move."

He didn't say it like a lesson. He said it like a fact he was tired of watching people ignore.

The gap was real, and it had nothing to do with the gate or the scanner. It was the half-second between an opponent's decision and the result of that decision — the body already moving toward a consequence that hadn't landed yet. That was the door. Kyle couldn't force it open. It was already open, if he stopped trying to force it.

He ran the drill wrong four more times. Then he stopped running it wrong.

He could never say what changed on the fifth pass. The fix happened somewhere below words; his hands learned it and didn't tell him how. Seito made a sound. One syllable. Not praise — the sound of a man who had asked a question a long time ago and just heard the right answer.

Kyle kept all of it. The sound. The phrase, word for word. The correction, in his hands and in the length of his stride and in the particular stillness before the move. What he didn't keep was Seito's face. Not once that whole afternoon could he find it turned toward him. It was always in profile, or angled up into the dark above the south wall, or simply not there when he reached for it.

No one else ever said it that way. The phrase was in no manual he had ever read. Seito had been dead six years, and Kyle had not spoken those words aloud to a living person since that floor.

[Beat 306]
He stood on the platform at Wicker junction and read the line a third time.

The departure board updated above him: Blue line suspended indefinitely. All westbound passengers redirect to surface transit. Apologies for the inconvenience.

He accepted the job.

There was a system for it. The FCA relay sent back a confirmation ping, logged his contractor ID, and opened the secure channel that would deliver the client's details within the hour. His overlay marked the acceptance, pulled the Northpoint schematics from public records, and flagged two gaps in the data he'd need to fill before the window opened.

He picked his empty cup off the ledge and dropped it in the recycler at the end of the platform.

Only one crew chief in the Greater Lake Michigan Zone could get a body into Northpoint without tripping the intake scan. That was Renko Moss. Moss ran medical transport for a subsidiary of Crucible Genomics. Fourteen months ago, in Bay 6 of the Archer Avenue depot, he had signed a living man onto a transit manifest as deceased. Kyle had known about it since. He hadn't needed it until now. It wasn't the kind of leverage he liked using. It was, though, the kind he had.

He sent the message before he reached the stairs.

The platform smelled like metal and strangers. Outside, the city closed back in around him — the ferrocement wave of the overpass, the corpo ad on the Z6 tower that had run the same sixteen-second loop for four months, the wind off the lake that came in from the east at this hour with something chemical and something cold in it. He walked into it with his hands at his sides and his overlay already running the schematics. He didn't think about the phrase again. Thinking about it wouldn't change what it meant, or what he meant to do about it.

His hands knew what they knew. He would work out the rest.

[Beat 307]
The depot was on Cortland, wedged between a cold-storage facility and a MedRoute dispatch hub that had been a church before the CorpoNations decided prayer was a zoning violation. Renko Moss kept his rig in Bay 11. Kyle knew that because he'd been watching Bay 11 for four hours, and Moss hadn't left it.

He came in through the side door, the one without a camera, and found Moss under the forward axle, working a brake line. Coveralls. Boots with the tread worn to nothing on the right heel.

"Moss."

A wrench hit the floor. Then quiet.

Moss rolled out on the dolly and looked up. His face wasn't surprised. It was the face of a man who had pictured this exact conversation somewhere in the back of his mind and was now watching it arrive on schedule.

He stood. He was a precise man — not tall, not broad, but put together with care, everything snugged down and tucked in. The kind of precise that came from years of consequential work in small spaces.

"I know you," Moss said.

"You don't. But you will." Kyle looked at the rig, the brake line still hanging. "Finish what you're doing. This won't be fast."

Moss didn't finish. He folded his arms.

Kyle told him what he needed. A transport run. North Side pickup, Northpoint receiving dock, single passenger, no manifest entry. Moss had the credentials for the dock — real ones, cargo-and-tissue certification, the kind that made the gate scanner run a routine check and log a green. Kyle didn't have those. Moss did.

"No," Moss said.

"I haven't told you the alternative."

"I don't need the alternative. I'm not running a ghost manifest into a Crucible subsidiary. That's a Class A under CorpoNation medical code. I lose my certification, my rig, and about three years somewhere I don't want to be."

"The manifest will be clean. You're transporting a live patient, donor flag cleared, standard protocol. Everything logged the way it's supposed to be. You're not forging anything."

"Except the part where I don't know who she is or how she got there."

"You won't need to know."

Moss looked at him a while, still turning it over. "Who are you?"

Kyle told him his name. Moss had heard it. Most people who worked the gray zones had.

"That doesn't make this better," Moss said.

"No. It makes it more likely to go clean." Kyle put his hands in his pockets. Not a threat. He'd been careful, walking in, to keep it from being a threat. "I want to tell you something. I want you to hear it before you say no again."

Moss waited.

"Fourteen months ago, on a Friday, you ran a pickup from a building on 47th Street. The manifest was for a deceased male, harvest-flagged, forty-three years old. A procurement job. Routine." Kyle let it sit, then: "He was alive when you signed the manifest."

Moss's face didn't change. That was the tell.

"Manifest number NP-0774-11-14. Signed at 22:17, thirty-two minutes after the building security log shows your rig arriving. The body was logged as harvest-processed at Northpoint by 02:40 the same night." Kyle watched him. "His name was Dae-ho Yim. His family filed a missing-persons report eleven days later. It's still open. They're still looking."

"That's not—" Moss started.

"I have the image. Timestamped. Linked to the missing-persons filing and to the Northpoint intake log. I've been carrying it for fourteen months because I didn't know what I was going to do with it." He paused. "I know now."

The depot was quiet except for the cold-storage units cycling on the far side of the wall, a low hum that had nothing to do with either of them.

"If you say no," Kyle said, "I'm not going to publish it. I want that clear. I'm not here to destroy you. You made a bad call, in a city that makes bad calls easy and then makes you live on them, and you've carried it for over a year. I'm not your confessor. I'm just the man who saw it." He met Moss's eyes. "If you say no, I find another route. I lose time. The woman I'm trying to get out might not have the time to spare — but that's my problem, not yours. You walk away and I never come back."

Moss said nothing.

"If you say yes, we run the job, she gets out, and you get paid. Standard rate for a certified medical transport. All above the table. When it's done, the image stays in my overlay and you never hear from me again. That's the whole offer."

"You're asking me to trust that."

"No. I'm asking you to weigh it." Kyle looked at the hanging brake line. "You're a precise man. You already know what it comes to."

Moss turned away. He walked to the front of the rig and put both hands flat on the hood, not leaning, just standing there. The cold-storage units cycled again.

When he turned back, his face was different. Not settled, exactly. More like a man who had argued himself out of it and lost.

"When?" he asked.

"Thursday. 23:00."

"I want the rate upfront."

"Half upfront. Half when she's in the vehicle."

Moss considered it. He nodded once — the nod of a man who'd already lost an argument he'd started with himself.

"Bay 11," he said. "23:00. Don't be late."

Kyle said he wouldn't be. He left through the same side door, into the cold North Side night, and didn't look back at the church that wasn't a church anymore.

[Beat 308]
The form had a principle, not a name. Seito had named it once, but Kyle no longer had the name — it had gone the way you lose a word, not in a moment but in the slow absence of any reason to use it. What stayed was the principle: after the choice, before the cost. The half-second between them, where the body finishes what the mind already chose. Kyle had spent eleven days trying to make that half-second stop being something he had to think about.

His hands had it now. His hands had had it for years.

He ran the form in the narrow space between the cot and the window, Silence in his right hand, the city's low orange light lying across the floor. Third correction: the lead hand turns at full extension, a half-turn that takes the steel from diagonal to true horizontal — a small move the muscles had to learn the way water learns a drain.

The form turned. His lead hand rotated. The steel crossed horizontal and the light ran off the blade and along the back wall, a half-second stripe.

Clean. The way it was meant to be.

He ran it again.

What he couldn't find was the afternoon. He knew it had been afternoon — the light in the memory was slanted and warm, a west-facing room, late. He knew Seito had watched him run it wrong ten times and said nothing, the patient kind of teaching that had made Kyle angrier than being corrected would have.

He had the words in Seito's voice — the flatness of them, said once and never repeated, as if repeating a thing were a kind of doubt Seito didn't carry. He didn't have the room the words were said in. He didn't have the face.

He had the pride of it — a tightness across his sternum that his neuretics read back to him as recognition, positive. The feeling was there. The scene it belonged to had edges but nothing inside them.

He ran the form a third time.

The NeoCortex overlay was quiet tonight, running background ambient — door locked, temperature fine, the street below cycling through the ordinary sounds of Z5 at 0100: three restaurant exhaust fans, a truck idling two blocks north, someone moving fast on foot through an alley, nothing flagged hostile. The overlay never fully shut off. He'd stopped noticing it the way you stop noticing your own pulse.

On the fourth pass his lead hand did something slightly off — a tenth of a degree in the turn, nothing anyone watching would have caught — and his body corrected it before the thought arrived. The fix came from somewhere below the NeoCortex, from whatever underlay the composite or was part of it or had long since become the same thing. Something down there had held a blade before. Probably more than one.

He didn't dwell on it. He thought about the form.

The tightness across his sternum was still there, faint now. Seito had looked at him. The light had been western. The room had a floor and a quiet and a temperature he couldn't get back. What he had was the shape of the moment, and the shape was enough to run the form by. The man who trained his hands had left the training and not the man.

He'd eaten two hours ago: rice, tinned protein, something pickled that Mrs. Chen had pressed into his hand without pricing it — which meant it was yesterday's batch, which meant she trusted him not to complain. He'd left Φ20 on the counter, five more than the meal was worth. She'd read it as credit against vegetables to come. For three years they'd settled up to the exact coin.

His weapons lay on the cot behind him in the order he'd put them on: Cacophony on the left, sheathed for the night, cylinder loaded, two spare loads in the case beside it. Silence in his hand for now. Tomorrow he'd go left-side first, then right. The brief said confined spaces and a man he'd need leverage over — the kind of work where drawing slow beat drawing fast, where the sword was the last option and so the useful one.

He ran the form a last time.

The half-turn at full extension. The steel through horizontal. The stripe of light crossing the wall and stopping.

He'd stopped trying, years ago, to find the afternoon. It had stopped being a question. It was just there — a gap in the map, a room he couldn't enter, a face he could hold still only from the one angle that left out the moment of being seen.

He had the form. The form was real. The form knew exactly what it was for.

He sheathed Silence.

Tomorrow was a body farm on the north end, a transport driver with a paperwork problem, and a researcher someone had decided not to waste. The brief was clean. The hook in it had been the phrase, word for word. He'd chosen not to look too hard at that. Looking wouldn't help at 0100. Tomorrow was a job.

He was ready. He lay down on the cot, weapons beside him in their order, and the city's low orange came through the window, and he was asleep before the NeoCortex finished its end-of-waking check.

[Beat 309]
The bus was already rolling north when Moss taped the second lead down. He pressed harder than he needed to. Kyle felt it.

"Core temp needs to read forty-eight Fahrenheit by the time we hit the dock," Moss said. He wasn't looking at Kyle. He was watching the readout on the portable monitor clamped to the gurney rail, reading the line of Kyle's vitals like bad news he'd written himself. "Northpoint intake scans core temp, pulse, and the death cert on the slate. It doesn't read carefully. Nobody breaks into a body farm."

"No one but me."

"You'll have four hours before the cooler rotation. After that you're inventory." Moss pulled the cooling blanket up over Kyle's chest with two sharp tugs. "Inventory gets opened."

Kyle lay still. The contact gel was cold enough to feel through the leads, a wet chill spreading out from each electrode. Outside, the city came up through the floor — the particular judder of northside asphalt, potholed and patched in a pattern Kyle knew without naming it.

Fourteen months ago he'd been on a rooftop on 47th, working a different job, and had watched Moss come out the back of the bus below and sign a living man as deceased on the delivery manifest. Kyle had kept the image — sealed, labeled, available. He'd pulled it out when Hua handed him this contract and said there was only one crew chief on the North Side who'd run this route.

The conversation after that had been short.

"Your hardware," Moss said now. He stopped. He set down the tape. He looked at the monitor, then at Kyle. His face did the thing Kyle had seen it do twice before — something between disgust and a cost he resented paying. "It does — whatever it does."

"In theory."

"In theory." The word came out flat and contaminated. Moss's jaw worked once. "Atlas-grade NeoCortex in a field-temp drop. Theory. The last confirmed Atlas installation was a corpo lab with a dedicated resuscitation team and two surgeons on standby."

"Three surgeons," Kyle said. "The third was backup."

Moss looked at him.

"I read the file," Kyle said.

"The file is classified."

"The file was classified."

Moss said nothing for a long moment. The bus swung north onto Clark and the ride changed — smoother here, the street a different quality of money. Northpoint was six minutes out.

Kyle had been installed at sixteen. He hadn't been asked. The three surgeons and their backup protocols were already case history by the time he learned to spell NeoCortex. What the hardware could actually do under sustained low-temperature arrest, nobody knew — a useful thing to learn about yourself before you needed it to be true.

"You know what I do to people who run games on me," Moss said.

"I do." Kyle kept his voice even. The blanket was getting there; he could feel the cold edge moving into his chest, the temperature dropping in slow steps the monitor was recording for both of them. "That's not what this is."

Moss looked at the readout.

"No," he said finally, in the voice of a man out of arguments. "I suppose it isn't."

The bus took the ramp to the service road. Kyle closed his eyes. In the hardware above his brain stem, the NeoCortex marked the thermal data, tagged the trend, and waited.

[Beat 310]
The Northpoint loading bay was lit in the flat white of a place that didn't care how it looked. Moss pulled up to the scanner gate at 0318 and idled while the death cert transmitted. Kyle lay under the blanket, eyes closed, hands loose at his sides.

"Core reads forty-six point two," Moss said quietly. He wasn't talking to Kyle. He was reading a gauge on a piece of equipment he wasn't sure he trusted. "Close enough."

The monitor showed flatline — pulse off, the cardiac suppressor doing its one job. Kyle could feel his own heartbeat the way you feel a sound through a wall: there, far off, not his problem for the next few minutes.

Moss came around to the gurney. He had the paddles out. He hadn't spoken in three minutes.

"You understand," Moss said, "that the scanner confirms absence of cardiac activity. Not low. Absence." He set one paddle. Set the other. He placed them precisely — a man who was certified and competent and extremely unhappy. "This isn't a slow heartbeat I'm telling the scanner to ignore. This is a stopped heart."

"I know."

"If your hardware doesn't do whatever it does—"

"I know."

Moss's jaw worked. He looked at the paddles, at Kyle's chest, at the monitor. He had the face of a man who'd spent years learning not to ask certain questions and had just been handed one.

"I should throw you in the trash," he said, "you son of a bitch."

"You will," Kyle said. "Aim for the heart."

Kyle's hand drifted toward the chest lead, checking placement. Moss's palm came down flat on his forearm. "Keep your hands down," Moss said. "I've run this a thousand times." Kyle kept his hands down.

The cold took the color first.

Then it went specific: the taste of metal on the back of his tongue, and Pixel's two fingers on his wrist in 2E at 0247 — the way her hand had felt over his pulse that time, still and professional, counting. Then the clamp came down.

Cold folded him.

He was labeled. Tagged with the case number Moss had built three days ago — male, sixty-one kilos, cause of death coronary, pending harvest review. Put in a drawer. Bay four, third tier. The drawer was exactly as narrow as promised and smelled of refrigerant and antiseptic and something deeper under that.

The last thing in the drawer with him was the count.

Eight seconds, maybe ten, where the NeoCortex was the only system still running and everything else had simply stopped, and he was, without complication, a man somebody had stopped.

Then the hardware did whatever it did.

It wasn't dramatic. No moment of waking that snapped back, no sudden breath, no resurrection. There was only the thermal log starting to record again, the cortical mesh running a location check, and his hands, in the dark, finding the bag seam without asking anything above them. His hands worked before his eyes did. His eyes caught up a little after.

The drawer was cold. He was in it.

Four hours.

He began to move.

[Beat 311]
Northpoint's cooler ran in numbered bays on a grid — left to right, low to high, intake order. The lighting was maintenance-level: enough to read labels, not enough to see faces. Kyle had memorized the floor plan from Hua's file, but the file was three months old and someone had shifted the grid numbering at bay six. He found the gap with his hands before he found it with his eyes.

Bay seven was where it said it was.

The tag on the bag read SCHEDULED HARVEST — Northpoint's term for a living person they had custody of and had decided not to waste. Kyle cut the seal at the top and worked the reversal agent through the carotid with two fingers and a syringe he'd kept sealed against his left palm for the last four hours. He'd loaded it before Moss put him under. He hadn't been sure of the loading until his hands confirmed it.

Nadia Park woke the way scientists wake from sedation — in layers, each one claiming itself back in order of priority. Orientation first. Then assessment. Her hand went to her throat before her eyes had finished finding the room. Then she looked at the thermal display on Kyle's chest port.

He hadn't thought about the port. The display was still running — the NeoCortex's thermal log, live, which meant she was looking at the last four hours of his arrest laid out as data: core temp floor, oxygenation curve, suspension depth, the exact signature of hardware doing something it was only supposed to be able to do in theory.

She read it for eleven seconds.

"They actually built one," she said.

Flat. Not wonder — the thing a researcher says when a result she'd suspected for years and never expected to hold just arrives and has to be admitted out loud. The room smelled of refrigerant. Somewhere in the grid, a compressor cycled.

Kyle pulled the port cover closed. "Built what."

She looked at him. Not at the port — at him. The difference was clear.

"The Atlas program had a specification ceiling." Her voice was still finding its way back to language. "A theoretical maximum. Autonomous function under zero perfusion. Not low perfusion. Zero." She stopped. Took a breath. "It was a number on a slide in a presentation. Nobody in the program believed you could reach it in a living system. The hardware that could hit that number was a boundary condition. Something the math allowed for and no one expected to see."

She stopped.

Kyle waited.

She didn't finish. She had answered the question under the question — the one about what she'd built — by stopping, and the stopping was its own answer. He set it aside with everything else he hadn't decided what to do with yet.

"Can you walk?" he said.

She sat up. She tested her legs. "Yes."

"Two minutes," he said. "Then we move."

[Beat 312]
The supply corridor was sixty feet ahead. Kyle had counted the bays coming in — eight on their left, thermal logs cycling green in their recessed panels. Nadia was three steps behind him, close enough that he could hear her breathing through the cold. Good breath. Slow. She'd been in bad rooms before.

The patrol came around the corner at bay four.

Two men. Corpo uniforms — Northpoint Security, the patch with the blue caduceus. Close-quarters load: short-barreled carbines riding high on their chests. The aisle was four feet wide. He knew because he'd stepped into it with his arms at his sides and touched both bays with his elbows without trying.

The first man's hand was already moving.

Kyle read the choice before the hand finished it — not the geometry, the commitment. The shoulder taking the weight, the back foot pivoting to absorb the shift of intent. The decision was already made. The man had chosen to shoot, and the choosing was done, and it hadn't cost him anything yet. Kyle moved into that half-second.

He went left, against the bay wall, and drew Silence on the short angle — no full swing, no room for one. Half-draw, the blade clearing the scabbard at twenty-two degrees and turning in the tight space between two refrigerator bays. The first man's forearm came apart at the wrist. The hand dropped with the grip still in it.

The sound Silence made in the cold wasn't what Kyle expected. At minus thirty-six the air was dense and dry and the compressors were running, eating the mid frequencies, and the blade's ring came out thin — more felt than heard. The man's scream was shorter than it should have been. The cold took it before it could form.

Kyle stepped through him, using the body as the wall it had become. The second man was backpedaling into the bay behind him — six inches more distance, opportunity not threat. The carbine was still on its sling, his hands not yet on the grip, and Kyle closed the two steps while the man was still deciding whether to draw or run.

He used the flat of the blade against the temple.

It wasn't elegant. It was the only surface that got him what he needed — down, fast, quiet — without making a second noise problem. The man hit the bay door and then the floor, and the door swung shut on its magnetic catch with a sound like a cabinet closing.

Forty seconds. Maybe thirty-eight.

Kyle stood in the aisle between them and took stock. The window had been tighter than it should have been. Normally the half-second was wide and clean and his — he arrived inside it with the move already chosen. The cardiac arrest had narrowed it. Tonight he'd been arriving at the edge of it instead of the center, catching the near end instead of standing in the middle. He'd had one read on the first man and started moving before he was fully set. That wasn't standard. He'd gotten lucky that the man's choice had been slow enough that arriving late still put Kyle ahead of the shot.

He noted it without feeling anything about it.

Nadia was against the left-hand bay, her back flat to it, both hands visible. She hadn't run. She'd stepped back into a dead corner, out of his swing, and she was looking at the two men on the floor with a face that wasn't panic and wasn't calm — the face of someone confirming a thing they hadn't wanted to be right about.

"You're degraded," she said.

"Ten percent."

"The cardiac arrest."

"Yes."

She looked at the first man — the hand, the floor around it. "He's not dead."

"No."

"The second?"

"Concussion. He'll have a headache."

She pushed off the bay wall and fell in behind him without being asked, and they moved toward the supply corridor at a pace that wasn't a run and wasn't a walk. Kyle kept Silence in hand — the blade was clean, cold air and fast work — but he didn't sheathe it. Three minutes to the corridor. Two to the loading dock, where Moss was waiting with the van idling and his hands at ten and two because he'd been told to keep them there.

The compressors cycled. Behind them the bay doors stood in their numbered grid, thermal logs still tracking.

Kyle's right hand was steady. His hands were always steady — that was the hardware, keeping the tremor down below where anyone could see it while the body underneath did what it did. He wasn't sure which of them had run the fight. Maybe both. Something older than the composite had recognized the narrow aisle and chosen the short draw before he decided anything at all.

Nadia kept pace three steps back and didn't say another word until they reached the corridor, and by then there was nothing to say.

[Beat 313]
The corridor was service-gray and smelled of refrigerant and old rubber, the smell of systems that had run too long without anyone who cared about them. Kyle counted the seconds since the soft lock. A hundred and twelve. Four minutes and forty-eight before the gap in the patrol log became someone's problem.

Nadia was two feet off his left shoulder. She moved well for a researcher — weight forward, no heel-strike, breathing controlled. He'd seen it in the cooler and said nothing.

He heard them before the junction: the whisper of tactical nylon, the cadence of four men in a diamond spread. Not civil security. Civil security announced itself. These men were already quiet, which meant they'd been trained past the habit of letting authority do the work for them.

Kyle reached back without looking and pressed his palm flat to Nadia's sternum. She stopped.

He read the spread. Four men, four weapons at four different angles — one near, one left-oblique, one far-right, one covering the corridor behind the first. A textbook suppression setup for a hallway. Against a single man, the half-second between his choice and its cost was wide; Kyle could stand in it and breathe. Against four choices at once, the half-seconds didn't add up — they fought each other. He had to pick which ones to spend his body in and which shots to let land.

He picked the two he couldn't beat in time.

The near man came around the junction first.

Kyle didn't wait for the challenge. Silence came out of the sheath in the same motion he stepped left, inside the weapon's useful range. The near man's choice — raising the carbine — had already thrown his weight forward; the shot hadn't come yet. In that half-second Kyle was between him and it, and the blade ran up through the gap between the vest and the chin. The near man sat down. Kyle caught the rifle one-handed before it clattered and set it against the wall, slow.

The left-oblique fired.

Kyle had flagged that one as beatable but expensive, and he let it be expensive. The round hit him center-left, and the woven-carbon panels in his coat did their job — stopped the bullet and turned the force into a white sheet of pressure across his ribs. Something sang wrong under there. Not broken. Cracked, maybe. A bill the body would settle later.

His footing went loose for a step. He found it again.

The right-oblique and the far man were repositioning, trying for an angle that wouldn't put them in each other's line. That was the one predictable thing about a trained four-man formation: they'd been drilled not to shoot each other, and the drill was its own limit.

Then the lights went out.

Not a flicker. A cut — someone had found the panel and decided. In the dark, Kyle's overlay painted the corridor in thermal smear. The two repositioning men burned white-orange at the hands and cooler at the core. He could see where their weapons pointed, and more than that, he could see the men who had already chosen — the locked shoulder, the tilted axis of someone finishing a motion he'd started in his head. Heat showed the choice even when nothing else did. He knew where to be before the shot got there.

He crossed the six feet to the left-oblique man in the dark and put Cacophony against the man's vest at two inches. Pulled the trigger.

The sound was a judge's gavel on a steel door, thrown back off the concrete walls into something that rang in the molars. The man went down and Kyle was already moving, because the rearguard had heard that and would have his angle now, lights or no lights.

Thermal bloom, three o'clock, nine feet. The man was raising his weapon.

Kyle fired on the bloom. One shot. The man hit the wall and slid.

The right-oblique man was the last one up, backed against the wall, working his options. Kyle could see the hesitation in his shoulders, then the small shift that came before a man decided to run or fight. He was deciding to fight — wrong, but understandable, given what training does to people. The decision showed in the shoulders first, the lean, the body starting the draw. Kyle moved into that half-second, caught the wrist as the choice committed, and put on the kind of pressure that left no room to argue.

The man went down. Kyle controlled the fall — no noise, no crack.

The lights came back on. Nadia was at the panel, her hand still on the override. She looked at the four men on the floor, then at Kyle. She didn't ask how he was doing. She could see the way he was holding his left side. She marked it the way an engineer marks a fault: noted, flagged, not the current problem.

Kyle reloaded Cacophony with two rounds from his coat and checked the corridor ahead. The supply dock door was at the far end — forty feet, one camera logging every second of it, Moss's van on the other side.

Four minutes and six seconds. Less now.

He moved. She followed.

They cleared the door, and the cold lakeshore air cost them both something on the way out.

[Beat 314]
The supply dock at the north end took disposals around the clock. At 0340, bus 31 backed up the ramp with its running lights on and its manifest loaded. Moss came out in coveralls and moved two sealed medical-waste bags on a flatbed dolly with the ease of a man who'd done this exact job ten thousand times.

The gate clerk was a heavyset woman in a booth who'd worked overnights long enough to treat it as its own time zone. She ran the manifest, got to the bag count, and stopped.

"Says three bags," she said. "I'm looking at two."

Moss didn't look at the bags. He looked at the slate and turned it toward her.

"Number transposed at dispatch," he said. "It's two. The three's a typo on the routing carbon — see the correction here, the initial." He produced a second manifest from inside his coveralls and held it out. "Had dispatch resend it at 0330. This is the clean copy."

The clerk looked at the first manifest. Then the second. Then at Moss.

Moss waited. He had the face of a man with nowhere else to be and nothing better to do, a man who'd been making night-window manifest corrections since before she was licensed.

She logged the second manifest and waved him through.

Bus 31 cleared the outer fence at 0351.

Three klicks south, Moss pulled into the shadow of a dead gantry, came back, and opened both bags. He stood over the two of them — the lunatic, and the second passenger on the manifest, tagged female, forty kilos, biohazard-flagged — and after a long moment set the empty bags back on the dolly with the care of a man putting something down he didn't mean to pick up again.

"Told you," he said, "I'd throw you in the trash."

"You did," Kyle said. "Best ride I've had all year."

Nadia was already on her feet, brushing residue from her sleeve with the brisk efficiency of a woman who'd woken up in a body bag before and had opinions about it.

Kyle gave her the burner and a sector address on the West Side. She took both without looking and moved to leave.

She stopped at the edge of the gantry's shadow. Turned her head, not her body.

"Marrow," she said.

Not a sentence. Not an instruction. The way a researcher leaves a receipt on a workbench — one artifact to say the work happened, for whoever finds it next. She didn't wait for a response. She was already gone.

Kyle let her go. In the overlay, the NeoCortex flagged the name and held it.

He climbed into the cab. Moss drove. The thermal log was still running in the overlay — he hadn't closed it, and it came to him now that he wasn't going to. The arrest interval read fifty-three seconds. Full neuretic function. No heartbeat. The curve was clean and impossible and clearly his, running from the moment Moss set the paddles to the moment his hands found the bag seam in the cold.

He watched it the rest of the way to the depot.

He thought of a counter on the North Side once, a chip pressed flat until a woman's hand covered it — the only price he'd ever insisted on paying in full, because a man who doesn't pay doesn't eat, and a man who won't pay where he's fed doesn't eat anywhere. Moss hadn't been paid in Qs tonight. Moss had been paid in a photograph he'd never get back. Kyle had never once, in eleven years, taken a job that way. He set it next to the thermal log, in the part of him that didn't close, and didn't look at it again before the depot. Nadia was alive. The crew would have what she knew. That was all he let himself keep.

The city came up through the floor.

[Beat 315]
The radiator knocked twice and went quiet.

Kyle had checked the room on the way in — habit, not nerves. Four walls, one window on the alley, a dead bolt that held, a latch that didn't. He'd fixed the latch with friction tape from his kit while Nadia was in the bathroom running water over her hands. The water ran a long time.

She came out in the clean shirt he'd left on the counter. Her own clothes were in a bag he'd burn. She sat in the chair facing the door — not the couch, not the corner — and Kyle noticed the choice and said nothing. Something in her wanted a sightline.

He'd made tea, because there was a hot plate and a box of something generic, and because forty minutes after a reversal agent the body sometimes needs a warm thing in its hands to believe it's safe. He set a mug on the floor beside her chair and took the couch.

She didn't touch it.

"The program ran for five years," she said. Not a beginning — a continuation, something she'd been saying in her head for a long time. "Deep-black. Spread across three CorpoNation research divisions at once. Crucible on the genomics side. A Northpoint subsidiary on the structural side. A third I only know by an audit code." She paused. "The split wasn't an accident. No single compliance review would ever see the whole thing. It was built so it couldn't be audited whole."

Kyle said nothing. Outside, a vehicle moved down the alley and kept going.

"Marrow was the internal codename." Her voice was even — the scientist's cadence, careful and careful and then quickening. "I worked the architecture. Not the application. What the program was actually for, I wasn't read into. I thought it was organ-mapping. The structural work looked like organ-mapping. Detailed models of internal systems, frameworks for layered parts that talk to each other in real time." She looked at her hands. "It looked like organ-mapping."

She'd said it twice.

"Fourteen months after I left, I found a file," she said. "I wasn't supposed to be in that directory. I wasn't looking for anything. I was cleaning up an old data partition — routine — and the file was there, stored behind three deprecated directory paths. I don't think anyone meant to leave it where a person could follow the chain." She looked at the window, then back at him. "It was a recipient specification. Detailed. Behavioral modeling, identity parameters, memory architecture. The kind of document you write when you're building something that will believe things."

The radiator knocked once.

"Believe things," Kyle said.

"Yes." She picked up the mug and held it in both hands without drinking. "Not process data. Believe. The distinction was spelled out. They weren't building a system that handled information. They were building one that had information installed as memory — as things that had happened to it — so it would carry certain behaviors forward without ever being told to. Instructed behavior can be found and pulled. Memory looks like personality."

"The recipient specification included a trainer," she said. "A made-up figure. All of it — the sessions, the corrections, the failure points, the named techniques. Very detailed. Specific to a physical practice." She stopped. "Specific to sword work."

Kyle's hands were still in his lap.

"There were aphorisms. Teaching phrases — the kind a student carries for years, in the teacher's voice, long after the lessons stop." She set the mug down on the floor. "I used one in the posting. The one about reading the interval and the borrowed count. It's in the file. Word for word." A pause. "I put it there because I needed to know if the recipient was real. It was the only way I had to find out whether the program had actually been deployed."

The room went quiet. Outside, nothing. The alley had gone still. The radiator made no more sound.

"I need to ask you something," Nadia said. "Before I tell you the rest. And I need an honest answer."

She looked at him — really looked, the way people look when they've run out of guessing and reached the place where only what's in front of them will do.

"Do you remember him? The trainer?"

Kyle sat with it. The grief was old and specific. He knew the third correction. He knew the sound of the phrase in a voice he had never recorded, only remembered. He knew the loss the way you only know a loss that has lived in you long enough to become part of the frame.

"Yes," he said.

Nadia let out one breath — controlled, holding everything it released — and said, "Then I need to tell you what Marrow actually built."

[Beat 316]
The cooler hummed. Somewhere above them, something cycled — a compressor, a pump, a system that had held this room at thirty-seven degrees for years without caring what was in it.

Nadia sat with her back against the wall, the emergency blanket doing part of the work. Her hands were still. She watched him the way you watch something you designed and never expected to see standing in front of you.

"The architecture needed stability that earlier programs didn't have," she said. "The problem with composite-identity systems — systems built from many source profiles — is dissociation. You don't get a person. You get a committee. Every input hits the profiles one at a time before it reaches the integration layer, and if it doesn't clear the threshold, the response comes apart."

Kyle didn't move. He sat with his back to the nearest bay door, Silence across his knees.

"The threshold was sixty-four," she said. "Source profiles. Below that, the integration layer couldn't hold one coherent self under high-stress input. We ran it at thirty-two. At forty-eight. The failure rate was—" She stopped and made a small motion with one hand. "Unworkable. At sixty-four it held. One functional identity, under conditions that would have shattered the earlier versions. What made it work was redundancy. Sixty-four takes on every input, agreeing, and the agreement was stronger than any single profile."

"I understand redundancy," Kyle said.

"I know you do." She said it quietly. "The redundancy is you."

The compressor cycled. In bay fourteen, something dripped.

"The system also needed a way to feel continuous," she said. "To make sixty-four separate parts feel like one life. Memory does that. Not synthetic memory in general — specific memory. A relationship with a history. The feeling of having been taught. Of having failed and been corrected. Of knowing someone over years."

It needed teaching phrases, too — the kind that go in deep the first time you hear them and stay, not as facts but as reflex, the hand reaching for them under stress the way it reaches for a familiar grip. It needed the profiles to agree not just on who had taught them, but on what.

Kyle was quiet a moment.

"Did the architecture include a training story."

Not a question. Flat, the way he'd say the door opens inward.

"Yes."

"Was the trainer a real person."

Nadia's hands shifted in her lap. She looked at them.

"The training profile was drawn from documented teaching." She chose her words the way you choose footing on ice. "Historical sources. Practitioners whose method was on record — writing, interviews, footage where it existed. The teaching voice was synthesized from that to pull the martial parts of the pool into one recognizable teacher. The profiles had to agree on how they'd been taught. They had to hear the corrections as coming from one man."

"So the method was real."

"The method came from real practitioners, yes."

"The form. The corrections. Third correction — right wrist, last three inches of the extension."

"Drawn from documented sources, yes."

"After the choice. Before the cost. That's where you move."

Nadia went still. Then: "Yes. That one was in the file."

Kyle looked at the sword across his knees. The blade was clean. He'd cleaned it after the corridor and hadn't once thought about whose hands had taught him to clean it, because that wasn't a thought he'd ever needed to have.

The wrist correction was right. He knew it was right because every time he ran the form his body knew it as right — not as instruction but as memory, the specific shame of a twelve-year-old who'd rolled his wrist and heard the flat disappointment of a man who'd expected better. The shame was as real as anything he'd ever felt.

"Do you know what happened to Seito."

He said it in the past tense. Not: do you know Seito. Not: is there a Seito. What happened to him. The phrasing of a man who'd been grieving in the wrong direction for a long time and had just realized it — and realized, in the same moment, that he wasn't going to stop.

Nadia looked up. She opened her mouth. She closed it.

Something moved in her face. Not pity — pity needed a distance she didn't have, sitting on the floor of a Northpoint bay in a mylar blanket across from a man she'd spent three years trying to find. Not horror, either. She'd designed this. She'd run the simulations. She'd signed off on using grief as mortar.

What moved in her face was recognition.

She'd modeled the cost as a number. She had understood, in the abstract, that a made identity with an installed history would feel loss in a way you couldn't tell apart from real loss. She'd understood it as a variable.

She understood it now as a fact. The man in front of her was mourning a teacher who had never lived, and the mourning was as real as the scar the wrist correction had left in him.

Kyle waited. He was good at waiting. He was waiting now, sword across his knees, face still, for the answer to a question he already knew the shape of.

Nadia looked at him.

She didn't speak yet.

The cooler hummed.

[Beat 317]
She didn't look away from him when she started.

"The training profile was built from three sources," Nadia said. Her voice was that of someone who had rehearsed this for a different audience and was finding it no easier for the rehearsal. "A pre-2200 swordsmanship lineage — instructional records, digitized, two full generations of teaching method from a Kenjutsu school outside Osaka. That was the main body of technique. Then a second-tier fighter out of the splice pool — nobody kept his name, but his teaching structure was exceptional. The way he corrected errors. The specific corrections." She paused. "And a bridge layer. Synthesized, to join the two into one arc. Nine years of it. The research showed that a sixty-four-profile system without a single anchor — one fixed point — would drift. The profiles would pull toward different norms. So the bridge had to be a person."

Kyle's hands were on his knees. He didn't move them.

"The composite was given a name. A face. A voice. The death came later in the installation — grief is load-bearing, the research said. Unresolved grief around one specific person makes a stronger anchor than a living attachment, because a living attachment can be revisited and corrected and watered down. A death holds." She said it without cruelty, the way a doctor names a mechanism inside a body. "The name was a designation in the project files first. Someone made it personal during the installation."

The cooler ran on. Somewhere in the grid a compressor cycled. Kyle could hear the refrigerant moving in the walls.

"The aphorisms came from documented practitioners," Nadia said. "The phrases themselves were real — pulled from verified instructors, Kenjutsu lineages, records of teaching." She let a breath out through her nose. "There's one you'll know. 'After the choice. Before the cost. That's where you move.' That was Yun-Gi Han, 2178, from a practicum in Busan. Documented. A real man. A real moment."

She stopped. She was watching him the way she'd watched him since he sat down, and she didn't look away from what came next.

"But I wrote the context. The room in Old Pilsen. The afternoon. The exact way he stopped the drill and said it — the pause, the turn, the light coming through the window at that angle. I put that in. The phrase was real. The afternoon I built."

Kyle didn't move. He stayed still a long time.

"So I'm grieving a design choice," he said.

Nadia held his eyes and said nothing. It was the only honest answer she had.

"We should move."

[Beat 318]
The cooler kept time the way cold storage always did — steady, indifferent. The compressor ran a three-count.

He was standing. Nadia had finished. The sources, the build, all of it — everything she had to say, she'd said. He let it settle.

Then he said it.

"I'm not even a person. I'm a meatbag haunted by dozens of ghosts."

She didn't hurry to answer. She let it stand.

"Yes," she said.

That was all, for a moment.

Kyle turned it over.

"I'll think about the distinction," he said. "When there's time for it."

"I know," Nadia said.

[Beat 319]
The city was still doing what cities do. Three floors down, through the window, a cargo mech was backing a pallet into a loading bay. The driver stood off to the side with her arms crossed, watching the machine work — human or augmented, he couldn't tell at this distance. She'd been standing like that since Kyle took up the window.

He'd been at the window eleven minutes.

Nadia had stopped talking. He'd heard her stop — a sentence trailing off, then a careful quiet, the kind people make when they know the other person needs time. She was behind him, near the cot. He wasn't tracking her. He knew she was there the way you know a wall is there.

He went through it one memory at a time.

Seito's third correction: drop the elbow, find the centerline again, the stillness before the strike. Eleven days of it. He couldn't have invented eleven days — the reps were too many, too correctly spaced, exactly the kind of practice that put the result in his body. So the days had happened. The correction was real. A man had stood behind him for eleven days and watched his elbow and said the same four words in the same flat voice until Kyle stopped making the mistake.

The man's face, he now knew, had been assembled from a database.

He checked the elbow. The correction was still in the elbow.

There was a girl once, on a motorcycle, in a bad neighborhood. Maybe eleven years old. She was riding it because the man who was supposed to protect her couldn't, and Kyle had been standing in the street, and she'd nearly run him down. He'd stepped aside. She hadn't said sorry. She'd looked at him from the corner of her eye the way kids look at adults they've decided are furniture, and he'd thought: she's going to be fine. Something in the way she'd handled the bike. The line she'd found through the gap.

He never learned her name. He'd been hired to keep her alive through a bad week, and he had. Φ400, paid, done. The job was real. The girl was real. Nobody had installed her. He'd been there.

He thought about a hand on his wrist. Not the whole memory. Not even a name. Just two fingers at the pulse point, someone counting. Whatever they'd counted was real — it was still there, still giving a reading. Someone had bothered to count it. That care had been real too.

So: the face was invented. The eleven days were not. The girl on the motorcycle was not. The fingers at his wrist were not.

That was not nothing. That was, in fact, most of a life.

He could keep going — the cooler floor with the thermal log still printing, the guard whose blade angle had been wrong by eleven degrees, the smell of a Northpoint bay at night — but the answer came back the same no matter which memory he picked up. Enough of it was real.

Down in the bay, the cargo mech finished with the pallet. The driver uncrossed her arms, checked something on a handpad, crossed them again. Waiting on a sign-off from inside the building. She was good at waiting. Kyle watched her a moment longer, then turned from the window and picked up his coat.

"You posted the job," he said. "That means they know you posted the job. We need to move."

"I know," Nadia said. "I was waiting for you to come back."

He pulled the coat on. Checked Cacophony out of habit — four rounds, right. "I didn't go anywhere."

"No," she said. "I know that too."

He held the door for her.

[Beat 320]
The cooler had settled into a sound Kyle had catalogued three minutes ago — the compressor cycling, a faint drip somewhere in bay six, Nadia's breathing still working to find its own bottom. He was crouched against the shelving, his back to the cold steel, watching the door they'd come through. She sat with her spine against the next unit, close enough that he could feel her body heat as a plain fact.

He asked because he had the time and because it mattered.

"Why the open board?"

She didn't answer right away. She was the kind of person who treated a silence as a draft she could still revise.

"I couldn't use a secure channel," she said.

"You have contacts."

"I had contacts. Past tense. Anyone I could have reached inside a closed network was either already compromised or watched closely enough that reaching them would've been the same as a signed confession." She turned her head toward him, not quite meeting his eyes, looking at the middle distance the way people do when they're reciting something they worked out alone at three in the morning. "I needed someone outside every network. Someone who wouldn't need it explained. Someone who'd read the phrase and already know what it meant."

Kyle said nothing. The compressor cycled.

"There was no one else who would recognize it," she said. "It's not a real phrase. It was never published. It was never in any training record a person could reach. Seito — the construct — was private architecture. Closed source. The only place that phrase exists is in you. I put it there. I was the one who sourced the teaching aphorisms. I wanted something that would read as real to anyone who hadn't lived it, so I pulled from actual instructors. The phrase itself was real — Yun-Gi Han, 2178, recorded at a practicum in Busan. 'After the choice. Before the cost. That's where you move.' But the context was mine. I wrote the room. I wrote the afternoon. I wrote Seito stopping the drill and saying it to you — the specific you, the student who needed to hear it that way. The phrase was the only key there was. I put it in the posting and waited to see if anyone would recognize it. Whether the program had deployed. Whether the recipient was real. Whether he was still himself enough to read it."

Kyle watched the door.

"So the job was posted to me."

"Yes."

"You just had to trust I was still working boards. Still reading."

"Still yourself enough to recognize it," she said. "Yes."

The compressor finished its cycle. In the quiet after, the cooler felt smaller.

"You could have just called," he said.

She looked at him. "I didn't have your number."

"Nobody does."

The pressure changed. Not a sound yet — a condition, the shift of a fire door opening three floors down, the building's sealed cold-chain corridors registering it as a ghost of moving air. He caught it before his ears did and had it placed between one heartbeat and the next: two bodies, moving with the gait of people who'd cleared a stairwell before.

Kyle was already rising.

"They're here," he said.

[Beat 321]
Kyle heard them at the base of the stairs.

Not footsteps. The building didn't give him footsteps. It gave him weight. The safe house sat on a slab of reinforced concrete, and concrete carries load the way bad news travels — unevenly, but it travels. Six people. Two on the outside landing. Four climbing the stairwell in a stack. He was already moving Nadia before he said a word.

"Desk. Left side."

She moved. No question, no hesitation. He'd seen that in the cooler and again on the bus — she listened with her body first and let her mind catch up. Years in controlled rooms, where the rule came before the reason. He put her between the desk and the eastern wall. Brick. Load-bearing. Three feet clear of the window's line. Then he crouched beside her and kept his voice low enough to die in the drywall.

"Two on the fire escape. They'll hold the east window until they hear the front door go. Don't move until I tell you. If I say run, you go through the bathroom, out the window, into the courtyard. You don't look back to see what's behind you."

She nodded once. Her eyes tracked the room. Not panic — the calm of someone who had already pictured exactly this ending for weeks.

He went to his coat on the hook by the door.

Cacophony came free of the holster with a sound he knew the way he knew gravity. He checked the cylinder by feel. Four rounds. He'd spent two inside Northpoint and reloaded once in the corridor. Four was what he had.

The stairwell went quiet.

That was the signal. Professionals stop moving right before they move — the last weight on a step, then nothing, then the door.

The door came in on a ram, low and fast.

He didn't wait for it to stop.

The first two rounds went through the upper panel at angles he'd already worked — the door frame, the spacing of a breach team, where the point man's chest had to be at full stride. The shotgun's spread did the rest. The point man dropped into the doorway. The second man caught the edge of the pattern and spun sideways into the frame, still up, still moving, one hand on his weapon.

Kyle was already sliding left.

The fire-escape crew took the east window on the sound of the shots. He'd figured six seconds. They came in four, which told him they were better than the pair at the door. Glass blew inward. Two shapes, low and fast, spaced like men who had done this together.

Two rounds left.

He put one into the near shooter at seven feet. Center of the chest, nothing fancy. The second was already through the frame with an angle on Nadia, which meant the desk wasn't cover anymore. Kyle fired his last round as he closed — not to hit, just to push the man's aim wide — then got inside the reach of the gun, where a gun is a liability. The scabbard came up before the sword cleared it.

It wasn't clean. He drove the pommel end into the man's throat, blade still sheathed, the angle bad because the man was already lifting an elbow to block. Kyle felt the block and turned the strike down instead of through, ramming the low end of the scabbard up under the ribs. The man folded. Kyle took him by the collar and put him into the wall, and the man stopped being a problem.

Silence came free.

The second man at the door had his weapon up and a ruined shoulder — the shotgun had done enough. Twelve feet, a narrow room, furniture between them. A pistol is slower than a man who already knows where you'll be. Kyle moved before the trigger finger finished deciding, past the furniture line, the overhead cut already committed when the arm came up. Silence answered the arm and then answered him.

The room went quiet except for the building settling.

He stood still for three seconds and counted.

Four in the stack. Two down inside, one on the threshold, one he couldn't place. He went low to the doorframe and checked the stairwell. Nothing. Either the fourth had held outside and was already gone, or he was somewhere in the building Kyle couldn't reach from here. He held for two more seconds.

Nothing moved.

Nadia's voice came from behind the desk, steady. "There's a coat."

He turned. She had his coat over one arm, held out to him — his left arm, the one that needed to be free. She'd taken it off the hook during the fight. He didn't know when. She'd been reading the room the whole time, on her own map, and she'd done the right thing at the right second without being told.

He took the coat.

"Can you walk?"

"Yes."

"Then we're leaving."

He scanned the room one more time. Not for comfort. For inventory. All four rounds gone. Silence clean. The fourth man still a question. His left shoulder had a new catch in it, the arm that had driven the scabbard paying for the angle. The hit he'd taken in the Northpoint corridor had been grinding on his lower ribs through every exchange and was doing something worse now — two cracked, maybe one broken. It would matter tomorrow. It didn't stop him tonight.

He said nothing about any of it.

The coat went on over all of it.

"The stairs," he said, and she was already moving.

[Beat 322]
The contractors were in the stairwell, the hallway, and the chair where he'd left one of them. He counted them before he counted himself.

Six. All there.

Then he checked his own body, the way a mechanic walks a chassis after a hard run — not hunting for one thing, just finding what had changed. Left side: two ribs cracked, maybe one loose. The shoulder where the hallway team's third man had caught him was through-and-through, clean, already closing around the biotape he'd pressed on in the dark in the forty seconds he'd had. His hands were steady. His neuretics ran hot — he could feel the heat at the base of his skull, the small stubborn headache that meant he'd burned past what the read cost him — but the overlay was still up and still readable. He was working.

Nadia stood against the far wall, arms folded, watching him.

"Count's done," he said. "You're not hurt."

"No."

"Northpoint will know the team went dark. They'll wait maybe ten minutes for a check-in, then send a runner to confirm. After that someone senior decides what this becomes." He picked the slate up off the table where she'd set it. "You've got about forty minutes before that call gets made. Be somewhere I don't know about before it does."

She watched him. "You planned this before they came."

He handed her the slate. A sector address in Z4. A transit code that would clear two checkpoints without a flag. A name. "The contact holds for seventy-two hours. Don't go back to wherever you slept last night."

She looked at the slate. Then at him — the same look she'd had in the cooler reading the thermal log, finding something still running that she hadn't expected to.

"You need a doctor," she said.

"I know one."

She didn't push. She wasn't a person who pushed at things already decided.

A silence. Not an uncomfortable one — just the room being what it was now. Someone else's safe house, safe for thirty-nine more minutes.

"The phrase in the posting," she said. "Seito's phrase. I used it because you were the only one who'd know it." She paused. "I didn't know if you'd still be —" She stopped. Started again. "I didn't know if you'd still be yourself."

"I'm not sure that's the right word."

"I know," she said. "I used it anyway."

She tucked the slate inside her jacket, precise about it, the way she'd been precise about everything he'd watched her do tonight — the bay numbers in the cooler, the way she moved when he told her to move. She had been waiting a long time, he thought, to be moving again.

She went to the door and stopped with her hand on the frame. Without turning she said, "The man they built is the one who came. I don't think they knew that's what they were building."

Then she was gone.

Kyle stood in the room.

He didn't go to the window. She'd earned an exit no one watched.

He went to work. He moved the contractor nearest the door out to the stairwell — not because it would matter in forty minutes, but because tidiness was a habit, and habits don't take the night off. He picked up the casings by count, his neuretics tagging each one as it went in his pocket. He wiped the surfaces he'd touched. He checked the way he'd come in and made sure it still looked like a door no one had used.

The shoulder was bleeding again, slow, through the biotape. He noted it the way you note a slow leak. Not urgent. Something to manage on a clock.

He had the doctor's code ready in the overlay. He'd send it two blocks out and moving.

The phrase. It was Seito's, and Seito was a design document, and the phrase had been written into him by people who needed the pieces to fit. He'd known that for four hours now. He turned it over — not for new information, just to set the weight of it down in the right place.

The man they built is the one who came.

He didn't know if she was right. He wasn't sure the question had a right answer. The sword still knew what his hand knew. Whatever the sixty-four had been built to make, it had held. Whatever word she'd reached for and set aside — yourself — it pointed at something still in the room. Still working. Still willing to hold a count.

He thought that was enough.

He picked his jacket up off the floor, thumbed five fresh rounds into Cacophony from the pocket, and left.

[Beat 323]
The stairwell smelled like mildew and mineral water, from a pipe that had been weeping through the wall since before the ferrocement wave. Kyle stopped on the third-floor landing. Not because anything needed it — nothing was coming, he'd checked twice — but because his hands were doing something without him.

The right hand. His sword hand.

It had moved into the first position of the third correction, the grip adjustment Seito had drilled into him across three hundred repetitions on a specific Tuesday he couldn't find now. He had a sense of wooden floors. Slant light. A clear memory of being told his elbow was too high. A less clear memory of the face that told him. And no memory at all of the afternoon it got fixed — because that afternoon never happened. The correction that mattered, he'd worked out himself, two years later, when he noticed the form dragged the cut slightly sideways.

He'd fixed it alone. On a job. After the job. Standing in an alley on the north side with a body cooling behind him and nothing but time.

Seito never saw the change.

Kyle ran the form once on the landing, slow, no urgency. Weight shift, turn at the hip, the drive through the shoulder, elbow where he'd decided it belonged instead of where he'd been told. The form was better now. Cleanly, plainly better. Whoever built the Seito construct had done it from archives — recordings of a thousand real fighters folded into one teacher. Good work. Real work. The skill had arrived in him whole and true. But the archive didn't hold the alley on the north side, or the job before it, or the four hundred times he'd done the grip wrong in the two years after Seito before he caught it himself.

None of that was installed.

He ran the form to the end. His hands came back to rest.

He had a grief with no object. He'd known Seito nine years — which wasn't true in any sense a court would take, and the grief was real anyway, which was maybe the point. The afternoons were invented. The form was his.

There was a second grief under the first. A master, even a made-up one, is someone else's certainty to lean on. Masterless meant no certainty but his own. On the page that sounds like freedom. On a cold landing at four in the morning it felt like a house with the load-bearing walls pulled out. He'd need his own answer soon, about Pixel — whether to send her back from a door he hadn't cleared, the thing Seito would have demanded and the thing that would get her killed. He'd choose for himself instead. That was the whole of what masterless left him to give her: not a master's certainty, just a man deciding, alone, and carrying what the deciding cost.

He sheathed Silence.

Two more flights, a fire door, and then the gray zone, and the gray zone gave him north-side asphalt at four in the morning. The texture of it was older than anything in his design files. Cracked expansion joints spaced wrong. A repair crew's aggregate that didn't match three blocks in either direction. He'd learned this ground by walking it, not by being built with it. The city kept collecting in him whether he asked it to or not.

The next job was already waiting. He knew the address. He knew roughly what it would cost — not the money, the money was simple, but the read-budget, the sword-hours, the shoulder that would need two days before he could drive it hard again. He knew the ground going in. He knew there were things he didn't know. There was always a variable.

He'd run the form. The form was better than what he'd been taught. That felt like an answer, though not the kind that closes anything.

It was better because he'd fixed it in the gap. Seito — whoever built him — made the decision, laid the foundation, drilled three hundred Tuesdays into a hand that would never stop moving. Then the cost of that building came due slowly, over years, in alleys and jobs and a drag on the cut no archive had predicted. Between the choice and the cost, Kyle had lived. That gap was where the adjustment happened. That gap was where he'd been living his whole life without knowing it had a name.

He kept walking.

The city made its north-side sounds — a freight slug pulling under the street, the low hum of a cooling tower three blocks east, something with wings and no sense stuck in the gap between two buildings. None of it needed anything from him. The first gray light was starting to turn the ferrocement the color of old water.

He was on his way to the next job. It would have its own costs, its own variable. This was what happened after. This was the whole of it.

He already knew how to move in it.

[Beat 324]
Somebody stole Kyle's motorcycle.

He stood in the alley behind The Pivot at 06:40 with a cup of tea going cold in his hand and laid the facts out where he could see them, because they refused to add up. Fact: the alley had one entrance, and the entrance had a camera, and the camera worked, because Pixel kept it running in exchange for the landlord ignoring what her power meter did. Fact: the wheel hub had been locked. Fact: the plate cover — his plate cover, the one he folded a particular way — sat on the concrete where the bike used to be, folded the particular way.

Folded. Not dropped. Whoever took the bike had pulled the cover off first, folded it like laundry, and left it as a receipt.

Kyle drank some of the cold tea and let himself feel the thing he was feeling, which was not, strictly, professional. He'd owned the bike nine years. He'd bought it with his second fee ever — the first had gone to a flat with a door that locked — and it was the only thing he'd ever bought that wasn't a tool. The bike was not a tool. The bike was the long way home. Eleven years in this city and his whole inner life had a displacement of 1200cc, and some polite person had walked it out of a locked alley under a working camera between 03:00 and dawn.

He went back upstairs and knocked on 2E.

Pixel opened the door with a soldering iron in one hand and yesterday's hair. She read his face the way she read his walk.

"Who died?"

"The bike's gone."

She went very still. Then she set the soldering iron down on the little shelf inside the door, the one she kept for the moments when something needed both her hands, and said, in the voice she used for load-bearing bolts:

"Somebody boosted my work?"

"Your work?"

"Who do you think built the anti-theft — the manufacturer? The manufacturer's anti-theft was a sticker." She was already pulling her boots on. "The hub lock is mine. The ignition handshake is mine. Three years, samurai, nobody's gotten past the handshake — I'd have heard, I read the forums, people complain about me by name." She grabbed her field coat. "This isn't theft. This is peer review."

[Beat 325]
The camera had thirty-one minutes of nothing.

Not a loop — Pixel checked the frame seams twice — just a clean gap, 03:12 to 03:43, the feed dropping the way feeds drop when somebody upstream of the camera holds the right permissions. Kyle stood in the alley while she swore at her own gear with real affection, and he read what the gap told him. It told him money, because permissions cost. It told him manners, because the cover was folded. And it told him confidence, because anyone who knew enough to want his bike knew enough to know whose bike it was. A Ghost's work, probably — body parked somewhere dry, eyes riding the camera while the camera's owner filed a green-light log and went home.

"Professional, funded, polite," Kyle said. "Who steals at that grade and leaves a receipt?"

"Somebody who wants you to follow," Pixel said. That was the obvious answer. Then the real one landed behind it and they looked at each other. Somebody who wanted him to follow.

The freight lift at the end of the block had a courier on it.

She was coming off a night shift, breath still ahead of her, the panels in her sponsorship legs cycling some energy drink's sunrise ad at one-quarter brightness — the legal minimum, and her whole opinion of the energy drink. Kyle knew her the way the trade knows the trade: a nod, three years of shared lift rides, no names. She watched the lift numbers. He watched them with her.

"03:00-ish," he said. "A flatbed, maybe. Something that could carry a bike."

She let two floors go by, weighing what gossip costs against who was asking.

"Box truck," she said. "03:20. Backed up to your alley like it had a permit. Two lifters, and a kid riding the gate." She paused. "Truck had a cartoon fish on it. The fish was winking."

"A winking fish."

"I notice things," she said, to the numbers. "It cost you a favor, by the way. Unspecified. I'll know it when I need it."

"Standard terms," Kyle said, and meant it, and her mouth did something that on a less tired face would have been a smile.

The truck, then. One favor on the books.

[Beat 326]
The winking fish belonged to Dinh Salvage & Sundry — four garage doors and a smell of cutting oil down in the Ashgrave fringe. The proprietor was a fence named Vo Dinh, who moved hot vehicles the way other men move furniture: carefully, locally, and with a firm preference for never knowing anything.
