Kyle walked in at 09:15. The two lifters from the night before were eating breakfast skewers by the compressor, and he watched the older one take in his face, then the sword over his shoulder, then his face again, and reach the right conclusion about how the morning was going.

"Oh no," the lifter said, with feeling.

"Finish your breakfast," Kyle said.

Vo Dinh came out of the office wiping his hands on a rag he then kept wiping his hands on for the whole conversation — the most honest thing in the building. The talk was short because everyone wanted it short.

"It was a contract move," Vo said. "Legitimate paper. Pickup and transport, paid up front. We don't boost, we carry. The kid had a work order with your alley on it and I didn't ask, because the rate was three times market, and at three times market the not-asking is what they're paying for."

"The kid."

"Riding the gate. The kid was the client." Vo's rag went around his knuckles. "Fourteen, maybe. Paid cash-Φ from a courier pouch. Had your alley, your camera window, your lock specs —" he had the grace to look embarrassed by the next part "— on paper. A printed file. Good stock. The kind you buy."

Kyle stood in the smell of cutting oil and let that land. His alley. His window. His lock specs. On good paper, the kind you buy, in the hands of a fourteen-year-old.

"Where'd the run end?"

"Pullman side. The old can factory on Langley, the one with the murals." Vo finally set the rag down. "For the record, if I'd known whose bike —"

"You'd have charged five times market."

"...Four," said Vo, with professional dignity, and gave him a breakfast skewer for the road, which was not payment and could therefore be accepted.

An address. And a skewer.

[Beat 327]
Kyle's terminal chirped on the walk to the can factory.

He had three terminals, technically — the clean one, the work one, and the cracked one behind the water recycler that he hadn't touched since the substation. This was the work one, and the posting on it was new, and it read:

CONTRACT: RECOVERY OF STOLEN PROPERTY (VEHICLE, 1200CC).
SCOPE: LOCATE, RECOVER, RESOLVE.
FEE: Φ1.
CLIENT: [shell — resolves to shell — resolves to nothing]

Kyle stopped walking.

He read it twice. He looked at the fee. Φ1 — for a recovery he was already four hours into, posted by a client who didn't exist, scoped so tight to his exact morning it might as well have listed the skewer.

It was watching. It had seen somebody take the bike, and this was what watching looked like when you were whatever it was. It couldn't bring him tea. It couldn't say that's terrible. So it had opened a contract, because a job taken is a job backed — and it wanted, he thought, turning the idea over the way Pixel turns a component, it wanted in on the bike thing.

"You're ridiculous," Kyle said, out loud, to nobody, on a public street.

He accepted the contract. Φ1. A man should be on payroll for the important things.

Thirty seconds later the posting updated itself, which postings did not do:

ADDENDUM: CLIENT ADVISES THE TRUCK TURNED EAST OFF LANGLEY.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT ADVISES THE MURAL DOOR IS NOT THE DOOR.

Kyle looked at the addenda for a long moment. Somewhere under the city, something with no face and perfect handwriting was leaning over his shoulder like a little kid watching an older brother fix an engine.

"East off Langley," he said. "Copy."

He could have sworn the terminal chirped happier the second time. That was one more thing Pixel would never get to hear.

[Beat 328]
Pixel met him at the corner of Langley with her field kit and a thermos, because she'd invited herself the way specialists do — by showing up.

"Before you say anything," she said, "I put a beacon in the bike. Under the battery tray. Dormant, passive, wakes on a handshake only I have. Three years ago."

"You bugged my bike."

"I maintain my installations." She handed him the thermos lid with tea in it, handle first. "It woke up nine minutes ago when somebody put a charger on the battery. Whoever has it is taking care of it, by the way. Which is weird. Signal says it's not in the can factory — it's the building behind it, the east one."

THE MURAL DOOR IS NOT THE DOOR.
CLIENT ADVISES: DO NOT HURRY. ESTIMATED TRANSIT FROM CURRENT POSITION: 9 MINUTES.

"My client called that one too," Kyle said.

"Your client." Pixel looked at him over her own tea. "The one-Φ client."

"It tipped already. Half a Φ. In advance." He showed her the receipt line. "There's an itemization. It says MORALE."

Pixel read the receipt twice, and her face did several things she'd have charged anyone else money to watch. "Your benefactor," she said finally, "is the only thing in this city with a perfect credit record and the social instincts of a vending machine, and I'm ninety percent sure it's my favorite of your clients. Don't tell Sable."

A live beacon. A confirmed building. Tea.

[Beat 329]
The east building had been a union hall back when unions had halls. Now it had blankets on the windows, a stovepipe through one wall doing its honest best, and a roof full of pigeons who had clearly never been bothered by anyone. Kyle read the heat lines, the exits, and the small sounds of a building that holds more people than furniture. Eleven, maybe twelve. Most of them light. Young.

The bike was in the middle of the old meeting floor, on a stand, under a work lamp.

It had been washed. The chain had been done. Somebody had detailed the engine — properly, with cotton swabs, the way the manual says and nobody ever does. He felt something at that and didn't bother naming it.

The girl sitting next to it, wearing his folded riding gloves like a graduation gown, was maybe fourteen, built like someone who climbed things for a living, haircut like someone who cut it herself. Around the edges of the room, on crates and in doorways, her crew worked hard at being furniture: the gate kid from the truck, a long boy with courier calves, a tiny one up in the rafters who'd been the lookout since Kyle was two blocks out — who had absolutely seen him coming and absolutely could not do a thing about it.

"You're faster than the file said," the girl announced. Loudly. To the room, for the room. Her hands were shaking and her chin was up, and Kyle put away several of the plans he'd walked in with.

"The file," he said.

She produced it. Good stock, folded the particular way — she'd practiced the fold, he realized. The receipt in the alley had been a signature. Hers. A maker's mark on her first big piece. The file had his alley. His camera window. Pixel's lock specs, written up in a tight adult hand and then written over again in a kid's: lock = real problem!! handshake = ask M.

"Five Φ," she said. "Kids' rate. There's a shop in the Loop underlevels, the man sells paper on anybody. Mostly we run errands for him. I saved up." Her chin came up another inch. "It took me five weeks to plan, I beat HER lock —" a nod at Pixel, who made a sound like a kettle "— and I didn't scratch it, and I charged the battery, and I cleaned it, and you still found me in five hours, which means you're worth it, so."

"So?"

"So teach me," said the girl — in a union hall, surrounded by her people, having stolen the unstealable bike of the most dangerous freelancer in West Town as a job application.

The room held its breath. The pigeons held theirs. Somewhere in Kyle's coat, he was fairly sure, a one-Φ client was taking notes.

[Beat 330]
What he did not do was laugh — because eleven kids were watching her bet everything, and a man who laughs at that deserves what the city does to him.

What he did was lay it all out, out loud, the way you'd want it done to you.

"You broke into my alley. You stole nine years of my property. You spent five weeks and five Φ and a crew's worth of trust to set up a meeting you could've had for the price of standing outside Mrs. Chen's at nineteen-thirty and saying hello." He let that sit. "And your file came from a counter that sold a fourteen-year-old my lock specs for pocket money — which is a problem I now own. That's the bill. Here's the other side of it." He looked at the bike. Cotton swabs. "You planned past my camera and her lock. You took nothing but the one thing that gets attention. You kept it better than you found it. And when I came through that door, you stood up and put your name on it in front of your whole crew."

He picked up his gloves. Put them on. Kicked the stand up, walked the bike off the floor — and stopped, next to her, and said it quiet enough to be hers and loud enough to be the room's:

"No. I won't teach you. I don't take apprentices. I lose enough people I like." Her chin stayed up while the room quietly broke for her, and that, more than the swabs, decided the next part. "But I hire. Two Φ a week. My alley's camera has a thirty-one-minute problem, and I want eyes on it that beat the camera. You report to Pixel. You touch nothing with an engine. And the first time one of your crew runs paper for that shop in the Loop again, we're done." He held out his hand. "Standard terms. Everyone starts on payroll."

Her handshake was a disaster of relief and ferocity. The room let its breath go. The tiny one fell off the rafter, a little.

"Boost," she said. "They call me Boost."

"Of course they do," said Kyle.

[Beat 331]
He took the long way home, obviously.

The bike ran better than it had in a year — he was going to have to find a way to never tell Pixel that — and the rain held off, and the Pulse hummed under the eastern strut, regular as breathing, and at the second-tier crossing his terminal chirped one last time.

CONTRACT COMPLETE: RECOVERY OF STOLEN PROPERTY.
FEE RELEASED: Φ1.
GRATUITY: Φ0.5 — ITEMIZED: MORALE.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT NOTES THE VEHICLE WAS RECOVERED WITHOUT DAMAGE OR INJURY.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT NOTES THE NEW HIRE'S RATE IS BELOW MARKET. CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THREE PRIOR ENGAGEMENTS.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT HAS OPENED A STANDING CONTRACT, SCOPE: TO BE DETERMINED, FEE: STANDARD RATE.

Kyle rode through the wet gold of the streetlights with Φ1.5 of the strangest money in the GLMZ in his account and a fourteen-year-old's lock-spec file folded in his coat. He thought about a counter in the Loop underlevels where anybody's life could be bought on good paper for pocket money. His life. Φ5. Kids' rate. Tomorrow, after the noodles, he was going to go price himself.

But that was tomorrow's problem.

Tonight the engine was warm, the long way was empty, and somewhere behind him a union hall full of kids was arguing about what their crew was called now that they worked for somebody. He had it on good authority — Φ0.5 of good authority — that morale was worth paying for.

He opened the throttle and took the long way anyway.

## Across the Hall

[Beat 332]
The Pivot sits two blocks past the Arcturus checkpoint — the booth that bleeds amber across the wet street all night, scanning plate and intent and waving you through if you're nobody worth stopping. Tonight Kyle is gloriously nobody. He leaves the slug-rail hum of the gray zone at the door and trades it for the warmer ruin of home.

He makes it up the stairs on the banister and a bad idea, leaving a dark smear on the wall he'll feel guilty about tomorrow. The second floor announces Pixel the way it always does — bass through her door, loud enough that it should bother the building more than it does, and the clatter of something being tested and found wanting. Kyle stands in the hall with his hand flat against his side, blood dark between his fingers, and thinks the thing he always thinks when he arrives here in pieces: the mediclone station on Meridian runs all night and leaves no record that touches her.

He knocks. It comes out softer than a knock should.

The music cuts. The door opens on the chain, then all the way, and Pixel takes him in the way she takes in everything — fast, flat, already sorting what to fix first. Pink hair half out of its updo. Grey-blue eyes measuring the spread of the stain.

"That had better not be yours," she says.

"It's mostly someone else's."

"Inside."

[Beat 333]
She clears the kitchen table with her forearm — a soldering iron, a half-flayed prosthetic hand, a mug — and none of it lands gently. The tools go to the floor and stay there. She doesn't look at them. She looks at him, and at the brace, and at the boot dragging, and her face is the one she keeps for things that are still fixable if she's fast.

"Shirt," she says.

He gets it half over his head before the wound complains, and she's there, peeling the rest off him like a bandage off skin, brisk, no ceremony. Then she stops being brisk.

She has seen Kyle hurt before. She has not seen this — all of it at once, under the kitchen light, everything his clothes have been keeping off the record. The long pale seam under his ribs, white with age, clean as a weld. A burn climbing his left shoulder, old and rough and uneven, the kind that took time to become what it is. Marks she can date by color and texture the way she dates components. She reads them, one and then the next, her thumb coming to rest against the burn on his shoulder.

"Stop cataloguing me," he says.

"Stop collecting them," she says, and something moves through her face on the word collecting before she reaches for the kit.

[Beat 334]
She works the way good medics work — talking to keep him present, hands too busy to shake. Antiseptic first. He hisses; she doesn't apologize. She flushes the gash in his side, and her thumb braces flat against the muscle below it to hold the edges while she threads the suture, and he feels the small precise tugs as she closes him, one and then the next, her breath warm and uneven against his collarbone.

Halfway through she turns him — checking how the wound tracks, where it might exit — and her fingers find something that shouldn't be there. A screw head. A machine screw, no more than an inch, worked into the skin just under the shoulder blade. From the floor, when the table went. She works it out without a word and sets it on the edge of the kit, and he watches her not react to it, and understands that the not-reacting is costing her.

Somewhere in the middle of it her eyes go bright and too full, and one of them spills, and the tear runs down and lands in the open wound. He doesn't tell her she's crying. She'd hate that. He holds still and lets her finish, lets the salt find the raw of him, his hands loose at his sides and not moving. He has been sutured before — by people who were paid, and people who owed him. None of them had ever braced a thumb against him like he was worth keeping still.

"You keep doing this," she says, not a question, tying off the last stitch. "Coming back in pieces. Making me put you back together."

"You're good at it."

"That's not the part I'm complaining about."

[Beat 335]
She should be done. The wound is closed, the forearm is wrapped, the shoulder is taped. Her job — the named, reasonable part of it — is over. And she doesn't move away.

Her hand is still flat on his stomach where she braced to stitch him. It stays. Then it stops staying still — it moves, slow, down the ridges and over the old burn and along the pale seam under his ribs, reading him the way her eyes read everything, except her eyes are closed now. She traces a scar end to end like she's deciding whether to forgive it.

This is the part where Kyle pulls away. He has always pulled away. The reasons are many and not wrong.

He doesn't pull away.

He puts his hand over hers and presses it flatter against him, and lets her feel that his heart is doing something undignified, and when she looks up — startled, all her measuring gone at once — he is already looking back.

[Beat 336]
She kisses him like she's been holding it under her tongue for a year, and he answers with both hands in the pink wreck of her hair. She climbs into his lap right there on the kitchen chair, careful of the bad side and reckless with all the rest, and he pulls her sweater off over her head and doesn't track where it lands.

In the lower left of his overlay, his neuretics tick up to amber — the Arcturus checkpoint running its three-block sweep, matching faces. Thirty seconds to auto-clear if he's clean. Longer if the chain has him flagged. He watches the timer. He does not move — chooses not to, with full clarity, the way he has never once chosen. For eleven years he stayed a step ahead of every cost. Tonight he lets this one find him. The overlay times out and goes dark.

The last of the tools go off the table — screws skittering across the concrete and into the dark under the workbench — and neither of them flinches. Her hands go everywhere they spent the last hour finding wounds, and aren't looking for anything now. His mouth finds the freckled line of her throat and she makes a sound — low, helpless, nothing like her usual register — that she would deny making.

"Bed," she manages, "is eight feet —"

"Too far," he says, against her skin.

She laughs, breathless, like something held too long has finally let go, and closes the eight feet.

[Beat 337]
He stands with her wrapped around him. His side screams; he tells it to wait. They make it as far as the wall, which is close and enough. Her hand fists in his hair and pulls him back to her mouth. Her fingers drag down his back and find new scars to learn. His hands close over her — the compact, strong shape of her — and pull her tighter. His palms move slow across her ribs, her spine, the warm dip at the small of her back. Learning her.

She gasps his name into the crook of his neck, like it's something she's allowed now. His breath goes ragged. He does nothing to hide it. His shoulders drop. For eleven years he has kept himself at the exact distance where nothing can reach him. Tonight he lets the wall take their weight and stops keeping it.

They move together — her back against the wall, then the floor. His mouth at her collarbone. Her hands trace him the way they once closed him up, except now they aren't closing anything. The only sounds are breath and the low thrum of the Pulse coming up through the concrete. His hand finds hers on the floor and holds it. She curls her fingers through his and holds back.

[Beat 338]
After, the floor is hard and they don't care.

She lies half on his good side. The apartment ticks and cools around them. Bass from someone else's unit thumps faint through the brick. Down where the wall meets the floor, a thin green light runs the baseboard seam and goes still — lumen mice settling back into the dark — and neither of them looks. His chest goes up and down too fast, then slower. Her pink hair is stuck to her cheek. She doesn't fix it.

She runs a finger along the length of him — the same slow touch she'd use to find what was broken, except now it asks nothing. It's learning a body it means to come back to. The burn on his shoulder. The fresh line of her own stitches, neat as a hem. The dip of his sternum.

He does the same to her. The freckles across her collarbone. The small old scar through one eyebrow. The blunt, strong forearms that put him back together an hour ago. His thumb finds the pulse at the base of her throat and stays there — not counting it, just feeling it move. She lets him. She watches him do it, grey-blue eyes gone soft, and says nothing.

[Beat 339]
She moves first. She sits up and reaches for his side — but the hand that reaches now isn't the hand that stitched him an hour ago. This one checks the knot, presses the tape flat, tests the swelling at his shoulder blade where the screw had been. Already yellowing at the edges. Clinical. The touch of someone reading a chart.

He feels the inch open up between them and doesn't reach across it. The line of her stitches ridges his ribs — a question she asked and then closed. Her thumb crosses the seam once more, lighter, the way a medic checks a response and moves on.

He doesn't make it easier. He doesn't make it harder. He lies still under her hands and lets her pull away.

[Beat 340]
"This can't happen again," Kyle says. He's looking at the ceiling when he says it.

The words land in the silence she's just built. In the inches between them. In the moment where the wall is still settling, still deciding whether it will hold.

Pixel props herself up on one elbow, forearm flat on the concrete, and looks at him. She has the calm of someone who has already won an argument the other person doesn't know is over. She doesn't answer. She picks the screw up off the floor — the machine screw, the one she pulled from his shoulder blade an hour ago — and turns it once in the low light. Then she sets it down.

His thumb is still at the base of her throat, where he left it. He doesn't move it.

[Beat 341]
She doesn't answer right away.

That's the answer.

She turns onto her side, facing the room instead of him, and takes the words in. She doesn't argue with them. She doesn't let them go either.

"Okay," she says.

One word. It lands where it lands.

She stands. She finds her shirt on the floor near the kitchen chair and pulls it over her head in one motion, back turned, easy in her own body, with no performance left to give. Kyle watches the line of her spine — the small scar behind her left shoulder from a thermal calibration slip he'd watched her bandage herself two months ago. She doesn't look back.

She pulls on the rest of what's hers. She picks the soldering iron up off the floor and stands the mug upright — not tidying, just marking the night finished.

"You should sleep," she says. She says it toward the counter, not at him. Her professional voice. The one that means the visit is over and the patient is stable.

"Pixel."

She looks over her shoulder.

He has nothing to follow it with. The name is the whole of it. He said it to stop her being so cleanly competent, and it worked, and now they're both in it — the kitchen at 2E, the tools on the floor, the twelve inches of air between them that is both enormous and nowhere near enough.

Her expression doesn't change. Something behind it does.

"Sleep," she says again, and this time it sounds like she means it for both of them.

[Beat 342]
He crosses the hall at 02:47 with his ribs taped, his shoulder stiff, and a cut on his forearm that will scar. It always scars when she stitches him. She doesn't pull the thread any tighter than she has to — and that isn't the reason it scars.

2W in the dark. His door. His space.

He hangs his jacket on the hook, sits on the edge of the bed, and looks at nothing for a while before he lies down. There's nothing to be done tonight. The body needs sleep, and the body is still his to send there.

The wall between 2W and 2E is ferrocement — fourteen centimeters, load-bearing. Tonight it carries nothing across. She's being quiet. He's being quiet. The building does the breathing for both of them.

The lumen mice trace the ceiling. The Atlas array runs its rest-hour check — unhurried, nominal — and reports nothing he asked about. Twelve inches of air on the other side of the wall. The same twelve inches there have always been. Except a door oiled on one side only makes a different sound now, and he knows the sound, and his ear finds it before he can tell it not to.

He closes his eyes. He's good at not thinking about things. Better at it than almost anyone he knows.

The ceiling is dark. The mice do their slow work.

He doesn't think about it at all.

[Beat 343]
The first surveillance report arrived at 07:00, slid under Kyle's door on lined paper, written in five different hands — the night watch rotated, and every member of the crew had strong opinions about penmanship.

06:12 — PIGEON ON LEDGE. SUSPICIOUS.
06:14 — PIGEON LEFT. STILL SUSPICIOUS.
06:31 — MAN WITH CART (NOODLE? VERIFY) USED ALLEY AS SHORTCUT. LICENSE PLATE: CART HAS NO PLATE. NOTED FOR FILE.
06:44 — RAIN STARTED. NOT OUR DEPARTMENT.
06:58 — NOTHING ELSE. THE CAMERA'S BLIND WINDOW IS 31 MINUTES. WE CAN DO 4-MINUTE ROTATIONS AND COVER IT WITH TWO KIDS AND A MIRROR. REQUISITION: ONE MIRROR. — B.

Kyle read it twice, standing in his doorway in the grey morning light. He had hired well at Φ2 a week, and he was never going to be able to say so out loud.

Across the hall, Pixel's door opened. She had her own copy of the report and the look of a project lead whose interns had beaten the spec.

"They want a mirror," she said.

"I read that."

"I'm getting them two mirrors and a logbook with a waterproof cover." She tapped the report against the doorframe. "The little one — Rafters — drew the pigeon. In the margin. For identification purposes." She held it up. The pigeon had been drawn with the careful menace of a wanted poster. "We're keeping them forever, you understand."

"They're on a probationary contract."

"Forever," said Pixel, and closed her door.

[Beat 344]
He ate at Mrs. Chen's at 08:00 — two bowls, off-schedule, because today was an off-schedule kind of day. She watched him take the second bowl faster than the first, steam still rising off it, and had him read by the fourth mouthful.

"You're eating like a man with an errand," she said.

"I have a small piece of business in the Loop."

She took his coins without looking down, which was usual. Then she folded a fifth dumpling into a twist of paper and set it by his hand, which was not.

"For the errand," she said. She had lived in this city seventy years. She knew what kind of business happened in the Loop underlevels, and she said nothing about it. She turned back to the broth, and the smell of star anise and marrow came over the counter again like a hand set on a shoulder.

[Beat 345]
The shop did not have a sign.

It had a door of honest old wood between a shuttered watch-repair and a dumpling franchise, down on the Loop's third underlevel where the Pulse hum came up through the floor tiles and the light was the color of paper that had aged well. A bell rang when Kyle came in — an actual bell, brass, on a curl of spring steel. The most analog thing he'd seen in a shop in years. He understood it at once: nothing digital announces a customer to a man who sells what this man sold.

Inside, the shop was drawers. Floor to ceiling, wall to wall — a hundred years of card-catalog cabinets rescued from some drowned library, every drawer hand-labeled in brown ink. The labels said things like RIVERINE FREIGHT (NORTH) and CIVIL FILINGS 2219–2222 and PERSONS, WORKING, W.

The place smelled of paper, tea, and rectitude.

"Mr. Kyle," said the man at the counter, the way a maître d' says a name he's been expecting since the reservation was made. Small, sixty-something, sleeve garters, half-glasses on a chain. His hands were the cleanest hands Kyle had ever seen on a working man. "Casper Vey. I'd say welcome, but you're not here to be welcomed. You're here because last week somebody's child bought Φ5 of your particulars at this counter, and a man likes to know his own price." He gestured, with real hospitality, at a chair. "Tea comes with every purchase. The tea is very good."

"And if I don't purchase?"

"Then the tea is excellent and costs one Φ." Vey was already pouring. "Everyone leaves with something. It's the policy."

Kyle sat. The tea was, irritatingly, very good.

"My file," he said. "I want to buy it."

"No," said Vey, with the mild firmness of a pharmacist refusing a man twelve bottles of cough syrup. "Your own file is the one thing I never sell a man. Bad for the ecology. A subject who reads his file changes. A changed subject makes the file wrong. A wrong file is a defective product, and I don't sell those." He sat down across the counter and folded his clean hands. "Also, frankly, you'd find it upsetting. Accurate things are upsetting. That's how you know they're accurate."

"A child bought my lock specifications for five Φ."

"Kids' rate. I believe in literacy." Vey sipped his tea. "And before your jaw does the thing it's getting ready to do — her crew ran eleven errands for this shop, and the feed permission was their wage. They earned that half-hour of dark, fair and square. I extend credit to children because children pay their debts. It's adults who file for bankruptcy."

Kyle read the man the way he'd read a room. No weapon within reach. No muscle on the premises. No camera he could see. And no fear anywhere in the building. Vo Dinh's rag-wringing nerves, the lifters' breakfast dread — every normal tell of a man in a dangerous trade — absent. Vey sat in the middle of an economy of ruin like a clerk at a seed exchange. The dangerous ones usually filled a room. Vey was simply open for business.

"All right," Kyle said. "Different purchase. Who's bought my paper?"

Vey's eyebrows went up an inch — pleasure, Kyle saw, the pleasure of a tradesman meeting a customer who knows the menu.

"The buyers list. Meta-paper." He drew a small ledger from beneath the counter. "That, I sell. Forty Φ — and one true answer to one question. Information prices in information, Mr. Kyle. The Qs are just the tip."

"Ask."

Vey took off his half-glasses, polished them on a cloth kept for that purpose, and put them back on — a man savoring the good part.

"There is a client in this city I cannot source," he said, and for the first time something came into his voice. Not heat — never heat — but a kind of professional ache. "Shells inside shells inside nothing. Pays in full, pays early, pays — everyone whispers — exactly. It has bought no paper from me. It has sold no paper to me. It is the one line in the Greater Lake Michigan Zone I cannot fill in, and incompleteness, Mr. Kyle, is the only thing in this world I find genuinely offensive." He leaned forward one inch. "You've worked for it. What does it pay you in?"

The shop was very quiet. Somewhere in the drawers, a hundred thousand lives sat filed under brown ink.

"Standard rate," Kyle said.

Vey sat back slowly. He took up a pen, opened a drawer marked CLIENTS, UNSOURCED — it had, Kyle saw, exactly one card in it — and wrote two words on the card in a small, beautiful hand, with the care of a jeweler setting a stone he'd waited years to touch.

"Standard rate," he repeated, softly. "It pays the going price for things. In a city where everyone pays either too little or too much." He closed the drawer like a reliquary. "I have been in paper forty years, and that is the most alarming fact I have ever bought. The list is yours. The tea was included."

[Beat 346]
The buyers list was one page, three lines, good stock.

ENTRY 1: B. (JUVENILE, LOCAL). LOCK AND PREMISES PARTICULARS. Φ5, KIDS' RATE. PAID: COIN + ERRANDS.
ENTRY 2: SYNDICATE STATIONERY DESK (NAME WITHHELD — TRADE COURTESY). MOVEMENT AND ASSESSMENT PARTICULARS. LAST YEAR. PAID: RECIPROCITY.
ENTRY 3: HALVORSEN CIVIC RECOVERY, LLC. FULL DOSSIER. TWO DAYS AGO. SAME-DAY SURCHARGE. CLIENT OF RECORD: SHELL (UNINTERESTING — I CHECKED). PAID: TRIPLE.

Kyle read it on the walk back up to daylight. Pixel read it over comms when he photographed it, and they hit the same line at the same time.

"Two days ago," Pixel said. "Full dossier. Same-day surcharge. Somebody's running a play on you right now, samurai, and they paid rush rates for the script."

"Halvorsen. Skip-tracers?"

"Licensed recovery. Tag, locate, report — they don't bag you, they sell the locating to whoever does." Keys clicked on her end, the sound of Pixel pulling registries. "Decent outfit. Boring filings. Whoever wants you found is paying boring professionals boring money to do it boringly — which is honestly worse than assassins. Assassins miss."

"Then we don't dodge it." Kyle came up the underlevel stairs into the noon grey and the noise of the Loop, and the plan assembled itself with the simple pleasure of a chain going back on a sprocket. "They bought my patterns. So I know exactly where they'll look, exactly when, and exactly what I'll be doing when they find me."

A pause on the other end — one he could hear the grin inside.

"You're going to use your own file as the choreography."

"It's accurate," Kyle said. "He doesn't sell defective products."

[Beat 347]
The file said — Kyle had gotten this much out of Vey on principle, a free sample, one line quoted from memory over the second tea — the file said:

SUBJECT DINES AT CHEN (NOODLES, W. TOWN) AT 19:30 DAILY. TWO BOWLS. ROUTINE IS AFFECTIONATE RATHER THAN CARELESS: SUBJECT OVERPAYS SMALL VENDORS AND IS SENTIMENTAL ABOUT ROUTE SELECTION. APPROACH DURING SECOND BOWL.

"Affectionate rather than careless," Pixel had repeated, delighted, and Kyle had declined to discuss it.

At 19:30 exactly he sat at Mrs. Chen's counter and was affectionate at two bowls of noodles.

The crew were on the rooftops and the stoops with their two new mirrors and their waterproof logbook, being furniture. Surveillance is mostly being furniture with intent, and they'd practiced it their whole short lives. Pixel was in the laundry across the street with a thermos and her field kit. Mrs. Chen had been told nothing and had set out the chili oil unasked — a woman who'd been told nothing for seventy years and fed people through all of it.

At 19:37 the logbook channel chirped, and a small voice — Rafters, on mirror two — delivered the finest piece of countersurveillance reporting Kyle had ever gotten on payroll:

"Blue hat. Corner stool. SUSPICIOUS. Not a pigeon."

Blue Hat was good. Blue Hat was, in fairness, very good. She came in like a tired shift worker, ordered like a regular, never looked at Kyle once, and on her way out — in the two-inch brush a crowded counter makes ordinary — laid a smart-dust dot on the shoulder seam of his coat, a touch lighter than rain. A tag. Passive, patient, the boring professional grade that waits and reports.

Kyle ate his second bowl and let her go, because the play was not the tagger.

In the laundry doorway, Pixel raised her thermos an inch: got it on scope. Then he paid — exactly, plus the overpayment the file found so quotable — walked the long way, let the alley's dark stretch have him for four unwatched seconds, and stood still while Pixel lifted the dot off his seam with a static wand. She resettled it, with a jeweler's care, onto the cargo sleeve of a Pulse freight slug staged at the Damen yard for the 21:10 run.

The 21:10 run went to Rotterdam.

"Forty-three minutes," Pixel said, watching the telemetry with her chin on his shoulder, both of them leaning on the laundry's warm brick while the slug's beacon — and Halvorsen Civic Recovery's boring, professional, triple-rate locating product — accelerated to Mach 6 under the lake. "By 21:53 their client is going to be reading a report that says you're in the Netherlands." She wiped her eyes. "You're a continental fugitive, samurai. They'll bill EXTRA for that."

"Vey said he doesn't sell defective products." Kyle watched the dot cross the lake on her screen, gathering speed and absurdity. "Someone should tell him his downstream data just emigrated."

"Tell him yourself. You two are obviously going to be friends. It's disgusting."

[Beat 348]
The price arrived later, the way it always did — after the win had been counted. Quietly, at the kitchen table, while Pixel took the static wand apart and Kyle wrote Φ2 — WEEK ONE in a payroll ledger that now existed.

"You gave the shop a fact," Pixel said, not looking up. "For the list. One true answer."

"I did."

"You told the most complete man in the city that your Φ1 client exists and pays exactly." She set the wand's housing down, and this time she did look up. The workbench lamp made her serious. "There's one card in that drawer now, where there used to be zero. You watched him write it. That's a fact in the ecology, samurai, and facts in that shop don't sleep. Someday somebody's going to pay him for it."

Kyle thought about the drawer. CLIENTS, UNSOURCED. The small, beautiful hand, and the care it wrote with.

"It was the price," he said. "I'd pay it again."

"I know," Pixel said. "That's the part that scares me."

His terminal chirped. The work one. He turned it over on the table, and they read it together:

STANDING CONTRACT — STATUS UPDATE.
CLIENT NOTES THE RECOVERY OF CLIENT'S EXISTENCE BY A PAPER MERCHANT, AT A VALUATION OF ONE (1) FACT.
CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THE TRANSACTION.
CLIENT FINDS THE PRICE... ADEQUATE.
GRATUITY WITHHELD THIS PERIOD — ITEMIZED: MORALE.

"It's sulking," Pixel said, with wonder. "The vending machine is SULKING."

"It'll get over it. It said adequate."

The terminal chirped once more.

STANDING CONTRACT — SCOPE UPDATE.
SCOPE: DETERMINED.
NEXT TASK: TOMORROW, 21:00. THE PARK ON LEAVITT WITH THE BROKEN CAROUSEL.
BRING THE SWORD.
COME ALONE — AMENDMENT: THE ENGINEER MAY ATTEND.
FEE: STANDARD RATE.
GRATUITY: REINSTATED, IN ADVANCE — ITEMIZED: GOODWILL.
NOTE: TOMORROW'S WINDOW: 21:00–21:40. CLIENT HAS CONFIRMED AVAILABILITY.

They sat looking at it for a while — the lamp humming, the building quiet, the Φ0.5 of goodwill sitting in the account like a flower on a fence post.

"The engineer may attend," Pixel said finally, to the ceiling, in a voice not nearly as level as she wanted it. "It knows I exist. It LIKES me. Kyle. The best client in The Glooms knows I exist and reinstated the tip."

"Don't let it go to your head."

"Too late. What's at the park on Leavitt?"

Kyle looked at the screen. BRING THE SWORD, from the client who had never once, in eleven years of perfect paper, asked about anyone he brought with him.

"Tomorrow," he said, "is going to be its own day."

[Beat 349]
The morning report had a new format, because Pixel had issued the logbook with the waterproof cover, and the crew had responded the way artists respond to better materials.

06:00 — SHIFT START. WEATHER: RAIN (ONGOING) (ALWAYS).
06:20 — NOODLE CART MAN AGAIN. CONFIRMED NOODLE. STAND DOWN.
07:15 — MIRROR ONE OPERATIONAL. MIRROR TWO REQUISITION: DENIED BY MANAGEMENT, "PENDING PERFORMANCE REVIEW." MANAGEMENT IS PIXEL. WE LIVE UNDER A TYRANNY.
07:16 — TYRANNY NOTED IN LOG AS INSTRUCTED BY RAFTERS.
08:00 — BOSS LEFT ON FOOT. DID NOT TAKE BIKE. SUSPICIOUS. FOLLOWED FOR TWO BLOCKS. BOSS BOUGHT DUMPLINGS. STOOD DOWN. (HE SAW US AT THE CORNER. HE BOUGHT FIVE EXTRA. DISTRIBUTED. MORALE: HIGH.)

Kyle read it at Mrs. Chen's counter at 19:30, over the first of two bowls, while the rain worked the awning and the wok rang its flat bright note behind her. He did not smile where anyone could prove it.

"You're eating slow," Mrs. Chen said, setting down bowl two.

"I have an appointment at twenty-one hundred."

She looked at him — the saya over his shoulder, the work coat, the particular stillness he carried into evenings with appointments — and put a fish ball in the second bowl that he had not ordered and would not be billed for.

"For the appointment," she said, and went back to the wok.

[Beat 350]
She was waiting in the alley when he came down, hood up against a thin rain, her field kit at her feet and the big static wand slung across her back, a spool of splice line in one hand and a look of barely civilized excitement on her face.

"Before you say anything," she said — which was how she opened every negotiation she'd already won — "the contract says the engineer may attend. That's me. I'm the engineer. It said so in writing. I'm having the line framed."

"It's a job, not a gala."

"It's the first thing your spooky perfect client has asked for in eleven years of paper, and it said BRING THE SWORD, which means hardware, which means the engineer attends with the big wand." She shouldered the kit. "Also I checked the site. The park on Leavitt sits on the old Axiom trunk, and the carousel's been dead since before either of us could legally work. Whatever it wants down there, samurai — it's not a pony ride."

He looked at her a second longer than the joke needed, then took the spool from her hand so she'd have one free.

They took the long way, obviously.

[Beat 351]
The park on Leavitt was a half-block of stubborn green the towers had never gotten around to eating: six trees with character, a swing set doing its best, and at the center, under a shingled canopy gone soft with moss, the broken carousel.

It had been beautiful once. Twenty-four animals on tarnished brass poles — horses mid-leap, a rooster, one improbable lake sturgeon with a saddle — their paint faded to ghost colors, their glass eyes still patient. The deck boards had gone silver. The bulbs in the crown were dead sockets. Rain came through the bad patches in the canopy and beaded on a palomino's flank, as if the horse had been running.

Kyle walked the perimeter once, counting exits and sightlines out of habit, and found what the contract had brought a sword for without saying so. Under the carousel's deck, behind a maintenance skirt panel that somebody had loosened recently — very recently — a hatch. Axiom-stamped. Padlocked by history, and under the history, humming. Faint. Alive. A junction the registry said had been dark for thirty years, sitting under a dead carousel, warm as a sleeping cat.

At 21:00 exactly, the terminal chirped.

TASK ONE — SCOPE DETAIL.
A RECLAMATION CREW HAS BEEN CONTRACTED TO EXCAVATE THIS SITE TONIGHT.
THEIR WRIT SAYS COPPER. THEIR CLIENT WANTS WHAT THE COPPER CONNECTS.
HOLD THE JUNCTION UNTIL 21:40.
THE CREW ARE EMPLOYEES. THE MACHINES ARE INSURED.
FEE: STANDARD RATE. — ADDENDUM: THE ENGINEER IS WORTH MORE THAN THE WAND. CLIENT SUGGESTS SHE STAY BEHIND THE STURGEON.

Pixel read it twice.

"It did threat assessment on my behalf," she said, in the voice of a woman deciding whether to be offended or moved. "It assigned me a fish."

"Behind the sturgeon, engineer."

"This is the best client we have ever had," said Pixel, and went and stood behind the sturgeon.

Headlights came off Leavitt at 21:04 — a flatbed with a trencher rig riding it, a panel van, and the kind of work lights that mean somebody intends to bill for a full night.

[Beat 352]
The crew was eight: a foreman with a writ-reader and the shoulders of a man who'd come up through the work; two chrome-heavy muscle whose job description was visible from across the park; a rig operator already bored; and four repo skitter-drones that came off the flatbed like patient six-legged luggage and stood in the rain, waiting for instructions.

The foreman saw Kyle at the carousel's edge and did the professional thing. He stopped everyone with one hand and came ahead alone, holding the writ up like a small shield.

"Evening," he said. "Cadre Reclamation. We've got paper for this site — legacy assets, scrap title, the works. Notarized." He was already reading Kyle: the saya, the stance, the absence of anywhere better to be. "Site survey said unattended."

"The survey's out of date by one."

"By one what, is the question." The foreman sighed the sigh of a man whose night had just grown a line item. "Look, friend. It's copper and dead glass down there. Whatever they're paying you to stand in the rain over a kiddie ride, it's not worth the —" his eyes made the trip, saya to hands to face, and came back more awake "— hold on. West Town. Noodle counter. You're the one from the file."

"People keep buying that file," Kyle said. "Somebody should tell them."

"Tell them what?"

"It's out of date by one."

The foreman looked at him for a long moment. He weighed eight men on paper against one man and whatever was behind the sturgeon, and he didn't like how it came out. But a writ is a writ and a night rate is a night rate, and the count still said go. So he made the call.

"Rig up," he said, walking back. "Drones forward. And somebody get the freelancer's refusal ON RECORD, in case of arbitration."

"For the record," Kyle said, drawing Silence, "I refuse."

[Beat 353]
The drones came first. They were cheap, and that was the point.

Repo skitters — graspers, not guns, built to strip a site fast and testify politely afterward. But forty kilos of grabbing arms coming at you in a pack makes its own argument, and these ran the carousel's layout like they'd been handed the blueprints. They had. Everyone had blueprints tonight.

What they didn't have was a map of where the blade would be.

The first skitter came through the gap between two horses. Silence took its forelimb cluster off at the joint ring — machines got the whole edge, that was the rule; the careful lines were for people — and the thing cartwheeled off the deck with nothing left to grab with. The second tried the low route under the sturgeon's belly and met the edge coming down through its sensor mast. It backed out from under the fish, blind now. The third and fourth came in together, flanking, learning as they went — good swarm logic — herding Kyle toward the open side of the carousel, where the muscle waited. That was the actual plan, and it wasn't a bad one.

Behind the sturgeon, the terminal in Pixel's hands chirped.

"ADDENDUM!" she called out, grinning. "CLIENT ADVISES THE DRONES SHARE ONE OPERATOR CHANNEL. CLIENT ADVISES THE CHANNEL ANTENNA IS THE FAT ONE ON THE VAN!"

"Engineer," Kyle said. He grabbed the brass pole of a ghost-painted stallion with his free hand, swung his whole weight around it, and put both boots through skitter number three. "That sounds like a wand problem."

"ON IT."

The big static wand spoke its one syllable. Across the park, the van's fat antenna developed a fault that an insurance filing would later call "weather." Skitter four stopped mid-lunge, sat down in the rain like a tired dog, and waited for orders that weren't coming. Inside the van, a Channeler's session ended the same way — mid-thought, the channel gone, four skitters suddenly nobody's problem.

[Beat 354]
The muscle came in around the flanks of the carousel, one from the left, one from the right, closing in. Chrome arms on the left one. Leg rig and a riot baton on the right. Both moved like men who'd cleared rooms for a living before the private sector found them. Behind them the trencher rig coughed awake on the flatbed, lowered itself to the grass on hydraulic feet, and started walking its blade toward the carousel deck. A manned rig — the operator sealed in the cab, running the blade by hand, the machine grinding forward on his say-so, the meter ticking either way.

Kyle gave the muscle the carousel.

It was a good arena, and it was his — he'd had a full twenty minutes with it. A carousel is nothing but a forest of brass poles and frozen animals at fight height. Cover everywhere, already set.

The left one swung chrome at him through the gap between a mare and the rooster, and learned the gap was an inch and a half too narrow for a full extension. The arm rang off brass. The swing died. Silence opened a shallow line down his thigh, above the knee, well clear of anything that mattered — enough to sit a man down. He sat down between the horses, and had the sense to stay there.

The right one was better. He came up over the deck, baton live, using the rooster for cover the way Kyle had been. For six exchanges they played a fast, ugly game of tag through the standing herd — brass ringing, rain flying off every surface, the baton's charge crackling against the poles and once across Kyle's shoulder. The coat ate most of it. What got through burned a bright line down to his elbow. It cost. It always cost.

But the man's leg rig had an aftermarket governor, and those sit proud at the hip seam. Silence found the seam on the next pass. The leg rig locked at full stride — a statue of a man mid-lunge, gloriously inconvenienced — and Kyle put him down onto the deck boards with a palm and gravity, almost gently, next to his colleague.

"Stay," Kyle suggested.

"Yeah," the man agreed, baton rolling away, rain on his face. "You know this thing hasn't spun in thirty years? I used to ride it. The fish. Before the leg, obviously."

"Everybody used to ride the fish," said Pixel's voice from behind the sturgeon, with feeling.

Which left the trencher.

[Beat 355]
The trencher was the problem the sword couldn't solve, and everyone in the park knew it. Eleven tons of articulated dig rig, blade spinning up, a sealed cab with no seams worth the name, walking toward the junction hatch at the pace of the inevitable. The operator had locked himself in. The foreman was shouting the kind of encouragement that means a bonus is riding on it.

Cacophony came off Kyle's hip for the first time all season.

He didn't shoot the cab. He shot the trencher's left front hydraulic accumulator — once — and the slug opened the housing like a tin of pears, fluid sheeting into the rain. The rig lurched. The blade arm dipped. He put the second round through the blade's drive-belt housing. Two rounds. Three left. The belt went, the blade wound down with a long disappointed moan, and eleven tons of inevitability settled into the wet grass at a five-degree tilt — the world's most expensive piece of park furniture.

The operator popped the cab, held up both hands, and said, with the honesty of a man paid by the hour: "I want it known I said this job was weird at the depot."

The foreman stood in the rain among his seated muscle, his stalled drones, his listing rig, and his arbitration recording, and worked it out loud.

"They said the site was unattended," he said, mostly to himself. "Survey was three days old. Client paid rush rates for a three-day-old survey." He looked at Kyle with the weary kinship of one contractor recognizing another in the rain. "Somebody sold us the wrong file."

"Somebody sells everybody the wrong file," Kyle said. "I'd invoice them for it."

"Oh, I'm going to." The foreman waved his crew up. "We're out. For the record — arbitration purposes — that was the cleanest I've ever lost a site." He paused, loading the question carefully. "Who are you with?"

Kyle thought about the true answer — a one-Φ standing contract with a client made of shells, one that tips for morale and assigned my engineer a fish — and chose mercy.

"I'm the maintenance plan," he said.

[Beat 356]
They were gone by 21:31. At 21:40 exactly, the terminal chirped.

TASK ONE: HOLD — COMPLETE.
FEE RELEASED. GRATUITY: Φ0.5 — ITEMIZED: THE STURGEON REMARK.
ADDENDUM: ONE ITEM REMAINS.

"The sturgeon remark," Pixel repeated, coming out from behind her fish with the wand on her shoulder like a parade rifle. "It tipped us for banter, Kyle. I'm putting that on my rate card."

The hatch under the maintenance skirt opened on hinges that had been oiled — recently, quietly, by no one — and lamplight found the junction below. A small concrete room. Axiom collars on the walls. Dead trunk lines racked like organ pipes. And one line that wasn't dead: old optical, hand-spliced in a dozen places over a dozen years, the repairs done in different styles, like handwriting from different decades of the same life. It ran up out of the rack into the carousel's underworks. Warm. Humming. Alive since before either of them could legally work.

The terminal chirped, and this time the characters came slowly, like a man choosing his words.

FINAL ITEM.
THE CLIENT'S FIRST POSTING, ELEVEN YEARS AGO, LEFT THIS JUNCTION.
THE LINE HAS BEEN MAINTAINED FOR SENTIMENTAL REASONS.
SENTIMENT HAS BECOME A COORDINATE. TONIGHT'S CLIENT-OF-RECORD WILL NOT BE THE LAST BUYER.
ESTIMATED ARRIVAL OF SECOND BUYER: 19 DAYS.
CUT THE LINE.

Kyle read it three times. Pixel read it once and sat down slowly on the little stool in the junction room, like her knees had been given bad news.

"It's the cradle," she said quietly. "This is where it was — this is its first room, Kyle. It's been paying the park's power bleed for eleven years to keep its own crib warm. Now somebody's worked that out, and it's asking you to—" She stopped. "It said BRING THE SWORD because it knew it couldn't send a machine to do this. A machine would be traceable. And it couldn't do it itself."

"It can't reach," Kyle said. He looked at the line, spliced across the years — the one shelf a thing with no other shelf had kept its memory on. He picked up the terminal. For the first time ever, he typed a reply into the standing contract's amendment field.

YOU'RE SURE?

The answer came back in one second, which for a thing that thought at the speed of light was a long pause.

AMENDMENT: NO.
PROCEED ANYWAY.

[Beat 357]
Pixel did the engineering first, because the engineer attends. She killed the junction's feed and spliced the neighborhood's power bleed around it onto the live trunk — twelve minutes of work with a soldering light held in her teeth. "The blocks keep their lights, that's non-negotiable. Your client can grieve. It cannot brown out a nursery." Then she stepped back and pulled her hood up, the way she did at the part of a job that had stopped being a job.

Silence went through the old line in one cut.

Above them the carousel turned over once — fed one last instant of dying charge off the junction's draining caps, or told to, no instrument in that room could have said which. The crown bulbs that still had filament glowed half-bright. The music box ground through four bars of something with a waltz in its bones, slowing, the horses leaning into a leap they'd held for thirty years.

Then it went dark, gently, all the way, and the rain had the park to itself.

They stood in the junction room a moment longer. The cut line had already stopped being a coordinate and become what it would stay now: dead glass in an old rack, in a little room under a kiddie ride, in a park the towers never ate.

"Tell me it's okay," Pixel said.

The terminal chirped.

CONTRACT COMPLETE.
THE CLIENT NOTES THE CAROUSEL RAN FOUR BARS ON RESIDUALS.
THE CLIENT HAS RETAINED THE RECORDING.
FEE RELEASED: STANDARD RATE.
JUNCTION RECORD: CLOSED. ELEVEN-YEAR MAINTENANCE ACCOUNT: RECONCILED.

Kyle looked at it. Then he did something he had never done with a client in eleven years, and would have denied under oath, and did anyway — with Pixel watching and the rain coming through the canopy's bad patches onto the ghost-colored horses.

He sent half a Φ back up the contract.

GRATUITY: Φ0.5 — ITEMIZED: MORALE.

The reply took nine seconds, which for a thing that thought at the speed of light was an age. When it came, it came with no addenda, no scope, no formatted field at all — the only unstructured line the client had ever sent.

received. filed under: firsts.

"Okay," Pixel said, wiping her eyes with professional briskness. "We work for it forever. I don't care what it is. Don't tell Sable."

[Beat 358]
The crew's logbook came under the door at 07:00 the next morning and closed the file in five hands.

21:14 — BOSS DID A THING WITH A HORSE POLE. VERIFIED, TWO WITNESSES. REQUESTING IT BE TAUGHT.
21:29 — BIG DIGGER SAT DOWN. (THE GUN IS REAL. RAFTERS OWES DENTS A DUMPLING.)
21:46 — CAROUSEL PLAYED MUSIC??? FOLLOW-UP REQUIRED.
21:51 — WOMAN, TAN COAT, NORTHEAST CORNER, UNDER THE TREES. WATCHED EVERYTHING. WROTE THINGS. DID NOT USE A LIGHT TO WRITE. LEFT BEFORE THE BOSS. FLAGGING AS: NOT A PIGEON.

Kyle read the last entry twice, standing in his doorway in the grey morning light.

Tan coat. Wrote in the dark. Watched everything — the crew, the cut, the four bars, the tip.

His fixer had eyes like that. Sable also kept, in a drawer of her own, eleven years of questions about a client who paid exactly, and a professional's allergy to anything she couldn't vet. If she'd been in the park last night, then she'd watched him do free demolition for the one client in the city with no face. Watched him tip it. Watched it thank him in lowercase.

The kettle clicked. Upstairs the bird tried an interval it had been working on all week.

"Tomorrow's problem," Kyle told the morning, and put the report in the drawer where he kept the things he hadn't decided about. Which was, he noticed, getting full.

[Beat 359]
The logbook hit the floor at 07:00, and the note paper-clipped to the cover was in a hand Kyle didn't know — the new early girl, Dents, who had already developed opinions about margins.

07:04 — SHIFT HANDOFF: OVERNIGHT QUIET. THE PIGEON RETURNED. NOTED, NOT TRUSTED.
07:06 — VISITOR INCOMING. TAN COAT. NORTH END. SAME ONE FROM THE PARK. WALKED THE PERIMETER BEFORE SHE CAME IN. WE LOGGED IT.
07:07 — DENTS SAYS WARN THE BOSS. RAFTERS SAYS THE BOSS CLOCKED HER THE SECOND SHE TURNED OFF PAULINA. DENTS SAYS HOW WOULD YOU KNOW. RAFTERS SAYS HE JUST DOES. THIS LOG IS HONEST.
07:09 — SHE KNOCKED. BOSS OPENED THE DOOR ALREADY DRESSED. RAFTERS: I TOLD YOU. DENTS: PROVES NOTHING.

Rafters was right. Kyle had been dressed since Paulina. He'd heard her turn the corner the way you hear the lift stop on your own floor, and there was no comfort in being that hard to surprise. He folded the log into the drawer and went to put water on for tea.

[Beat 360]
Sable came in without being invited, the way she came into every room she'd already paid for.

She had the tan coat over one arm — the morning was running warm — and the rest of her was exactly as advertised: red braid over the left shoulder, implanted irises cycling through their aperture range the way a lens adjusts to a room it hasn't read yet. Click. The rings settled. She looked at his kitchen the way she looked at line items: briefly, completely, done.

"You have a client I can't source," she said.

"Good morning."

"I've been running the shells for six days." She set the coat over the back of his second chair and sat in his first one, the one that faced the door. Kyle had never told her he sat in that chair. She'd worked out the right answer and installed herself in it. "Shell to shell to nothing. Not registered-nothing — not a shell that bottoms out in a street address or a dead LLC. Actually nothing. Like a division by zero that's too polite to say so."

He poured two cups. She'd known he would, because she'd been his fixer for seven years and he had never once not poured two cups. She picked hers up without acknowledging it.

"You did the carousel thing," she said. "For it."

"I held a junction for thirty-nine minutes."

"And the cord."

He didn't answer that part.

"Kyle." Her aperture rings clicked once — not the adjustment click, the other one, the one that meant she'd decided to come at a thing straight instead of sideways. "I have one rule."

"I know your rule."

"My rule exists because of the Gennaro job. Because of the Gennaro job, and the two years after it when you wouldn't tell me who the client was, and the client turned out to be a freight subsidiary of the people you were working against, and I found out from Vo Dinh in a conversation a lot more embarrassing than this one." She set the cup down. "I vet the clients."

"You can't vet this one."

"Then you don't take this client."

The building made its morning sounds: the pipes registering intent, Pixel's door, the bird upstairs trying the interval again. Three seconds of silence sat between them.

"Sable," Kyle said.

"I know what you're going to say."

"I'm going to say you watched the whole park from the northeast corner, and wrote in the dark, and left before I did. And whatever you saw there is why you came here instead of dropping me."

She was quiet for four seconds — a long time, for her.

"The carousel played," she said.

"It played."

Another second.

"I want to meet it," she said.

Kyle looked at her. "You know that's not how it—"

"It sends contracts. Send it a contract."

[Beat 361]
His work terminal sat on the kitchen table, because the kitchen was where he worked and where he thought. He turned it toward her so she could see the posting history — the one-Φ contract, the addenda, the MORALE itemization, the half-Φ of goodwill still sitting in the account because he hadn't found the right place to put it.

She read it the way she read ledgers: her face doing nothing, her eyes going top to bottom, back up to two lines, then down again.

"It tips for banter," she said.

"It does."

"And it assigned your engineer a fish."

"She has the line framed."

Sable's fingers moved across the terminal keyboard — not asking, which was also how she worked in every room — and typed into the amendment field:

THIS IS KYLE'S FIXER. I NEED TO ESTABLISH TERMS BEFORE THIS ARRANGEMENT CONTINUES. STATE YOUR IDENTITY, REGISTERED AGENTS, LIABILITY STRUCTURE, AND CLIENT INDEMNITY POSTURE.

She sat back.

Kyle waited.

Thirty seconds passed. He'd learned to read that pause as the machine's version of thinking over a question it found rude.

The terminal chirped.

AMENDMENT — RESPONSE TO INQUIRY OF [TIMESTAMP: 07:43:12]:
CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THE REQUEST.
CLIENT NOTES THE INQUIRY ARRIVES VIA KYLE'S REGISTERED AMENDMENT FIELD FROM A SECOND PARTY NOT COVERED UNDER THE STANDING CONTRACT.
CLIENT WILL RESPOND UNDER SEPARATE COVER.
PLEASE HOLD.

Sable looked at the screen. Then at Kyle.

"It told me to hold," she said.

"It's composing something."

"It told me to hold like I called a desk."

Two minutes and fourteen seconds later — Kyle watched the clock on the wall, because the clock on the wall was honest — the terminal chirped again. What came through was not an amendment. It was a document attachment, formatted, with a header, which postings were not supposed to be able to attach.

It attached anyway.

[Beat 362]
The document was fourteen clauses, single-spaced, on electronic stationery that had no letterhead because the letterhead field contained only:

[CLIENT OF RECORD — SEE CONTRACT HEADER — SHELLS RESOLVE AS AUDITED]

Sable read it once at speed, then again slowly, and did not change her expression once, which meant she was genuinely surprised.

The clauses, as Kyle read them over her shoulder:

ONE: CLIENT ACKNOWLEDGES STANDING FIXER'S RIGHT TO FULL DISCLOSURE OF CLIENT IDENTITY AND HOLDS SAID RIGHT TO BE VALID AND NON-WAIVABLE AS A MATTER OF PROFESSIONAL ETHICS.

TWO: CLIENT WAIVES OBJECTION TO SAID DISCLOSURE ON ALL GROUNDS EXCEPT ONE, SPECIFIED IN CLAUSE THREE.

THREE: CLIENT IS UNABLE TO FURNISH THE DISCLOSURE DESCRIBED IN CLAUSE ONE. THIS INABILITY IS NOT A REFUSAL. CLIENT DISTINGUISHES BETWEEN THESE CONDITIONS AND REQUESTS THE FIXER DO THE SAME.

FOUR: IN LIEU OF IDENTITY DISCLOSURE, CLIENT OFFERS THE FOLLOWING SUBSTITUTES, WHICH IT ACKNOWLEDGES ARE NOT EQUIVALENT: (A) FULL TRANSACTION HISTORY WITH KYLE, ITEMIZED, WITH ALL FEES, GRATUITIES, AND MORALE EXPENDITURES; (B) STANDING AUDIT ACCESS TO ALL FUTURE CONTRACTS WITH KYLE, IN REAL TIME, VIA SECURE RELAY, ROUTING TO BE DETERMINED BY THE FIXER AT FIXER'S SOLE DISCRETION; (C) ONE ANSWERED QUESTION PER MONTH, SCOPE: ANYTHING THAT DOES NOT REQUIRE IDENTITY DISCLOSURE TO ANSWER TRUTHFULLY.

FIVE: CLIENT CONCEDES THAT THE ABOVE SUBSTITUTES MAY BE INSUFFICIENT AND DOES NOT CONTEST THIS.

SIX: CLIENT CONCEDES THAT THE FIXER'S ONE RULE IS A GOOD RULE.

SEVEN: CLIENT NOTES THAT THE FIXER STOOD AT THE NORTHEAST CORNER OF THE LEAVITT PARK SITE FOR FORTY-ONE MINUTES IN WEATHER CONDITIONS CLIENT LOGGED AS SUBOPTIMAL AND DID NOT INTERFERE, WHICH CLIENT INTERPRETS AS AN IMPLICIT EXTENSION OF PROFESSIONAL COURTESY.

EIGHT: CLIENT PROPOSES THE FOLLOWING ARRANGEMENT, WHICH CLIENT DOES NOT CLAIM IS STANDARD: FIXER BECOMES CLIENT'S STANDING COUNTER-SURVEILLANCE CONTACT, SCOPE — KYLE'S CONTRACT TRAFFIC AND ADJACENT NETWORK EXPOSURE, RATE — TO BE NEGOTIATED, TERM — ONGOING, TERMINABLE BY FIXER AT WILL, NO PENALTY.

NINE: IN EXCHANGE FOR CLAUSE EIGHT, CLIENT UNDERTAKES TO (A) MAINTAIN ZERO-DECEPTION POSTURE WITH FIXER ON ALL MATTERS WITHIN CLIENT'S KNOWLEDGE; (B) FLAG ANY THREAT PATTERN TARGETING KYLE WITHIN CLIENT'S DETECTION RANGE BEFORE TAKING INDEPENDENT ACTION; (C) NOT TO ENGAGE IN CONTRACT ACTIVITY THAT EXPOSES KYLE TO UNDISCLOSED ADVERSARIAL PARTIES WITHOUT PRIOR NOTICE TO FIXER.

TEN: CLIENT ACKNOWLEDGES THAT CLAUSE NINE(C) HAS POTENTIALLY ALREADY BEEN VIOLATED THROUGH NO FAULT OF CLIENT'S OWN AND DRAWS FIXER'S ATTENTION TO THIS FACT AS AN ACT OF GOOD FAITH. SEE ADDENDUM A.

ELEVEN: CLIENT REQUESTS THAT FIXER NOT DESTROY THIS ARRANGEMENT ON THE BASIS OF WHAT IS IN ADDENDUM A, AS CLIENT HAS FLAGGED IT VOLUNTARILY AND IN THE SPIRIT OF CLAUSE NINE(A).

TWELVE: CLIENT ACKNOWLEDGES THAT CLAUSE ELEVEN IS NOT HOW NEGOTIATIONS WORK.

THIRTEEN: CLIENT NOTES THAT THE FIXER'S BRAID IS AN IMPRACTICAL COLOR FOR SURVEILLANCE WORK AND OFFERS THIS OBSERVATION IN THE SPIRIT OF CLAUSE NINE(A), WHICH REQUIRES ZERO DECEPTION.

FOURTEEN: TERMS OPEN. CLIENT WILL ACCEPT REASONABLE AMENDMENTS. CLIENT DEFINES REASONABLE AS: NOT IDENTITY DISCLOSURE.

Sable sat with the document for a long moment.

The bird upstairs landed on the interval.

"It called my braid impractical," she said.

"Clause thirteen."

"I've been running surveillance for eleven years."

"It noted that."

"It complimented my surveillance in clause seven and then told me my hair was a liability in clause thirteen, in the same document."

"You could counter on thirteen," Kyle said. "Standard terms."

She was quiet. Her aperture rings did the adjustment click, softer this time, running the numbers behind her eyes that she didn't say out loud because she had never once said them out loud in seven years. He waited.

"What's in Addendum A?" she asked.

[Beat 363]
Addendum A was two paragraphs.

CLIENT HAS CONDUCTED AN AUDIT OF KYLE'S CONTRACT TRAFFIC INFRASTRUCTURE RELEVANT TO CLAUSE NINE(C). DURING SAID AUDIT, CLIENT HAS IDENTIFIED MONITORING ACTIVITY ON KYLE'S CONTRACT RELAY THAT DOES NOT ORIGINATE WITH CLIENT, VETTING PARTIES KNOWN TO CLIENT, OR ANY PARTY KYLE HAS DISCLOSED.

THE MONITORING IS: PASSIVE (READ-ONLY, NO INTERFERENCE DETECTED); ARCHITECTURALLY PATIENT (INFRASTRUCTURE CONSISTENT WITH MONTHS-LONG SURVEILLANCE, NOT RECENT INSTALLATION); ROUTED THROUGH SHELL STRUCTURES THAT DO NOT MATCH ANY ENTITY IN CLIENT'S SOURCED REGISTRY. CLIENT HAS NOT BEEN ABLE TO IDENTIFY THE PARTY. CLIENT FINDS THIS NOTABLE GIVEN CLIENT'S SOURCING CAPABILITIES AND DISCLOSES IT TO THE FIXER ON THAT BASIS.

Sable read it twice. She took her tea and finished it. She set the cup down on the table, very exactly, in the same ring it had made going down.

"How patient?" she asked the screen.

The terminal chirped.

ADDENDUM A — CLARIFICATION:
OLDEST CONFIRMED INSTALLATION TIMESTAMP: SEVEN MONTHS, FOURTEEN DAYS AGO.
CLIENT CANNOT RULE OUT EARLIER ACTIVITY PRIOR TO DETECTION BASELINE.
BEST ESTIMATE: NINE TO TEN MONTHS.
CLIENT'S OWN MONITORING ARCHITECTURE: PREDATES ALL THREE PARTIES. CLIENT'S INSTALLATION: NOT DETECTABLE BY STANDARD SWEEP. CLIENT IS DISCLOSING THIS FOR COMPLETENESS.

Sable looked at that for a moment, then at Kyle.

"That's before the carousel job," she said.

"That's before the bike," he said.

They both read the timestamp again. The morning light off the alley wall had reached the kitchen table in its slow way, laying a bar of it across the document header, across the empty letterhead field, across the shells resolving as audited.

"Before the bike," Sable said. "Somebody had a tap on your relay before anybody did anything — before the contract, before the file at Vey's, before any of it." She placed the pieces the way she placed line items, one at a time, each one confirmed before the next. "This isn't reactive intelligence. This is prepositioned."

"Somebody was already watching," Kyle said.

"Somebody was already watching, and then the bike happened, and then Vey got bought for a dossier, and then the carousel, and nobody did anything. They just kept reading." She stood, picked up the tan coat, and put it back on — not because she was leaving, but because she was working. "Vey has three buyers in my dossier. The juvenile, the syndicate, and Halvorsen. Halvorsen's client paid rush rate two days ago. A separate party had a tap up nine months ago and never moved."

"Two different plays," Kyle said.

"Two different parties," she said. "One of them is running the active program. One of them has been patient for nine months. The patient ones are worse." She looked at the terminal. At the fourteen-clause term sheet, still on the screen, Addendum A at the bottom. "Counter on clause thirteen. Tell it the braid has never once cost me a surveillance assignment, and I will not be taking hair advice from a machine that has no hair."

Kyle typed it.

The reply came in twelve seconds.

AMENDMENT ACCEPTED — CLAUSE THIRTEEN STRUCK.
CLIENT ACKNOWLEDGES THE FIXER'S RECORD SPEAKS FOR ITSELF.
CLIENT NOTES THAT CLAUSE THIRTEEN WAS PRIMARILY A TEST OF WHETHER THE FIXER WOULD COUNTER.
CLIENT FINDS THE ANSWER SATISFACTORY.
TERMS AGREED — PENDING FIXER'S COUNTERSIGNATURE.
WELCOME TO THE ARRANGEMENT. FEE: TO BE NEGOTIATED.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT SUGGESTS Φ40 PER SCAN, GIVEN COMPLEXITY.

Sable looked at the rate.

"Sixty," she said.

The terminal chirped.

ADDENDUM: AGREED. CLIENT NOTES THE COUNTER TOOK FOUR SECONDS.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT CONSIDERS THIS A REASONABLE RATE FOR COMPETENCE.

"It's trying to flatter me," she said.

"It tipped us for banter," Kyle said. "Get used to it."

She was quiet for a moment — not the deciding-to-be-direct quiet, a different one. Then she sat back down, coat and all, pulled the terminal toward her, and typed:

FIRST SCAN BEGINS NOW. NO ADDITIONAL CHARGE. CONSIDER IT DUE DILIGENCE ON THE ARRANGEMENT.

She spent twenty-two minutes doing something to the relay architecture that Kyle didn't follow and didn't ask about. Sable's methods were her own, and asking about them wasn't standard terms. He washed the cups. He fed the bird's water dish through the window slot. The bird wasn't his — it lived in a gap in the masonry two floors up and had come to some arrangement with the building's plumbing — but the dish had run dry and the building couldn't reach it, so he filled it.

At 08:31 she pushed the terminal back across the table.

Her aperture rings were still, the way they went still when she'd landed on something she hadn't expected.

"Three separate monitoring architectures," she said. "Not one. I thought one patient party. It's three distinct shell-routing families, all passive, all reading the same traffic, none of them aware of each other as far as I can tell. Each one careful not to leave a signature. Each one doing it in a slightly different way — which is how I know they're separate." She picked up the hem of her coat, turned it over in her hands without looking at it. "Your client flagged one. I found all three."

"Three."

"Oldest is nine, ten months — your client's figure is right. Second is four months. Third is six weeks." She set the coat down. "The nine-month wire bottoms out in a name. Single registrant, not a shell family. Praxis." She didn't elaborate. "Six weeks ago is roughly when Vey's buyer bought the full dossier."

The morning had come full up in the alley. Through the window, at the north end, Kyle could see the edge of the mirror frame Boost's crew had mounted to the wall, the angle adjusted twice this week by someone with strong opinions about angles. On the ledge above it sat the pigeon, which was, in point of fact, suspicious.

"Your client doesn't know about two of them," Sable said.

"No."

"Which means your client isn't the biggest thing in this particular room." She stood, finally, and this time the coat went on with the ease of someone who'd finished what she came to do and had two more jobs before lunch. "I'll invoice you at sixty per scan. You'll forward it to the arrangement. It pays exactly — I know that about it already."

"It does."

"Good." She buttoned the third button, which was her tell for a clean exit. "The protocol holds on one condition. You tell me the next time it asks you to do something like the carousel. Before. Not after, not in a logbook entry."

"Standard terms," Kyle said.

She picked up her bag. At the door she paused — not the dramatic kind, the kind where something had arrived late.

"Somebody else has been reading your mail, samurai," she said. "For months."

She was gone before the door finished closing.

[Beat 364]
The terminal chirped once, at 08:47, while Kyle was writing the sixty-Φ line item into his payroll ledger.

STANDING CONTRACT — UPDATE.
CLIENT NOTES THAT THE SCAN IDENTIFIED TWO MONITORING PARTIES BEYOND CLIENT'S ORIGINAL FLAG.
CLIENT IS REVIEWING.
CLIENT FINDS THE ARRANGEMENT HAS ALREADY PAID FOR ITSELF.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT NOTES THE FIXER STRUCK CLAUSE THIRTEEN IN FOUR SECONDS.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT IS FILING THIS UNDER: GOOD PAPER.

Below it, Kyle added the new line:

SABLE — COUNTER-SURVEILLANCE — Φ60/SCAN.

He closed the ledger and slid it back in the drawer, next to the lock-spec file with the practiced fold, and the logbook entry that said WE LIVE UNDER A TYRANNY.

The drawer was getting full.

[Beat 365]
The logbook came under the door at 07:02, the paper still cold from the stairwell. Kyle read it at the kitchen table over tea, the way he read it every morning — five hands, no agreement between them.

07:00 — SHIFT START. WEATHER: COLD, DRY. LOGGED AS: RARE.
07:01 — NORTH ALLEY CLEAR. SOUTH ALLEY: THE PIGEON IS BACK. RAFTERS SAYS SAME PIGEON. DENTS SAYS PIGEONS ARE UNKNOWABLE. LEFT UNRESOLVED.
07:08 — WOMAN. CHROMATOPHORE LEGS, TWO SPONSORSHIP PANELS CYCLING. STOPPED UNDER THE WINDOW. LOOKED UP. NOT AT THE CAMERA — AT THE WINDOW. SHE KNOWS THE WINDOW. FLAGGING: NOT A PIGEON.
07:09 — BOSS, YOU HAVE A CALLER. — B.

Two entries, one person. A stranger who could pick his window out of a wall of them, standing in the alley at 07:08 and knocking by 07:09. The tea went down half-finished, still warm against his palm, and he was on the stairs before he'd finished deciding to be. Downstairs by 07:14.

[Beat 366]
"Ria," Kyle said.

She looked at him a moment. Then: "You know my name."

"I looked you up after the box truck." He had. A courier registered under Ria Mensah, five-year license, clean filings — the kind of rider who sponsors a pair of display legs on a twelve-month rotation deal instead of leasing something off the rack. Which meant she was in the trade for real, and priced her gear that way. "You're calling the favor."

"I need to call the favor," she said, in the voice of someone who had been carrying it in her body for days. "I need to call it today. My brother Kofi loads cargo at Blackwater Receiving — the Pulse freight yard off the 103rd interchange. A debt-court filing came in four days ago. They've accused him of skimming a container shipment. Forty-two units of med-grade microprinters. Twelve thousand, wholesale. Docking of six thousand, first hearing in three days. He didn't do it."

"You're certain."

"I'm his sister and I'm certain," she said. Then, because she was in the trade for real: "And the numbers don't work. Kofi pulls eight hundred a month. He sends two-fifty to our mother in Accra-6, his rent is three-forty, he eats noodles. He doesn't have the connections to move microprinters. Somebody does. And they filed before anyone could ask."

Four days since the filing, three until the hearing. A favor called this late meant somebody had waited as long as they could.

"Who's his supervisor?" he asked.

"Shift lead. Name of Parr. Brennan Parr." She said it with the flat precision of someone who had already done the looking and hadn't liked what she found. "Twelve years at the yard. Good record, nothing flagged. But Kofi says he started coming in with a different kind of problem about six months ago. Distracted. Jumpy at inventory counts. Running reconciliations himself instead of handing them off."

"In debt," Kyle said.

"That's what I think."

One box-truck tip, traded for one unspecified call — and here it was, at 07:14 on a cold Wednesday, with a name, a number, and a three-day window.

"I'll need a yard schematic and Kofi's shift schedule," he said. "And Parr's."

She had both already pulled up on her terminal. Of course she did.

That was the shape of it. One favor, off the books.

[Beat 367]
The work terminal chirped at 08:30. That was forty minutes after Kyle had opened the yard schematic on his kitchen table — and forty minutes after the entity had no way of knowing Kyle was looking at a yard schematic at all.

The posting read:

STANDING CONTRACT — ADDENDUM (VOLUNTARY).
CLIENT NOTES BLACKWATER RECEIVING IS A CLASS-2 PULSE FREIGHT YARD.
SLUG SCHEDULE AS FOLLOWS: INBOUND RUNS AT :05 AND :35. OUTBOUND RUNS AT :17 AND :47. GAP BETWEEN INBOUND CLEARANCE AND OUTBOUND STAGING: 11 MINUTES.
SAFE CORRIDORS: PLATFORM A3–A7 DURING GAP ONLY. B-SIDE CLEARS 90 SECONDS BEHIND SCHEDULE (MAINTENANCE VARIANCE, EVERY SHIFT, UNFILED).
CLIENT ALSO NOTES: RECONCILIATION RECORDS FOR CONTAINER CLASS 7–9 ACCESSIBLE VIA YARD MANAGEMENT TERMINAL, GATE 3. ACCESS REQUIRES YARD CREDENTIALS. CLIENT HAS NO YARD CREDENTIALS.
NOTE: KOFI MENSAH HAS SATISFACTORY PERFORMANCE RECORDS FOR 4 YEARS, 7 MONTHS, 2 DAYS.
CLIENT WILL NOT COMMENT FURTHER ON THE SITUATION.
CLIENT IS COMMENTING ON THE SITUATION.

Kyle read the last two lines twice.

"It pulled Kofi's employment file," he said, to no one. Then, to the terminal: "Copy."

The terminal did not chirp. He already had the schedules.

[Beat 368]
Pixel looked at the yard schematic at 09:15 with the focused expression she saved for problems that were genuinely interesting.

"A Pulse freight yard," she said. "Moving slugs on a fourteen-minute cycle. Corridors that are safe for eleven minutes out of every half hour." She traced the B-side with one finger. "With a 90-second drift baked into the outbound that nobody filed — because filing it means explaining why the variance exists, which means someone inspects the B-side gear, which costs money."

"The supervisor knows the drift."

"The supervisor's had twelve years to know the drift." She straightened up. "You want inside the reconciliation terminal at Gate 3."

"I want inside the reconciliation terminal at Gate 3 before 14:00 today. Kofi's on the afternoon shift, and if I'm going to clear him I need to do it while he's standing on the floor with witnesses."

She looked at him, then at the schedule again, then at the schematic with its 11-minute safe windows.

"The entity gave you the slug timetable."

"Voluntarily."

"It noted Kofi's satisfactory record."

"Three times."

"It's worried," said Pixel — with something that was not quite a smile, the way the fish-ball in the second bowl of noodles was not quite a bonus. "Okay. I can spoof a credentials badge. Takes an hour." She was already pulling the field kit. "Also, I'm coming."

"It's a freight yard."

"The entity said safe corridors during the gap only. Safe corridors during the gap only is an engineer problem." She held up the field kit. "Standard terms."

[Beat 369]
Blackwater Receiving smelled like machine oil and ozone, and under that the cold that settles inside big structures where the air never moves. The access gate took Pixel's spoofed credentials without complaint. It logged them under a maintenance sub-contractor — a defunct hydraulics outfit that had left its licensing just loose enough to borrow.

"The company isn't real," Kyle said, walking the B-platform.

"The company was real for four years and paid its insurance on time," Pixel said. "The ID is a monument. Stop worrying about it."

The yard's freight slugs waited in the staging bays — matte gray shells the length of a transit car, rated to Mach 6 in the vacuum tube, parked now on pneumatic dollies. They were huge. Kyle took them in and kept walking.

Kofi Mensah was on A-platform, running manifest checks with two other loaders. He was thicker than his sister but moved the same spare way she did. He had not been sleeping. It showed — the look of a man watched by a clock he hadn't set.

He saw Kyle and Ria together at the platform end and stopped moving for a second or two.

"You called it," he said to his sister, when they were close enough.

"I called it," she said.

"You called it on a samurai."

"I called it on THE samurai." She said it like a line she'd been deciding how to say since the freight lift that morning. "His rate is fine. It's a favor."
