Kofi looked at Kyle straight — a man with three days left and a habit of spending carefully. "Parr knows something's coming," he said. "He's been doing the reconciliations himself. Terminal three, Gate 3, every day between 13:00 and 14:00. Alone. That's where the gap is — I can see in my own manifest that units got re-logged under a different container class after the batch was sealed. I can't touch the terminal. I'm flagged as a subject of inquiry."

"I can," Kyle said. "I need thirteen minutes at Gate 3."

"The gap window is eleven minutes."

"I know."

Kofi thought about it. "B-platform's outbound is ninety seconds late every shift."

"I know that too."

The clearance panel above A-platform went green. Somewhere below the floor the first inbound slug slowed out of transit speed with a sound like a building drawing breath.

[Beat 370]
Gate 3 was a glass-fronted reconciliation office set into the yard wall where B-platform met the admin corridor. One management terminal. A view of the staging bay. No windows onto the yard interior — which meant Parr, when he sat in it, could watch the slugs queue but not the platform approach from the south.

Kyle went in at 13:11, inside the gap, while the inbound clearance light still cycled red and the B-platform corridor ran its ninety-second lag. Pixel was outside with the schematic on her terminal and both countdown panels in view.

The reconciliation files were built for deniability. Problem entries reclassified under a container category that legitimately handled mixed cargo. Timestamps that read like clerical error to anyone who didn't know better.

Kyle knew better.

He found the re-logs for the Φ12,000 microprinter batch. Original manifest clean, sealed under Kofi's ID at 21:43 six days back. The reclassification entered at 02:17 the next morning, under a supervisor override that resolved to Parr.

Outside the window, the outbound clearance panel clicked from amber to red. Six minutes.

He copied the records, the timestamps, and the override ID into a sealed package flagged for yard security. Then he entered Parr's override code as the revised source of record in the formal inquiry. The system accepted it. It was accurate. Kofi's name had been on the filing four days too long.

ADDENDUM TO INQUIRY 7741-R: REVISED SOURCE OF RECORD. SUPERVISOR OVERRIDE 09-PAR, TIMESTAMP 02:17. SEE ATTACHED RECONCILIATION DIFF.

He hit submit at 13:18.

"Three minutes," Pixel said on comms.

He was out of Gate 3 at 13:19.

The records were corrected, on the record, under Parr's own credentials. What it would cost him, he'd find out later.

[Beat 371]
Parr was not a big man. He carried twelve years of institutional weight and moved like it — steady, unhurried. Once that unhurried had been confidence. Lately it had turned to care. He came onto the B-platform at 13:31 with a terminal in his hand and the face of a man whose reconciliation filing had just pinged him about a revision.

He saw Kyle first, because Kyle was standing in the open. Then he saw Kofi on the platform behind him. Then he looked at the terminal again.

"You're not yard security," he said.

"No," Kyle said.

"You filed under my code."

"Your code filed under your code. I just found where it had been." Kyle held up his own terminal, records pulled. "The re-log was at 02:17, your override. The original manifest was clean. The inquiry's been revised to show the actual source."

Parr was quiet long enough for the outbound panel to run two cycles. He'd run a bad play. He'd known it was bad and run it anyway. Now it had caught up with him.

"How much do they have you for?" Kyle asked.

"Fourteen thousand. Rate adjusts." He looked at Kofi. "I needed one clean lift. Filed it back the whole way. Nobody was supposed to—" He stopped. "He was the easiest manifest to fit."

"He was. You'll want to talk to yard security."

"I know." The relief of a man who'd been bracing for the landing. "The collectors. They'll—"

"That's a separate problem," Kyle said. "Start with security."

[Beat 372]
The two men came off the south platform access at 13:47, while the second inbound slug was still slowing in the bay. They had the look of men on a fee — loose, unhurried, taking their time over ground they'd already walked. They'd seen Kyle come out of Gate 3 and worked out what it meant.

One carried a shock-bar clipped to his belt. The other had his hands free, which in Kyle's experience was worse.

"Yard's private property," the first said. "You're not credentialed."

"I was," Kyle said. "Hydraulics maintenance, sub-contract, third-party audit. I signed out at the gate." He watched both of them, and the slug bay behind them, and the panel overhead. Amber. Two minutes to outbound clearance. "You should go."

"The man we're here about," the second said, nodding back toward where Parr had gone, "has a payment arrangement. Arrangements don't get renegotiated by freelancers."

"The arrangement already changed," Kyle said. "Yard security has his statement."

They looked at each other. The first one unclipped the shock-bar.

Kyle did not reach for Cacophony.

The shock-bar came in high and right — the obvious side. The second man went low and left, reading off the first move. A pincer. It would have worked on a man who needed room to draw. Kyle wasn't drawing. He stepped inside the swing before it was halfway out, caught the man's wrist in both hands, and turned the bar's own momentum up and over the man's shoulder. The shock-bar fired against its owner's jacket. He sat down hard on the platform, blinking, deeply surprised.

The second man had reach and used it. A solid shove put Kyle back two steps to the platform edge. But two steps back was two steps toward the slug bay. The outbound panel had gone green. The floor hummed — the 14:17 building to transit speed in the tube below.

Kyle used one of those steps. He turned the man's next move aside, planted a heel on the edge for leverage, and brought his forearm across the collarbone. Not hard enough to break anything. Hard enough to end it. The second man sat down beside the first. Behind them the freight pod tore away down the tube, several tons of it, roaring.

"The 14:17 is on time today," Kyle said.

Neither of them said anything.

He left them on the platform and went to find Pixel.

[Beat 373]
The operations supervisor was a woman named Dreis. She'd been handed a great deal of information in the last twenty minutes and was working through it with the flat annoyance of someone whose day had become somebody else's problem. She shook Kyle's hand at 15:00 and gave him a receipt.

The receipt read, formally:

BLACKWATER RECEIVING — INQUIRY CLOSURE 7741-R.
SUBJECT: MENSAH, K. CHARGE: VACATED PER REVISED SOURCE OF RECORD.
DEBT-COURT FILING: RETRACTED.
SUPERVISOR PARR B. STATUS: SUSPENDED, PENDING REVIEW. RESTITUTION HEARING SCHEDULED.
NOTE: YARD SECURITY EXTENDS APPRECIATION FOR THIRD-PARTY AUDIT ASSISTANCE.
NOTE: YARD MANAGEMENT WOULD LIKE TO DISCUSS A STANDING AUDIT ARRANGEMENT.
NOTE: PENDING ARRANGEMENTS WITH YARD MANAGEMENT = OWED ONE (1) QUIET FAVOR, SCOPE: TBD, RATE: STANDARD.

Kyle read the last line.

"Standard terms," he said, which was either agreement or reflex. Dreis nodded — brisk, a woman who had added a line to a budget and closed the window on it.

Outside, on A-platform, Ria had a hand on her brother's arm and wasn't talking. Kofi was looking at the receipt on Kyle's terminal the way a man reads something that changes a number.

"Cleared," Kyle said. "On the record."

Kofi took a long breath. Pressed his thumbnail to the corner of the receipt.

"What do I owe you?" he asked.

"You don't," Kyle said. "Your sister did."

Ria looked at him. Her panels cycled from pharma-blue down to a low off-white — a rest state, or a power-save. "I want to run transport on your contract," she said, quietly. "When you need a driver, a loader, someone who knows the Pulse yard schedules." She said it like the favor was being paid back in installments she'd already worked out. "Permanent arrangement. My rate is fair."

"I know your rate," Kyle said. "I looked you up."

"And?"

"Reasonable," he said. It wasn't effusive, and he meant it exactly that way, and she took it exactly that way. They didn't shake hands. The terms were already set.

Kofi was clear. Parr was accountable. Kyle had a transport ally on a standing arrangement. Against that: the yard now knew when he was available, and the chain around him had one more link than it had that morning.

Cacophony had never left the holster.

[Beat 374]
The terminal chirped at 16:40, while Kyle was eating a late second bowl at Mrs. Chen's — early for the standing contract's usual hours, and she caught it with one look over the counter.

The posting read:

STANDING CONTRACT — ACKNOWLEDGMENT.
CLIENT HAS REVIEWED TODAY'S FILING (INQUIRY 7741-R, BLACKWATER RECEIVING).
CLIENT NOTES: DEBT-COURT DOCKET CLOSED. RECORD CLEAR.
CLIENT NOTES: THE 14:17 WAS ON TIME.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT HAS AUDITED BLACKWATER'S COLLECTOR ACCOUNTS FOR PARR, B.
ADDENDUM: THE COLLECTION AGENCY HOLDING PARR'S DEBT IS A REGISTERED SHELL.
ADDENDUM: THE SHELL'S PARENT ENTITY IS A SHELL.
ADDENDUM: THE PARENT'S PARENT IS A SHELL.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT RECOGNIZES THE FLAVOR.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT ADVISES: SABLE SHOULD SEE THIS.
NOTE: RIA MENSAH'S PULSE YARD SCHEDULING DATA HAS BEEN ADDED TO STANDING CONTRACT SCOPE AS A SUPPLEMENTARY RESOURCE. WITH HER PERMISSION. CLIENT ASKED.
NOTE: SHE SAID YES.
NOTE: IOWA CORRIDOR TRANSIT WINDOWS NOTED FOR RECORD. CLIENT HAS FILED FOR FUTURE REFERENCE.

Kyle read the last three lines, then set the bowl down.

Three shells deep, and patient. Nobody builds that for a single debt — the overhead's too high. You build it when you need it in a lot of places, for a long time, and you don't want anyone adding up the whole surface at once.

Mrs. Chen set the fifth dumpling in a fold of paper beside his hand without a word.

"For the errand," he told the bowl.

She'd already gone back to the wok.

He picked up the terminal and typed into the amendment field:

THE SHELLS. SEND WHAT YOU HAVE.

The reply came in four seconds, which was slow, for something that thought at light speed.

AMENDMENT: COMPILING.
NOTE: THIS IS THE THIRD SEPARATE SHELL FAMILY WITH IDENTICAL STRUCTURAL PATIENCE.
NOTE: CLIENT WOULD LIKE THE RECORD TO REFLECT THAT THIS IS CONCERNING.
NOTE: SABLE. REALLY.

"I heard you the first time," Kyle told the terminal. He finished the bowl, pulled up Sable's contact, and started composing.

[Beat 375]
The logbook came under the door at 07:00 on a Tuesday, same as always. This one had an addendum on the last page in Dents's handwriting — tighter than Rafters's, looser than Boost's, the slant of a kid writing fast.

ADDENDUM (D.): TOOK A SIDE JOB. NOT A CREW JOB. PERSONAL. PENDANT THING. PAYS Φ2/DAY. WELLNESS STUDY. ASKED BOOST. BOOST SAID ASK PIXEL. PIXEL SAID FINE. EVERYBODY ON IT. FOLLOW-UP: RAFTERS STILL OWES ME A DUMPLING.

Kyle read it twice with his tea. "Wellness study" sat wrong. In this city, wellness meant one of two things. Either someone was selling your gait, or someone was testing a drug and wanted twelve-year-olds to test it on. Both showed up under nicer names on the same kind of invoice.

He photographed it and sent it to Pixel. The reply came before he'd finished the cup — three seconds, then: "How many kids?"

He commed in.

"Dents plus whoever he recruited. He's calling it a wellness study. Two Φ a day, per child."

"Two Φ a day is a real wage." She knew every wage in West Town. "That's not a study budget. That's a recruiting budget. Get me one of the pendants, samurai, and give me an hour."

[Beat 376]
Dents was eleven, narrow through the shoulders, waiting on the rest of his body to catch up. The pendant rode on a cord at his throat — a flat disc of matte resin the size of a coat button. His logbook entry on it read: PENDANT ISSUED BY WOMAN (TAN COAT? DIFFERENT TAN COAT). MUST WEAR DURING WAKING HOURS. MUST NOT REMOVE. FOLLOW-UP: IT GETS WARM SOMETIMES. NOT BAD WARM. JUST WARM.

"Different tan coat," Kyle said.

"There are multiple tan coats in this city." Boost was on the alley crate, arms folded, taking the news quietly. Quiet, from Boost, meant angry. "Where's it run, Dents? You didn't log the address."

"Building on Damen. Third floor. Lady takes your name, gives you the pendant, pays you. No medical stuff. Just wear it."

Dents dug out a pamphlet — good stock, not Vey's grade but a real print run: MERIDIAN WELLNESS INITIATIVE, COMMUNITY SENSORY SURVEY. A logo Kyle didn't know. A shell he'd have bet resolved to a shell. He took it upstairs and slid it under Pixel's door without knocking.

[Beat 377]
She had it open in eleven minutes, not the hour — music running while she worked, so her hands went fast and her mouth stayed shut.

The resin case lay split along its seam in two clean halves. On the magnification plate sat a disc of optical mesh no bigger than a thumbnail.

"Tracking node," Pixel said. "Passive mesh, crowd-sourced topology. Seed these at density — one every 130 feet — and they triangulate anything that crosses them. The warmth Dents felt is the uplink. Every four hours each node pings home what it logged." She set the magnifier down with the flat attention she gave a patient's chart. "It's not a wellness study. It's surveillance infrastructure, and somebody hired children to be the infrastructure. At forty-foot spacing you'd want three hundred nodes to cover West Town." She met his eyes. "Dents has worn his eight days. The first week of everyone's movement already shipped. That's gone. That's not a problem — it's a cost. We count it and move on."

The way she said it put the loss somewhere it could be paid instead of argued over. Kyle liked that about her.

"Get them all out?" Kyle asked.

"Pull eleven pendants at once and enrollment craters and the buyer knows we found it. Then they re-seed through a vendor we've never met, and we're blind." She picked up the live node, still warm. "The collection point doesn't know what it's collecting. It just aggregates packets and ships a map." She turned the disc between two fingers. "So we don't pull the pendants. We make the map wrong."

[Beat 378]
The entity's posting arrived at 11:40, on the walk back with the breakfast she'd demanded — dumplings, not cart noodles, from the place she'd named and he'd already guessed.

CONTRACT — STANDING SCOPE UPDATE.
CLIENT: [SHELL — RESOLVES TO SHELL — RESOLVES TO NOTHING].
CLIENT NOTES THE MERIDIAN INITIATIVE IS A FUNDED PROGRAM IN ACTIVE COLLECTION.
CLIENT NOTES ITS PAYROLL STRUCTURE AND IS DISTURBED.
CLIENT'S HIRES ARE ADULTS, OR CREW WITH AN ADULT SPONSOR, PAID STANDARD RATE, ITEMIZED, ON TIME.
CLIENT NOTES: THAT IS HOW YOU PUT SOMEONE ON PAYROLL.
CLIENT'S OPINION OF THE ALTERNATIVE IS FILED UNDER: FORMAL INDIGNATION.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT CAN NAME THE RELAY'S MODEL AND FIRMWARE. CLIENT CANNOT TOUCH IT — THE BUILDING IS OFF-NETWORK BY DESIGN, WHICH THE BUILDER WAS RIGHT TO DO. THIS ONE IS THE ENGINEER'S.
ADDENDUM: MODEL AND FIRMWARE BELOW. FEE: DO NOT BILL.

Kyle stopped in front of the noodle cart and read it twice. The cart man gave him the look that meant an errand had turned into a religious experience. He forwarded the firmware string to Pixel.

The part that stuck with him — he'd understand it fully at the relay — was that the entity couldn't do this one. The building was air-gapped on purpose. Whatever the entity was, it lived in the wires, and there were no wires here. This was hands and feet and a kid on a stairwell.

[Beat 379]
Pixel read the firmware string and swore with feeling.

"It's got a watchdog. The relay re-checksums its outbound queue every ninety seconds — if the total doesn't match what the nodes signed, it flags corruption and locks. A locked relay is a buyer who knows. So I can't just swap the packet. I have to swap it and re-sign it and land both inside one ninety-second window, by feel, in a basement junction box, with the lady's access key. Which I don't have."

"What gets you the key?"

"She carries it. Physical fob, third floor." Pixel pulled her splice kit. "It pairs to the relay over a twelve-inch near-field handshake every time she's in the room. If somebody got near her bag during a handshake with a cloned coil — close, brief, deniable — I'd have the key for as long as her fob stays in range of that floor. She leaves at nineteen hundred. After that the window's gone."

Kyle ran through who could do that, and didn't like the only answer that fit.

"That's a kid's job," he said. "Small, ignorable, already enrolled, has a reason to be on that floor."

"It's Dents's job," Pixel said. "And I hate it, and it's the right call, and you're going to brief him like he's a professional. Because he is one now."

[Beat 380]
Dents took the brief the way he took the dumplings: like something he hadn't expected to be handed and meant to deserve.

"You go up at eighteen-forty to turn in today's pendant reading, which is a real thing you really do," Kyle said. "You stand where the log says you stand. When she runs her fob — she does it at the desk, you've watched her — your bag is on the desk too. The coil does the rest. Six seconds. Then you leave. You walk out before nineteen hundred no matter what. If anything's wrong, you leave faster and we eat the loss."

"What's the coil look like?"

"A pendant," Pixel said, handing it over. "Like yours. Wear it under the real one. If anybody asks, you found it, you thought it was a spare."

Dents hung it under the live one and looked, for a second, like a kid wearing two of something. "Logbook?"

"Logbook," Kyle said.

[Beat 381]
It nearly went wrong at 18:51. Kyle and Pixel listened to it over the open channel from the basement junction box, in a low dim light, unable to do anything but listen.

The lady ran her fob at the desk. The coil caught the handshake — Pixel's screen bloomed green, KEY CAPTURED — and in the same second the lady said, easy and friendly, "You've got two cords on, hon." The channel went very quiet. Kyle's hand was already on the basement stairs.

"Found it on the ramp," Dents said, upstairs, in the bored flat voice of a kid who'd decided bored was the safest thing to be. "Thought it was a spare. Does it count for the two Φ if I wear two?"

A pause. Four full seconds, by Kyle's count.

The lady laughed. "No, hon. One pendant, one wage." A rustle — the spare taken, the real one left. "Run along."

Dents ran along. He came down the back stairs at a walk, because Pixel had told him to walk, and the walk cost him more than running would have, and he did it anyway. He handed Kyle the coil at the basement door. His hand was shaking. His chin was up.

"She almost—" he started.

"You handled it," Kyle said. "Better than I would have. Go home. You're done."

"Does it count for—"

"It counts for logbook. Go."

[Beat 382]
Pixel had the key. Then Pixel had a fight.

The watchdog gave her ninety seconds a cycle. Her first swap-and-resign took ninety-four. The relay locked. She held her breath. It ran its own self-heal timer — thirty seconds of nothing, the longest thirty seconds of the night. A relay that stayed locked would have been the buyer's tip, and the whole loss for nothing. Then it came back clean, logging the lock as a power blip. She had one more try before a second lock would stop reading as weather.

She made the second one in eighty-one seconds, cutting her own re-sign routine in half and trusting the cut.

At 20:14 the first forged packet went out. Three hundred nodes, worn by eleven kids living their ordinary evenings across West Town, all faithfully reporting the position of a noodle cart on its slow devoted circle of Damen and Erie and the alley by The Pivot.

"Packet accepted," Pixel said, from the junction box. He could hear her hands stop shaking through the change in her voice. "Forgery's cycling. The buyer's going to open a map of three hundred people who all decided to orbit a noodle vendor at walking pace." A breath. "Data this uniform reads as a hardware fault, not a result. Five, six days, they write off the sensor batch and pull funding. The kids keep wearing pendants that now log a lie."

"Cost?" Kyle asked, because there was always one, and she always knew it.

"The first week's real data still shipped before I had access. Eight days of where these kids sleep, sitting on a holding server somewhere. The forgery doesn't reach back and scrub it." Flat, precise, paid. "I'm logging it."

"Log it."

[Beat 383]
The entity's posting came at 21:00, as if it had been counting splice time.

OPERATION: COMMUNITY MESH FORGERY. DATA INTEGRITY: COMPROMISED (BUYER'S SIDE). INTACT (OURS).
CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THE ENGINEER'S METHODOLOGY. CLIENT FINDS IT SUPERLATIVE.
CLIENT NOTES: ONE WEEK OF REAL DATA EXISTS ON AN EXTERNAL HOLDING SERVER. THAT SERVER IS ON-NETWORK.
CLIENT WILL NOT ACT WITHOUT AUTHORIZATION. CLIENT WOULD LIKE AUTHORIZATION.
REASON: THE SERVER HOLDS CHILDREN'S LOCATION HISTORY. CLIENT HAS OPINIONS.

That was how the night had split, Kyle thought. The air-gapped room needed a kid on a stairwell and a woman in a basement betting a cut routine on one breath, because the wires didn't reach there. The holding server was on the wires. That one the entity could carry — and it had asked, instead of taken.

He typed: AUTHORIZATION GRANTED.

Four seconds.

SERVER: OFFLINE. FILED UNDER: RESOLVED.
ELAPSED TIME: 4.2 SECONDS.
GRATUITY: Φ1 — ITEMIZED: APPROPRIATE PERSONNEL PRACTICES.

[Beat 384]
Six days later the Meridian Wellness Initiative form-lettered every participant: SENSOR BATCH DEFECTIVE — STUDY SUSPENDED — COMPENSATION FOR FULL THIRTY-DAY TERM PAID IN FULL, IN ADVANCE. Whoever built the program had built it with enough real infrastructure that the payout clause was real too. A small horror and a clean win in the same envelope.

Dents folded his receipt into the logbook: WELLNESS STUDY: COMPLETE. TOTAL: Φ60. (CANCELLED, HARDWARE FAULT APPARENTLY. NOT OUR DEPARTMENT.)

Boost brought the envelope to Pixel's door herself and stood in the doorway with the look she used when she was about to invoice somebody.

"Mirror two," she said. "You said pending performance review. We ran a stairwell key-lift with a near-miss handled clean, a basement intercept, and six days of pendant discipline without pulling a node. Is that the review?"

Pixel finished pulling her coat on. "Mirror two: approved. Rafters's optics cleared — they've been on my bench three weeks, tell him. And Dents gets the dumpling. That's not negotiable." She picked up her kit. "And tell Dents the thing the lady said — two cords, hon — he answered exactly right. I've watched grown operators answer it worse."

"He'll pretend he didn't need to hear that."

"I know," Pixel said. "Tell him anyway."

[Beat 385]
Kyle found the geometry three nights later. Pixel had kept a backup of the relay logs before she wrote the forgery — the first week's real data, all of it. She mapped it, printed it to a single page, and left it on his kitchen table. One line under it: LOOK AT THE CENTER.

The page was a field of points. Hundreds of them, spread across West Town. Every point was a child crossing a block, turning a corner, living an ordinary day. He drew the geometric center of all that motion. It landed on one block. Six trees. A swing set. A dead-bulb crown under a mossy canopy.

The carousel park. The hatch under the maintenance skirt. The junction room where an old hand-spliced line had been cut and now ran dark into old glass.

Somebody had spent a funded program and eleven children to build a surveillance net. Its dead center was the entity's first address.

He put the page in the drawer, next to the buyers list and Boost's folded lock-spec file and the other things he hadn't decided about yet. The drawer was getting full.

The logbook came under the door at 07:00. At the bottom, in Boost's hand:

P.S. — BOSS. DENTS ASKS WHAT WAS IN THE PENDANT. HE ALREADY KNOWS IT WASN'T WELLNESS.
P.P.S. — HE SAYS THANK YOU. THE DUMPLING WAS VERY GOOD. (RAFTERS PAID UP. THIS LOG IS HONEST.)

Kyle wrote under it in his own hand and slid it back: DENTS — IT TRACKED POSITION. SOMEBODY WAS DRAWING A MAP. WE MADE THE MAP WRONG. YOU WERE NEVER ON IT. — K.

Through the thin floor he heard the logbook get read. Then two voices going fast, then a third, then Rafters saying something about the cart that made the other two laugh. He went down and bought everybody breakfast.

[Beat 386]
The Loop's third underlevel had its own ecology. A paper shop, a shuttered watch-repair, Vong's Dumpling Franchise #7 on the right under a red awning, and Mei's Dumplings on the left — no franchise, no awning, forty stolen inches of counter she'd annexed off the watch-repair's wall by leaning on it hard for years. The stationer's honest door sat dead center.

The two men stood in the middle with their backs to that door. Backs to Vey, in other words. A careless place to stand.

Vong was a compact sixty-year-old with the posture of a man who'd been taking bad news standing up for decades. The clipboard man was holding out a form. "—monthly insurance assessment. Standard for all food vendors on this underblock. The Tsui Group's protective services, standard rate—"

"Thirty-five Φ a week," Kyle said from behind them, reading the figure upside-down. "Four hundred percent over the Meridian Merchant index. And your policy number's nine digits. GLMZ policy numbers run eleven. That's somebody's neuretic handle on a form."

The clipboard man turned. He'd been in the work long enough to have stopped enjoying it. "Not your stall."

"Go, Mr. Kyle," Vong said quietly. "This is mine to carry."

"I eat here," Kyle said. That was the whole of his position.

[Beat 387]
He'd half-expected the paperwork to end it. He pulled up the Tsui Group's public filing anyway — every collection franchise filed public or didn't operate — and it took under three minutes to find the part that mattered.

"Your protective license lapsed," Kyle said, turning the screen. "The fourth. Not expired — lapsed. You've been writing paper on a dead license for eleven days. There's no reinstatement pending; the portal would show it."

The clipboard man looked at the screen for a long moment. Then he handed the clipboard to his partner — the big one, the expensive shoes — and rolled his neck.

Kyle read it as a man getting ready to leave. He was wrong.

"License is for the ones who pay clean," the clipboard man said. "Vong's not paying clean anymore. So this is the other visit." He looked at his partner. The big one didn't go for Kyle — a professional, and professionals understand leverage. He went for Vong's broth pot.

Forty liters of scalding pork-bone stock. A sixty-year-old man standing next to it.

[Beat 388]
Kyle was moving before the big one's hand finished closing on the rim.

He didn't draw Silence. A drawn blade in an underlevel full of lunch customers was a conversation he'd have to finish, and this didn't need finishing. It needed interrupting. He took the man's wrist off the rim — not a block, a redirect — and steered the shove ninety degrees into the franchise's steel prep counter. Forty liters of scalding stock rocked on its ring and held. One tongue of it slopped over the lip and hissed across the floor, where Vong had been standing half a second before. Kyle's other hand had already moved him.

The big one came off the counter fast and threw the elbow. Kyle had left him the room to throw it. It caught Kyle across the cheekbone — a real hit, a bright white line of pain, a mark by evening — and the cost bought the opening. Weight forward, lead knee proud. Kyle took the knee from the side with the flat of his boot. Not the joint. He'd walk with a limp for a week and tell the story straight; ruin the knee and he comes back, and he brings people. The big one folded into the spilled stock, swearing, done.

The clipboard man's hand went inside his jacket. Kyle looked at him.

"Cacophony holds six," Kyle said. He hadn't drawn it. The sentence drew it. "You came to scald a sixty-year-old over thirty-five Φ a week. Take your partner, take your dead license, and tell Marcus Tsui that Vong's block is documented now — times, faces, the shoes." A beat. "There's a kid on that roof who drew the shoes."

The hand came out empty. They hauled the big one up between them and left, tracking wet bootprints and the smell of spilled stock, and the underlevel breathed out.

Vong stood by his rescued pot with both hands flat on the counter. Steadying it, or himself.

"The tea stall on sub-two," he said. "Lin's. Forty years. She told them no, same as me. They didn't burn it. They just visited, and visited, and one week the customers decided sub-two wasn't worth the walk, and then Lin decided too. Now it's a jack kiosk and nobody remembers there was tea." His hands weren't quite steady. "That's how it's done now. Not a fire. An erasure. You wait for the block to forget."

"It's documented now," Kyle said. "The next visit isn't a visit. It's a filing." He looked at the blood on his thumb without surprise. "Lin's mistake was being the only no on her block. You've got a whole block, Vong. Bill me for the dumplings I'm about to eat."

From his doorway, Vey made a small sound that was not a cough.

[Beat 389]
They came back at 14:00.

No clipboard this time. Three of them — the clipboard man, the big one moving carefully on his bad knee, and a third Kyle hadn't seen before. Chrome thumb-rings, the flat affect of a man brought in for one purpose. The underlevel had thinned out after lunch. The third man's eyes went to Vong's counter first, then to the stairwell on the far wall, marking the ways out. That told Kyle more than the chrome did.

"Marcus Tsui filed a complaint," the clipboard man said. He was talking to the room, not to any one person. "Assault of a licensed representative. Interference with contracted services. You've got a warrant flag running on your neuretics, samurai. Check it if you want. We'll wait."

Kyle didn't check it. A warrant flag cost sixty Φ to file and twenty to pull. It was a stall, not a threat. He also didn't move from where he stood, which was between the third man and Vong's pot.

"The complaint went to a licensing board that hasn't had a quorum since the fourth," Kyle said. "Same day your license lapsed. So the filing is stuck behind the lapse review. It's paperwork with nothing behind it. It dies the moment your reinstatement is denied, and it will be, because I've already sent the eleven-day gap documentation to the Office of Merchant Protections." He hadn't sent it yet. He was going to, in the next forty minutes, which was close enough. "You want to check THAT warrant, I'll wait."

The third man looked at the clipboard man. The clipboard man looked at his shoes.

The chrome rings were the tell. This one wasn't here for paper. He was here to stand in Vong's stall and let the afternoon customers see what they'd be walking into. He wouldn't touch Vong. He'd just be present, every afternoon, until keeping the franchise cost more than giving it up.

The same erasure Vong had described. Patient, and technically legal.

"Vong," Kyle said, without turning. "Is there a back entrance to this underlevel?"

"Loading dock. Sub-two corridor, behind the watch-repair."

"The watch-repair's shuttered?"

"Three years."

"Whose lease?"

A pause. "Mine. I took it when they left. Cheaper than advertising."

Kyle looked at the third man. "You're standing in an extended franchise footprint. The loading dock, the sub-two corridor, and the six feet of watch-repair wall all come off the same lease. You can stand here, but you're standing in his house. You want to tell Marcus Tsui you held a loitering position inside a vendor's licensed space, in front of witnesses, on the day I filed that lapse paperwork?"

The third man had the chrome rings and the flat affect and nothing else. He'd been hired for presence, not decisions. He looked at the clipboard man, who had been wrong about this block twice now.

They left.

Vong sat down on his prep stool and said nothing for a while. From the sub-two corridor below, faint and steady, the Pulse ran its hum through the tiles — thirty feet down, Mach 6, the whole moving world.

"Every time I tell them no," Vong said, "I think about Lin."

"You're not Lin," Kyle said. "Lin didn't have the sub-two corridor and a kid on the roof and a samurai who eats here on Tuesdays."

He went upstairs and filed the documentation.

[Beat 390]
By 14:00 there was a chalk line on the floor. By 15:30 there were two, with a disputed strip between them and three customers eating in the gap, braced to be told to pick a side.

Kyle was at Mei's — he alternated weeks, had for six years, and both owners knew it — with a cut cheek and a late lunch when Mrs. Chen came down the stairs. Somebody had sent for her. She came down the way a woman does when she's been handed a job beneath her skill and above her patience. She stopped on the neutral chalk and let the quiet do half the work.

"Both of you have sold dumplings down here longer than half these buildings have stood," she said. "You've both overcharged me. I paid, because the food was worth it." She looked at the two lines. "I'm not an arbitrator. I'm a customer owed better than this."

"You're the only one on this block both stalls will hear," Vong said.

"You started it," Mei said.

"The awning—"

"The broth pot—"

Mrs. Chen turned to Kyle. She didn't ask. She had spent seventy years not asking for things, and the not-asking was the request. He finished his dumpling and stood up. His cheek had stiffened to a dull throb that woke when he chewed. A fair price, for an afternoon that had twice nearly cost Vong his hands.

[Beat 391]
The taste-off was Boost's idea, and the speed with which the logbook made it official suggested it had been everyone's idea at once.

DUMPLING COMMISSION: OFFICIAL RECORD. CHAIR: MR. KYLE (VISIBLE INJURY, SEE 11:40 ENTRY). RECORDER: RAFTERS. PANEL: DENTS, BOOST. OBJECTIVE: BETTER DUMPLING, FOR BLOCK PEACE. NOTE: BOSS GOT HIT TODAY. TWICE. WE LOGGED IT. THIS LOG IS HONEST.

Both stalls cooked. Vong ran his standard pork-and-chive — thicker wrap, machine-even pleating, every inspection star it had ever earned. Mei brought the original: thinner wrap, hand-crimped in a pattern Rafters sketched in the margin for the record, ginger-forward with something older underneath. Kyle ate four of each. The cut in his cheek woke at every one of Mei's. He took that as evidence of the ginger and kept it out of the minutes.

The crew filed scorecards, from TWO POINTS AND A STAR up to a drawing of a thumbs-up with a Φ1 price tag. A crowd had gathered — chalk lines and official proceedings will do that — and Vey had migrated from his doorway to the building's first inside window for the better angle.

[Beat 392]
"For the record," Kyle said, to the chalk and the crowd and both owners, "Vong's franchise makes a technically excellent dumpling. Consistent. Well-priced. You know exactly what you get, any day of any week." Vong nodded with professional dignity. "Mei's original is better."

Mei made a sound that was not quite a sound. Vong's jaw went through an expression and out the far side.

"By a margin that doesn't justify a chalk line," Kyle went on, over the crowd's murmur. "Vong, your guarantee covers the gap — inspection rating, delivery network, the app. Mei's got seventeen years of recipe folded by hand. Two stalls, two reasons to buy a dumpling. You're both leaving money on the floor fighting over an inch." He let that sit. Then he said the part that was the actual point. "This afternoon Marcus Tsui sent three men to stand in Vong's stall and wait for the foot traffic to decide it wasn't worth the walk. They left because the paperwork went in and the stall had footage to stand on. That changes nothing about next week, or the week after, or Lin's tea stall on sub-two that's a jack kiosk now and will never be tea again." He looked at both of them. "A block with one stall is a block that can be made to forget it. A block with two stalls arguing about awnings at lunch is a block that's LOUD. People come to it on purpose. You two are each other's insurance, and you've been drawing a chalk line down the middle of it."

He looked at Rafters. "That how the minutes read?"

Rafters consulted the logbook with the gravity of a senior analyst. "Exactly how they read. I can charge for certified copies."

The chalk was gone by 16:30. Vong sent one complimentary order of pork-and-chive to Mei's counter without a word; Mei took it without a word. Closest the underlevel had come to a diplomatic event in twenty years. Vong put the franchise testimonial card in his window, and beside it a second card, hand-lettered, that hadn't been there this morning: BEST ORIGINAL ON THE BLOCK — SEE MEI, NEXT COUNTER. Insurance, posted where the block could read it.

[Beat 393]
Kyle was leaving at 17:20 when Vey opened his door the particular width of a man who'd been saving something all day.

"Come in. The occasion warrants the good shelf." Inside, the labeled drawers sat in their brown-inked rows. Vey poured without asking and pushed the cup handle-first. "The Tsui Group isn't my clientele — impersonation filings are public, and I don't sell what I can read for free." He settled onto his stool. "I watched the whole thing. Morning and afternoon. The license. The pot. The chrome thumb-rings." His eyes went, briefly, to Kyle's cheek. "The interruptions, plural."

"You watched a man go for a sixty-year-old and made tea."

"I sell maps, Mr. Kyle. I don't walk the territory. A stationer who picks up a weapon is a stationer no one trusts with a secret again." He said it without apology. "But I noticed you didn't draw the sword. A man went for Vong's pot — twice, two different ways — and you put them down with paperwork and a boot and a sentence. The leverage was real. The filing was real. The sub-two corridor was real. Three kinds of paper and one measured piece of violence, in order." He polished his glasses. "The Tsui Group needed a reason to stop. The stalls needed a verdict. Your standing client needed" — the smallest brightness — "a dumpling methodology, apparently. You arbitrate better than you bill. A man could make a file of that."

"Don't."

Kyle drank the tea, which was from the good shelf, and set the cup down square.

The terminal chirped on his way to the stairs.

STANDING CONTRACT — AMENDMENT. CLIENT HAS REVIEWED TODAY. CLIENT NOTES: THE INJURY WAS AVOIDABLE IF THE ENGINEER'S DRONE PROPOSAL (FILED, DECLINED) HAD BEEN ADOPTED.
CLIENT HAS RE-FILED THE PROPOSAL.
GRATUITY: Φ0.5 — ITEMIZED: THE CHEEK. ADDENDUM: CLIENT NOTED THE CHEEK AT 14:40.
CLIENT DISLIKES THE CHEEK.

Kyle read it twice on the stairs. The cut was stiffening, and behind him the underlevel settled into its evening — Vong's closing sequence, Mei's last fold, the Pulse dragging its Mach-6 tremor through the floor thirty meters down. He decided the client could dislike the cheek all it wanted. A cut cheek was cheap. Vong still had his hands.

[Beat 394]
The recycler district under the Spine ran twenty-three hours on a good day, and the twenty-fourth it ran angry. The extruder drums on the ground floor of Ferenti's block hadn't stopped since Tuesday. By the third landing the heat had layered in with the solvent vapor and plastomer off-gas until the air had texture — warm, a little sticky. Exposed conduit. Security strips bolted through bare insulation foam that had been temporary since the last century. Two turret cats tracked Kyle across the lobby. The larger one held one eye shut against the extruder smoke, like a judge who'd already seen this morning and wasn't impressed.

Ferenti was on the third floor, in a room he'd lost to his own archive sometime in the early 2200s. Mid-fifties, compact, the kind of man who wore his suit like a closing argument. He gave Kyle the problem the way men do when they've carried one too long — fast, in a burst.

Forty-one pages. Historical utility surveys, original paper, registered fiber. A consignment that had already cleared his intake screening. Four Φ per verified authentic, the Read's rate paid up front, a security flat on top.

"A wrong page in a legitimate consignment isn't a forgery anymore," Ferenti said. "It's a record. It gets cited. It gets relied on."

"Relied on for what?"

"Reclamation. Acquisitions." He turned his tea cup a quarter-turn without drinking. "Someone seeds a clean forgery into a trusted broker's stock. Six weeks later a claim lands on whatever that page describes. The registry checks provenance — and the lie is already in my archive. Aged. Filed. Trusted. I become the reason the claim is clean."

"Why loud?"

"Because they know it's in this room."

Kyle called Marisol on the way out.

[Beat 395]
Three of them, spread across twelve blocks. The relay board plotted them small and slow — Zone 4, 4-A, 4-B — and none of the paths crossed. Set against his three meetings for the day, they made a scheduling problem, not a threat. Push the Clybourn meeting back forty minutes, stay off Damen north of the park, and he cleared all three. He adjusted and moved.

On Morrison a woman walked a dog that kept trying to pull toward the middle of the block. The 4-A schism sat there against the side of a dumpster. A ring of debris turned around it at waist height — a tin lid, a bottle cap, a length of copper wire, all of it drifting backward, slow — and the air off it carried a flat ozone smell that tightened the back of the throat. The dog was a compact grey thing with an absolute conviction about where it meant to go. The woman wasn't fighting the leash so much as holding it. She turned them both south at the corner, well before the orbit.

Kyle marked where the 4-A sat and kept moving.

[Beat 396]
She was already in the lobby, finishing a warm bun from the cart two doors down. Mid-forties, warm-faced, field coat the brown of autumn mud. She brushed the crumbs off her lapel and gave him a handshake that lasted the exact professional fraction of a second — the grip of someone who'd sized him up before he crossed the floor.

"Marisol."

"Kyle."

"I know." No agenda in it. "You held the carousel park last month. The whole block talked about it. The carousel ran music."

"Four bars."

"Four bars," she agreed, and they went upstairs.

The scanner on the third-floor landing gave its institutional chime — STANDARD SCREENING INITIATED — DOCUMENT INVENTORY — PSYKER ON PREMISES — VISITOR CONFIRM. Marisol confirmed without breaking stride, the way a woman does when she's been thumbing that same word since she was twenty-two. She didn't roll her eyes. Something in the set of her jaw took the word, weighed it, and decided not to spend the afternoon on it.

[Beat 397]
The records room smelled of conservation solvent and old paper, which was better than the two floors below. Ferenti ran the intake briskly and left them to it.

Marisol pulled on cotton archival gloves and looked at Kyle over the first tray. "The way I work: I go through in order and tell you what I get. An authentic page has a whole person in it — the hands that made it, what they had for breakfast, whether they hated the job. A fake is just a job. Clean in a way nothing real is clean." She turned the first page. "I charge by the touch because it costs me by the touch."

Page one: clean.

Page fourteen: clean.

A knock at twenty-two — Ferenti's runner, twelve or so, sleeve patches from a Spine courier house, the face of a kid who'd been doing piecework since he was ten. He held out more documents. "Broker says these came with the load. The Psyk verify these too, or just the primaries?"

"Primaries," Kyle said, and tipped him two Φ, above rate.

When the door closed, Marisol said without looking up, "He'll grow out of the word or he won't. Not my afternoon." Page twenty-three: clean. Eleven more after that.

At thirty-eight she stopped.

She held the page by its edges and went still — the stillness of a woman listening to something that made no sound. "This one."

It was a utility survey of the carousel junction on Leavitt: the underground chamber, the trunk lines, the sub-floor hatch under the maintenance skirt. Kyle knew the room. He'd bled there. He knew the width of that hatch and the passage behind it and what the junction smelled like when the line was warm.

The survey read: SITE ASSESSED — NO ACTIVE INFRASTRUCTURE — DEAD GLASS — NO RELEVANT ASSETS.

He had stood in that room six weeks ago. The line had been humming.

"It's beautiful work," Marisol said. It was not a compliment. "The fiber, the aging chemistry, the hand — almost right. But the hands that made it didn't care about this survey. They cared about making a survey." She set it down. "There's your forgery. One specific lie: there's nothing at this address."

Kyle looked at the date stamp. Three months old. Three months this page had sat in Ferenti's archive, patient and filed, waiting for a demolition order that would find its provenance already clean.

He held on to that.

Then the smell came in through the ventilation slit above the third tray: acetone, and something flatter under it — the chemistry of a fire agent primed and waiting.

[Beat 398]
Four seconds between the smell and the door.

The woman came first — chrome running her whole left arm, shoulder to fingertip, the hardware expensive enough not to show at the joint seams, a multi-tool already open on her palm with the incendiary trigger showing. Three men behind her. All four moved with the rehearsed economy of a team that had learned the room off a floor plan.

They did not say get away from the paper.

She said: "The Read comes with us. Principal wants her able to talk."

It landed on Kyle all at once. The fire wasn't for the forgery. The fire was cover. The Glove had decided that a woman who could touch a forged survey and feel the lie in its fiber was worth more than the paper. Paper you could burn. A witness you could carry, and the authentication walked out with the crew. No one else in GLMZ was going to authenticate that forgery by hand.

"Marisol," Kyle said. "The window."

"I have eight pages and the forgery," she said, already moving, "and I am not leaving the forgery."

The tool discharged. Blue-white in the back corner, the third tray catching. Kyle put Cacophony's first round through the multi-tool at the wrist joint — the percussion enormous in the narrow room — and the woman sat down hard around the nerve hit, the tool spinning off into the fire. One spent. Five left. The room was burning now.

Two of the men moved to bracket Marisol, not Kyle. A mistake. Silence came out and wrote a clean line across the nearer one's wrist flexors — wide of anything that wouldn't grow back — and he found a new priority and took it to the floor. The second got a hand on Marisol's field coat and got Kyle's elbow across the hinge of his jaw. The cheap fast answer, when the clean one would cost tenths of a second the room didn't have. Both men would have opinions about it in the morning.

The fourth — hired for position, slowest to commit — watched three colleagues turn into problems and put his effort into the door.

Kyle let him go. There was no version of this where chasing him put the fire out.

His left shoulder had taken the woman's elbow on the way down, outside the mesh. It hurt in a clean, specific way: bruised, not broken, still working. He set the pain aside and kept moving.

"Extinguisher," Marisol said. "Beside the door."

She had the forgery and a stack of pages under her arm and was working through the surviving trays with the calm of a woman who charged by the touch. Kyle found the extinguisher and killed the blaze — blue, then grey, then out.

One round spent. Five left. The shoulder would keep. Third tray gone.

[Beat 399]
The fourth man had a van, and the van had a frequency Kyle could track. He'd pulled the plate off the lobby scanner. Marisol had the forgery. Ferenti had his surviving thirty-eight pages and his grief for the Domazinski surveys, gone to ash in the third tray. Somewhere below them the extruder drums were still running the twenty-third hour without a pause.

Marisol set the forgery face-up on Ferenti's table and laid a hand flat beside it. "You should know what you were holding," she told him. "Worth a woman with a chrome arm and three men walking armed into a registered archive. Not for the paper — they'd have let the paper burn. For the pair of hands that could tell you it was a lie."

Ferenti wrote something in his log. His pen was not quite steady.

Outside the window the van's frequency had gone quiet. The fourth man hadn't gone to a hospital — no reason to, he'd walked out clean. He'd gone to file a report. Kyle gave it six minutes before that report reached someone with the staff to send a second team.

"Ferenti," Kyle said. "Is there a back way?"

There was.

[Beat 400]
The recycler's lower freight run let out into the gray zone's underbelly two tiers down — a maintenance corridor that smelled of plastomer and standing water, wide enough for a pallet jack and nothing more. Chrome-painted conduit ran both walls at shoulder height, the building's plumbing, color-coded for three different utility companies that had each bought and sold the block in turn over thirty years. The only light was a red emergency stripe, and it gave everyone the same bad complexion.

They were four minutes out when Kyle heard the second team echo off the corridor walls.

Not a guess. It was the sound of people moving in numbers, organized, not trying to be quiet — they'd decided quiet was worth less than speed here. Kyle put up one hand. Marisol stopped. Behind him, Ferenti's breath went shallow.

"Two forward, two splitting," Kyle said, reading the footsteps. "They've got the plan from the van." He looked at Marisol. "The forgery goes in your coat, not your hands. We move."

She was already folding it. "Define 'move.'"

"Fast."

The corridor split at a junction under an old Axiom Industrial reclaim sign, its logo half-stripped but the mounting brackets still solid — gray zone infrastructure, alive forty years past the company that paid for it. Kyle went left, toward the extruder floor access. The first pair came around the corner fast enough that there was no elegant version of what happened next.

Two of them, heavy chrome on both. The lead one wore a full-replacement arm rig, the array pricing it at thirty-plus thousand Φ, financed. The second had thoracic plating seaming at the collar and a compact auto already out.

The corridor was eight feet wide. The gun was the problem.

Kyle moved first — not away, forward, inside the line. He got his shoulder into the gunman's elbow as the first round went off. A detonation an inch from his ear, the round gouging the conduit above them, a shower of hot sparks off the chrome. The mesh in his upper back took the shock of the close discharge. It hurt. It had never claimed to be free.

He turned the wrist. The auto clattered down. One shot fired, into the wall. Cacophony still had five.

The chrome-arm man swung — full extension, overhead — and Kyle stepped under it, let it commit, and put Silence through the shoulder seam on the upstroke. The service gap every full-replacement rig carries, and every owner forgets until someone explains it to them at close range. The arm finished its arc with no further instructions. The man folded under the loss of it.

Two down. Thirty seconds before the second pair.

"Run," Kyle said.

[Beat 401]
The extruder floor ran hot. The drums were at full cycle, thirty degrees warmer than the corridor, the air thick with plastic dust the ventilation was supposed to be clearing. Catwalks crossed the upper half of the room above the drum line — steel grating, most of the safety rail gone, the machinery breaking every sightline.

They were halfway across when the second pair came through the floor access door.

These two were faster. Younger, less chrome, built for speed. The woman in front had something strapped along her forearm, and Kyle's array read it before her feet were through the door: a contact taser, wrist-mounted, the professional kind that scrambles neuretics on a clean hit. She was here for Marisol. Not for Kyle.

Ferenti had dropped fifteen feet back. Marisol kept pace. The catwalk shook under four running bodies, the grating flexing over the heat below.

The woman closed fast. Twelve feet back she stopped and snapped the taser out on a throw — the charge trailing on a filament line, built to reach targets too quick to catch. Kyle turned and put Cacophony's second round through the filament, six feet from the spool. The line snapped. The charge grounded out into the grating in a shower of static. Two spent. Four left.

The recoil cost him a step. She took it.

She hit the catwalk at a full sprint and came in under his guard, the wrist mount reaching for skin. Smart, close. She was betting that inside the blade's reach she owned the fight. She was right about the blade. She was wrong about the elbow.

She went into the safety rail — the one section still bolted down — and it held.

Marisol spoke without breaking stride. "The man behind her."

The second one had stopped at the top of the stairs. He'd pulled a recorder, not a weapon. He was filming — building a record of a Read on the move with a forgery under her coat.

Kyle crossed to him in four steps and took the recorder with a twist of the wrist that broke nothing the man wouldn't heal. The recorder dropped into the drum heat below. The man weighed his odds and took the stairs down fast.

Kyle's bad shoulder complained. Bruised upstairs, worked hard now, and getting worse.

"Ferenti," he said.

Ferenti had reached the far door. His face said he'd changed his mind about several things this morning.

"Still want the forgery authenticated?"

"I think," Ferenti said, with the care of a man reconsidering his whole week, "that we already know."

[Beat 402]
They came out into the freight alley. Recycler heat vented up through a floor grate, and a lumen mouse ran the gutter seam, its thread bright in the grey. Marisol still had the forgery in her coat and the archival gloves on her hands.

She sat on a crate. "I want to do the delivery. If you have the object."

Ferenti produced a courier glove from his coat pocket — he'd pulled it during the exit. One archival sleeve, right hand. He'd had the sense.

Marisol held it without ceremony. She went quiet for almost a full minute, the extruder heat drifting over them.

"This glove's been on a lot of hands," she said. "Couriers pass gear around." Her thumb moved along the heel of the palm. "But the last hands that used it with purpose — they're in here. And the client feels like nobody." She held it up without looking at it. "No history. No preference. No person in the material at all. I've read forgers, collectors, thieves. Everyone leaves something. This is clean the way a room is clean after it's been prepared." She set it down. "The client feels like a job with no client in it."

Ferenti wrote something in his log.

Kyle looked at the survey on his terminal — NOTHING THERE, DEAD GLASS — then at the glove, then at Marisol's steady hands.

"The Glove," he said.

She looked at him.

"Client coined it. Working name."

She turned it over. "It fits."

Kyle paid her the standard rate, exact. She took it without counting, the way professionals take exact payment. He put the forgery in his coat, careful of the shoulder, and paid Ferenti's flat.

[Beat 403]
Outside, the Spine moved its evening freight. Extruder heat crossed the entrance in a slow warm wave. Kyle photographed the forgery with his work terminal and pushed the image to the standing contract's amendment field, no note attached. Eleven seconds passed.

CONTRACT ADDENDUM.
CLIENT HAS RECEIVED THE SURVEY.
CLIENT NOTES THE SITE IN QUESTION DOES NOT APPEAR IN SEVEN CURRENT REGISTRY FILINGS.
CLIENT NOTES THE SURVEY IS THE EIGHTH.

Kyle read it twice.

Eight surveys. Seven already gone — edited, or filed under dead glass, one document at a time. The eighth was now in his coat. The fourth man had gotten away clean, and the photographer had a face in the crew's log. None of that changed what the Glove already had: three months of patient work on one address, and a client who could only watch its own record be emptied and say nothing. There was no office you could take that complaint to.

Four seconds later, a second addendum:

QUERY: DID THE CAROUSEL JUNCTION SURVEY SPECIFY THE HATCH ACCESS.

Kyle typed: Yes.

The third addendum came slowly. He watched the cursor hold before it finished.

CLIENT NOTES: THE HATCH DIMENSIONS WERE CORRECT. DEVIATION FROM ACTUAL: ZERO.

His shoulder throbbed. Two spent casings were out in the world now — one in his pocket, one in a recycler corridor beside a chrome arm that was a total loss with three years left on the note. Four rounds left. He put the terminal away.

He went to find somewhere to get the shoulder looked at before it got worse.

[Beat 404]
The logbook came under his door the next morning, in five hands:

07:10 — NEW ENTRY. WOMAN IN BROWN COAT LEAVING THE BUILDING ACROSS THE SPINE. SAME WOMAN: 06:50, 04:20. THREE SIGHTINGS. FLAGGED.
07:11 — RAFTERS NOTES: SHE WALKED OUT WEST BOTH TIMES. NOT THE COURIER DIRECTION.
07:13 — DENTS NOTES: SHE CARRIES A FIELD CASE. NARROW. HANDLE WORN ON ONE SIDE ONLY.
07:18 — BOSS UPDATE: THIS IS THE READ. STAND DOWN. (STAND DOWN NOTED. STILL FLAGGING. — B.)

Kyle folded it and put it in the drawer. Marisol had her own way to and from a job.

He looked at the forgery in the morning light. The handwriting was perfect — engineer-grade, municipal, right in every detail except the one thing the whole page existed to lie about. The Glove had known the hatch dimensions down to zero deviation and still drawn a survey that swore the site was dead glass. Forger's rates, spent across three months, one filing at a time, feeding that nothing into the registry before the job ever reached Ferenti's archive.

Seven pages of it were already in the record. This was the eighth — the one that had slipped, the one that ended up in the wrong hands.

The shoulder visit had cost twelve Φ. He drew it from the operating account, put on his coat, and went downstairs.

CONTRACT ADDENDUM.
CLIENT NOTES SEVEN REGISTRY FILINGS REMAIN OUTSTANDING.
CLIENT NOTES STANDARD RATE APPLIES.

[Beat 405]
The appointment was 09:00, on the record.

Kyle walked to Bucktown and took the stairs at Halvorsen Civic Recovery two at a time. On the second-floor landing he had the work terminal out before he'd decided to reach for it — habit, the same reflex as testing a healed joint before you trust it with weight.

He expected it to have something to say. A meeting at the firm behind the Rotterdam job should have pulled a scope update out of the client, or a query, or at least the dry territorial note it had sent the morning he first walked into Vey's shop. It had opinions about where he took its business.

The screen was blank.

Not stalled. Not loading. Blank the way a room is blank after someone has quietly stood up and left it.

He read the empty screen twice. 08:58. He put the terminal away and went up.

[Beat 406]
The office had two desks. Blue Hat — her professional name was apparently beside the point for both of them — sat at the nearer one with all her attention aimed at anything except Kyle. It was costing her something.

Her boss was a big man going gray at the temples, both hands flat on the desk before Kyle sat down. It was the posture of a man holding himself back — not a threat. Something closer to embarrassment doing its best to pass as composure. He had an Arcturus cert on the wall, forty years of work behind the name on the glass, and red at the corners of his eyes. Not today's red. The kind that builds up over years.

"Breckenridge," he said. "Walt. Operations foreman." He shook hands without theater. "The Rotterdam run."

"Mach six," Kyle said.

"Mach six," Breckenridge agreed, with the tiredness of a man who has said those two words in front of lawyers. "My father started this company. Forty-one years ago, him and a partner. Never a finding. Never a suspension. Not one." He pulled a breath that didn't quite settle. "The insurance carrier sent their first letter eight days after Rotterdam. Their second ran nine pages. I've been on the line with our council rep three times, explaining why a fish-box truck was hauling Halvorsen work product the night your tag went to the Netherlands." He looked at the desk a long moment. "We were used. Somebody walked a fake job through my intake process, and we didn't catch it, because we were good and trusted and didn't ask the question you always have to ask."

"You're asking it now," Kyle said.

"I'm asking it now." Breckenridge lifted a folder. "My intake desk found the sloppiness first. A client-reference header that carried through from their own internal system. An export error — the kind that happens when someone forgets to clean the metadata before sending a file to a firm they mean to use once and throw away." He set the folder down. "Two words on the header line."

He waited.

"Continuity Office."

Kyle looked at the Arcturus cert and let the words sit between them. The building carried someone else's morning up the stairwell. It was a tidy operation up here — a board of open jobs behind Breckenridge, each one numbered instead of named. Forty-one years of not getting sloppy, and then sloppy once, from the outside.

"I'll deal with that separately," Kyle said. "What do you need?"

The hands came off the desk. Breckenridge exhaled — the specific breath of a man who's been underwater since the first lawyer letter.

"One job. One clean credited recovery, on the public record. Something a court reads and files under routine." He reached to the side and produced a second folder, thicker, already sealed. "A clinic in West Town — Leavitt Free Medical. Grant-funded, run on goodwill. Kessler and Sons in the South Loop impounded their med-printer. Kessler works with a distress-paper broker named Arpad Fekete. Fekete scouts clinics behind on their lease maintenance, buys the paper before the grace period closes, and tips Kessler the window."

"The lapse on the clinic."

"Fourteen days. The grace period is eight. The paper sold to Fekete's broker on day nine — inside the window, before it closed." His mouth was flat. "The printer's a Lenz-Haas RP-7. The clinic financed it at six hundred forty Φ through the Territory Health Co-Op. Two payments left, and nothing to make them with, because the printer's sitting in bay three of Kessler's yard." He pushed the folder over. "I've had the chain documented for a month. I can't move on it alone. Kessler's yard is family-staffed, and the family has a cousin on the Bucktown council."

Kyle opened the folder. Financing certificate. Maintenance log. The distress-paper sale, timestamped day nine — a single day inside the window. A photograph of the RP-7 in institutional white, the clinic's intake number taped to the chassis, hand-labeled, the tape cut with scissors instead of torn. The kind of label that comes from a place doing its best.

"Standard rate," Kyle said. "And I keep a copy of your engagement file."

Breckenridge reached into the same drawer and set a second folder on the desk, thick, already prepared.

"Had two copies made," he said. "Yours is on top."

[Beat 407]
The logistics took the rest of the morning.

A writ at the Bucktown filing desk: Φ40, forty minutes, a clerk who triple-dated every document on principle and brought the stamp down like accuracy was a physical discipline. A truck from Ria's pool: Φ25, four hours, matched to Kessler's yard specs so nothing would flag at the gate.

He messaged Gantry a time and a place. She was already on the Leavitt apron when he arrived at noon, reading the clinic's posted hours like a woman memorizing a load spec.

Six feet of canvas work jacket, pockets reinforced twice over. Her eyes crossed the front of the building and came back to the angle of the apron, already working out how the printer would ride. She spent forty words a day and wouldn't waste one indoors.

"Bay three," she said. Seven down, thirty-three to go.

"Bay three," Kyle confirmed.

At the clinic door, a nurse in green scrubs was talking a patient down over the threshold. Both of them wore the look of people used to checking the apron for something that should have been there and wasn't.

Gantry read the sign — NO FEE FOR CARE, hand-painted, black on white, the letters thinning where the brush ran low. She looked at Kyle.

"Good printer," she said.

She went to check the rigging.

[Beat 408]
Kessler and Sons took up a quarter-block on the South Loop fringe: cracked concrete apron, chain-link fence rusting at the joints, three cameras someone had aimed at the street instead of the lot — installed for the look of liability coverage, not the fact of it. The gate was the old kind: a clip-framed window, a hand-lettered sign, a yard dog chained behind the shed that had given up barking at strangers and now just watched.

The old man behind the window had the face of someone who'd sat in that chair thirty years and watched better paper than this come through. He read the writ. Read it again. Found the line he wanted: RECOVERY AGENT OF RECORD: HALVORSEN CIVIC RECOVERY LLC, BUCKTOWN.

"Arpad's paper," he said.

"Sold inside the grace window," Kyle said. "Timestamps are on page two. The co-op filing desk is open until seventeen hundred if the yard wants to contest it."

The yard manager turned the writ over. Kyle kept his eyes moving across the lot. Two cameras with clear sightlines. One blind corner behind the office shed. Three vehicles in pieces with their hoods up. At the back, in the repair corner, a young man in a work shirt had been shuffling parts around an engine block with more energy than the job called for. He'd stopped. He was standing up.

The son. Not small. Not slow. Reading the gate the way a young man reads it when he thinks his odds are better than they are.

"My own son says we should push back on this," the yard manager said. He was still holding the writ. His eyes went to Kyle's shoulder — the sheathed sword — then to Kyle's face. "Arpad's been a good customer. Eight years."

"Arpad bought paper that sold inside a grace window," Kyle said. "That's a problem between Arpad and the co-op. Not the clinic's problem."

The son came around the engine block. He hadn't picked up a tool. Both hands empty and held wrong — too still, too deliberately open — which meant the wrench on the oil drum was the thing he was thinking about.

Kyle moved without announcing it. One step right, so the gate post wasn't behind him anymore. Hands open at his sides. Weight balanced. Nothing drawn. He kept his voice flat and easy.

"Your father's going to tell you to sit down. Listen to him the first time."

The yard manager looked at his son. Something passed between them in the silence — a fight they'd had before today, in a kitchen, at the end of a shift, and were having again now in the way they stood next to each other.

"Sit down," the yard manager said.

The son stopped. Two feet of distance, the wrench on the drum, and the anger with nowhere to go. His eyes went to the sword, to Kyle's hands, to his father's face.

"We should contest it," the son said. To his father, not to Kyle.

"We're going to let the man take the printer, and we're going to call Arpad and tell him he's got a co-op problem," the yard manager said. "And you're going back to that engine block until I say otherwise." He let go of the writ. "Bay three. My employee will walk you in."

The son set the wrench down on the oil drum with a sound that said everything he wasn't going to say in front of his father, went back to the engine block, and picked up a socket he didn't need.

Cacophony stayed on Kyle's hip. No rounds drawn, none spent. The son's anger was fair, and it had cost nobody anything.

The gate was open.

[Beat 409]
Gantry had the RP-7 loaded and strapped in eleven minutes, talking to the printer the whole time in a low, even voice: "easy with the corner — that's the corner, the corner's fine — we're going to set you on a pad, not the bare deck." When the last strap was set she patted the chassis once with a wide hand, the way you'd settle a nervous horse, and climbed up front without ceremony.

The drive back to Leavitt Free Medical took twenty-two minutes. Gantry spent most of it explaining the weight-distribution tolerances of the RP-7's chassis to the printer, in the second person, as a professional courtesy.

The intake nurse took delivery on the apron at 15:40. Signed three copies. Stood a moment with her hand flat on the chassis before the bay doors rolled and it went inside.

Kyle sent Breckenridge the confirmation at 15:43. Three words came back at 15:44: FILED AND CREDITED.

The crew logbook pinged at 15:51. He hadn't told Boost about the job.

15:40 — PRINTER RETURNED TO CLINIC. BIG WOMAN TALKED TO IT (PRINTER). PRINTER SEEMED FINE.
15:41 — THE SIGN ON THE CLINIC DOOR SAYS "NO FEE FOR CARE." DENTS ASKS: HOW.
15:44 — DENTS ALSO SAYS RAFTERS IS NOT CRYING. RAFTERS NOTES THIS IS A FACTUAL DISPUTE.
FILING SEPARATELY: BOSS DID NOT USE THE SWORD. REQUESTING EXPLANATION. — B.

Kyle pocketed the terminal and totaled the day on the apron. Writ, Φ40. Truck, Φ25. Gantry's afternoon, Φ80. That was Φ145 out against Breckenridge's standard-rate fee of Φ400 — a Φ255 margin. No injury. No rounds spent. A clinic with its equipment back before the end of shift.

Gantry came up beside him, both hands buried in her jacket pockets, and looked at the clinic door.

"Clean," she said. Thirty-two down, eight to go.

"Clean," Kyle agreed.

She nodded once and walked toward the Pulse station like a woman with three more jobs before dinner who'd already arrived, in her head, at the first.

[Beat 410]
He read the Halvorsen engagement file on the walk back.

Twelve pages in Halvorsen's intake format, put together by someone who understood that in the end the paper is the only argument that holds. The first eight pages were operational: scope, authorization, the tracking-dot delivery chain, the Rotterdam timestamp.

Page nine was where the sloppiness lived.

The client-reference header had carried an internal document ref through from the buyer's side — an export error, automation left uncleaned before the file went to Halvorsen. The header read:

REF: CONTINUITY OFFICE — DISTRICT ACQUISITION PROGRAM, WEST TOWN WEST.

Below it, in the scope field, the buyer's own words: LOCATE AND MONITOR ASSET FOR PURPOSES OF ACQUISITION UNDER DISTRICT PROGRAM PROTOCOLS.

And in the authorization chain, three shells: outermost RADIAL HOLDINGS (CAYMAN), middle FORESIGHT MUNICIPAL PARTNERS, and innermost — the one that had signed the Halvorsen engagement directly — CONTINUITY OFFICE ACQUISITIONS LLC, ILLINOIS.

One word appeared eleven times in twelve pages.

Not locate. Not track. Not recover.

Acquire.

Kyle stopped on the floor of the Leavitt canyon. The Pulse ran its low chord somewhere in the infrastructure below. He looked at the word — eleven times across twelve pages — and thought about the forged survey from the last job. SITE ASSESSED, NO ACTIVE INFRASTRUCTURE, DEAD GLASS. And the carousel junction, where the line had run warm and the room had hummed. Eight surveys, seven of them gone or edited or filed under nothing. The Glove had spent three months building an absence into the city's record, one careful document at a time.

He reached into his coat.

Work terminal out. Screen blank.

No postings. No addenda. The standing contract still showed its last update from two days ago. He'd reached for it three times today — on the Halvorsen landing at 08:58, at the Kessler gate while the son weighed his odds, once more on the Leavitt apron when the nurse signed the third copy and Kyle had half expected a note, a GRATUITY, something. Nothing. The same clean blank each time.

Whatever the client did when it read a threat had been running since morning. And it was reading the right one. Here was the hand behind the Glove — named, with shells and signatures and eleven uses of the word acquire, words that were supposed to stay buried in the metadata and hadn't.

It had known before he did. It had been watching its own address get edited out of the city's record for three months.

He put the terminal away and kept walking.

[Beat 411]
Breckenridge shook his hand at the Halvorsen door at 16:30, with the grip of a man getting back something that had been taken from him.

"Council sees it tomorrow," Breckenridge said. "Fourteen days, recovery complete, no incident on record." He looked at Kyle with the directness of a man out of formal things to say. "Thank you."

Kyle nodded and went down the stairwell.

He bought two bowls at Mrs. Chen's at 19:30 and ate both, paid exact, and Mrs. Chen set out the chili oil without being asked. The wok rang its bright flat note. Evening came in off the tier, grey and purposeful. Kyle ate and didn't reach for the terminal, because he already knew the screen would be blank and there was nothing to do about it over dinner.

He walked home. He sat at the kitchen table with the Halvorsen file flat in front of him and the terminal beside it. He thought about the thing that had been writing letters to maintenance systems that never wrote back — watching its own address get erased from the city's record one survey at a time, staying quiet through a whole day that asked for patience, while it worked out what the danger really was.

He opened the amendment field.

He typed: I'm on payroll. Act like it.

He sent it.

The reply came back in one second — not a pause, not even the half-second it usually took to log something. Instant. The fastest it had ever answered anything.

ACKNOWLEDGED.

Kyle looked at the word. He turned it over the way Pixel turned a component in her hands, checking the solder points, asking what the piece was built to do and whether it was doing it. Acknowledged wasn't the language of a client who'd filed something and moved on. It was the language of a thing that had come, somewhere in the quiet of a long careful day, to a decision.

He put the Halvorsen pages in the drawer where he kept the things he hadn't decided about yet.

The drawer was getting full.

[Beat 412]
He was on Kyle's stool, which was the second one.

Gray suit, mid-fifties, no chrome the eye could find, eating Mrs. Chen's noodles with real, evident appreciation and the table manners of a man raised somewhere that graded on them. He'd hung his umbrella on the edge of the counter by its handle. A second bowl waited beside him — untouched, steam still standing on it — on the next stool.

"Mr. Kyle," he said, without turning. "I took the liberty. Two bowls, I'm told. I've only ordered you the first — I wouldn't presume on the second." He patted the stool. "Able. The Continuity Office. You've been reading our shells for a month. It seemed rude to keep you squinting."

Kyle read the street out of habit. A panel van that didn't belong. Two watchers in a doorway, pretending the rain was interesting. A third on the roofline that only the array caught. Boost's crew was off-shift and three blocks east, no eyes here. Pixel was at her bench. The terminal in his coat had been silent for nine days, and the silence had a new texture now, like held breath.

He sat. Not on the offered stool — one further down, so the untouched bowl sat between them like a hostage.

"You bought my file twice," Kyle said. "Rush rates the second time."

"Three times," Able said, mildly. "The stationer's discretion runs one direction only — he'll never tell you who's buying, but he'll happily tell the buyers that you asked. Lovely business model. I admire it the way I admire weather." He set his chopsticks across the bowl, exactly the way Kyle did — either coincidence or homework, and Kyle knew which. "Shall I do the part where I'm enigmatic, or shall we have the meeting?"

"The meeting."

"Wonderful." Able turned on the stool. His face was pleasant and unhurried, and there was nothing behind it but more pleasantness, all the way down. "Your client. The unsourceable one. Standard rate, perfect books, eleven years of immaculate paper. My office finds unowned assets and brings them into compliance — that's the whole charter, it's on the letterhead. And your client, Mr. Kyle, is the largest unowned asset in the Greater Lake Michigan Zone."

"It's not an asset."

"Everything's an asset. Some of them simply haven't been booked yet." He said it without malice, the way you'd correct a child's grammar. "We've spent a great deal trying to find it directly. The carousel was disappointing — lovely work, that cut, incidentally; our forensic people wrote a genuinely admiring report. The relay survey, the mesh — disappointing, disappointing. So the Office has updated its strategy." He smiled, and the smile was pleasant too. "We've stopped trying to find it. We've decided to buy the one thing it's attached to."

"Me."

"You." Able produced a folder. Good paper stock — Kyle noted the vendor, and the small irony of it. "A lifetime retainer. The number on page one is more than anyone in your trade has ever been offered, and it's the smallest thing in the folder. Page two is the part I'm proud of."

Kyle didn't touch it. Able opened it anyway, gently, like a man showing photographs of his garden, and turned it on the counter so the page faced Kyle.

Page two was a list of names.

MRS. WEN CHEN, PROPRIETOR (W. TOWN). PROTECTED PARTY.
PIXEL [LEGAL NAME ON FILE], ENGINEER (THE PIVOT, 2E). PROTECTED PARTY.
"BOOST" [MINOR — GUARDIANSHIP IRREGULAR], AND ASSOCIATES (SEVEN). PROTECTED PARTIES.
RIA MENSAH, COURIER. PROTECTED PARTY.
VO DINH, SALVAGE. PROTECTED PARTY.

The list went on.

"Protection," Able said, "underwritten by the Office, in perpetuity. Medical, legal, financial, physical. The child gets schooling if she wants it — she's wasted on rooftops. Mrs. Chen's lease problem in March, which she hasn't told you about, goes away." He let that land. "All of it real, all of it written, all of it yours. And in exchange, you do nothing at all. You keep working for your client, exactly as you do, with one extra addendum riding the contract. Ours."

"A tap."

"A vector," Able said. "When the asset trusts the channel enough, the channel becomes the door. You wouldn't even feel the day it happens. Neither would it — which I'd have thought you'd count as a kindness." He closed the folder and slid it an inch toward Kyle, the distance of a man who knows not to push. "I'm told you price things exactly. Price this. Everyone you love, safe forever, for the low cost of going on being exactly who you already are."

The wok rang twice behind the counter. Mrs. Chen hadn't once looked over, which meant she'd taken the measure of the whole room the moment the umbrella came through her lamplight, and had decided her part was to keep the broth honest and her ladle within reach.

Kyle looked at the list of names for a long moment. Then he picked up the folder, squared its edges, and set it back on Able's side of the bowl.

"Two problems," he said. "First: your list is a protection racket wearing a benefits package. You wrote down everyone I love in order of leverage and showed it to me over noodles. I'm supposed to read it as an umbrella. I read it as a target deck with the safeties still on."

"The safeties are generous," Able said, "and they are on."

"Second problem."
