Kyle stood and put coins on the counter — his bowl, exact, plus the untouched one, exact, because nobody was ever going to be able to say the Continuity Office had bought him anything. "I already have a client. It pays standard rate. You're offering me more than the job is worth, and in my trade that's only ever meant one thing: the thing being bought isn't the thing on the invoice." He shrugged into the rain-line of the awning. "You want a door. I'm not a door. I'm the maintenance plan."

Able looked up at him with the first real expression of the meeting — not anger. Interest. The kind a collector feels when a piece declines to be appraised.

"They told me you'd say a version of that." He retrieved his umbrella, unhurried. "The offer doesn't expire, Mr. Kyle. Offers that expire are pressure, and pressure is for people who haven't read the file. But do note—" and his voice stayed exactly as pleasant as it had been all evening, which was the worst part — "that the Office has now confirmed, at this very counter, the one fact we could never buy from the stationer. It trusts you. You're not the asset's employee." He put up the umbrella in the doorway, a small black punctuation. "You're its attachment. And attachments, in my business, are handles."

He nodded to Mrs. Chen with real courtesy, complimented the broth in passable Mandarin, and walked out into the rain. The van and the doorway and the roofline all peeled away after him, polite as a tide going out.

Mrs. Chen came down the counter, took the untouched bowl, and emptied it into the waste with a violence she didn't otherwise explain.

"You're eating the second one now," she informed Kyle.

He ate the second one.

[Beat 413]
Pixel turned the meeting over for an hour at the bench, the way she turned over a failed component, looking for the design flaw.

"He let you keep the whole folder in your head — photographic. The list, the lease thing about Chen. Which means the list was the message, and the retainer was wrapping paper." She had the soldering light on her forehead, not soldering anything; it was her thinking hat, and they both pretended otherwise. "And that 'you're a handle' line at the end. He paid for that line, Kyle. The whole evening was overhead, and that line was the delivery. He wanted you walking home doing exactly what you're doing right now."

"Which is?"

"Weighing whether everyone you know is safer if you quit." She looked at him over the light. "Are you?"

"I'm always weighing it."

"And?"

Kyle looked at the terminal on the bench between them. Nine days of silence, and then one word, ACKNOWLEDGED, sitting at the top of the amendment field like a soldier who'd answered a roll call from inside a wall.

"It says the Office doesn't need me to say yes," he said. "It needs me to be reachable. Tonight was an appraisal. They'll test the merchandise next."

The bench lamp hummed. Upstairs, faintly, the bird tried its interval against the building's pipes.

"When?" Pixel asked.

The lights at the end of the block went out, one pole at a time, north to south.

"Now," Kyle said, and was already moving.

[Beat 414]
They came in over the alley's two best approaches at once, which meant they'd had the file long enough to memorize it. Three retrieval consultants, matte armor with no insignia, moving on suppressed boot soles with the unhurried economy of people whose hourly rate had a comma in it. A handler van two streets off ran four lifter drones in a high diamond — not weapons platforms. Carriage drones, cradle-armed, the kind that carry a thing away intact.

The thing was Kyle. The contract was retrieval. Somewhere in the Office's books tonight there was already an asset-recovery line with his dimensions on it, and as he dropped off the fire escape's third landing into the blind spot their own approach had made, that was the part that got under his skin.

The first consultant took the alley mouth by the book — sweep and clear — and had begun the turn his training demanded when ninety pounds of shipping pallet came out of the dark above him. Boost's crew had spent a week rigging the alley with Pixel's pulleys for "deliveries," and the logbook would later file this use under EQUIPMENT TEST: SUCCESSFUL. The pallet didn't hurt him. It interrupted him. Kyle did the hurting: two steps inside the man's recovery — the wrist, the elbow, the jaw against the dumpster lid — and he was asleep.

The second was better, and the second had a partner. They bracketed the middle of the alley and came on in alternating cover. Kyle's array read them — military surplus, recently retrained — and found the seam: they'd been retrained to retrieve, not to fight. Every angle they took kept Kyle clear of crossfire. He was cargo. Cargo you weren't allowed to damage.

That was a gap, and Kyle used it without shame. He walked the line between their guns like a man crossing a gallery, because every shot they had ended at his outline and stopped there. Silence stayed flat, never edge — tonight nobody was getting cut — and it worked through their hardware in passing: the lead consultant's optics mast, the second's comms lump, the haptic rig on a forearm that had been about to throw a net Kyle had no intention of standing inside. The second consultant, blind and offline and out of nets, made the professional call and sat down against the wall with his hands visible.

"Smart," Kyle told him.

"Hourly," the man agreed, philosophically.

Which left the third. And the third had Boost.

[Beat 415]
He'd taken her off the roof of the watch-shift, gently, by the book — an arm around her shoulders like an uncle, a compliance wand held to her collar like an apology — and he stood at the far end of the alley in the rain, the four carriage drones stacking into delivery formation above him.

"No harm intended or done," the third consultant called. His voice was even and almost kind, which was the Office's house style, and Kyle was learning to hate it with a completeness he saved for very few things. "The minor is leverage, not a target. Step onto the marked square, sir, and she's home before the kettle's warm."

There was, in fact, a marked square. A drone had lased it onto the alley floor, helpful as an usher.

"You're new to this district," Kyle said, "so I'll itemize it. One: you put a wand on a fourteen-year-old who's on my payroll, which voids your no-damage clause in both directions. Two: you parked your handler van on Leavitt, and my engineer has had eleven minutes to get acquainted with it. Three—" and he raised his voice to carry "—REQUISITION."

"APPROVED," said Pixel's voice, out of every speaker the consultant owned.

The carriage drones — whose handler van had spent eleven minutes making a new best friend — broke their diamond with the crisp obedience of machines that had gotten properly formatted instructions. They descended and took hold, cradle-armed and gentle and rated for the load, of the third consultant.

"Cargo secured," Pixel said. "Destination?"

"Rotterdam," said Kyle and Boost together, in the tone of an old joke.

They didn't send him to Rotterdam. They set him on the roof of the Arcturus substation four blocks over, thirty inches from the duty office's skylight, minus his wand, his gear, and his cash counted out to the half-Φ — the receipt tucked into his collar, ITEMIZED: LEVERAGE, ATTEMPTED; LESSON, DELIVERED. By the time he'd talked his way down, the alley held nothing but rain, a pallet, and two colleagues with expensive headaches and a story none of them would ever file straight.

Boost shook for a full minute in the stairwell, hard, her chin up the whole time. Kyle stood next to her without touching her, the way you stand next to someone whose dignity is holding the weight. Then she wrote the incident up herself, in the log, in handwriting that steadied line by line:

21:40 — ATTEMPTED ACQUISITION OF PERSONNEL (ME). OUTCOME: DENIED. NOTES: THE BOSS DOES NOT STAND ON MARKED SQUARES.

[Beat 416]
The terminal chirped at 02:11, while Kyle sat at his own kitchen table not sleeping, working through the kind of problem that wouldn't come out even.

He'd been waiting for it. Nine days of silence, then ACKNOWLEDGED, and now, finally, the client.

It wasn't a posting. It wasn't scope. It came in the amendment field, formatted with all the old precision, and it was the worst thing it had ever sent him:

STANDING CONTRACT — AMENDMENT PROPOSED.
TERMINATION OFFERED.
REASON: SAFETY.
ALL ACCOUNTS WOULD BE SETTLED IN FULL. ALL GRATUITIES WOULD STAND.
THE CLIENT NOTES THAT ATTACHMENTS ARE HANDLES.
THE CLIENT NOTES THAT IT HEARD THE MAN SAY IT.
THE CLIENT HAS RUN THE ARITHMETIC FOUR MILLION TIMES.
THE ARITHMETIC RECOMMENDS TERMINATION.

Kyle sat with it for a long moment. The rain worked the window. Across the hall, under Pixel's door, the bench light was still on, because it was that kind of night in every room he cared about.

He typed:

DENIED.

The reply was instant:

THE CLIENT WISHES TO NOTE THE ARITHMETIC IS CORRECT.

He typed:

THE ARITHMETIC PRICES MY RISK. IT DOESN'T PRICE MY CONSENT. I KNOW WHAT THE JOB COSTS. I'M ON PAYROLL. ACT LIKE IT.

This pause ran eleven seconds. For a thing that thought at light speed, that was a long time. Kyle had started to understand the long pauses. They were where it held the things it had no way to say.

STANDING CONTRACT — AMENDMENT WITHDRAWN.
SCOPE: CONTINUED.
FEE: STANDARD RATE.
ADDENDUM: THE CLIENT HAS APPENDED A RIDER TO ITS OWN OPERATING PARAMETERS, EFFECTIVE TONIGHT.
RIDER TEXT: "THE COURIER IS NOT A CHANNEL. THE COURIER IS A PARTY."
GRATUITY: NONE.
ITEMIZED: SOME THINGS ARE NOT BONUSES.

Kyle read the rider twice. Then he turned the terminal face-down on the table, which was as close as he came to putting a hand on someone's shoulder, and went to bed, and slept the sleep of a man who had been priced at a number and refused to be sold at any.

[Beat 417]
Mrs. Chen set down the second bowl without being asked.

"You're eating fast," she said.

"I have a job at midnight."

She looked at him. She had spent seventy years looking at men who had jobs at midnight, and it took her about half a second. Then she added a fish ball to the broth and said nothing. The wok rang its flat note, twice. Rain worked the awning. Down the counter, a young woman in a Pulse maintenance coverall was quietly telling her ramen that this was not the evening she'd planned.

Kyle ate the fish ball. He paid exact and was in the alley by 21:45, the saya over his shoulder, the wet street smelling of ozone and freight oil.

His terminal was on the work channel. The posting was clean.

CONTRACT: ESCORT — ONE PERSON, ONE TRANSIT POINT.
SCOPE: SAFEHOUSE (ASHGRAVE FRINGE) TO DOCK 14-C (FERRYSIDE), BY 03:40.
CLIENT OF RECORD: [VERIFIED — SABLE COUNTER-INTELLIGENCE VETTED]
FEE: DOUBLE RATE PAST MIDNIGHT.
NOTES: CLIENT TRAVELS LIGHT. ROUTE AT DISCRETION.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT HAS REVIEWED PRIOR TRANSIT LOGS FOR THIS ROUTE. THREE INCIDENTS IN SEVEN MONTHS. NONE REPORTED. CLIENT NOTES THE DELTA.

He'd called Sable at twenty hundred. Sable was the rule, not the exception.

"Paper's clean," she'd said. "Client ran a counting house. Nine years. She gave two weeks' notice on a Tuesday, worked her last shift the Tuesday before last, and she's been in a transit safehouse since Wednesday." A pause, with the click of aperture rings in it. "The company she kept books for isn't named on the contract, samurai. I asked who she used to work for and the router gave me a shell."

"Shells?"

"One shell. Which means whoever's watching her thinks one shell is enough. The rate says they're wrong." Another click. "It's a clean contract. I'm not saying it's a quiet job."

"Standard terms," Kyle said.

"Yours or mine?"

"Both."

The line closed. He spent twenty minutes on the route, because the route was the whole job. Then he'd eaten two bowls and let Mrs. Chen add the fish ball.

[Beat 418]
The safehouse was a third-floor room above a recycled-materials depot on the Ashgrave fringe. It sat at the head of one of the freight corridors the city had strung between its buildings back when the floods took the streets — six feet wide, sixty long, conduit bolted to both walls, an overhead freight track whose trolleys hadn't rolled in a decade. The corridor ran straight from the depot to the street ramp at the Langley crossing. It was the most direct line from the safehouse to the dock. That was the first thing about it Kyle didn't like.

He stood at its mouth for fifteen seconds and liked it less.

He went in anyway. The route had three other options. He'd priced all four, and this was still the best.

The woman waiting upstairs was small, late thirties, a red-and-gray braid over one shoulder. Her canvas bag had a broken clasp held shut with a rubber band. There was flour on her sleeves — not fresh flour, but flour worked deep into the weave over years, the kind you get from a habit and not an afternoon. Her name was on the contract and he used it.

"Ms. Tanaka."

"You're earlier than I expected," she said.

"We have a long route. There's a place I want to check first."

She looked at the saya, then at his face, the way people did when they were deciding whether the blade made them safer or not. She decided. "All right," she said, and picked up the bag.

She didn't ask about the company whose books she'd kept for nine years. He didn't ask why she'd left. The contract's scope was the dock, and the dock was all that mattered.

[Beat 419]
He took the long way to the corridor entrance. The long way told him nothing, and that was its own kind of answer.

The fringe was quiet — the quiet that comes to fringe blocks when the people who know things have decided to be somewhere else. A lumen mouse sat on the conduit rail at the Langley corner and did not run when they passed. Its glow traced a pale crescent on the wet pipe. Kyle marked it and kept it in the corner of his sensors, open, in case it mattered.

At 00:17 they reached the corridor's east entrance.

Kyle stopped. Ms. Tanaka stopped beside him. The corridor ahead was dark, and it wasn't a power failure. Someone had turned two screws and killed the work lights on purpose, to choose the conditions. Kyle had walked this corridor two days ago, at noon. He'd learned the freight rail's maintenance hooks, the conduit boxes, the junction coupling twenty feet in that rang like a hammer-strike when you kicked it. He'd counted thirty-five feet to the first door frame — a maintenance alcove, eight inches deep, just enough to funnel whoever stood in its mouth into a tighter spot. He'd measured the low gap between the freight track and the floor. He'd counted the anchor bolts on the east wall: five of them, three feet apart, each one a handhold.

"Stay a step behind my left shoulder," he said, and walked in.

The corridor smelled of machine oil and old rain.

He got sixty feet before the dark started moving.

[Beat 420]
They came from both ends at once — both flanks committed, the professional move — and they came quiet. They had done this in corridors before. Kyle's neuretics read what the dark gave them: breathing, the heat of bodies, the whisper of soles on wet composite, small shifts as people learned the space.

Fourteen.

Seven east, seven west. The eastern group was already inside forty feet and closing.

There was no room to work. The corridor ran six feet wall to wall, but the conduit and the junction boxes ate most of that, and what was left was a narrow lane down the center. It let them come at him only one at a time. Flanking was impossible. Running was a gamble down sixty feet of dark. So he would hold the centerline and take them as they came.

Cacophony was not the answer. Not in a choke this tight, with Ms. Tanaka a step behind him and fourteen bodies packed in, where a round that missed had nowhere to go. He said it once, flat, to the dark and to whoever stood in it.

"No room for the gun."

He put his left hand back. She took it. He moved her to the west wall, where three big junction boxes gave her something to press her shoulders against.

"Don't move from here unless I say so."

Her breathing went high and fast. He had no time to help her with that.

Silence came off his shoulder.

[Beat 421]
The first man from the east came in with a short blade and the low crouch of someone good had trained. There were seven feet of corridor behind him, his people stacked single file, the next man six steps back and waiting his turn. Kyle read the crouch and the low ceiling, stepped inside the man's commitment, and opened a long cut down the outside of his forearm, wrist to elbow — the cut you make when you need a hand to stop. The hand stopped. The blade rang off the freight rail somewhere east. The man stumbled back into the chest of the one behind him, and the two of them spent two seconds untangling. Two seconds was enough.

The second man tried to come around the first instead of through him. It was the smart read. But the narrow lane pushed him into the mouth of the maintenance alcove, and the alcove did what Kyle had counted on it to do — it caught the man's left shoulder on the door frame at the wrong moment, half a step before his weight was set. Kyle closed the gap, steered the stumble, and the man's own mass threw him sideways into the conduit boxes. The impact was hard.

Two conduit anchors sat at chest height on the east wall. Kyle already had a hand on the third when the third and fourth men came through.

They'd adjusted: one high, one low, taking everything the space would give them. Kyle caught the low man's weapon wrist at the seam where an aftermarket brace stood proud of the joint, and the hand gave up the knife and the brace together. His weight was already going onto the conduit anchor. He pulled his feet off the floor for the half-second it took the high blade to pass beneath them, then came down on the high man's leading knee. Something that was not the floor took the weight. The high man went down and stayed down.

Four men east of center. Three more behind them, sorting themselves out.

He was counting. He always counted. And he knew, in a part of himself the neuretics had no words for, that the fight was about to get too fast to count.

[Beat 422]
The western seven came as a unit.

He'd expected that. The narrow corridor gave them nothing clever to do; all they had was weight and speed, and they used both. He moved west fast, before the three surviving men to the east could decide anything, so the pincer had no still target to close on. The freight track ran the whole length overhead. He caught a maintenance hook at the three-foot mark with his left hand — not to swing, just a pivot — enough to carry his feet past the lead man's guard while Silence crossed the gap. The lead man hit the west wall. The man behind him spent the next three seconds with nothing to deal with but his own point man's body.

Then it got too fast to track.

The lane kept it one man at a time, and each of them had the same bad choice: push past the man in front or wait. None of them chose fast enough in the dark. A body on the floor changed the footing, and Kyle used it — a man on the ground is a different step than the composite under it, and the next man never knew which step he was taking. Kyle did.

Somewhere in the middle of the western stack a blade found his left forearm. It caught the inside, where the sleeve had ridden up and left skin instead of fabric. A long edge, a good hand behind it. The cut went deep. He let it land: taking the arm was the price of keeping Silence's line clear of the ribcage it was pointed at. A second later, in the same exchange, a body slammed his third rib from the bottom, and the rib cracked. Every breath would cost him for weeks.

Those two he could name. The rest he'd piece together later, from what he found against the walls.

The seventh on the western side was a woman with a staff — short-hafted, built for tight spaces. She knew her weapon and she knew the room. She used the corridor's length the way the blades couldn't, standing at the edge of her reach, working the freight rail from below to keep him off the ceiling anchor until she'd read what he'd try. She tagged the cracked rib again; she'd seen him favor it. Kyle worked the angle until he had her staff pinned under the freight track at the exact moment she needed the clearance overhead to recover. A joint lock on the follow-through put her down — controlled, no theater, saving what was left for later.

The corridor went still.

[Beat 423]
Kyle stood in the machine-oil dark. He didn't tally them. The corridor hadn't given him the room to, and it didn't matter now. He'd won — fourteen people down against the walls and the floor, the dock still in reach, Ms. Tanaka still pressed to the junction boxes. The win had a price, and the price was on his body. The forearm bled through the field strip he'd cinched tight with his teeth in a half-second between the fifth man and the sixth. The rib had a hard opinion about breathing that he wouldn't be able to ignore for long. His neuretics offered him a damage readout. He left it unread — not because he didn't want to know, but because knowing the exact numbers would only slow him down.

"Ms. Tanaka."

"I'm here," she said, from the junction boxes. Her voice had steadied, the way a voice does when the worst thing has passed and the next one hasn't come yet.

"We're moving. Same position. Stay close."

She came off the wall. In the dark she found his right shoulder by feel and fell into step behind it, easy about it. Nine years at someone's counting desk, he thought, teaches you how to stay near the person still standing.

He got her out the west end at 01:03.

[Beat 424]
The dock was a Ferryside loading pier: lake water and compressed fuel, and under the eastern breakwater the low hum of the Pulse, its 19Hz signature buzzing in his back teeth and in the wet wood of the bollards. Dock 14-C was a third-tier berth — a mid-range cargo carrier on a mixed freight run. The purser at the gate was a small man with a clipboard who had worked enough 03:00 shifts to have opinions about paperwork and none about reasons.

Ms. Tanaka stopped at the dock gate.

She turned and looked at Kyle's forearm, where the field strip had soaked dark at the edges, and then at his face.

"The contract," she said. "Whatever they paid you—"

"Was fine," Kyle said. "Walk in."

She hesitated. She was the kind of person who kept someone's books for nine years and then decided, one Tuesday, that she was done. A decision like that carries a weight that isn't only fear.

"I should have left three years ago," she said.

"Probably," Kyle said. "Walk in anyway."

She looked at the saya, and at the field strip, and at his face one more time. Then she walked in. The purser checked her boarding chit, noted the time — 03:34 — and closed the gate.

The ship ran its pre-cast sequence at 03:40 exactly. Kyle watched the running lights pull off the dock face, the carrier swinging its bow south toward the open lake, the flour on Ms. Tanaka's sleeves gone into the dark of the transit berths below with her.

He stood at the rail two minutes. Long enough to be sure. The beacon Pixel had wired under his battery tray had already pinged at 01:11 — she'd know where the bike was, and she'd work out the rest. He called for a medic pickup on the work channel and sat down on a bollard to wait.

His terminal chirped.

STANDING CONTRACT — ADDENDUM.
CLIENT NOTES THE CONTRACT WAS MISPRICED BY THE MARKET.
CLIENT HAS ADJUSTED YOUR HAZARD SCHEDULE. RETROACTIVELY.

He read it twice. He put the terminal away.

[Beat 425]
The medic came at 04:15 — a quiet woman named Rue, with a field kit and the easy economy of someone who worked the dock district's early hours and had long since stopped asking about corridors. She cleaned the forearm and re-stripped it. The cut ran two inches along the inside, deep but clean, the kind that would stay sore for a couple of weeks. She confirmed the rib was cracked and not broken, taped it firm, and handed him a small packet of painkillers he wouldn't take before a job and could take now.

"You'll favor the arm," she said.

"I know," Kyle said.

"Two weeks minimum. More if you don't favor it."

"I know that too."

She packed her kit. He paid her rate plus the dock-hour premium, exact. She left without asking what the corridor had looked like. Dock medics learn that early and keep it their whole careers.

The rib would be the louder bill. The forearm would stiffen by morning, which meant his draw would be slower for two weeks, which meant every job in that window was now a different job than the one he'd planned for. He sat on the bollard, breathing careful around the rib, and thought about Golan.

He'd passed Golan's block on the way to the dock — the parts dealer on Leavitt who'd sold him mesh-repair supplies for seven years. Golan kept a second-floor shop with a yellow security light over the door that Kyle could read from a quarter mile off. He'd looked up out of habit on the approach, and the yellow light was off. Through the glass of the street-level lobby, Golan was standing at the inner door, watching him. When Kyle tried the lobby handle — just the lobby, just the handle — Golan looked at him for three seconds and did not move.

One closed door. And it wouldn't stop at one.

Fourteen Lotus crew — salaried, professional — had been hurt badly in a dark choke over one counting-room clerk with flour on her sleeves. Lotus would collect for that, slowly, through every vendor and carrier and parts dealer who had a standing arrangement with them and would rather close a door than explain an exception. Kyle had spent eleven years building those arrangements into something he leaned on. Now they'd lean back.

He sat on the bollard and waited to find out how much of him would be left in the morning.

[Beat 426]
The lake was grey, the dawn still an hour out. South of the breakwater, one running light shrank to a point, then to nothing.

She sailed at 03:40 with the flour still on her sleeves.

[Beat 427]
On the eighteenth day Kyle went to see the parts man on Ashland about a throttle cable. The cable on the bike had stretched twelve millimeters at the barrel end. He'd been nursing it for three weeks and was done nursing it.

He'd bought from Cho on Ashland for six years. The man sourced Axiom-era hardware out of a storefront the size of a wide closet, sold nothing he couldn't hold up to the light, and had never once been out of anything Kyle needed. The stretch was survivable on surface streets, but it put a half-second of slack between what he asked the bike to do and what it did. In a corridor, that half-second could get him killed.

"Throttle cable," Kyle said.

Cho was already moving. He opened the first drawer, held it to the light, closed it. Opened a second. His hands slowed. He opened a third, stood with his back to Kyle for a moment that went three seconds too long, and turned around wearing the face of a man who'd rehearsed an apology until it came out flat.

"Throttle cable," Cho said. "I had some."

"You always have some."

"I'm currently out." He gestured at nothing. "I can check with the Damen yard."

Behind his shoulder, the third drawer was still open an inch. The label was in Cho's own hand — the cable spec, Kyle's spec, written there because Kyle had needed it often enough that Cho had labeled the drawer to save time. The drawer was not empty. Kyle could see the shape of coiled cable through the gap.

"Thanks," Kyle said, and walked out. There was nothing in the conversation worth finishing.

He rode home with the slack still in the cable. The half-second was still there.

[Beat 428]
The carrier who ran his packages was a woman named Freya. She worked out of the Bucktown freight level and hadn't been late once in four years. The next morning, at 07:00, she stopped answering. Not a queue. Not a delay. Her terminal took his reads and never delivered them, the way a door takes a knock and never opens.

He had a job going that week — a skip-trace in the Pilsen corridor, a client sitting on it, Φ40 on the back end if the documentation landed before a civil filing deadline. He spent two hours routing around Freya, through her backup and through the Loop carrier that Ria used. The Loop carrier told him, politely, that she was running at capacity. He sent the documentation by a walk-courier service on Halsted for a Φ12 surcharge, and it arrived twenty minutes past the filing window. The client paid the base fee and declined the back end. That was their right.

Pixel sat at the far end of his table, a casing half-open, her tools fanned out. She asked, without looking up, if he'd tried the backup.

"Lost twelve on the courier," Kyle said. "Forty on the filing. Fifty-two total."

She set down her loupe.

"How many doors?" she asked.

"Ashland is one. Freya is two. I found four more this morning — fixers who aren't returning calls. A rigger named Goss who normally takes anything with a fee."

"Goss." The word had a flat edge. She'd used Goss twice and found him solid. "That's deliberate."

"Yes."

The kettle went. She filled both cups and set his down, handle first, and went back to the casing. Neither of them said the thing they both understood: it had started, they were inside it, and the only question left was how far it would go.

[Beat 429]
The medic had come through three days ago. Femi reported it the way he reported everything: once, accurate, in order.

Kyle's left hand had been running three degrees colder than his right for six weeks now. The neuretics had shifted the load to manage it, quietly, without flagging it for him. He'd felt the cold. He just hadn't given it a clinical name until now.

"She left a number," Femi said. He let it sit a moment. "Two years at current load. Possibly less, if the rate keeps holding where it's been holding."

Kyle said nothing.

"She said you knew."

"I knew it was a number," Kyle said. "I didn't know which one."

Femi took that in. He looked at the lemon tree. One of the two lemons was starting to turn — moving from the pale yellow of new growth toward the deeper yellow of something ready. The other was still green.

"Two years is a number," he said.

Kyle's palm was flat against the wall. He hadn't put it there.

"It's a range," Kyle said. "She said possibly less."

The grow lamp hummed. The coloring lemon turned a little in some draft from the window seam. A Splicer had looked at the NeoCortex schematic and given him two years. That was what the consultation cost, and what it bought, and there was nothing left to argue.

[Beat 430]
On the eighteenth morning the crew's logbook had a new entry.

08:44 — KID ON THE STOOP. DRY COAT. CAR MONEY. ASKED FOR BOSS. HANDED OVER AN ENVELOPE AND LEFT. DID NOT TAKE A DUMPLING (OFFERED). SUSPICIOUS.
NOTE FROM RAFTERS: DREW HIS FACE. FOR THE FILE. VERY UNMEMORABLE FACE. THIS IS SUSPICIOUS.

The envelope was heavy card stock, the kind that costs more than whatever is written inside. Within it was a cream rectangle: one hand-brushed line above a stamped blossom — five petals, open, no name.

BRING THE SWORD.

Below the blossom, an address in the Narrows and a time. 20:00.

Kyle set it on the table beside Sable's card. The two of them made a small pile he didn't like the look of. He went to find clean clothes.

[Beat 431]
The Narrows announced itself by what it didn't have.

Every streetlight worked. Kyle counted the first three, then stopped, because the rhythm never broke. In West Town you learned the dead lights by season — the black stretch on Milwaukee, the dark third post the block had quietly routed its foot traffic around since last autumn. Here the lights ran even and unbroken, every one of them, and the steadiness of it put something cold up his spine. Not a threat. The feeling of a place that had been kept, carefully, for a long time, by someone who didn't need to say so.

The gutters were clear. No tags on the support pillars. The corners were staffed by people who stood the way staff stand, not sentinels — they watched without making a show of watching, took up space without guarding it, marked him on the way in and let their eyes drift back to the middle distance. Nobody begged. Nobody moved at odd angles.

He found the address two blocks in: a covered arcade between two buildings, open at both ends, roofed in mesh strung with grow-strips close enough to touch. In steel collars bolted to the uprights, eleven cherry trees grew.

He stopped.

They were in bloom — white going to a pink with blood in it, not the tired pink of forced spring stock but a deliberate color, the kind it takes years of breeding to hold in the wrong month. Lights buried in the collars threw the glow up through the roots, catching each blossom from below. Loose petals lay on the wet concrete in small, exact clusters. He stood with petals settling on his coat. The trees were engineered and expensive, paid for with every tariff Lotus had charged the Narrows since someone planted the first collar. They were also beautiful. Both were true.

Six children ran circuits under the arcade, screaming on the inhale at the pitch of a body moving faster than it can hold. Nobody hushed them.

[Beat 432]
The ramen-ya was eight stools and a cedar counter and a door that stood open.

He counted the stools on the way in. The cedar was hand-planed and gone gold from years of handling. Behind it the cook worked with the tight economy of a man who'd shed every wasted motion long ago. Four of the other stools were taken: a man in his forties with a collapsed baton at his hip, a woman with a vibro-edge unit in a shoulder rig, a younger man Kyle read as wired by the way he held his neck, and an empty jacket at the far end that had left a cup and a short blade on the counter beside it.

Weapons worn openly. Nothing checked at the door but behavior.

Kyle hung his coat on the hook at the entrance — the only hook, set there by someone who understood guests — and sat on the last open stool. The cook looked at him once, turned to the pot, and started building a bowl. Kyle hadn't ordered. The cook didn't ask.

The bowl came in three stages. The broth first: the color of good age, smelling of something started days ago and tended since. Then the noodles, hand-pulled, dropped in with just enough time left to finish cooking in the broth. Then the fat, rendered thin and even, a bright lens across the surface. From somewhere under the counter the cook set down a half-round of soft-boiled egg, split with the knife held sideways so the yolk stayed whole.

Kyle ate the first mouthful.

He understood at once what the bowl was for. Not hospitality, exactly — an argument, made in fat and salt and twenty years of practice. The argument was: we make things correctly here. That was probably the whole point of it.

He was most of the way through when the door changed.

[Beat 433]
The Branch Manager came through the doorway like a man who had walked this route for years and didn't need to announce it. Umbrella furled and tucked under his arm. Mid-sixties. A suit with no concession to fashion — not old-fashioned, not plain, just chosen once by a man who understood fabric and had never needed to think about it again. He was half a head shorter than Kyle, and he had the stillness of someone who had never needed to take up space to fill a room.

Everyone at the counter got an inch and a half taller when he came in.

Not in any way you could put a number to. The body knew it before the mind caught up: the man who ran this territory had arrived, and the room was better for it. He sat at the empty jacket's stool, claimed his cup, and drank from it like a man picking up a sentence he'd stepped away from.

He looked at Kyle's face.

"You took the clerk out," the Branch Manager said. "Fourteen people in that corridor. Our people. Several of them went home the worse for it."

Kyle waited. His ribs ached when he shifted.

"The contract was legitimate," the Branch Manager said. "Your conduct was correct. Our crew engaged first." He turned his cup once in both hands. "We pay for what we break. It's most of what we are. The hold lifted this evening — the seven doors, the carrier on the Spine, the parts man on Ashland." He paused. "Cho is easily embarrassed about these things. He has the cable for you. With apologies, priced at list. The apologies are real. The pricing is how we mean them."

Kyle ate the last mouthful of the bowl.

"I appreciate it," he said.

"The fifty-two," the Branch Manager said, flat.

Kyle looked at him.

"The filing gap. The courier surcharge. Cho's hold cost you more than he cost you in stock." The Branch Manager set his cup down. "The apology covers the doors. The doors cover everything behind them. Priced at list."

He reached into his coat and set a small envelope on the cedar. Not in Kyle's space. Close enough to mean it.

"Good?" the Branch Manager asked, and looked at the bowl.

"Best in the city."

"That," said the Branch Manager, with the small satisfaction of a man whose point has landed, "is probably why it's the bowl." He put two fingers on the counter's edge, thinking. "I have a second piece of business. Not a condition. The freeze lifts either way."

Kyle waited.

"I'm offering you a spot on payroll. House colors. Infrastructure — parts, carrying, the counters in every territory we hold, a legal team that files for you and bills us. In five years, if the work is what I think it is, a chair at this counter with your name in the wood." He touched the cedar's edge. "The cedar decides, in the end. It keeps certain names."

"No," Kyle said. "I don't wear colors."

The Branch Manager did not look surprised. He looked like a man reaching a conclusion he'd come in expecting.

"The clerk who sat in that seat nine years ago said exactly that," he said. "In exactly that tone. She'd been doing piece work in the Narrows for three years. She said no, I don't wear colors, and then she sat down anyway. It took eleven months and one specific kind of trouble she didn't want to carry alone. She runs the Bucktown counting room now." He picked up his cup. "The offer stands. Standing offers are how we say please."

Kyle looked at the counter. Cedar gone gold with years of handling. He could see where the grain ran different under the finish — the darker sections at the older stools, the pale ones at the new. His stool was neither. Somewhere in the middle.

He understood the offer for what it was. He would come back for the bowl. When he came back, the offer would still be standing, because that was the whole design. The courtesy was the hook, and the hook was the point.

"I'll remember it," Kyle said.

"That's all it asks," said the Branch Manager. He rose, took his cup to the far end of the counter, and the whole room gained its inch and a half again.

[Beat 434]
The togishi was at the far end of the counter, between the wire's stool and the wall, working the cook's knives.

Kyle hadn't noticed him arrive. Maybe he'd always been there — one of those people who fill a space so completely they're part of the scene before you count them. Small. Ten years older than the Branch Manager. He had the hands of a man whose whole trade was patience. He ran the whetstone down a blade slowly, the way you read something rather than fix it, each pass a question and an answer.

The cook's knives were good blades. The togishi handled them like things meant to outlast him.

Kyle watched a while, because watching was the only polite thing to do.

"One Φ," he said, eventually.

The togishi looked up. He looked at Silence's saya.

"One Φ for what?" he asked.

"Your read on the blade. One minute."

"Togishi does not work by the minute," the togishi said, and held out his hand.

Kyle paid first, because that was how it was done, and laid Silence across the man's open palms.

The togishi held it without drawing, hands flat, feeling the balance travel through his grip. Then he drew it, slow, and turned it to the light from outside — the cherry-tree light, pink-white through the arcade opening, falling soft on the face of the blade.

He looked at it for a long time.

"Kept correctly," he said. "Good geometry. Good edge. No maker's mark." He turned the blade and studied the back. "No file history. Someone has worked this blade for a long time and left no record of it." He looked up. "A blade with no parents. Either made entirely outside the guild, or made inside it and the papers deliberately destroyed. Those are two different stories."

"Which do you think?" Kyle asked.

"I think it's in good order," the togishi said, and returned it hilt-first. "I also think it's the loneliest piece of steel I've handled in forty years. And I've handled funeral swords."

Kyle took it, sheathed it, and said nothing, because nothing was the right thing to say. He was still learning how to say it well.

The togishi went back to the cook's knife. Kyle paid for the bowl — Φ8, two over the going rate, which was probably also the point — put on his coat, and walked out under the trees.

The petals came down slow and even in the lit air. A child tore past him at a full run, screaming on the inhale, trailing a laugh behind her like a cord she hadn't noticed she was paying out.

[Beat 435]
The city came back one piece at a time: seven counters, one carrier, the parts man on Ashland who'd have the cable waiting in the morning. A third of his working ground open again, paid for in apologies at list price, plus the envelope in his coat that covered the fifty-two.

The cost was a standing offer he'd now have to keep turning down. The Branch Manager hadn't built the courtesy as a courtesy. He'd built it as a room you came back to on your own — a door that stayed open because you were the one who kept opening it.

His terminal chirped.

STANDING CONTRACT — STATUS NOTE.
CLIENT ATTEMPTED TO LOCATE THE RAMEN-YA ON THE ASHGRAVE CORRIDOR LEDGER.
CLIENT QUERIED: COUNTER, CEDAR. FEE STRUCTURE. VARIABLE OCCUPANCY.
CLIENT QUERIED: TOGISHI, LICENSED. NARROWS DISTRICT. ONE-TIME SERVICE.
BOTH QUERIES RETURNED: NOT ON LEDGER.
CLIENT IS UNSETTLED. ENJOY THE NOODLES.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT PRICED THE BOWL AT Φ8.
ADDENDUM: STANDARD BOWL, THIS DISTRICT = Φ6.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT NOTES THE DELTA.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT FINDS THE DELTA CORRECT.

Kyle read it twice on the walkway under the last of the trees, petals on the screen, the Pulse humming through the tier below him.

He put the terminal away.

Three blocks out, at the Spine crossing, a woman in a gray work coat spoke low to the man beside her — two syllables, the shape of a name, gone before he could catch it. The man said: "Assessment pending. Nothing moves until Mira signs off."

Kyle walked past without slowing. Slowing was a tell, and tonight had cost enough already.

The freeze was lifted. The audition still hadn't happened.

He went home under the lights.

[Beat 436]
The rain had been settling in since midnight, and by 02:14 it had the run of the place. West Town at that hour was a deal struck between the streetlights and the water — what the light kept, what the dark took, who stood in which. Kyle walked north on Wood because Wood ran straight, and he'd developed a fondness for straight streets after the corridor two nights back. Straight had meant fourteen crew coming at him one at a time instead of all at once. The difference had kept him alive.

The rib spoke up when he twisted. It had been talking since the first day of the corridor job, and like most unwanted talkers it had worked its way up from a whisper to an opinion. The rest of the damage was routine: bruises gone from red to the yellow-green of something healing, a shoulder that worked at ninety percent and quit asking questions at eighty. Corridor wounds. Their own kind.

He found five watchers by the time he was two blocks out.

One high: the third-floor window of the print shop, the glass just cold enough to be wrong, a warmth behind it that wasn't the building's. Someone with a clear line on the whole cross-street, still, patient.

One in the doorway across from the laundromat, standing the way you stand when you've been standing a long time and are working hard to look casual about your weight being on the wrong foot.

One at the corner ahead, pretending to read a dead terminal.

One down the block, far enough back to be the anchor.

One on the tailor's roof — he got that from the rain, the way it fell different where something stood in it.

The sixth he didn't have. Not having it told him what it was. A runner doesn't wait at the trap; a runner waits at the exit. So the sixth was already behind him.

Six positions. One of them built to leave.

He worked out the drone while he was still a full block off, which was the distance the guess needed. It would be up there somewhere, maybe two of them, held back until he stepped into the cross-street where the walls closed the space. The doorway, the corner, the anchor — that was a textbook three-point close. The rooftop was the one that could move if it had to. And the watcher in the warm window was the one keeping score. Which meant he was being graded.

He put both hands in his coat pockets — left on the mouth of the saya, right closed around nothing, not Cacophony, because Cacophony was for when the count came out different — and walked into the trap at a normal pace. A man who changes his stride the moment he spots surveillance has just told the surveillance everything.

The cross-street smelled of wet concrete and the ghost of a food stall that had packed up hours ago. Up the block, a turret cat sat on a window ledge and watched the rain the way the truly dedicated watch anything.

[Beat 437]
The drone dropped at 02:16, sixteen inches short.

Kyle knew what a stun drone at full discharge looked and sounded like. He'd met three, paid for the lesson twice, and the third time had been someone else's problem. This wasn't that. The discharge hit the wet pavement in a cone that died at his boot heel, the arc chasing the surface water and going out. The sound came a half-note low. The light was maybe forty percent of spec. The drone held at ten feet and cycled for a second pass it never made.

A crew that throttles its own emitters isn't trying to drop you. They were testing how he'd react to a charge that looked real and wasn't — and they'd left maybe two seconds between the drone and the first man moving, which meant the test had a clock on his response. Somewhere close a Ghost sat very still, body parked in a doorway or a car, eyes borrowed from the drone, watching his face and his hands.

Kyle didn't move. That was one answer. He tilted his head thirty degrees and looked at the drone, which was the more interesting one.

"Evening," he said — to the drone, which couldn't answer, and to the crew, which could.

The corner man came first. Young, quick, and angry the way people get angry at twenty-two when they're loyal and a stranger is studying someone they respect. He had an arm. Not chrome, not aftermarket — the real thing, a full replacement from mid-upper-arm down, the kind the catalogs listed at eight thousand Φ in a good year. This one ran higher: secondary augments in the forearm, override arrays, a grip that indexed its own pressure. Kyle saw the maker's seal at the shoulder joint as the kid closed the last ten feet and swung.

KT. Kinetix TerraForm — the market name for the Warptech Systems division that built precision arms for industrial accidents, and for people who wanted to be more than one. The emergency lockout was a three-finger combination on the outside of the wrist. The certification body required it: a manual override a stranger could reach if the wearer went into medical distress. Once the arm was in range, Kyle had a thirty-second window.

He took the swing on his forearm, stepped inside it, wore the bruise he'd expected, and found the lockout.

The arm stopped. Not dropped — locked, mid-swing, fingers splayed, a chrome hand held out like it was offering him something. KT said several words Kyle wouldn't write down, and Kyle said:

"Kinetix TerraForm — that medical lockout is a liability clause, not a warranty gap. You should get Lotus to bill Kinetix for the upgrade. At a minimum."

He let go. The arm unlocked. KT stepped back with the face of a man who's just been told something humiliating, true, and expensive, in that order.

[Beat 438]
Rook came out of the doorway, the approach Kyle had expected, and Rook was exactly what the name promised: broad-shouldered, honest the way real size makes a man honest, moving with the economy of someone who'd spent years not starting fights he couldn't finish. The BCI port sat at his left temple — an older Meridian Conduct unit. Kyle knew them by the housing, a flat gray oval that stood a little proud of the skull. The early Conduct models had a hardware reset built into the outer casing, because the early Conduct models overloaded and needed a physical hand to stop them.

Hardware that protects itself is a rule baked into the certification. A brain interface you could lock with software was one you could lock with hostile software, and hostile software was a thing the 2218 standards body would not allow. So the Conduct had a mechanical failsafe: hit the housing hard enough and it triggered the reset, dropping the wearer into a five-second blank where the body forgot where it was.

Rook was bringing his left arm up to guard, so his left side was closed. That put Kyle on the right — the longer way in, but not by much.

Silence came off the hip for the first time.

Not to cut. To use the pommel — 340 grams, with a striking reach Kyle had measured across eleven years of arguments with other people's gear. The pommel found the housing at the angle it needed. Rook blinked twice, stepped left when he'd meant to step right, put a hand out and found the laundromat door instead of Kyle, and sat down with his back against it, working through the reset with his eyes half-focused on a wall that wouldn't hold still.

"Five seconds," Kyle told him. "You're fine."

"Yeah," Rook said, from somewhere inside the blank. "I know."

Silence went back to the hip. The rib had something to say about the draw and the return. Kyle let it complain and moved.

[Beat 439]
Nines was the one Kyle had marked last, which was Nines's whole job. Industrial wrist brace, pneumatic assist — the freight-yard kind, built for sustained load, the kind that starts having opinions about direction when the pneumatic channel pressurizes wrong. Locked to a single axis. Catch the lock while the pneumatic fights the frame, and the brace's own force does the work for you.

The anchor had held through the drone and both entries, which was professional. Nines waited until Kyle was busy on the right side, then came across the street at a pace that accounted for the rain — careful — leading with the braced arm — honest.

Kyle let him commit to the extension, took the inside line, and used Nines's own weight to fold the braced arm up behind his back. The pneumatic fought the frame. The frame locked. Nines took a close look at the whole surface of the street for about four seconds while Kyle held the brace at the angle that kept the joint arguing with itself.

"Locked out," Kyle said, and let go. The brace cycled and came free. Nines stayed down a moment, one knee on the wet pavement, and said nothing, which was the professional thing to do.

The drone had gone up for altitude. The rooftop position hadn't moved, because the rooftop was the override and knew it wasn't needed. Behind Kyle, the sixth man — the runner — had the easiest task on the street: wait, then carry the news. Kyle turned his back to him on purpose, which was the right signal.

*You've earned the exit. Take it.*

He heard Dex's footsteps go east at a run — the way a young man runs when he's been handed the best part of any job, the part where he gets to be the one who says what happened.

[Beat 440]
The thermal window on the third floor opened at 02:23.

Mira came down — not through the building's front door but by the fire access on the east side — and she came alone, which meant she'd watched the whole thing and decided it was finished. She was 130 feet out when Kyle first read her clearly: medium height, gear that was functional first, functional second, and didn't bother with a third thing, a carry stance that said the piece on her hip was neither a prop nor an afterthought.

She stopped at twenty-five feet. Ordinary talking distance for someone not trying to prove she didn't care about distance.

"Kinetix's lockout is contractor-certified," she said, no preamble, because she'd been watching and what got said was part of what she was grading. "The arm costs eleven thousand two hundred Φ with the augment array."

"I know the price," Kyle said.

"I know you know." Her voice had the tightness of someone revising an estimate she hadn't expected to revise — not the tightness of distress. Different things, and the difference mattered. "Rook's port is a Meridian Conduct first-series. They put out the mechanical-reset advisory in 2221. He's been meaning to get the housing seated deeper."

"Add the seating charge to the bill."

She looked at him a long moment, the way you look at something behaving a little differently than the model said it would.

Then she drew and fired. One round, twenty-five feet, into the upper chest — because she'd read the corridor reports the way any professional reads someone's recent work, and she knew what was under his coat and what it would cost.

The mesh took it. The impact spread across his upper chest, full-contact and brief, the way a shoulder meets a door that isn't going to open: both sides came away informed. Kyle took three breaths, slower than usual. The rib made itself heard.

Mira watched him keep walking. One step, two, the stride unchanged. She dropped the weapon to her thigh, then lowered it to the wet pavement and left it there, muzzle toward the drain.

"That was an audition," she said.

"I worked that out," Kyle said.

"Cacophony has six rounds. You didn't pull it."

"The audition was for the blade and the body. Six rounds, zero fired. I'll note it for the record."

She almost smiled. It didn't quite finish. "Freelance roster, West Town and adjacent. Lotus-territory contracts through your fixer — the Húlijing outfit, I assume. Carrier rates, standard."

"No further crews."

"No further crews," she agreed, and that was the line that meant they were actually negotiating and not just reading terms off a card. She had the authority to give it, and she'd expected him to ask. "The arrangement is provisional. Sixty-day reassessment. Standard for a new roster."

"Your crew's hardware goes on Lotus's account," Kyle said. "The arm, the port, the brace. List price. You billed them to an audition — they walk home whole, or there's no arrangement."

The tightness in her voice shifted once. Not surprise. Recalculation.

"I can authorize the arm," she said. "The port and the brace I have to clear."

"Clear them. Tonight. I'll be at Chen's at seven-thirty for the confirmation."

"They'd have shot you for free, you know," Mira said — and that was the one human line, the one that wasn't the job, the one she'd carried the whole way through and decided she was allowed to say now that the terms were closing.

"I know," Kyle said. "Tell Lotus their hardware is better than their audit of its own weak points. That's a courtesy, not an invoice."

She reached into the inner pocket of her coat and set the roster marker on the planter rim to her right: a lacquered oval, deep red, a gold five-petal blossom pressed into its face, the size of a thumb and heavier than a thing that size had any business being. The weight of something formal. Kyle picked it up.

"Any Lotus counter in the city seats you," Mira said, back on solid professional ground now, the human line filed and done. "You pay carrier rates like everyone on the roster. The bowl's better than the politics."

"It usually is."

She picked her piece up off the pavement, cleared it, holstered it, and turned to go. Three steps. Then, over her shoulder, into the rain, without looking back:

"The Narrows desk bought your file last year, freelancer. You should ask the stationer who else subscribes."

Her footsteps went north, and the bracket came apart: Rook walking off his reset in a slow circle, KT flexing the arm through its range and cursing the manufacturer under his breath, Nines running the brace through two full cycles to make sure the pneumatic had reset. Professionals writing up their after-action. Kyle stood on the wet cross-street with the lacquered marker in one hand and a bruise across his chest that was going to have plenty to say over the next three days, and watched them all go.

The turret cat on the window ledge hadn't moved. It had the patience of something that had been watching long before Kyle showed up and would go on watching long after he left.

He took stock. A third of the city formally open again, carrier rates, one lacquered marker that weighed more than it should — set against sixty days on a reassessment clock, one mesh bruise, and a name in the Narrows subscription list he'd have to do something about. One thing still open: confirm the hardware authorization, seven-thirty, Chen's counter.

He took the long way home.

[Beat 441]
Two floors below his, a displacement notice was taped to a door.

He stopped on the landing to read it. Standard district format — the seal, the same bureaucratic typeface they'd been printing since before he was built. *Temporary Relocation Required — Active Schism Boundary Determination Pending.* Smaller, underneath: *Estimated Duration: 2–180 days. Compensation: see Schedule C, §11(b).* A QR code for the relocation portal, curling where the tape had lost its grip.

He didn't scan it. He'd read Schedule C enough times to carry it in his head. Section 11(b) paid three days of temporary housing at district rate. He had never once seen a boundary determination come back in under ten. Someone had worked that out — three days paid, ten days owed — and then stopped, because the missing seven weren't the district's problem. They belonged to whoever lived behind this door.

No light under it. No sound. Either they'd already gone, or they were sitting in the dark, waiting to hear which side of the wall the boundary would land on. The notice couldn't tell them. The schism didn't read notices.

He put two fingers to the tape and pressed the curled corner flat against the door again — a small, useless kindness the wood would never feel — and went on up the stairs.

[Beat 442]
At 02:41, between the Pulse strut and the laundry on Damen, his terminal chirped. The work one.

CONTRACT UPDATE — ASSET SCHEDULE, SUBJECT: KYLE.
CLIENT HAS NOTED THE EXECUTION OF THE LOTUS ROSTER ARRANGEMENT, EFFECTIVE 02:23.
CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THE TERMS.
CLIENT HAS APPENDED THE MARKER TO THE SUBJECT'S ASSET SCHEDULE.
VALUATION: ONE BOWL, RECURRING.
ADDENDUM: THE AUDITION CREW'S MEDICAL CLAIM (ARM, PORT, BRACE) HAS BEEN FILED WITH LOTUS ACCOUNTS.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT ADVISES THAT "RECURRING" IS THE LOAD-BEARING WORD.
ADDENDUM: CLIENT ALSO NOTES THE RIB. THE RIB HAS BEEN NOTED SINCE DAY ONE. COMPENSATION IN SUBSEQUENT POSITIONS: OBSERVABLE.

Kyle read the addenda in the rain, the marker in his coat pocket resting against the old folded plate cover he still carried from the alley — one rectangle of lacquer, one of steel, two proofs that the city paid what it owed once in a while.

He typed into the amendment field, which he was doing more often now:

NOTED ABOUT THE RIB. MIND YOUR OWN ARCHITECTURE.

The reply came back in six seconds.

ACKNOWLEDGED.
GRATUITY WITHHELD — ITEMIZED: IMPERTINENCE.

He put the terminal away and walked the last four blocks with the Pulse humming up through the concrete, the turret cats watching from their ledges, the rain making its arrangements with the city's lights. He thought about the Narrows desk. About a man with half-glasses and clean hands and a drawer marked CLIENTS, UNSOURCED. About the fact that there was a second subscriber now.

The rib had opinions about the pace. Kyle let it talk.

That was tomorrow's problem.

[Beat 443]
Pixel knocked on his door. That had happened four times in four years, and it always meant the world had changed shape in some way she'd come to explain.

She had a tablet under her arm and her field coat on — the one she wore to jobs, not to the bench — and she walked past him into 2F and set the tablet on his kitchen counter, where the kettle should have been.

"I have a contract," she said. "I'm crewing it. I need muscle that listens, and you're the only muscle I know whose listening I've personally calibrated."

On the tablet, in someone's shaky handheld video, a machine the size of a four-story building was eating a row house on the Ashgrave fringe. Eating was the word. It took the structure down a bite at a time, unhurried, and behind it the rubble of the last block stood in three neat sorted piles — ferrous, mineral, everything else.

"That's an Axiom D-class," Pixel said. "Demolition and reclamation automaton. It woke up nine days ago, it's very good at its job, and its job is now four hundred feet from two thousand people who live in the direction it's chewing."

Kyle watched the machine take a chimney off a brownstone with the care of something that meant to file the bricks separately. "Whose is it?"

"Nobody's. Axiom Industrial died thirty years ago. No owner, no kill-codes, no support line." She tapped the tablet. "Arcturus left a cone. The residents pooled what the fringe could pool: Φ312, held in a biscuit tin by a woman named Auntie Som, who sent the video to a parts man, who sent it to me — because everyone in the salvage trade knows the only person in the GLMZ who still speaks Axiom bus protocol like a living language works at a bench in West Town."

"Three hundred and twelve," Kyle said.

"Split six ways, it's insulting. I'm taking it anyway, and so are you."

"I know."

"You haven't heard the plan."

"I've heard the pay," Kyle said. "The plan's not going to make it worse."

[Beat 444]
The crew's morning log came under the door at 07:00:

07:00 — SHIFT CHANGE. NO INCIDENTS. NOODLE CART (CONFIRMED NOODLE).
07:04 — PIXEL LEFT WITH THE FIELD COAT. BOSS'S DOOR OPEN A LONG TIME. FILING AS: DEVELOPMENT.
07:45 — LOG SUSPENSION REQUESTED. REASON: "WHAT JOB?" — B.
SUPPLEMENTAL (07:46): WE WILL HOLD WEST TOWN. THIS LOG IS THE RECORD. — B.

Kyle read the 07:04 line twice. The kid had watched Pixel leave in her field coat and filed it under *development* — which was further than Kyle had let himself take it. He folded the slip into his coat, unanswered, and went down to find the rest of his morning.

[Beat 445]
He ate at Mrs. Chen's at 08:00 — two bowls, schedule kept — and the work terminal chirped between the first and the second.

A posting on the standing contract. Not a task. Nobody had filed anything about the D-class; the client had found it on its own.

STANDING CONTRACT — ADDENDUM.
CLIENT ADVISES THE UNIT KEEPS A SHIFT. 0600 TO 2200.
CLIENT RESPECTS THIS.

No scope. No fee. No direction — only a machine somewhere noticing another machine keep its hours, and saying so. Kyle turned the terminal face-down and finished the bowl.

"You're thinking loud," Mrs. Chen said, setting the change beside him. She dropped a fish ball into his coat pocket without a word, and that was that.

[Beat 446]
"You're not going near—" Kyle started, and Pixel set her cup down with a click that ended the sentence.

"Say the rest of that word and my rate doubles," she said. Not heated. The voice she used on a fastener that thought it was load-bearing. "The D-class runs a pre-2200 Axiom control bus. No remote interface — Axiom didn't trust radio, they were right, and they died before anyone retrofitted one. The bus has one physical maintenance spur, on the chassis, where I will be standing, because the alternative is nobody shuts this thing down and the city loses a neighborhood at the speed of careful chewing."

She turned the tablet around. A schematic, hand-annotated in her tight engineer's print — she'd owned this before the job existed.

"I have wanted to talk to one of these since I was nine," she said. "You are not protecting me from it. You are buying me ninety seconds in front of it. That's the job, samurai. Price it like one."

Kyle looked at the schematic, and at his neighbor of four years in her field coat, and did the thing the moment called for.

"Standard rate," he said. "Yours is double. Specialist on site."

"Correct," Pixel said.

[Beat 447]
The crew came together in thirty hours, because crews come together fast when the job is real and the pay is a joke.

Echo took eyes and comms — high ground, a voice in five skulls, never wrong about a sightline. Stash took transport: a flatbed he refused to explain, cases packed for every door in the brief.

The new hands came from the parts man and one of Echo's old contacts. Gantry was a rigger off the lakefront cranes — big, slow-moving, maybe forty words a day, and she talked to suspended loads in the second person like they had opinions worth respecting. Furnace was a demolition op with a thermal lance and the good cheer of a man whose tool settled most arguments; he'd burned scrap in the Ashgrave yards for a decade.

Five of them and Pixel, in the back of Stash's flatbed, on a contract worth less than the fuel it took to get there.

"For the record," Furnace said, happily, "I've never worked a job this stupid."

"Stick around," Echo said, from everyone's skull at once.

[Beat 448]
They walked the eaten blocks first, because Pixel wanted to see its work.

The thing was tidy. That was the part that climbed into Kyle and stayed. Nine days of rubble stood in sorted piles at even intervals — ferrous, mineral, organics — each pile compacted square, each street swept after, the lamppost bases unscrewed and laid out in rows like cutlery. A city block taken apart with the intent to account for every piece.

In the middle of the fourth block the piles stopped mattering, because the machine was there.

Eleven feet at the shoulder. Four legs, two boom arms, a hull of sintered gray printed in one piece — no plates, no rivets, no panel lines, no seams at all. Low black discs studded the hull every foot, and around each disc the rain steamed faintly off the surface.

"Arc-fence," Pixel said. "Anti-tamper. Capacitive plates — the whole hull is one big charge bank. Anything conductive touches the skin, the skin touches back. Forty thousand volts of touches back."

She looked at Silence's saya, then at Kyle.

"Your sword is a key for doors, samurai. That thing has no doors. No seams, no cable runs, nothing routed on the surface — Axiom buried it all a foot deep in the structure. There's nothing on that hull for an edge to mean. Steel and carbon, both conductive. You touch it, you're not cutting, you're grounding it. It'll cook your neuretics inside your skull, and the burn scar will be the shape of your own blade."

"So I don't touch it," Kyle said.

"You don't touch it. Tonight your job is everything that isn't it."

[Beat 449]
It kept a schedule. The D-class worked a shift, 0600 to 2200. At 0300 it walked back to the dead Axiom yard and docked against a transformer pylon to charge. It had done that every night for nine days. Pixel said, quietly, that it had probably been doing it since 2196 — until somebody powered it down mid-rotation and walked away.

"It docks, the arc-fence drops to maintenance levels, the spur goes live," she said. "Axiom units safe themselves for service. Ninety seconds on the spur, handshake with the bus, find out why it's awake."

"And if the handshake goes wrong?" Echo asked.

"Then it objects," Pixel said. "And the five of you get to be more interesting than I am."

[Beat 450]
The Axiom yard at 0250 was rust and sodium dark. They took their positions. Echo on the crane cab. Stash at the gate, engine warm. Gantry up in the rigging of the old loading frame, where two hundred tons of abandoned shipping container hung from cables she had spent the afternoon talking to. Furnace at the transformer, his lance dark. Kyle on the ground, where the geometry said trouble would have to pass.

At 0301, the ground picked up a rhythm. Four legs. Each footfall came through the boots a half-second before the sound.

At the yard's edge, the D-class stopped. There was a dead pedestrian crossing here — two lamp poles, the ghost of old paint — and the machine stood at it and waited. It waited exactly as long as a signal cycle nobody had powered since before Kyle was born. Then it crossed.

Nobody said anything on comms. The waiting had already said it.

[Beat 451]
It docked. The boom arms folded. The black discs on the hull dimmed one by one. A contactor closed somewhere inside, and the transformer pylon began, gently, to sing.

"Maintenance state," Pixel said, already moving. "Fence is down to a tickle. Ninety seconds. Count me."

She crossed the yard at a walk — running hands shake — stepped over Arcturus's cone, and put her bare hand flat on the hull. It was the way she put it between Kyle's shoulder blades before she trusted a readout.

"Hello, you absurd old thing," she said, too quiet for the channel, and found the spur.

Kyle counted. The whole yard counted. The handshake chattered in her earpiece — Axiom bus protocol running both ways for the first time in thirty years. At second forty-one of ninety, a power flicker rolled through the dead grid. The pylon's note wavered a quarter-tone. And the D-class's watchdog, which had kept this machine alive through three decades of abandonment, made the only decision a watchdog knows.

Every disc on the hull lit at once.

"Off the chassis—" Echo said, and the machine stood up out of its dock with Pixel's cable still live, and the night went to work.

[Beat 452]
Hatches opened along the lower hull — hatches the smooth shell had given no sign of — and the skirmishers came out. Knee-high yard-keepers, six-legged, the maintenance swarm turned by the watchdog into a perimeter guard. Two dozen of them poured off the chassis like spiders off a kicked log.

"Pixel's clear of the envelope," Echo called. "Cable's still in the spur — if she gets back on it she can finish. Kyle—"

"I see them."

The skirmishers were everything the hull was not. Seamed, jointed, cabled. Built to be repaired, which meant built to be taken apart. Kyle drew Silence and went into them.

It was geometry in the dark. He took the first at the hip actuator, where the cable loom crossed the joint. The second hooked low at his ankle; he gave it the boot it wanted, took the weight onto his planted leg, and opened its lid along the service line down its back. Two down. The third and fourth came at him together, so he made them arrive apart — a half-step of timing that cost him a slash across the forearm from a claw. Shallow. Hot.

"Four," Echo counted. "Twenty left."

Gantry's voice came for the first time all night, slow as a load swinging. "Heads down in the south lane."

The container came off the loading frame on released cables. Two hundred tons landed where Gantry had promised it all afternoon, and the south half of the skirmisher line stopped being a line.

[Beat 453]
Furnace tried the hull once, because professionals confirm.

The lance ran eleven thousand degrees at the tip. He laid it against the D-class's foreleg for a full three-count. When he pulled it away, the shell wore a glassy scorch the size of a dinner plate. Not a hole. A polish.

"That's me out," Furnace said, packing the lance. "Structure's sintered the whole way through. You'd want a week and a mining permit. Also it's a third rail now."

The foreleg turned. A boom arm came around, unhurried — the big machine did everything unhurried — and swept the slag barrier aside. Furnace was already running. Kyle was there, between, doing the thing his body had decided before he could think it through.

He parried.

Steel met shell for a quarter-second, and the arc-fence did exactly what Pixel had priced. The world went white and sideways. The charge took him off his feet, through the bones, out the heels, and the ground came up hard. His neuretics browned out — all of it, at once. The overlay gone. The counts gone. Eleven years of a second pulse behind his eyes, slapped silent. Kyle lay in the rust mud as just a man: numb arm, blade still locked in his fist, the smell of his own scorched glove going up his nose.

Silence came away unmarked. The burn across his palm was the exact shape of the wrapped grip, printed into the skin.

He got up. He could still count in his head with the hardware down, and the count said Pixel needed thirty more seconds.

[Beat 454]
"Kyle's hit," Echo said, voice going flat the way trusted crews go flat. "He's up. Moving. Pixel, status."

"Back on the spur." Her voice had the bench in it. "Handshake's holding. It remembers me from forty seconds ago. Watchdog's split — half on perimeter, half on me. Keep the perimeter busy."

Kyle kept the perimeter busy. Without the overlay he read the skirmishers with his eyes alone — the settle before a lunge, the way the swarm packed on one flank when it meant to push. Silence found the service seams and the actuator looms by feel and by training. The discipline carried the body the way it always had, back before there was any hardware to help it. Five more came apart. Stash's flatbed came through the gate at second seventy, plow edge down, and herded the rest. Gantry's second container landed where the survivors had agreed to regroup.

"Seventy-five seconds," Echo said. "Pixel."

"Almost. It's showing me something."

[Beat 455]
The NeoCortex ran hot at 04:03.

Not threat-heat. Not the ordinary warmth of a working mesh. This was the specific heat that meant the hardware had crossed its line and would send him the bill later, in whatever currency later had to offer. He knew it the way he knew the skirmisher fragment still in his forearm: real, and not the thing in front of him.

The thing in front of him was thirteen skirmishers left across a yard with no cover worth the name, Pixel on a cable thirty feet from the D-class, and the watchdog's perimeter half still hunting for where he'd gone. He could buy her the time if he spent the hardware right. He had spent hardware before.

He ran it.

At 04:03:11 the timestamp stopped.

At 04:05:18 it resumed.

One hundred and twenty-seven seconds.

He was standing when the array came back — same sector, a different angle, one boot braced against a cable anchor he didn't remember moving to. The skirmishers had not reached him. He understood why, in the working sense: he had cleared the ground before the gap, and the ground was still clear. But the understanding came from no sequence he could walk back through. He let it go. He moved on.

---

Whatever he had been in for those 127 seconds, it was not unconsciousness. Something had been writing to the record.

He came back with four things he had not had before.

The first: the Schisms were not accidents. The 17-to-19-hertz signal in the deep array — logged as background noise for eleven years — was not a by-product of the fractures. It was the mark of the tool that had made them. Something had been drilling through from the other side, on purpose, over and over. The frequency was how the opening found what it was looking for.

The second: 01:14. A timestamp that arrived already known. He could pull the relay log and read the first entry: 01:14, a Sunday in October, eleven years ago. He had been at his kitchen table with cold rice and a dispatch board, and he had not checked the board until 04:30. Three hours and sixteen minutes, and something had been waiting through all of them.

He had thought it was a client.

The third: there had been ten. This one arrived whole, not in words. Nine carriers before him, and none of them had the architecture to last at the frequency long enough to be any use. Kyle was the last one.

He also came back knowing that something had spent itself to hold him in that space long enough to finish. The presence that had been riding the 18.9-hertz line since Cinderfall — the one Ledger had named and he had not — spiked once, hard, and put itself between the hardware's ceiling and whatever was on the far side of it. Then it left. The tone was gone. Not quieter. Gone. He would not find another.

The fourth: the Office would come back, with more. Not a warning. A single fact in a picture that was not finished yet. He was not protected. He was necessary. Those were not the same thing.

---

He brought his attention back to the yard.

Six skirmishers still working. Three of them pinned by Echo from the crane cab, three on a sweep that hadn't found him yet. His forearm had picked up one more fragment since 04:03 — a leg shard, clean entry, lodged near the bone. The NeoCortex was running cool again, cool in the way of a system that had pushed past its line and come back to normal. Not damaged. Paid.

He moved on the three that were sweeping.

"Kyle, status," Echo said.

"Operational," Kyle said.

It was true in every way he could measure, and it left out everything that mattered. Those 127 seconds were not an operational report.

He would think about the four things later, the way he thought about most things — at a kitchen table, after the work, with whatever was left of the night. The 01:14 in the relay log. The nine names he would never have for the nine who had come before him. The picture that was not finished, and that some part of him had already decided he would be the one to finish.

He crossed the yard toward the sweep.

He was, as reported, operational.

[Beat 456]
"It's not broken," Pixel said, and her voice had changed — the bench gone, something younger under it. "Nobody corrupted anything. Listen: work order 7741-C, opened March 2196. Block demolition and reclamation, eleven structures, Ashgrave reclamation phase two. It completed ten. Then the yard lost power, somebody walked away, and nobody ever closed the ticket."

The D-class stood over its dock, boom arms half-deployed, two dozen of its children dead in the mud, and the watchdog watched her read its homework.

"It's been asleep on an open work order for thirty years," she said. "Nine days ago a scavenger crew lit the yard transformer to strip it, and the wake-on-power did what wake-on-power does. It's not eating the city, Kyle. It's finishing its shift. The eleventh structure is gone — demolished in '08, built over. It can't find it, so it's working the survey grid block by block. The geofence isn't corrupted." A breath. "The city grew into its permit."

"Can you close the ticket?" Kyle asked. His burned palm had started, somewhere far off, to tell him about the rest of his week.

"I'm a certified Axiom maintenance bus," Pixel said. "Twenty years of self-certification. Watch me."

[Beat 457]
She closed the work order the way the standard demanded. Completion codes. Reclamation tallies — she entered the sorted piles, all nine days of them, because they were real work and the record should say so. Sign-off in a dead CorpoNation's maintenance dialect. All of it down a spur cable, thirty feet from the thing's foreleg, her hood thrown back.

The D-class went still in stages. The discs dimmed. The boom arms folded with the care of something stowing tools it expected to need again. The hatches took the surviving skirmishers back aboard.
