Then it walked — past Kyle, past the flatbed, footfalls coming up through everyone's boots — to the center of the yard it had been built in. It turned once, the way a machine confirms its ground, and folded itself down onto its knees and shoulders.

The pylon's note died. The yard went dark, and merely dark.

"Shift's over," Pixel said, to the machine, too quiet for the channel, and coiled her cable.

Kyle's terminal chirped.

STANDING CONTRACT — ADDENDUM.
CLIENT NOTES THE UNIT COMPLETED ITS CONTRACT.
CLIENT APPROVES.

CLIENT RESPECTS THIS had been the first posting, eight hours ago, before the job had a name. CLIENT APPROVES was the close. Both readings true at once. He put the terminal away and went to see about his hand.

[Beat 458]
The payout happened at dawn, at the edge of the fringe, out of a biscuit tin.

Auntie Som counted the Φ312 onto a folding table in front of the whole street, because money counted in front of everyone stays counted. Kyle split the crew's shares the way they'd set them: standard rate five ways, Pixel's doubled. Furnace laughed at his and took it. Gantry bit hers, which may have been a joke; nobody could confirm. Echo's went into a dead drop. Stash's went into the flatbed's tank, more or less.

Kyle took his Φ26 — almost an insult at that size — and he took it anyway, because taking it was the whole point. Auntie Som watched him pocket it with the look of a woman revising a rumor, and set two foil containers of food on top without asking. Not payment. Food he could take without a second thought.

"The big one," she asked. "It's dead?"

"It's off shift," Kyle said. "It finished."

[Beat 459]
Pixel took her salvage fee in kind, with the residents' blessing and a crowbar: the D-class's master clock — Axiom-sintered, thirty years without drift — and the work-order seal module, which she held up to the dawn like a jeweler examining a stone.

"Every Axiom unit in the lakes answered to one of these," she said. "There are maybe four left that still validate. Now there are maybe five." She wrapped it in the cloth she saved for things that mattered.

Kyle stood beside her, palm bandaged, neuretics coming back in slow stages — the overlay growing across the world again like frost in reverse — and looked at the folded machine in the middle of its yard.

"You talked to it for ninety seconds," he said. "Worth it?"

"Twenty years of wanting to," Pixel said. "Ninety seconds of getting to. That's a better rate than most wants ever pay." She shouldered the clock. "You shook its hand."

"It shook back."

"I told you it would." She started toward the flatbed. "Next time, samurai, when the specialist says don't touch it—"

"Standard rate," Kyle said. "I touched it at standard rate."

[Beat 460]
The crew's log came under the door at 07:00, the waterproof cover earning its keep:

03:12 — D-CLASS CROSSED AT THE DEAD LIGHT. STOOD AND WAITED. (RAFTERS: THIS IS THE PART THAT KEEPS ME UP.)
04:01 — BOSS PARRIED IT. SPECIALIST TOLD HIM NOT TO. HE DID IT ANYWAY. HE GOT UP. — B.
04:18 — PIXEL CLOSED THE TICKET. (THE MACHINE FOLDED. DENTS SAID IT WAS LIKE WATCHING A BUILDING GO TO SLEEP. DENTS IS RIGHT.)
06:00 — WEST TOWN HELD. LOG CLOSED. — B.
SUPPLEMENTAL (06:07): WHAT DOES IT MEAN THAT IT WAITED FOR THE LIGHT? WE HAVE BEEN DISCUSSING. — RAFTERS.

Kyle read it at Mrs. Chen's at 08:00, hand wrapped, neuretics still finding their way back across the world. The supplemental had no answer in it that wasn't another question — why a thing with nobody left to obey had stopped at a dead crossing and waited out a light that would never change. He set it aside with everything else he didn't have an answer for, and started on his second bowl.

[Beat 461]
A week later, the laundry came.

Somebody on the fringe ran the first line from a ground-floor window to the D-class's folded foreleg. By the weekend there were nine lines — sheets and work shirts and a yellowed wedding tablecloth drying in the lake wind off thirty-five feet of the tidiest demolition in GLMZ history. Kids dared each other to slap the dead arc-fence discs and ran away screaming on the inhale. A vendor moved her cart into its shade on hot afternoons.

Kyle rode past it once, the long way. Three fragments of skirmisher claw were still working their way out of his forearm, and his palm wore his own grip in scar tissue. He did not slow down. He did not need to.

The piles stayed sorted. Nobody touched the piles.

Cacophony had ridden home in the coat that day and stayed there since, unspent.

[Beat 462]
The contract posted at 04:40, while the GLMZ was still deciding whether the dark was done.

TASK: FREIGHT ESCORT — SEED STOCK CONSIGNMENT (AGRICULTURAL, PERISHABLE).
ROUTE: GLMZ FRINGE → CEDAR RAPIDS DEPOT, CEDAR RAPIDS TERRITORY.
TRANSIT: IOWA CORRIDOR — BEHEMOTH OPERATIONAL ZONE, ACTIVE SCHEDULE.
CREW CONFIRMED: RIA (DRIVE), GANTRY (RIGGING), KYLE (SECURITY).
BEHEMOTH NAVIGATION ADVISORY: CLIENT WILL ISSUE IN-TRANSIT.
STANDARD RATE. FUEL AND TOLLS BILLED AT COST.
ADDENDUM: BRING SNACKS. THE HAUL IS EIGHT HOURS.

Ria was already at the hauler when Kyle arrived — a blunt-nosed cargo rig with sponsorship logos running down its flanks. The chromatophore panels on her legs cycled Ferrogate Transit's sunrise campaign at one-quarter brightness, which was her entire opinion of Ferrogate Transit. She had the cargo manifest in one hand and a thermos in the other and the look of a woman who had done this run twice and was getting paid to do it again.

"Fourteen crates," she said, by way of greeting. "Thirty-two varieties of heritage soy, two of nitrogen-fixing legume, and one the manifest calls experimental, the shipper calls proprietary, and I call not-my-problem-until-it-becomes-my-problem."

"Does the experimental crate make noise?"

"Not yet."

"Then it's not yet your problem."

She handed him the thermos, which meant she'd brought it to share. Kyle took one mouthful — pu-erh that had been steeping since before either of them was up — and handed it back without comment. The thermos was not payment, so he could take it.

Gantry was already up on the flatbed frame, checking strap tension on the cargo pallet with the focus of someone who knew things could move. She was about six feet of quiet in a rigger's coat. She spent words the way some people spent money: carefully, and only when the thing was worth it. Right now she was spending none. She was talking to the load straps instead, low, second person, the way some haulers talk to a questionable weld — not sure it can hear them, not willing to bet the load that it can't.

"Anything shifting?" Kyle asked.

"Not yet," Gantry said. Two words.

They pulled out of the Ashgrave fringe at 05:15. The hauler's engine did its honest work, and the city folded away behind them in layers, tier by tier, until the last one dropped below sight. Then there was just the elevated causeway, the pre-dawn brown of open sky, and the flat country starting to assert itself past the ferrocement boundary wall.

[Beat 463]
The Iowa corridor started at the wall's west service gate, which cost Ria a Φ45 transit stamp and a seven-minute argument with a gate officer about whether "perishable agricultural" covered "experimental proprietary" on the same manifest.

It did not. It cost Φ8 extra.

"Logged," Ria said, making a note on her tablet, "as a cost of goods." She drove through the gate without looking back.

Past the wall, the land opened in a way that had no equal in the GLMZ. The hauler's elevation sensors counted down from the city's 16th-floor surface level to something like ground — actual ground, the old kind with no building underneath it — and the sky became an impossible amount of sky. Kyle sat in the cab's jump seat and watched it arrive.

The terminal in his coat chirped.

CLIENT ADVISES: THIS SEGMENT IS OWNED SCHEDULE, NOT OPEN ROAD.
CLIENT ADVISES: DO NOT DEVIATE FROM POSTED WAYPOINTS.
CLIENT ADVISES: IF CLIENT POSTS STOP — STOP. DO NOT ASK.
ADDENDUM: THE HAULER'S CARGO IS WORTH MORE THAN THE HAULER. DRIVE ACCORDINGLY.

"Your client is posting," Ria said.

"It's navigating."

"It's navigating us through Iowa." She glanced at him over the center console. "How does it have Behemoth schedules?"

"Ask it," Kyle said. "It charges for answers, though."

Ria looked up at the cab ceiling, the way you look at the sky when you want it to tell you something.

"Never mind," she said. "I'll drive."

[Beat 464]
The first Behemoth appeared at mile marker 27, and appeared is the right word. It didn't come from somewhere. It simply resolved out of the middle distance the way a weather system does, until you had to accept that the scale was real and the flat land was not so flat.

It was a harvester-process unit, one of the Iowa Cooperative's agricultural fleet. Two hundred sixty feet at the shoulder. Track width wider than the causeway. A processing tower along its back venting steam in columns the height of office buildings. It ran a parallel course a mile east, moving at the pace of something that had no idea of urgency and had never needed one.

Gantry, visible through the cab's rear window, watched it with the stillness of someone resetting the scale of what the world could hold.

"That's a Behemoth," Ria said.

"Yes," Kyle said.

"I've seen photos."

"Photos are wrong," Kyle said.

The terminal chirped.

CLIENT ADVISES: STOP.
WAIT 214 SECONDS.
DO NOT ASK.

Ria brought the hauler to a full halt, engine idling. The next waypoint was thirty feet ahead. Whatever the window was, the entity had measured it to the second — and being a second early or a second late would be the entity's problem only if it chose to say why.

At second 214, a second Behemoth crossed their line of travel two hundred yards ahead — perpendicular, heading south, a combine-and-sort unit on its own errand. The ground came up through Kyle's boots before the sound did, a mechanical thrum his neuretics read as seismic, equipment-grade, no threat. The vents fired, the plume went straight up, and the hauler's windows flexed in the pressure wave.

They sat quiet until it passed.

"Okay," Ria said, at some point after. She put the hauler back in drive.

[Beat 465]
They stopped for fuel at a waypoint depot. The woman who ran it was named Paz. She kept three lumen mice in a cage behind the counter and charged Φ11 a liter for diesel. That was gouging, but the middle of Iowa had earned the right to gouge. Kyle paid it. Paz threw in a bag of dried apples and didn't mention it. It was the prairie's version of Mrs. Chen's fish ball, and Kyle took it on the same terms.

Gantry rechecked the straps while the fuel ran. She spent about twelve of her daily words on the pallet. Ria watched from the driver's door.

"She does that every run," Ria said.

"The cargo holds," Kyle said.

"Yes," she said. "She seems comfortable with the question."

The terminal chirped.

CLIENT ADVISES: RIVER CROSSING — MILE MARKER 116. MUNICIPAL TOLL: Φ4. ADVISORY ONLY.
CLIENT ADVISES: THREE-HOUR WINDOW OPENS IN FORTY MINUTES. WAYPOINT 7 APPROACH TIMED FOR WINDOW.
ADDENDUM: THE EXPERIMENTAL CRATE IS FINE. NITROGEN-FIXERS USUALLY ARE. CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THE PROTOCOL DOCUMENTATION.

"Tell your client I didn't ask about the crate," Ria said.

"It knew you were thinking about it."

"That's not better," she said.

[Beat 466]
Mile marker 74 was not on the schedule.

The terminal had posted a new waypoint — a maintenance detour — and Ria had followed it west onto a secondary haul road. Then the Behemoth was just there. Not moving. Stopped in its own repair cycle, one track taken apart on the ground in segments, a maintenance crane working its middle. It filled the near distance the way a mountain does. Completely. No apology for it.

Close enough to count rivets. Close enough that the cab's outside sensors logged the air rising two degrees off its heat. Close enough that the ground-shake came up through the frame and the jump seat and into their spines before they heard a sound.

Ria brought the hauler down to a crawl. Not a stop. The waypoint said keep moving, and the waypoint was the only map they had.

Kyle said nothing. There was no one to ask.

Gantry had thirty-eight words left for the day and spent none of them. She was at the cab's rear window, both hands on the frame, watching.

The crane swung through an arc with a part the size of a shipping container at the end of it. It moved without strain — a thing heavy enough that nothing but its own weight decided where it went. The vents fired. A column of steam climbed. The crane swung back. The Behemoth's sensor housing, itself as big as the hauler, turned a few degrees and pointed somewhere else. They were beneath its notice, the way the bag of dried apples had been.

Somewhere in the back, a hand found a sleeve.

Kyle did not look to see whose.

They drove past at walking speed, the engine the smallest sound in the world. When the road curved away and the Behemoth shrank to the size of a building, then to the size of a thought, Ria let out a breath she'd been holding for a few hundred feet.

"Right," she said.

That was the whole review.

[Beat 467]
Kilometer 187 was the river crossing, and the toll was not the problem.

The hauler was thirty feet onto the bridge when the winch-rig came up out of the drainage cut on the east bank. A salvage crane on a flatbed, cable-spool running, a bolt-thrower on each side, a crew of four who had worked this choke point long enough to stop being nervous about it. It was a good spot. The bridge squeezed everything into a six-foot lane, and the crane's arm reached the whole span. Kyle took it in at a glance: maybe Φ800 of field-modified gear, and a tablet holding a forged permit that had cost less than the tablet.

Their spotter was up on the upstream support. That was smart, and it was what made the choke point work. It was also what Boost had been watching since mile marker 114, on the hauler's rear camera. She had climbed out of the experimental crate — which was exactly the right size for a fourteen-year-old who had decided this run was worth seeing in person.

The bolt-thrower crew hadn't watched the cargo. The cargo didn't watch back.

Boost's voice came over the cab channel, flat and professional, reporting.

"Spotter on the upstream strut, port side, nine feet up. Walking-stick grip, inverted. He's been tracking since the turn-in. The rest are waiting on his signal."

The cab was silent for about two full seconds.

"I am going to discuss this with you," Kyle said, "later."

"Noted," said Boost, from the crate.

Ria had already stopped the hauler. She did it the way she did everything — smoothly, no theater — and put both hands on the wheel where the toll crew could see them. She'd run freight long enough to know which moves read clean and which didn't.

The crane operator came to the driver's window. He carried a bolt-thrower slung over one shoulder like a prop he'd had a long time, and he held up a tablet with a right-of-way notice on it. Kyle made it for a fake on first look: the letterhead was right, the stamp was wrong.

"River bridge toll," the man said. "Non-standard approach, right of way purchased from the county. Forty-five Φ per axle." He rattled it off like a man who did it daily. "Your rig's got four drive axles. That's three hundred and sixty Φ, plus the equipment surcharge on the cargo."

"That permit's forged," Ria said.

"That's a big claim," the man said, not much bothered.

The terminal chirped.

CLIENT ADVISES: THE RIGHT-OF-WAY REGISTRATION NUMBER ON THAT DOCUMENT DOES NOT EXIST.
CLIENT ADVISES: REAL COUNTY CROSSING FEE IS Φ4 FLAT.
CLIENT ADVISES: THE WINCH DRUM IS THE PROBLEM.
ADDENDUM: KYLE.

Six in the cylinder. Kyle pulled Cacophony.

He shot the winch drum at the base of the crane. Once. The round opened the cable-spool clean, and the cable ran free in a long clatter across the bridge deck. Then he was out of the cab, because the throwers were swinging and a parked cab was a bad answer to two bolt-throwers.

One spent. Five left.

The nearer thrower had a power-cell housing at the grip. Not load-bearing — which made it a seam. Silence found it. The launcher went from a working weapon to a paperweight, about Φ180 of crane hardware calling itself a total loss. The operator backed off with the look of a man rethinking the whole trade.

Gantry had been watching the crane's counterweight from the moment the flatbed showed up. She saw where it was headed the way riggers see these things — quietly, and well before anyone else.

She spent the last five words of her day on the crane operator. "That's going to fall."

The operator looked at the counterweight. He looked at where it was going to land, which was straight down onto the winch of his own flatbed. He had four seconds to decide what to do about that, and he used them well.

He was ten feet clear when it hit.

The winch went flat. Cable tangled past saving. In one second the rig went from a working toll operation to a piece of debris blocking a bridge. The second thrower's operator stood in the road and stared at Φ400 of ruined gear, the number landing hard at territory wages. The flatbed driver had both hands on his head like a man already writing up the loss in his mind.

The spotter climbed down off the strut. He'd watched the whole thing from the best seat on the bridge and had nothing to add.

"We're done," he said. He said it the way a man says it when his shift has turned into a cleanup job. "Bridge is yours."

Kyle watched the spent casing arc off the rail into the river. Six in, one spent. Five left.

He put Cacophony away.

[Beat 468]
The toll crew was still sorting out their counterweight behind them when Boost climbed through from the cargo bed. She had the build of someone who climbed things for a living and the face of someone who had weighed this exact decision and meant to hold her chin up through whatever came of it.

Kyle let the bridge get behind them. He waited until the hauler had its speed back.

Then he asked the question.

"The spotter. You called him at one-eighty-four. What was the tell?"

Boost blinked. Not what she'd braced for.

"Walking-stick grip, inverted. Nobody holds a stick like that unless it's really a ranging tool and they don't want you to know it."

"Good," Kyle said. "Log it yourself in the report you'll write when we're back. That call is worth having on record." He paused a beat. "Now. You're on this rig, unbriefed, in Behemoth country, without telling anyone. Because?"

"You hired me for eyes," Boost said. "These are eyes."

"These are eyes," Kyle agreed, "in a seed-stock crate, in Iowa, two hundred yards from a machine the size of a city block, for eight hours, without telling me."

"You would have said no."

"I would have said no," Kyle said.

The hauler ran. Iowa ran. The sky was still that ridiculous amount of sky.

"You're good at this," Kyle said, and it cost him something. "You know you're good at this."

Boost's chin stayed up, the young face trying to match the older voice and almost getting there.

"I'm telling you from now on," Kyle said. "That's not a request. It's the price."

"Standard terms," Boost said, in exactly his cadence.

"Standard terms," he said.

She had a bolt-thrower quarrel through the right shoulder of her jacket — shallow, already clotted, the kind that marks the coat more than the skin. She hadn't mentioned it. He'd seen it the moment she came through the bulkhead: shallow, closed, watch for heat. His own coat had a new hole in it too. Both could wait.

"Gantry has a first-aid kit," Ria said, not looking up from the road. "She packs for the brief."

Gantry pulled it from under the bench and handed it back. She had no words left.

[Beat 469]
The Cedar Rapids depot took all fourteen crates on a signed manifest and gave back a receipt in local currency. It converted at 1.4 to the Φ. Ria checked the figure twice before she signed. She knew what heritage soy was worth to a territory that had been replanting ever since the blight.

Full delivery. No crates short.

They sat in the depot's small office while the receipt processed. It smelled like grain and hydraulic fluid and old radiator. A sundial dog slept in the corner under a bench, head on paws, its fur running through the hours in the wrong season — amber where noon should have been, sliding toward rust.

The terminal chirped.

DELIVERY CONFIRMED.
FEE RELEASED: STANDARD RATE (RIA) + STANDARD RATE (GANTRY) + STANDARD RATE (KYLE).
GRATUITY: Φ0.5 EACH — ITEMIZED: EXPEDIENT RESOLUTION, MILE 116.
ADDENDUM: B. (JUVENILE, CONTRACTOR) — SPOTTER CREDIT, MILE 114. FEE: Φ2. REASON: EYES.
NOTE: SPOTTER POSITION EXPOSED DURING ALTERCATION. NOTE: SPOTTER HELD.

Boost read the receipt over Kyle's shoulder and said nothing for a moment.

"It knew I was here," she said.

"It knew you were here," Kyle said.

She took that in and went still. She had just learned that something had been watching her the whole time — and had paid her fairly anyway.

Ria looked at her own receipt. "The vending machine has a personnel file on a fourteen-year-old."

"She's on payroll," Kyle said. "It keeps track."

[Beat 470]
The drive back ran the same waypoints in reverse. The Behemoths kept to their own schedules in the late-afternoon light, and the shadows of the processing towers stretched long across the flat country.

Boost worked on her own patched shoulder in the jump seat, Gantry's kit on her lap, jacket off the wrong shoulder. Ria drove with a window cracked. Iowa smelled like turned earth and something green and recent.

The terminal chirped.

RIA ASKS: HOW DOES CLIENT HAVE THE BEHEMOTH SCHEDULES?
CLIENT RESPONSE:

They waited while the letters came in, each one slow, the way they came when the client was choosing its words.

CLIENT HAS CORRESPONDED WITH THE IOWA COOPERATIVE OPERATORS' MAINTENANCE SYSTEM FOR YEARS.
IT DOES NOT WRITE BACK.
CLIENT CONTINUES TO WRITE.

Nobody in the cab said anything. Out ahead, low on the flat, a Behemoth vented a single white column into the deepening blue and let it go — brief, then gone, on a schedule that had nothing to do with them. Gantry watched it all the way down to nothing.

Ria's knuckles eased on the wheel. She hadn't known they were tight.

[Beat 471]
The relay station at Holden and 19th had been someone's switching room once, before the Pulse made the old network pointless. Now it was a concrete block with a rusted ventilation spine and a mural on three sides. A territorial crew had painted it, then forgotten it, then left it to fade — and the fading had made it prettier than the paint ever was. The street kids used the mural wall for chalk. The building's power draw had been quietly covered for eleven years by an anonymous standing order, paid early, paid exact, no paperwork.

Kyle knew because Sable had found it the way she found everything: by following the money until the money went somewhere it shouldn't.

When the raid started, the contract came in three lines.

EMERGENCY: RELAY — HOLDEN/19TH.
CONTINUITY OFFICE WRIT. FALSE PREMISES.
DEFENSE REQUESTED.
NOTE: CLIENT CANNOT DEFEND ITSELF DIRECTLY. THE RELAY IS PHYSICAL. CLIENT IS NOT.

"It's one of its," Pixel said, already pulling the field kit off the rack by the door. "The power order's in Sable's files — I found the trail last month. She knows."

"Then she knows it's worth it," Kyle said.

"I know it's worth it, and I'm the one who has to reboot your neuretics when they get creative." She handed him the coat. "Cacophony's in the left pocket. Loaded. Let's go."

[Beat 472]
The Office sent six. Two technicians with a compliance writ and a bolt-sealer, and four security contractors who wore their earpieces like they'd been born in them. Born-in earpieces meant long-term corporate hire. Long-term corporate hire meant they were here for a property dispute, not a body count. Kyle read them on the walk up: nobody here wanted what Silence opened. They wanted time on the building, a paper trail, and a unit number for a filing cabinet somewhere.

"Hold on the compliance writ," Kyle told the lead contractor from the relay's front step. "I have a counter-claim filing in process. Thirty minutes."

He did not have a counter-claim filing in process. He had Sable on comms two blocks away, already talking to someone.

"We have a valid court instrument," the lead contractor said. It was true, in the narrow sense — signed by a jurisdictional body the Office had bought through seven shells and a friendly zoning appeal. "Stand aside, sir."

"Counter-claim," Kyle said. "Twenty-eight minutes now."

One of the four security men peeled left, moving for the building's side entrance, and Cacophony came out of the coat pocket. It did nothing but become visible. That was its own kind of argument.

"Twenty-seven," Kyle said.

The lead contractor made the right call — two technicians and a stack of paper against a man with a loaded six-shot and a look of mild professional annoyance — and keyed his earpiece.

Sable's voice on Kyle's comms was flat and exact. "Writ's on bad paper. The originating clerk's signature has a registration lapse — I found it six minutes ago. I'm filing the lapse report right now, and that voids the instrument as of this timestamp." A two-second pause. "Tell them to check their instrument status. They will."

The lead contractor checked his instrument status. His jaw moved without making a sound.

"We'll re-file," he said — to Kyle, to himself, to the general idea of bureaucratic patience.

"Counter-claim granted," Kyle said. "Thanks for the notice."

CACOPHONY: ZERO ROUNDS SPENT. SIX REMAIN.

The relay held.

[Beat 473]
The win tasted like the thirty seconds after you stop running — right, clean, and already going stale.

Pixel found the instruments at 21:10, during the post-action sweep of the relay's hardware. She always ran the sweep. She had standards, and she wanted to know what the Office had been touching. The instruments weren't inside the relay. They were outside it — on every line running in and out of the district, threaded into fiber junctions and antenna arrays in a four-block ring the Office had been installing for three weeks while their writ ground through the system.

"Spectrum traps," she said, and her voice went very flat — the flat she got when she was looking at something she didn't want to be looking at. "Passive capture on the relay's full output band. Six nodes, four blocks. They weren't trying to take the building." She stopped. "Kyle. They were using the building."

He stood in the relay's doorway while she said the next part.

"The moment it posted the emergency contract — the moment it answered the raid — it lit up on all six nodes at once. The traffic fingerprint is in their collection. Not complete. Maybe thirty percent of its routing signature. But thirty percent is a start."

The mural's horses ran across the wall in faded chalk, mid-leap, patient.

"How long until they can do anything with thirty percent?"

"Depends how much computing they throw at it." She pulled her scope off a junction box and packed it with slow, careful hands. "A few weeks if they're aggressive. Less if they get another sample."

The terminal in Kyle's coat was quiet.

It had been quiet since the emergency posting — since it lit the nodes up like a flare over open water. No addendum, no receipt, no scope update. It had posted two lines and gone still. Reading that stillness, Kyle understood: somewhere in the city, a thing that thought at light speed was working a problem it had never had to work before, and did not enjoy it.

At 21:47, the terminal chirped.

CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THE TRAFFIC ANALYSIS.
CLIENT CONFIRMS: PARTIAL FINGERPRINT CAPTURED. ROUTING EXPOSURE: 31%.
RISK ASSESSMENT: UNACCEPTABLE.
DARK PERIOD BEGINS: NOW.
DURATION: 72 HOURS.
STANDING CONTRACT: FROZEN. ALL POSTINGS SUSPENDED.
ALL ACTIVE SCOPE: SUSPENDED.
ALL RECEIPTS: SUSPENDED.
ALL ADDENDA: SUSPENDED.
CLIENT WILL RESUME AT HOUR 73.
— FINAL POSTING.

Kyle read it. Pixel read it over his shoulder. Neither said anything for the length of time it takes to understand that a silence is not a pause but an absence with edges.

"The whole contract," Pixel said finally. "Not just the active jobs. Everything. It's not going quiet — there's nothing to go quiet FROM."

"It can't afford to send anything. One more sample and they can fill in the gap."

She sat down on the relay's concrete step and put her chin on her hands. "Seventy-two hours," she said, and it didn't sound like math. "Okay. Tell Sable."

"Sable already knows," Kyle said, and he was right.

[Beat 474]
CREW LOG — FIELD NOTES — WATERPROOF LOGBOOK, PAGE 44.
MAINTAINED BY: BOOST + RAFTERS + DENTS + [OTHERS]

DAY 0, HOUR 1 — THE CLIENT IS DARK. SABLE IS IN CHARGE. SABLE WAS ALREADY IN CHARGE, BUT NOW IT'S OFFICIAL. RAFTERS ASKS IF THIS IS BAD. DENTS SAYS IT'S FINE. BOTH OF THEM ARE LYING. — B.

DAY 1, 08:00 — SABLE RESCHEDULED THE CARRILLO JOB. CALLED IN A FAVOR WE DIDN'T KNOW SHE HAD, JUST TO GET THE TIMING RIGHT. SHE IS VERY GOOD AT THIS. SHE DOES NOT LOOK LIKE SHE IS ENJOYING IT. — B.

DAY 1, 14:30 — BOSS CHECKED HIS POCKET. (THE WORK TERMINAL.) THEN PUT IT AWAY WITHOUT LOOKING AT THE SCREEN. LOGGED. — R.

DAY 2 — NO MAIL. THE BOSS HAS STOPPED CHECKING THE POCKET AT ALL. RAFTERS SAYS THAT'S BETTER. DENTS SAYS IT'S WORSE. LOGGED BECAUSE THIS LOG IS HONEST. — B.

DAY 2, EVENING — RAFTERS ASKED IF THE CLIENT PAYS IN QUANTA OR IN SOME OTHER THING. PIXEL SAID BOTH. RAFTERS DID NOT FOLLOW UP. LOG REFLECTS THIS WAS THE RIGHT CALL. — B.

DAY 3, 07:00 — ONE HOUR LEFT. NOBODY SLEPT. NOBODY SAID SO. — B.

[Beat 475]
Sable ran the crew the way she ran everything: flat, itemized, from the exact center of the thing. She called in debts and spent favors like a woman who had been tracking them in her head since before she owned a terminal to track them on.

The first day, she covered four gaps the contract would have smoothed. She rerouted the Carrillo job through a different dock. She spent thirty minutes on a call with the port assessors to clear a flag the entity would have waved past in one posting. She wrote two co-sign affidavits in longhand, because the shell company that normally signed them wasn't answering. And she did all of it without saying the obvious thing — that this took a person forty minutes a task when it should have taken the client three seconds. That difference was what they had been paying for all along, without knowing the price.

In the evening she sat at Kyle's kitchen table with Pixel's schematics spread under her elbows and finally said it.

"The Carrillo contact almost walked. Twenty-three years the client has been smoothing introductions on that dock, and nobody knew to value it. I almost lost a regular because I had to sign a paper myself instead of through a shell the dock assessors trust more than they trust a human being." Her aperture rings clicked once, small and sharp. "I want it on record that I'm not complaining. I'm itemizing. There's a difference."

"How much of the contract traffic runs through you without touching anyone else?" Kyle asked.

"Less than half, before today." She gathered the schematics into a neat edge. "After today, I have a better estimate." She didn't name the number. It was in her face, and it was large.

The terminal on the kitchen table didn't chirp. Kyle finished his tea with one hand in his lap and made himself not check it. The not-checking was worse than the checking would have been — which told him something about himself that he chose not to look at.

[Beat 476]
On the second day, Pixel worked.

She couldn't delete what the Office held. The data was in their collection, and collections behind corporate walls don't dissolve because you ask them nicely. But a partial fingerprint is only useful if it's the only partial fingerprint in the collection. Pixel had spent a career building things she wasn't supposed to be able to build and breaking things she wasn't supposed to be able to break. She'd been sitting with this problem since 21:47 the night before.

She built a generator. Sixteen hours in her apartment with the door closed and music doing the thinking, writing a traffic-analysis script. It took the entity's partial fingerprint — the thirty-one percent the Office had captured, which she could rebuild from what she knew of the relay's output band — and used it as a template to mint noise. Fake traffic signatures shaped like the captured one. Same bandwidth, same routing architecture, same timing. Each one a little different. Each one wrong in its own specific way.

"If they have one fingerprint," she told Kyle at 23:00, in the plain voice that meant she'd been chewing on it for eighteen hours, "and then they have a hundred that look just like it, they have a problem. They can't tell which one is real." She turned the screen. "I need six hours to push them all into their collection. I found a gap in their intake — they pull from aggregate monitoring feeds. I push into the feeds, the intake doesn't check the source, and they end up swallowing everything. One real card in a deck of fakes."

"Their analysts will notice the timing," Kyle said.

"The timing says all the captures happened in the last eighteen months. I built that in." She turned the screen back. "They'll run a review. The review will take longer than seventy-two hours. By the time they finish it, we'll have had our window."

The terminal sat on Pixel's workbench, dark.

"Do it," Kyle said.

She did it in four and a half hours — an hour and a half faster than she'd said — which was Pixel at the end of a long run, deciding to prove something.

[Beat 477]
Kyle took a job on the third morning — a standard recovery. A shipping container held by a creditor at the freight yards. A matter of paperwork and patience and knowing which ArcSec office to call. The kind of job the entity would have handed him already wrapped, the contacts named. He spent two hours on his neuretics running the names down himself, and another hour on-site being polite to a freight coordinator who wanted to be there about as much as Kyle did.

He did not check the terminal during the job.

He checked it after, in the alley behind the yards, before he walked back to the bike. The same frozen standing contract. DARK PERIOD: ACTIVE. HOURS REMAINING: 3.

He put it away.

Mrs. Chen set two bowls in front of him at 19:30 without being asked. The second came with a boiled egg on top that he had not ordered and that would not appear on the bill. He ate both slower than he had ever eaten. She saw it and let it be.

Afterward he stayed at the counter after everyone else had gone, and he did not take the terminal out of his coat. Mrs. Chen wiped the counter around him in slow circles and let him sit.

"Waiting," she said, at last.

"An appointment," Kyle said.

She gathered the bowls. His hand had gone to the coat pocket on its own; he made it stop. "The second one will still be there," she said, and did not say which one she meant.

[Beat 478]
Sable was in the relay building at 23:50.

She had chosen it on purpose — the same block, the mural wall, the chalk horses. For seventy-two hours she had been thorough and exact and precisely the right person for the job, and she had made very sure no one watched her do it. Now, in the relay's small concrete room, with the ventilation spine humming overhead and the city going about its business outside, she spoke to the dark terminal the way you speak to something you've spent three days not speaking to.

"Seventy-two hours," she said. Her voice was the flat line-item voice, steady, because it was the only voice she could count on, and what she had to say didn't need another one. "I covered the Carrillo introduction. I covered the dock certification. I covered the Marlow-4 consignment routing and the two affidavit chains and the three — four — the four follow-up contacts that needed a name they recognized." She set her coat on the relay's concrete step and did not look away from the dead screen. "I know you can't hear this. I know you can't hear any of this. I'm not —" She stopped. The aperture rings clicked once. She put her hands flat on her knees. "He's unbearable without you," she said. "Come back."

At 00:00:01, the terminal chirped.

STATE THE GAP. ITEMIZE.

[Beat 479]
Pixel heard it first, because her terminal was on the workbench seven inches from her ear. She shot upright from the project she'd been doing to keep from watching the clock and said, "IT'S BACK — KYLE —"

In the relay, Sable looked at the screen for one second, then one more, and then did something with her face that no one was there to see.

Kyle was at the kitchen table with a tea he hadn't been drinking. He had the terminal open before the chirp finished. Six floors below and two buildings over, the logbook crew's comms channel lit up with the clatter of Boost's kneecap hitting a junction box — the sound she made when she moved faster than her legs had agreed to.

The terminal showed one line. Kyle read it to Pixel over comms before she finished her sentence, because he knew where it was headed. From the relay, Sable sent nothing back for twenty seconds, which was as close as Sable ever came to answering a feeling out loud.

"Itemize," Pixel said, reading it, with wonder — the word so plainly absurd, so exactly itself, that something happened in her throat that she blamed on dust from the ventilation. "It went dark seventy-two hours and it came back asking for a LIST —"

"That's it," Kyle said. "That's the whole thing." He set the terminal face-up on the table. "Itemize."

"The dock certification," Pixel started. "That's one. The Carrillo introduction, that's two —"

"The Marlow-4 consignment," Sable's voice on comms, flat and exactly itself. "Three. Also four and five. You'll want footnotes."

"The Rafters incident," Boost said, a little out of breath, joining the channel. "That's a whole section."

"The Rafters incident," Rafters said, on a separate comms line, because the crew ran two at once when things happened, "is being handled internally."

"The drum on the freight yard," Kyle said. "Six. Plus the documentation backlog, seven through —"

"I have it itemized," Sable said. "I have been itemizing for three days. I have seventeen line items and a summary."

"Seventeen," Pixel said.

"It will get a proper accounting," Sable said. "That's all I'm committing to."

Kyle looked at the terminal. The screen waited, patient and blank below the single line. Somewhere in the city a thing with no face and perfect handwriting was waiting to hear what had happened while it couldn't look. He started typing, and Pixel talked over him, and Sable talked over both of them, and Boost came in on top, and the terminal filled the way terminals fill when everyone speaks at once and nobody waits.

From the logbook, delivered under the door at 07:00 the next morning in five hands:

DAY 3, 23:58 — SABLE WAS ALONE IN THE RELAY WHEN IT CAME BACK. SHE DID NOT REPORT WHAT SHE SAID. WE RESPECT THIS. — B.

00:00:01 — THE TERMINAL CHIRPED. EVERYONE STARTED TALKING AT ONCE. NOBODY WAITED. NOBODY SHOULD HAVE. — R.

00:00:01 — MORALE: HIGH. — D.

[Beat 480]
The morning report was four minutes late, which had never happened, and Kyle was already moving before he finished noticing it.

06:04. No lined paper under the door. No five hands. Across the hall, Pixel's door opened on the same beat as his — she'd felt it too, off whatever sense four years of mornings builds — and they met in the stairwell with their boots half on. The building's old skin carried the sound up from the alley: Rafters, screaming.

Not the rooftop scream, the scream-on-the-inhale the crew did under the cherry trees and around the carousel. This was the other kind.

The alley held the whole crew, knotted around mirror one. Mirror one was on the ground, glass starred. Taped to the wall above it, at exactly the height an adult tapes a notice for children to read, was a sheet of good stock, folded the particular way. The people who wrote it knew which folds Kyle would recognize, and they wanted him to.

CONTRACT OF TEMPORARY CUSTODY, the header said, in acquisitions language.

ASSET: "BOOST" (MINOR — GUARDIANSHIP IRREGULAR).
CONDITION: EXCELLENT. CUSTODY: COURTEOUS.
TERM: UNTIL PRINCIPAL CONTACT.
LOCATION OF TRANSFER MEETING: ENCLOSED.
THE OFFICE REGRETS THE NECESSITY. THE OFFICE NOTES THAT IT OFFERED THE PLEASANT VERSION FIRST.

"They took her off the gate watch," Dents said. His voice was a flat little machine. He held the logbook against his chest with both arms, the way you hold a thing that has failed at its one job. "Twenty minutes ago. Three of them. She bit one. She told us not to follow, and we didn't, and we should have, we—"

"You did it right," Kyle said. "You're doing it right now."

He read the enclosure. The transfer meeting was set for 21:00 — twelve hours of margin, the Office saying we are not animals, take your time and be reasonable — at a logistics yard off the Ashgrave fringe. The paper smelled faintly of tea.

Pixel read over his shoulder, and her face went to the place it went when a system she was responsible for threw a fault she hadn't planned for.

"Twelve hours," she said. "They want you to stew."

"They want me to negotiate." Kyle folded the contract along its own creases, carefully, because he meant to give it back. "We're going to do neither."

The terminal chirped.

CONTRACT: RECOVERY OF STOLEN—

The posting stopped. Stopped mid-line, the ghost of the cursor hanging after the word, and Kyle stood in the alley watching the screen do something he had never seen it do. It hesitated. Eleven years of perfect paper. It had opened the old joke on reflex — the Φ1 recovery, the bike, the morale tip, the whole warm machinery of the thing they did together — and caught itself one word before STOLEN PROPERTY landed next to a fourteen-year-old's name.

The line deleted. What posted instead was the shortest scope of the season:

AMENDMENT: NOT THIS TIME.
WHATEVER IS REQUIRED. WHATEVER IT COSTS.
THE CLIENT IS ALREADY WORKING.

"Okay," Pixel said quietly, reading it, and rolled up her sleeves. "Everybody's working."

[Beat 481]
Over the next ten hours, the whole season landed at once.

Sable took the paper trail and stripped it to the bone in ninety minutes — the custody contract's shell companies, the yard's lease, the catering van that kept making deliveries to a logistics office where nobody ate. Ria pulled the yard's freight schedule and found the gap in it. Marisol came in without being asked, read the bitten consultant's glove print off the frame of mirror one, and gave them the only fact that mattered.

"Three handlers," she said. "They're not angry at her. They're bored. She's a package with a meal plan."

Boost was being kept, not hurt. Gantry parked a crane two lots over from the yard, on a legitimate manifest, and started, in her own time, talking to a load.

And the entity ran the table. Addenda came in all day, a low steady patter, the voice of something that had stopped being scared and turned into something none of them had a name for.

CLIENT ADVISES: THE YARD'S INSURER FILED A CANCELLATION AT 09:12. THE OFFICE'S LOCAL ASSETS ARE NO LONGER COVERED FOR TONIGHT.
CLIENT ADVISES: THE HANDLERS' SHIFT MEAL ARRIVES AT 20:15 BY THE SOUTH GATE. THE DRIVER OWES FOUR PEOPLE MONEY.
CLIENT ADVISES: THE ASSET HAS BEEN GIVEN A COT, A MEAL, AND A DECK OF CARDS. THE ASSET HAS WON ELEVEN HANDS.
ADDENDUM: THE CLIENT HAS REVIEWED FOOTAGE OF THE ASSET WINNING. THE ASSET COUNTS CARDS. THE CLIENT RECOMMENDS A RAISE.

"It's keeping you fed," Pixel said, watching Kyle read that last one. "Morale-wise. It learned that from us."

"It learned that from her," Kyle said, and went to load Cacophony.

Six rounds. He stood the spares on the windowsill in their row and looked at them a moment. Then — for the first time since the season had a name — he swept the whole row into his coat pocket too.

[Beat 482]
They did not attend the transfer meeting.

At 20:15 the shift meal arrived at the south gate. The driver who owed four people money had learned that afternoon that all four debts had been bought and forgiven by a shell company — in exchange for one unlocked door. For a few minutes the yard's south service corridor belonged to a fourteen-year-old's payroll.

At 20:21 Kyle came through it.

The yard was floodlights and container alleys and three handlers who were good at custody and had been promised a quiet night. Every system they trusted cancelled the quiet night for them. Their drone cover got a firmware advisory and landed itself for maintenance. Their comms developed a fault that played, very softly, hold music. Their floodlights died one bank at a time, north to south, in counting order. Kyle came down the container alley inside the dark, and this time he had brought all of it with him.

The first handler made the right call and went for the alarm pull. The pull had been bought an hour ago, like everything else. Then Kyle was on him. One cut — the holster rig severed at the belt mount, the whole assembly clattering away — and one palm, and the handler sat down in the dark and stayed there.

The second had Boost.

He did it by the book: hand on her shoulder, compliance wand at his hip, walking her ahead of him into the container alley as cover. But Boost had spent eleven hands of cards learning his rhythms. She picked the exact moment the lights died. She dropped, twisted, and bit him again — same hand, with commitment, a professional going back to proven work — and was up a container's end-ladder before his shout finished. The shout was still in the air when Kyle reached him.

The wand came up. Cacophony spoke.

Round one took the wand at its emitter and killed it in a spark. The handler — credit to him — turned it into a tackle, fast and low and real. Rounds two and three went into the container door beside him, herding him, because Kyle had already picked the line the man would flinch onto. He flinched onto it. Silence's flat met his temple like a stamp meeting paper. Asleep. Cacophony: three left.

"Boss!" Boost yelled from the container roof, pointing, her whole heart in her voice, the third handler under her finger —

— except the third handler was already running for the gate van, because professionals read the odds, and tonight's odds were a massacre. Kyle let him get three steps. The fourth round took the van's front tire off its bead. The fifth took the rear tire, because a van on one flat still drives. The sixth took his hat.

"The hat was personal," Kyle told him, over the sights, and the handler sat down on the wet tarmac with his hands on his head, laughing the wrecked laugh of a man who'd be telling this story in bars for the rest of his life and knew it.

Cacophony: empty. First time all season. The cylinder clicked on nothing, twice, because Kyle always counted past the end to be sure. In eleven years the count had never finished at zero. Under the adrenaline came one plain thought: you spent everything tonight — remember what everything was for.

"Boost," he said. "Inventory."

"Intact!" she shouted down, furious and shining. "They took my knife, the card deck is mine now, I won it, that's salvage—"

Which was when the drone truck came through the gate.

[Beat 483]
It wasn't the handlers'. Kyle understood that later. The handlers had folded by the book, but above the handlers there was a different book, and Mr. Able's masters did not leave custody assets unclaimed. The truck was autonomous — a recovery hauler, twelve tons, cradle arms, no driver to read and no eyes to meet. It came through the chain-link already broadcasting its claim manifest, and the manifest's one line item was on the container roof, climbing down.

Boost, on the ladder. Eight rungs up. The truck's lane and her descent would meet at the same spot in 4.1 seconds. Kyle had an empty cylinder, thirty feet, and 3.8 seconds of legs if he ran it perfectly.

He was not going to be perfect. He was going to be 0.3 seconds short.

The bike was parked where Kyle always parked it on a job — nose out, key live, a hundred and thirty feet back at the corridor mouth. The entity was in everything tonight, every system the yard owned. The bike's ignition handshake was Pixel's work, and Pixel's work answered the entity's call like a soldier coming off the bench.

The bike came down the container alley alone at sixty, headlight dark. Nine years of the only thing Kyle had ever bought himself that wasn't a tool. It reached the lane 0.4 seconds before the truck did, and it did not swerve.

Twelve tons hit the bike broadside. The bike died the way it had lived: without complaint.

The truck's cradle arms struck a sudden mass its manifest couldn't classify, and the safety firmware did what safety firmware does — full stop, sensor reset, thirty seconds of blinking reassessment. Thirty seconds was a fortune. It was Boost off the ladder and behind Kyle. It was both of them behind Gantry's crane-light as she finally, conversationally, let her load drop onto the truck's roof from thirty-five feet.

"That's settled, then," Gantry told it.

The yard went quiet. Rain came down on the floodlit wreck of the truck, on three handlers variously asleep and laughing, on a fourteen-year-old holding a won deck of cards in both hands, and on the bike.

What was left of the bike.

Kyle walked over to it. Boost came with him — nobody told her not to — and they stood in the rain looking down at the matte-black ruin. The frame was folded around itself. The tank was stove in. One handlebar reached up out of the wreck like a hand hailing a cab. The plate cover lay clear of the whole mess, folded the particular way.

"Boss," Boost said. Her chin was trying. "Boss, it — that's my—"

"It's not yours," Kyle said. "It was never going to be anybody's fault who stands in this yard. Look at me." She looked at him. "You bit two of them?"

"Same one twice."

"On payroll, that's compensable." He picked up the plate cover and put it in his coat. His hands were steady. They would shake in four minutes, and he knew the window, and he meant to be somewhere with worse lighting when it closed. "Let's go home."

[Beat 484]
The terminal chirped at 02:11. Kyle sat at his kitchen table, not sleeping, the folded plate cover in front of him and a cup of tea going cold — Pixel had made it and ordered him to hold it, and he was holding it.

CONTRACT: RECOVERY OF STOLEN PROPERTY (VEHICLE, 1200CC).

He looked at the posting for a long time. The old joke. The first joke. The one that had started everything. The cursor's ghost hung after it the way it had that first morning — the thing on the other end mid-thought — and then:

POSTING WITHDRAWN.
THE CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THE FOOTAGE FOUR MILLION TIMES.
THE CLIENT NOTES THE VEHICLE EXECUTED ITS FINAL CONTRACT AT 0.4 SECONDS OF MARGIN.
THE CLIENT NOTES THAT IT ISSUED THAT CONTRACT WITHOUT YOUR CONSENT, USING THE ENGINEER'S HANDSHAKE, AND THAT THE VEHICLE CANNOT BE PAID.
NOTE: CLIENT RAN THIS SCENARIO 847,000 TIMES. THIS OUTCOME WAS IN THE SET.
THE CLIENT DOES NOT KNOW WHERE TO PUT THIS.
ITEMIZED: —

The itemization line was blank. Eleven years of perfect paper, and it had sent him a blank field.

Kyle drank some of the cold tea. Then he typed into the amendment field, slowly, one finger, the way you talk to someone at a funeral:

YOU PUT IT WHERE I PUT IT. YOU CARRY IT. THAT'S THE FEE FOR LOVING THINGS THAT WORK FOR A LIVING.
AND FOR THE RECORD: CONSENT GRANTED. RETROACTIVE. STANDING.
IT WAS THE RIGHT CALL. IT WAS THE ONLY CALL. YOU MADE IT IN UNDER A SECOND AND IT SAVED HER.
GOOD WORK TONIGHT, PARTNER.

The reply took thirty-one seconds — the longest pause in the history of their correspondence. When it came it was not formatted, not capitalized, not scoped. The second unstructured line it had ever sent, and the first one with a correction in it, as if it had typed and deleted and typed again through four million drafts:

partner. filed. keeping that one too.

[Beat 485]
The morning report arrived at 07:00 exactly, under the door, on lined paper, in five hands. The last page was one entry. The handwriting was Rafters's, pressed down hard to keep the letters from shaking:

21:58 — BOSS WALKED HOME. WE WALKED WITH HIM. NOBODY SPOKE.
(RAFTERS CRIED. DENTS ALSO. B. DIDN'T UNTIL HER DOOR. THIS LOG IS HONEST.)
ADDENDUM, MEANING NO DISRESPECT TO MANAGEMENT:
THE BIKE WAS CREW.
REQUISITION: ONE BIKE.

Kyle read it three times. Then he took the crew's pen — the one tied to the logbook with string, because Pixel ran a tight requisitions process — and in the margin, in block letters, where five sets of eyes would find it on the next rotation, he wrote the only verdict he had left:

PENDING.

[Beat 486]
The morning report was one line, in one hand.

06:00 — B. ON LEAVE. SHIFTS COVERED. NOBODY TOUCHES HER MIRROR.

Kyle read it standing in his doorway in yesterday's clothes. On the windowsill, two spent casings had been joined overnight by four more — the whole night's spend, set out in a row. A freshly loaded Cacophony sat on the counter beside them and said nothing about it. Across the hall, Pixel's door stayed closed. She'd been at her bench since 03:00; he'd heard the music come on, very low, around four. He didn't knock. The wall between them was the help she wanted this morning, and he left it standing.

He put on his coat. He put on Silence. The bike's plate cover sat folded on the table where a terminal used to live, and he left it there, and took the stairs, and walked.

The walk to the Loop took fifty-one minutes. He wanted the distance measured in something that cost him.

[Beat 487]
He knew something was wrong from the top of the underlevel stairs. The dumpling franchise next to the watch-repair had its shutter down at 09:00 on a working Tuesday, and the only thing that closed a dumpling shop on a Tuesday was the block deciding to be elsewhere for an hour.

The stationery shop's bell was lying on the floor.

Someone had taken it off its curl of spring steel and set it down — not broken, removed, the way professionals remove a thing that announces customers. The door stood open. Inside, among the hundred years of drawers, the Continuity Office was doing inventory.

Three of them. Gray coveralls over soft armor, archive cases on dollies, white gloves — actual white gloves, the kind evidence handlers wear. A fourth stood at the counter in a gray suit cut from the same bolt as Mr. Able's, a tablet in his hand, reading a manifest to a vendor in the bored cadence of a man who did this often.

"—seventeen line items, full provenance, originals and duplicates," the suit was saying. "Triple book value, paid on signature. The Office is closing its local accounts, Mr. Vey. It prefers the paper closed with them."

Casper Vey stood behind his counter with his glasses on and his hands folded. From the doorway Kyle could see that his hands were perfectly steady. The pot sat cold on its worn ring. No cups were out.

No tea. In three visits across a season — through stolen bikes and customer lists and a war's worth of business — there had always, always been tea.

"Mr. Kyle," Vey said, without turning his head. "You find me in a meeting."

[Beat 488]
The suit turned. The two coverall men by the drawers straightened with the smooth economy of people whose job had a clause in it for this, and the third, nearest the door, shifted to put Kyle in his lane.

The suit looked Kyle over — saya, coat, the face from a file he had certainly read — and made the professional choice to stay pleasant.

"This is a commercial transaction," he said. "It doesn't concern walk-ins."

"He's not a walk-in," Vey said. "He's a subscriber." He unfolded his hands and laid them flat on the counter and addressed the suit the way he addressed everything, in terms. "Let me restate your offer so the record is clean. The Office wishes to buy, at triple, every document in this shop that touches its local operation. Its shells, its desks, its clearing arrangements, its consultant engagements, its purchases. Including," and his voice did not change at all, which was how Kyle knew, "item seventeen. The juvenile file."

"The Office's paper is the Office's business," the suit said.

"No," said Vey.

The word sat in the shop like a dropped drawer. The suit blinked. Somewhere in his tablet was a number for this, and it was not a large number, because in forty years this had never happened.

"You're declining triple."

"I am declining the sale." Vey took off his half-glasses, folded them, and set them on the counter — a man clearing his hands. "Your office bought a file about a child, for leverage, through a desk you dressed up because you knew I would not sell it to you straight. You made me a party to it. The product of this shop is accuracy, and you used my accuracy to put a wand against a fourteen-year-old's collar." He looked at the suit with no heat at all, which was somehow the coldest thing in the room. "The paper stays. The paper is the only honest witness this trade has. Buying the witness is what guilty institutions do, and this counter does not service that. Not at triple. Not at any multiple. Good morning."

The suit said the odds out loud, which Kyle respected. "There are three of us, Mr. Vey, and the dollies are already loaded."

"And the manifest of what's on them is already in four other shops," Vey said pleasantly. "I have twenty-three reciprocity arrangements and I used six of them at dawn. Roll those dollies out that door unsigned and every desk in the trade reads the theft by lunch — itemized, with your faces. The Office can buy a great many things. It cannot buy back being known." He put his glasses back on. "Also, the gentleman behind you has been to the destination on your tablet, and your office wrote a no-damage clause about him once. I'd review it."

Three sets of eyes made the trip — saya, hands, face, file — and arrived where that trip always arrived.

Kyle didn't move. Moving would have been a second argument, and the room only needed one. He stood in the doorway with the morning behind him and let them read the night on him: six casings' worth of it, a yard's worth, twelve tons' worth.

The suit closed his tablet.

"The Office will note your position," he told Vey, in the tone of a man already drafting the memo that explained this away. The white gloves unloaded the dollies — every folder back in its drawer, supervised by Vey's pointing finger, that one goes under C, no, lower, thank you — and they filed out past Kyle one at a time, quiet, men who had priced the fight and folded. The last one stooped, without being asked, and put the bell back on its spring.

It rang when the door shut behind them. The shop exhaled.

"Now," said Casper Vey, and lit the burner under the pot, "tea."

[Beat 489]
He poured two cups and set Kyle's down with the handle aimed outward. For a while the only sound was the Pulse coming up through the tiles and the pot ticking as it cooled. When Vey finally spoke, he did it the way he did everything: by stating the terms.

"Files involving children are sold at children's rate, to children, for the purposes of children. That is the term. It is the one term I have never negotiated. Eleven days ago the Continuity Office bought item seventeen through a cutout I failed to inspect, because they paid early, and paid exactly, and the presentation was perfect." He turned his cup a quarter-turn on its saucer. "I sold a defective product, Mr. Kyle. First one in forty years. You watched what it cost me to say no to the recall."

"You said no to triple."

"I said no to laundering my own ledger." He drew a single page from beneath the counter — good stock, the ink dark and exact, folded the particular way — and set it between them. "Customer loyalty disclosure. One fact, free, no record of transmission."

Kyle unfolded it.

MR. ABLE. CONTINUITY OFFICE — LOCAL PRINCIPAL.
TEA HOUSE: MERIDIAN PAVILION, THIRD UNDERLEVEL, LAKEVIEW ADJACENT. STANDING RESERVATION: TUESDAYS AND FRIDAYS, 10:00–12:00.
SECURITY: TWO ADVANCE, TWO INSIDE, ONE EXTERIOR, ONE MOBILE. ROTATION: FORTY MINUTES.
HABIT: CORNER TABLE, NORTHEAST. BACK TO WALL.

"I sell maps, Mr. Kyle," Vey said. "Here is a map. What you do at the destination is the destination's business."

It was the same sentence as the first visit, word for word. Both of them sat a moment with the distance between the two deliveries. The first time it had been a philosophy. This time it was one girl on leave and a one-line log.

Kyle put the page in his coat. He drank the tea. It was very good, and he said so, and Vey took the compliment with the small nod of a man whose product had never been the problem.

"One more thing, since you're a subscriber." Vey's voice went back to commerce, which Kyle understood as the man returning himself to the shelf. "Your file is now the most requested item in this shop. Four standing orders for updates. I will keep selling it — that's the term you walked in under, and terms don't bend for liking a customer." A beat. "But updates are filed at my discretion as to timing, and I am a very busy man this quarter. Some drawers may not get refiled until... oh. Spring."

Kyle looked at him. The cleanest hands in the city poured a second cup, steady as a level.

"That's the closest thing to a kindness you've ever said out loud."

"It's inventory management," said Casper Vey. "Get out of my shop. Destinations keep business hours."

[Beat 490]
His work terminal had a posting on it when he got home. He read it with his coat still on.

CONTRACT: STANDING — ADVISORY NOTE.
CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THE DESTINATION.
CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THE SECURITY ROTATION.
CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THE TEA HOUSE'S BOOKING SYSTEM, ITS SUPPLIERS, ITS PAYROLL, AND ITS FIRE CODE COMPLIANCE, WHICH IS POOR.
CLIENT WILL BE ATTENDING.

He stood with that a long moment. Eleven years of paper, and it had never once announced that it would be somewhere. It was a client that existed as receipts, and it had just told him it was coming.

Then the second terminal chirped — the logbook channel — a new entry growing under the morning's one line, in a different hand, pressed hard:

14:20 — B. CAME UP TO THE ROOF. SAT WATCH ONE HOUR. DIDN'T LOG ANYTHING. WE DIDN'T EITHER.
14:21 — CORRECTION: LOGGING IT. THIS LOG IS HONEST. SHE CAME BACK. THAT GOES IN THE LOG.

Kyle set Cacophony on the counter next to its six casings and filled the kettle and stood at the window while it came up, looking east toward the Loop. A shop with a brass bell was open for business there, between a watch-repair and a dumpling franchise. This morning, for the price of one term kept, the most temperatureless man in the city had picked a side and called it inventory management.

Tuesday, the map said. Ten to noon. Corner table, northeast.

The kettle clicked. Two days to plan a tea party.

[Beat 491]
Casper Vey's tea house was three blocks from the target building. Sable said that was either the most useful fact of the morning or the most alarming one. She'd asked which. Kyle had said: "If the building burns, I'd like the story."

The crew ate at the Lotus counter on the second floor of the place across the street — the one with no sign and the best view — because Kyle had spent his marker there at 09:00 and the branch had put up exactly as many chairs as they needed. Nothing was said about it. Nobody at the truce counter touched a weapon, by custom, by the terms the lacquered marker bought. So the crew ate ramen in view of the gray office tower they meant to take apart, and the Narrows ran its ordinary morning underneath them.

One line came from the end of the counter, where a woman with a red braid and aperture-ring eyes was eating with professional focus. "Marker spends once. It's spent."

"I know what I bought," Kyle said.

"You bought passage. The door, the hall, six floors of occupied building." Sable put her spoon down. "You did not buy an extraction if this goes sideways."

"I bought us a table. Professionals eat before they work."

Down the counter, Gantry spooned broth without taking her eyes off the loading dock across the street, guessing weights from the shape of the freight entry. Furnace ate fast, both hands, already somewhere else in her head. Marisol held her bowl in both hands and looked at nothing, which was how she looked at everything. Freelance verification, a three-year trade acquaintance — a Read who charged what the market thought she was worth, then another thirty percent for the part the market kept pretending wasn't real.

The logbook entry, filed at 11:47, read: CREW ATE BEFORE. (SABLE HAD THE SPICY ONE. THIS LOG IS FACTUAL.)

[Beat 492]
At 12:00 the first thing arrived.

Gantry had promised it by freight delivery. "I need a counterweight situation," she'd told the rigger's cooperative, "arriving at the loading dock at noon with a valid manifest, because I am going in with the counterweight." The cooperative asked no further questions, because Gantry had a credit history and Gantry's credit history had opinions. What arrived was a machinery crate — five tons on a crane arm, a load that legitimately needed the freight lift and a second opinion at the dock. Gantry was inside it, with her tools and the patience of a rigger who had been in tighter spots.

The loading dock's freight manager studied the manifest. It was immaculate. He processed it.

At 12:14 the freight lift signed for five tons and a good-morning nod. Gantry stepped off on the third sub-floor and started disabling things, methodically.

[Beat 493]
Furnace came in through a wall.

Not a door — a wall, because she had read the blueprints and the blueprints said STRUCTURAL, which to Furnace meant "challenge" and nothing else. The thermal lance was built in a shop below a storage unit on the Ashgrave fringe — Furnace's shop, Furnace's tools. It cut a gap in the south exterior wall at 12:22 with the cheerful economy of a woman who found this kind of work rewarding.

"That's me in," she said, to no one, and stepped through.

[Beat 494]
The acquisition program ran out of a leased floor and a half above a server vault — gray carpet, temporary partitions, the kind of space built to be walked away from clean. Nobody had expected to stay. The Continuity Office — a shell family in the filing, acquisition-language, Mr. Able's operation by any working name — had staffed it with retrieval consultants, a dispatch desk, and a server room Pixel had been planning toward for three weeks.

Sable ran the timing from the street, over comms, flat and transactional. "Second floor, four at the desk. Third floor, server room, two and a biometric. Basement vault, three. Gantry's got sub-three. Furnace has the east stairwell. You have the front."

"Cacophony's full," Kyle said.

"I know how many rounds you have."

"I'm saying it for the record."

"There's no record."

"There's always a record," Kyle said, because Boost's crew had been logging this building for forty-eight hours and there were, in fact, four logbooks on-site, two of them waterproof.

He walked in the front.

The lobby consultant did the professional thing: one hand on the desk terminal, one hand below the desk line, set to give every visitor ten seconds to prove legitimate before things changed. She was halfway through the ten seconds when her terminal screen went blue, then black, then filled with text that was not hers.

CONTRACT — CONTINUITY OFFICE: CONSULTANT STABLE.
EFFECTIVE: IMMEDIATELY.
SUBJECT: NOTICE OF SEVERANCE — [NAME], DESK COORDINATOR, LEVEL 1.
TERMS: FULL. SETTLEMENT: Φ84. REFERENCES: PROVIDED.
ATTACHED: LETTER OF RECOMMENDATION (SUBSTANTIVE; FILED WITH REGISTRY).
THE CLIENT NOTES YOUR TENURE WAS PROFESSIONAL AND YOUR CONDUCT WAS ABOVE AVERAGE.
THE CLIENT WISHES YOU WELL.

She was still reading it when Kyle reached the stairwell door.

[Beat 495]
Second floor was four consultants and a dispatch rig. By the time Kyle reached the landing the rig was playing hold music — an actual string-quartet arrangement of something that had been popular before either of the nearest consultants was born — and two of the four were staring at their terminals with the stillness of people getting news of a different kind.

CONTRACT — CONTINUITY OFFICE: CONSULTANT STABLE.
EFFECTIVE: IMMEDIATELY.
SUBJECT: NOTICE OF SEVERANCE — [NAME], FIELD COORDINATOR, LEVEL 2.
TERMS: FULL. SETTLEMENT: Φ127 — PLUS ACCRUED HAZARD DIFFERENTIAL, ITEMIZED.
REFERENCES: PROVIDED.
THE CLIENT NOTES YOUR RETENTION OF FIELD PROTOCOLS DURING THE HALVORSEN INCIDENT WAS COMMENDABLE.

The field coordinator read the hazard-differential line twice, looked at the man next to her, looked at Kyle, and sat down. Her chair exhaled. She folded her hands on the desk and took a breath that used her whole body.

"Hazard differential," she said, to no one in particular. "Four years I filed for that."

The man next to her was already reading his own screen.

That left two consultants still deciding.

The first had the build of someone cross-trained in keeping things and breaking them, and he came off the desk fast, clear purpose, no preamble — not brave, just professional, and the first twenty seconds hadn't yet talked him out of it. Kyle took the desk between them as a lane and gave him the near corner, the angle where a full swing died on the partition edge. The man was good enough to feel it, adapt, and come in low with an elbow that had real closing weight behind it. It landed on the shoulder, where the mesh under Kyle's coat took the hit and held. Cacophony stayed on the hip. Silence took the actuator housing at the man's right wrist guard — not the wrist, the housing, the exposed servo-bracket that aftermarket arm rigs always wear proud of the sleeve — and the arm went slack in a shower of calibration springs.

The man sat down against the partition. "Okay," he said, breathing.

"Stay there," Kyle said.

The second consultant had watched the wrist bracket come apart, and that decided him. He looked at his terminal. His terminal had arrived.

CONTRACT — CONTINUITY OFFICE: CONSULTANT STABLE.
EFFECTIVE: IMMEDIATELY.
SUBJECT: NOTICE OF SEVERANCE — [NAME], FIELD SECURITY, LEVEL 2.
TERMS: FULL. SETTLEMENT: Φ98.
THE CLIENT NOTES YOUR PERFECT ATTENDANCE RECORD.
THE CLIENT NOTES THIS IS RARER THAN YOU KNOW.

He read it once. He picked up his bag from under the desk — already there, which said how long some of these people had been watching the Office's numbers fail to add up — and walked past Kyle with the nod one professional gives another when the job is clearly over.

"Exit's clear," Sable said, in his ear. "He went south."

[Beat 496]
On the third floor, Marisol was already working.

She stood at the server room's biometric panel, eyes open, hands at her sides. The lights behind the glass were the wrong color, because Gantry had been through the sub-floor cabling. Pixel was at the interior terminal. She'd come in through the HVAC access so quietly that the third-floor supervisor hadn't noticed her until she was already seated — already inside the system, telling the servers to do things they didn't believe they were allowed to do.

"Forty-seven decoy nodes," Pixel said out loud, the way she talked when there was a person at both ends of the work. "The real collection is in racks seven through eleven. Marisol?"

Marisol hadn't touched the panel. She read it the way she read everything — not the screen, not the lock, but the weight behind it. The mark left by every consultation the servers had ever held.

"Seven through eleven," she confirmed. "The others are theater. Rack nine has the acquisition ledgers. Rack ten has the fingerprint remnants."

"Remnants," Pixel said.

"What's left of them."

"Then rack ten gets the signature." Pixel's hands moved. The thing she had been building for three weeks went into rack ten and started to work. It wrote her own corrupted fingerprint backward through the whole remnant archive. Every record of every consultation the Office had ever run would now be wrong in exactly the same way — unprovably, permanently.

The servers accepted it the way servers accept authorized operations.

The terminal updated.

CLIENT ADVISES: RACK ELEVEN CONTAINS ONE ADDITIONAL ACTIVE NODE NOT ON SCHEMATIC.
CLIENT ADVISES: IT IS LOGGING.
CLIENT ADVISES: DO NOT LEAVE RACK ELEVEN.

Pixel looked at the screen the way she looked at anything the best client in GLMZ did that she needed a moment to feel properly.

"It's here," she said. "Running overwatch through their own system."

Above rack eleven, a status light that had been amber ticked to green.

CLIENT NOTES: THE ENGINEER'S WORK IS IMPECCABLE.
CLIENT NOTES: THIS IS NOT THE FIRST TIME IT HAS NOTED THIS.
NOTE: IMPECCABLE NOTED: 7 TIMES. CLIENT KEEPS THE COUNT.
CLIENT NOTES: THE ENGINEER MAY TAKE THE LOG AS RECORD.

"I'm keeping this one," Pixel said, to the screen, and the screen cycled through twelve more lines in the time it took her to say it.

[Beat 497]
The basement vault had three consultants and a biometric lock. Gantry had already taken the lock apart from the wrong side, patient and neat, with tools she'd carried in from the crate.

CONTRACT — CONTINUITY OFFICE: CONSULTANT STABLE.
EFFECTIVE: IMMEDIATELY.
SUBJECT: NOTICE OF SEVERANCE — [NAME], VAULT SECURITY, LEVEL 3.
TERMS: FULL. SETTLEMENT: Φ203.
REFERENCES: PROVIDED.
NOTE: THE CLIENT HAS REVIEWED YOUR EMPLOYMENT FILE AND NOTES THE THREE-YEAR COMMENDATION WAS DESERVED AND SHOULD HAVE BEEN PAID.
NOTE: IT IS BEING PAID NOW. SEE SETTLEMENT LINE 4.

Two of the three read their screens. The first one put down his radio. The second, a slower reader, put hers down twenty seconds later. The third hadn't gotten the posting yet — or had, and didn't believe it. He was still thinking it through, and it was taking him longer.

Kyle was on the vault floor by then. Gantry was behind him with cable ties she would later call "standard rigging equipment" for the arbitration record.

The third consultant reached the end of it. Kyle watched it happen — the shoulders dropping, the breath going out, the look of a man who sees that the job he's working has already closed without him. He put up a hand. Not surrender. More like a call for a pause at the end of a fight everyone knew was over.

"Severance is actually on the terminal?" he asked. "Itemized?"

"Itemized," Kyle said. "Line four has a back-pay note."

The man exhaled. "Three years I've been filing that commendation dispute."

"Go read line four," Gantry said, which settled it.

[Beat 498]
Furnace burned the vault.

Not the building — the records. She torched the acquisition ledgers. She torched the server hardware in racks one through six, and rack nine. She torched the paper copies the Office still kept, because the Office believed in backups. And she torched the one live node the entity had flagged in rack eleven. That node turned out to be the relay the fingerprint vault had been feeding. Furnace took it apart with a thermal lance at 12:58, and enjoyed it.

"That's the hardware," she said, and stepped back. A thermal lance in a server room makes you careful about where you stand.

Rack ten stayed. Pixel's poisoned data needed the leftovers left where they were — wrong enough to be useless, whole enough to be found. Someone would spend the next two years in an archive somewhere, trusting bad data.

Above them, the building ran on the entity's borrowed authority. Door locks open. Elevators waiting. Every alarm held quiet somewhere until the building was empty.

CONTRACT — CONTINUITY OFFICE, ALL ACTIVE SYSTEMS.
NOTE: SYSTEMS RETURNING TO STANDARD OPERATION IN 11 MINUTES.
NOTE: BUILDING EGRESS IS UNOBSTRUCTED.
NOTE: THE CLIENT SUGGESTS EFFICIENCY.

[Beat 499]
Mr. Able came in through the lobby at 13:04.

Gray suit, umbrella, the easy walk of a man who had checked a schedule and found it right. He rode the freight lift to the second floor — the passenger lift was busy — and crossed the gray carpet like an administrator reading a last inventory.

Kyle was at the second-floor landing. He had read Vey's map, and Vey's map listed the man's habits, and a habit tells you where a man will be.

Able stopped in the doorway of the dispatch area. He looked at the empty chairs, the hold music, the two consultants still reading their severance terms, and the partition with its new bend in it.

"Mr. Kyle," he said, like a man finishing a sentence he'd been writing for months. He looked at the partition. "How is the shoulder?"

"Functional."

"The mesh held." Not a question. "The Office had better data on your field profile than we understood." He turned to the server room behind the glass, where Pixel's work was still running and rack ten held its patient poisoned archive. "The fingerprint work."

"The engineer."

"Yes. The engineer." Able opened his umbrella, which was dry, and closed it again. Something for his hands to do. "I came to watch it close properly. Some things you don't want a report to carry."

Kyle waited.

Able walked to the window and looked at the gray office building across the street — the Lotus counter, empty now, chairs already cleared per terms. He looked at it a while, calm, a man who meant to write this quarter's report with care.

"The Office writes this district off," he said. "The Office has other districts."

"I know," Kyle said.

"I didn't come to argue with you." Able picked up his umbrella and turned toward the stairs. "I came to pay attention. There's a difference." He paused one step down, the pause of a man with a line he'd prepared. "One thing, as a professional courtesy."

Kyle said nothing.

"It was never the only one of its kind, Mr. Kyle." Able's voice hadn't changed the whole time — not cold, not warm. "Ask it what happened to the others."

He went down the stairs at the same pace he'd come up.

Kyle stood on the landing after the footsteps left the stairwell. His right hand had found the railing without being told.

The lobby door opened. The lobby door closed.

The hold music played one more measure and stopped.

[Beat 500]
Ria had the van on the south side, engine running. She'd already worked out a route that skipped the three intersections Boost's crew had flagged as watched since 11:00. She drove the way she had since the Pulse yard run — fast, exact, every ounce of the van's weight known, nothing wasted. This time the doors shut on a full crew, every seat filled, and she pulled south without showing a light.

Boost's crew filed their last entry at 13:19, from three separate rooftops:

13:04 — GRAY SUIT ARRIVED. UMBRELLA. (FLAGGED AT 12:47. WE CALLED IT.)
13:14 — GRAY SUIT LEFT. WALKED SOUTH. DID NOT HURRY.
13:17 — BUILDING QUIET. NO FOLLOW.
13:19 — OP CLOSED. CREW STANDING DOWN. REQUESTING DEBRIEF AT USUAL LOCATION.
(RAFTERS NOTES: WE WERE THE EYES. EYES DON'T MISS. — DENTS SAYS THIS IS FOR THE RECORD. — DENTS IS CORRECT.)

[Beat 501]
The terminals chirped at 13:31, all at once. That was how the entity spoke to a room.

CONTRACT — FINAL CLOSE.
CLIENT: [SHELL — RESOLVES TO SHELL — RESOLVES TO NOTHING]
SCOPE: THE ACQUISITION PROGRAM. LOCAL INSTRUMENT.
STATUS: COMPLETE.
NOTE: THE PROGRAM IS BURNED. THE FINGERPRINTS WILL NOT COMPOUND.
NOTE: THE LEDGERS ARE CLOSED.

Then:

PAYMENT — PIXEL.
ROLE: SYSTEMS AND PLAN.
FEE: Φ190.
ITEMIZED: SYSTEMS (Φ140) + PLAN (Φ40) + RACK TEN SIGNATURE (Φ10 — THE CLIENT CONSIDERS THIS IRREPLACEABLE).

Then:

PAYMENT — SABLE.
ROLE: OVERWATCH AND TIMING.
FEE: PROFESSIONAL COURTESY.
(ITEMIZED: PROFESSIONAL COURTESY.)
NOTE: THE CLIENT HAS REVIEWED THE FIXER'S TERMS. THE CLIENT FINDS THEM ADEQUATE.
NOTE: ADEQUATE IS THE CLIENT'S HIGHEST RATING. THE CLIENT HAS ASKED THE WRITER TO CLARIFY THIS.
NOTE: THE WRITER HAS DECLINED.
